# Pseudo-Lull, Testamentum — Complete English Translation
#
# TRANSLATED from the reconstructed Latin text assembled from three period editions:
#   1. Testamentum (1566 Cologne/Byrckmann) — Theorica, Practica, Compendium, Index
#   2. Liber Mercuriorum (1567 Cologne/Byrckmann) — 52 chapters on mercury preparations
#   3. Practica de Furnis (1600 Basel/Libelli aliquot Chemici) — 27 chapters on furnaces
#
# Translation: Claude Code, March 2026
# Method: Parallel agent translation from Latin transcription, 97 chunks, 8 agents per batch
#
# ORIGINAL STRUCTURE (c. 1332, composed London, St. Katherine's near the Tower):
#   Part I:   THEORICA (96 chapters) — speculative/theoretical alchemy
#   Part II:  PRACTICA (32 chapters) — practical laboratory operations
#   Part III: LIBER MERCURIORUM (52 chapters) — mercury preparations
#   Part IV:  PRACTICA DE FURNIS (27 chapters) — furnace operations
#
# APPENDIX (separate text, included in 1566 edition):
#   COMPENDIUM ANIMAE TRANSMVTATIONIS
#   INDEX (covers Theorica + Practica only)
#
# Note: Page headers retained from the Latin transcription for cross-reference.
#       Chapter headings preserved in Latin. Alchemical letter-cipher references kept as-is.

--- Page 9 (Significationes Literarum, first part) ---

SIGNIFICATIONS OF THE LETTERS

A  Signifies God.
B  Signifies Mercury.
C  Signifies saltpeter [sal petrae].
D  Signifies vitriol.
E  Signifies the menstruum [solvent].
F  Signifies bright luna [silver].
G  Signifies our Mercury.
H  Signifies pure sol [gold].
I  Signifies the composite of luna.
K  Signifies the composite of sol.
L  Signifies earth in the composition.
M  Signifies water in the composition.
N  Signifies air in the composition.

This figure is to be placed at folio 2v.

--- Page 10 (folio A ij recto, Epistola) ---

TO ALL
SEEKERS OF
the chemical art, Ludolph Ver-
demann sends greetings.

Since I saw, most excellent lovers of natural and occult arts, that many counterfeit books and false recipes, as they call them, were circulating everywhere among students of alchemy, by which very many have been deceived and are still being misled, I thought I could do nothing more useful than to set forth some work of this kind from which both the truth of nature might appear and the falsehood long established among many might become

--- Page 11 (folio A ij verso, Epistola) ---

EPISTOLA

known. Not a few friends urged me to this, whose authority I ought not entirely to scorn, nor whose wishes I should plainly disregard. And indeed, since Raymundus Lullus came into my hands -- a philosopher furnished with every kind of learning -- I was easily moved both by the friends of this art and by their honorable request to carefully transcribe his Testamentum, and to give to the printers the Compendium of the Transmutation of the Soul, expanded in many parts. The Testamentum was not printed without cause, yet the printed work confused many a generous reader and led them into error. Raymundus himself divided it into a Theorica and a Practica, and dedicated it to Edward V, King of the Eng-

--- Page 12 (folio A iij recto, Epistola) ---

EPISTOLA

lish, in the year from the birth of Christ 1432. The Compendium of the Transmutation of the Soul was indeed published at Frankfurt, but mutilated, and scarcely half complete, and disfigured by many errors. By us, however, it has been so perfected and purged that it plainly appears to be a different work from the earlier one. These books, therefore, I offer to all of you who are students of these arts, so that from them you may perceive and come to know the truth of things and nature itself, in which matter alone the perfection of the entire art consists. For here Lullus will demonstrate that very mineral source from which the true Mercury and sulfur are extracted. Do not suppose, however, that you will understand him immediately, but rather persuade yourselves for certain that gradually, with much study and diligence, you will at last come to

--- Page 13 (folio A iij verso, Epistola) ---

EPISTOLA

know his mind and hidden science most excellently. For the writings of the Philosophers are understood not from the outer shell of words but from the powers of nature. For very often they concealed beautiful and difficult things under verbal coverings and veils, lest those most excellent arts should reach those unworthy of such great mysteries. For the secret, as the author himself testifies in chapter 29, is given only to the sons of Philosophy -- that is, to inquirers and seekers of truth. Nor indeed is it possible for those to rightly understand nature who are not occupied with every thought and impulse of the mind in investigating its hidden operations.

--- Page 14 (Significationes Literarum, second part) ---

OF THIS TESTAMENT.

O  Signifies the earth of the composite of sol.
P  Signifies the water of the composite of sol.
Q  Signifies the air of the composite of sol.
R  Signifies the fire of the composite of sol.
S  Signifies the white stone.
T  Signifies the medicine of the red body.
V  Signifies the heat of the secret furnace.
X  Signifies the dry fire of ashes.
Y  Signifies the heat of the bath [balneum mariae].
Z  Signifies the separation of the liquors.
ʒ  Signifies the alembic with the cucurbit.

This figure is to be placed at folio 2v.

--- Page 15 (Diagram) ---

[DIAGRAM: Large circular cosmological/alchemical diagram with concentric rings. At the center is "Frigidus" (cold). Moving outward through concentric circles, the diagram shows: inner rings containing "Essentia" and "Aether"; middle rings with labels including "Aer densatus" (densified air), "Ignis elementaris" (elemental fire), "Ignis celestis" (celestial fire); text along curved bands reading "Solutio grossi in simplum" (dissolution of the gross into the simple), "primus R gradus" (first R degree), "Quintus & sextus" (fifth and sixth); the outermost ring contains the four elements at cardinal positions: "Ignis" (fire) at top, "Aqua" (water) at bottom, "Aer" (air) and "Terra" (earth) at sides. Additional labels include "Calor" (heat), "Æther", and various process descriptions in the curved bands between rings. The diagram illustrates the elemental and essential relationships in the alchemical theory of the Testamentum.]

--- Page 17 (Epistola, continued) ---

EPISTOLA.
For, as the same author says in chapter 31,
if the son of the art is negligent in
reading through and scrutinizing, he will
go blind to practice. Truly, perpetual
diligence is necessary for this matter,
and thus it will come about that you
perceive the mysteries of the art with
profit, namely THE MENSTRUUM OR
VEGETABLE G., by which one alone
the perfect metallic bodies are trans-
muted into prime matter, that is, into
sulphur and quicksilver: from which
thereafter true medicine is created, and
not before, as may be seen in chapter 66.
And by this diligent consideration it
will come about that you will both despise
the recipes of false Alchemists,
in which not even a spark of
truth shines forth, and follow that
path which nature in all her works

--- Page 18 (Epistola, conclusion) ---

EPISTOLA.
prescribes. This will be not only
a source of the greatest pleasure to you, but
also of the greatest usefulness. Therefore
I ask and beseech you most earnestly
that you receive my benevolent will
toward you, and my friendly admonition,
with grateful minds; and when YOU HAVE
MADE THE HIDDEN MANIFEST,
and have attained the desired end,
that in the manner of the Philosophers
you conceal all things from those alien
to this art, and give thanks to God,
thrice Greatest and Best, for this
benefit. Farewell. Cologne, the eighth
day before the Kalends of March
[February 22], in the year from the
birth of Christ 1566.

--- Page 19 (folio 1, Praefatio) ---

IN THE NAME
OF OUR LORD JE-
sus Christ, here begins the Testament
of Master Raymond Lull,
and first concerning
the Theorica.

O God, who gloriously
exist as almighty, for the sake
of loving, cherishing, and
remembering Thee, we begin
the present art, so that it may be made clear to the
sons of truth what was in part hidden from us, and
so that there may be for them the completion of so
most excellent and noble a part of philosophy,
which we shall make manifest in this our
abbreviated volume, which concerns transmutation
not merely perfect but most perfect, to which the
sophisticates can never attain, and without

--- Page 20 (folio 2, Praefatio) ---

PRAEFATIO.
cause they defame this renowned science.
And therefore we leave this said book to the
sons of our teaching in the manner of
a testament, which we divide into three
principal books, namely the theorica,
the practica, and the codicil, which three
parts are distinguished into four distinctions,
which are circular figures, definitions,
mixtures, and different applications,
for the second practical book
and for the first theoretical book.
This book is divided into four distinctions,
and they are first circular figures,
definitions, mixtures, and different
applications for the second practical book,
and the first theoretical book.
In the name and reverence, power
and wisdom of heaven, for you, son of learning,
we intend to clarify the gift given
to us, through a necessary process, with-
out which full knowledge of that thing which

--- Page 21 (folio 3, Praefatio, conclusion) ---

PRAEFATIO.
is one, composed of those entities
which are the matter of nature, which through
dissolutions have been discovered and continue
to be discovered, and which in ordinary ways
do not suffer themselves to be seen. And on this account,
because the doctrine of dissolution, having been
thoroughly sought, yearns, sighs,
and desires the end, and to arrive at the end
of consummation, may Thou, eternal and supreme
Power, deign to illuminate Christians and
those believing in Thee, to see and
know the truth fashioned by the ancients,
without which this science, which
is superior to the nobler part of philosophy,
cannot be made whole, and to lead us
within, because now the time
has come for us to set forth
its definitions.

--- Page 22 (folio 2, Cap. I) ---

RAYMVNDI LVLLI

On the definitions of this art, and on
the lament of nature, and what the
theorica is, and what the
practica is.

CAP. I.

Real entities standing in their primordial
and successive principles give knowledge
and the cause of understanding the nature
of bodies, and of the means and known extremities,
which are the cause of the true permutation
and transmutation of these things, according
to their roots, in spherical form
and enduring species through their dispositions,
insofar as they are permutable and
wholly transmutable into the true and useful
form of the perfect things, preserving the essence
insofar as it is possible to lead it
to the supreme and perfect temperament.
But in this book, when we speak in a
discontinuous manner about physics, we have
a mandate from the mistress Lady Nature.
For she herself appeared to us manifestly, and
opened her works to us, and having opened them
showed them. Therefore weeping and groaning against us,

--- Page 23 (folio 3, Theorica, Cap. I continued) ---

THEORICA.
crying out she said: Alas, what shall I do henceforth,
since they are trying to take away and steal
my instruments from me, and they wish
to plunder my secrets and lay me bare.
And those things which I formed by the license
of my said master, they wish to dispatch
me to death. This is the grief which the Lady
Nature spoke before the priest, and with
ever-groaning heart the truth poured forth
tears, because there was no heart which,
however compassionate in its suffering,
was moved, and if indeed there was any grief,
it was that they themselves [experienced] the very
pain and desperation which she
had on account of the good works which she had
performed in this world, to such a degree that she wished
to abandon them, nor did she care to do them any longer,
if only she could obtain permission from God
her master, when she had petitioned him concerning
that matter. And on this account we received the mandate
that we must keep her instruments secret
and guard them from the hands of her enemies.
And all these things we shall declare in the second book,
if you are able to enter. And therefore this first
book is divided and partitioned into five
parts and five distinctions, through
general chapters.
Since, moreover, of this part of philosophy

--- Page 24 (folio 4, Cap. I continued) ---

RAYMVNDI LVLLI
the practica cannot be taught without the preceding
theorica, we say that the theorica is in that
part in which the dispositions of transmutable
and miscible bodies toward temperament
are taught and demonstrated,
insofar as is necessary for the
perfection of our mastery. The practica, indeed,
is that part in which the form and method of
correctly working is described, according to the
requirement of the disposition previously known
through the theorica.
And therefore, because neither the dispositions of the
aforesaid bodies nor their forms can be
rightly known except through the consideration of
natural and unnatural things
of nature, for better understanding we divide
the theorica into three parts, since the dispositions
of the whole body pertain to genera
no more than three, namely temperament,
intemperament, and neutrality.
First it must be known that temperament
is comprehended under natural things,
because formally its complexion is
composed of subtle and unified parts.
In the compositions of which parts the
philosophers have spoken at length, broadly
accepting, insofar as they were able to
comprehend the continuation and aggregation
of parts, when the transforming agent combined them.

--- Page 25 (folio 4r, Theorica Cap. I-II) ---

THEORICA.                                              4

its composition is received. Intemperament, however, is comprehended under those things which are contrary to nature, and therefore it is called by another name corruption, or truly a distancing from temperament, with which the whole exists and without which nothing does. Neutrality, indeed, contains the middle of the aforesaid parts, by another name called the ligaments of the said extremities, namely of temperament and intemperament; it is otherwise called the middle disposition between two contraries.

On the temperament of bodies, and intemperament, and neutrality, and on medicine, and on natural, unnatural, and contra-natural instruments.

CAP. II.

And therefore you must take heed that true temperament is the natural disposition of perfect bodies, through which natural actions are perfected in the perfection of medicine, in which they are perfected without any other intermediary, entering through the whole; and by this it must be the disposition of the quality of all bodies, and not of the ac-

tion.

--- Page 26 (Raymundi Lulli, Cap. II cont.) ---

RAYMVNDI LVLLI

tion. For if the medicinal body is not well tempered, it would wholly cease to transmute imperfect bodies to perfection, which is accomplished with due projection. It is then truly called a potent temperament only when it is apt to complete the action which is given to it through the complexion of its temperament, with the complement that is given to it by the way and method of known projection. This action renders and orders the medicine apt and suitable through its temperament. Toward the knowledge of this, the intention of him who labors to bring our work to completion with a noble temperament must be principally directed and guided through the instrument of prudent wisdom. For considerations that are not concerned with temperament ought in no way to be fitting for this matter. For they are foreign and far removed from the will and considerations of the artisan, and through this such a contemplator is made an exile from the whole without good, just as one who is a stranger and alienated through his own superfluous work and the fantasy by which he has searched. Nevertheless, temperament is acquired through the ingenuity of nature, or by the will of the master in fermentation through the intellect, for the adornment of things

pertain-

--- Page 27 (folio 5r, Theorica Cap. II cont.) ---

THEORICA.                                              5

pertaining to and miscible with the science and informative art constituting one habit in medicine, or diverse or many habits.

Since temperament, insofar as it perfects action, is not more clearly known, nor more rightly preserved or found, than through the said science -- for whether it be one habit or diverse, yet at all times there is one sole terminus, and one method of preserving the right limits for producing the said temperament unto its end, which we wish to signify by C. Intemperament, or non-temperament, is an unnatural corruptible disposition, which comes about through elemental dissolution, through which the actions of individual things and of nature are immediately corrupted, through the distancing or instability of their temperament, which had come about through the continuity and binding of their parts composed in composition. And that compound we wish to signify by G, in the conversion of D and F into G.

Neutrality, neutrally middle, or mediocrity, is the disposition in which, by way of a middle, the actions of its subject are habituated toward the conservation of the species, as far as the beginning of the experience of sense is concerned, and it is to be signified

by

--- Page 28 (Raymundi Lulli, Cap. II cont.) ---

RAYMVNDI LVLLI

by E, which is descending from B and F, returning into D, through which C is formed, as we promised to say in the practica, which is the second part of this book. Since we said that our theorica is divided into three parts, and from those parts it is fitting for you, son, to descend, therefore you must consider that all parts of the theorica are taken in two ways, namely by taking the terms more strictly than they could be taken according to the nature of their genera, according to what

they

--- Page 29 (folio 6r, Theorica Cap. II cont.) ---

THEORICA.                                              6

they are in themselves. And sometimes according as they are right signs and more proximate according to nature toward some signifiable respect through knowledge in temperable bodies. But for the son of doctrine, the first consideration is to duly proceed in the order of intellectual doctrine, as it is found declared in the treatise on questions, in the questional chapter of the abbreviated art, in the tenth question, concerning which the second book of this science and art is completed. For a noble memory and noble instinct and appetite must be placed and fixed in the heart of the artisan, to whom our secret must be manifested, in the revelation which is moved by the creator of all intelligence. For by the consideration of this second part, without making a semblance of nature, we shall set forth according to the requirement of our mastery through a process divinely revealed to us, and our secret, which is the perfective instrument of all our mastery and of all nature, and we shall show and teach the son of the art -- which, if he be faithful and good, he will not reveal, and especially not to enemies of the sons of the art and science, nor to those who work against

nature.

--- Page 30 (Raymundi Lulli, Cap. II-III) ---

RAYMVNDI LVLLI

nature. For truly never has that which one will find in the second part of this book after the theorica come forth from a human mouth.

On the principles of nature in the universal, and how all things were extracted from the confused mass, in comparison to the great world, concerning the four elements; and concerning the four primordial principal principles; and concerning all things, reducing them through the great form of the world to our mastery, which is the lesser form, as the lesser world, through a miracle.

CAP. III.

The primordial and succeeding natural principles in the work of nature are universally all extremities that can exist, and their middles, as we shall declare the nature of the primordial principles under the power of nature and of God, the supreme king and

emperor

--- Page 31 (folio 7r, Theorica Cap. III cont.) ---

THEORICA.                                              7

emperor of heaven, who alone in the Trinity is without beginning and without a creator, and is superior to all the aforesaid succeeding principles, which are named extremities and middles in their works of nature. This nature the supreme God first created from nothing by His pure liberality and will, in a certain pure substance which is called the fifth essence [quinta essentia], in which all of nature is comprehended.

[DIAGRAM: Upper circular diagram with three concentric rings. Center: "Natura." Second ring labeled "2", "3". Outermost ring: "Angeli" (top), "Mundus" (right, curved), "Celum" (bottom). Number "1." appears outside outermost ring at top right.]

From the better and purer part of this substance, divided into three parts, the Most High created the angels; from the sec-

ond

--- Page 32 (Raymundi Lulli, Cap. III cont.) ---

RAYMVNDI LVLLI

ond, the heavens, planets, and stars. And from the third, less pure, He made the lower world. And the son of doctrine must understand this not as we deliver it, but rather as everything was created simultaneously by the will of the supreme God, without any operation of succession, and without any preceding matter that would look to a succession of kind -- for otherwise it would not be creation, nor a divine operation, which looks to the creation of an entity coming forth scientifically through creation from nothing into true substantial entity. Wherefore, son, what we shall say and have already said, understand it with a scientific spirit, and not in a hagiographic or common manner, for thus we speak with respect to the nature of operations, which you must assimilate in our mastery. And therefore we speak with the language of example, and so that you understand us well, as has already been said, and not as it is written. For the supreme creator divided this part into five. For He created from the purer part the fifth essence of the elements, participial with the celestial thing, which we signify by O. And from this He made four parts: from the first and purer of these He made fire,

which

--- Page 33 (folio 8, Theorica Cap. II) ---

THEORICA.

sought out the first element. From the second substance after it, being more pure, He made air, which is the second element. From the third, indeed purer after air, He made water, which is the third element. From the fourth, however, being less pure, He made earth, which is the last element. The first we wish to signify by P, the second by Q, the third by K, and the fourth by B. Inasmuch as nature in elemental things is more perfect, it is perfected in the whole with a small perfection. For one is perfected by another, through the concordance of their properties and of their fifth instrument, partaking of the celestial thing, and in that which is perfected by those elements in the whole, which is the perfect perfection of a part of the world. For nothing more perfect can be made than by the imitation of divine knowledge, as in creation and composition, and nothing more perfect than these can be made by the imitation of divine knowledge. Therefore it cannot produce a more perfect perfection than what it does, except in respect of the perfection that comes by the Creator's will, not through the miraculous. For then it is without addition in making, unless it is perfected by the imitation of divine knowledge, as in human na-

--- Page 34 (Theorica Cap. II) ---

RAYMVNDI LVLLI

ture, which is rectified by divine intelligence, just as a beautiful and good work is perfected by a good craftsman. Through this, the son of the art can understand the nature of the primary elements, after the division of the substance of the primordial earth. He should not understand, however, that their substance is a simple fifth element, but the fourth, or third, or second, or first elemental substance derived from the fifth part, which we call the primordial element and simple substance, through which the four elemental substances are elemented, each one according to its own form, as has been declared above. These four elements thus created remain in reason. The creation of their natural part having been declared, up to the time of the priest [Adam], by whose sin nature went forth, which was called genus, and from him from that time onward was mixed. But afterward humans died, birds, beasts, fish, and certain things growing from the earth were destroyed and shaken, through the destruction of generation, which was turned from corruption into generation, and afterward from generation into corruption. And likewise the world was dissolved from impure bodies, and the elements were corrupted, through which

--- Page 35 (folio 9, Theorica Cap. II) ---

THEORICA.

corruption all things made and living by nature are and were of short duration. Because nature in its gross and corrupt matter cannot make anything perfect from it, as it used to do in its beginning, which it no longer has the power to continue as its first work. Indeed, nature makes use of imperfections tending toward corruption, because from day to day it finds the nature of the elements less pure, because that which it has placed in the work cannot sustain a perfect work, but always gradually diminishes. Nevertheless, nature always composes according to its ability, and puts together the stronger parts of the bond. And through this doctrine the son of the art can apprehend the truth of the Savior, that at the end of the world He will consume all things, when He comes to judge the age by fire. He will see everything that will not be of the truth of the said elements, and everything that will be composed or mixed of evil and impurity will be confounded and cast into the abyss. But everything that the aforesaid fire finds of virtues and pure truth, He will place above His sphere for eternity, and it will be above the blessed and happy; and the evil and impure upon the dam-

--- Page 36 (Theorica Cap. II) ---

RAYMVNDI LVLLI

ned falls upon the damned. Through this, moreover, the consideration can be elucidated and declared that every thing in the end will go to its own proper place, whence it was first moved. It is also to be understood that this earth which we cultivate is not a true element, but is elemented from the true fifth elemented substance, afterward through the fifth substance an elemental body well elemented, since earth was formed from the fifth cause, and then it is found empty of formative virtue, like a body without a soul, and like putrefaction gathered together in one mass, in matter without form, composed from the aforesaid infection through the direct action of the elements upon one another. At the center, however, is pure earth, the true element, which fire will not be able to burn on the day of terror, and so likewise with the other elements. You, through this elucidation, may clearly understand, if you add the principal material substance from which, without division, all things were formed in one, although through the division of things we were dissolved, yet that species was not separated which is the fifth essence, because the substance holds the entire principality of the composite elements. And thus you may know that there are three radical principles of all things: artificial, exemplary, and material.

--- Page 37 (folio 10, Theorica Cap. II) ---

THEORICA.

artificial, exemplary, and material.

The first radical principle, the artificial, is God, the creator of all things. The second principle, the exemplary, is moved by God Himself, who is called Wisdom. The third succeeding principle, which is matter, created by God, that is by Wisdom, which is moved by Him, is the primordial element, which we call hyle [prime matter]. This we have declared to you, if you have well understood, that indeed you should not presume to seek it in its simple species in order to begin our work, although without it we could neither know our work nor bring it to completion. But we ought to seek it in its composite species, and purge it, and evacuate it through fire, so that it may be pure and clear, as we said above, that all elements were created from the said substance, and vivified by it, for creating and corrupting. Because it is necessary, as naturally appears, since every thing existing beneath the lunar globe is created and formed from the said matter, which is called hyle, that from it there is a greater influence in every elemented thing, more in one than in another, as we find through nature, and that it rests therein. Through

--- Page 38 (Theorica Cap. II) ---

RAYMVNDI LVLLI

this, moreover, you may be certain that nothing can exist without it, being created and not generated, because it is the collector and binding-together of every elemental body in the work of nature, and from it we call it nature, and it is the primordial principle of everything elemental, that is, of all elemented things, because it is simple in its substance. The elements, which are the primary matter of nature, were created thus through divine separation, namely earth, water, air, and fire, which are elemented bodies from the primordial principle and the simple element that is in them. And from this manifestly proceeds the erutical methanorical [?] doctrine, which says that the species of the elements was found in its own sole terms in its said principle, which is the primordial principal of its things. Therefore attend to this matter from here onward, and understand that it is the pure subject, which is the union and motion of forms, and in which every form is retained in potentiality, because it contains infinite fluxibilities and instabilities in motion according to the likeness of those forms, both extreme and intermediate, which it receives into itself. And therefore by likeness it is named by some the receptacle of forms,

--- Page 39 (folio 11, Theorica Cap. III) ---

THEORICA.

which are born from celestial matter, which would be above its own nature. And by other philosophers it was called forestum [a thicket], comparing it to a gross and crude thing, by reason of its potentiality. And by other philosophers it was called potentiality, because it has no form actually, but contains every form through potentiality, as though enclosed, like silence without hearing, or those who do not see, nor can be seen on account of darkness. And thus this matter ought to be understood, as though nothing were understood about it. And therefore it is written that it is the form of the world; in what manner, however, the elements are ordered in the world, from which all things are generated, will be most clearly apparent, and we shall declare it in the following chapter.

On the lesser form, and on the principles of simple mineral nature through extremities and means.

CAP. III.

Since the greater form has been determined, afterward descending, the lesser will be determined. In which are the princi-

ples succeeding in the work of nature, more proximate according to the extremities and means of their nearest operations. And since we have said above that the principles are universally all the extremities and all their means, here we say that the first and principal mean of the ex-

earthly or the extremes, are the extremes of the elements, that is, the elements themselves, and they are the elemental extremities, and are signified by B. The second are vapors composed from these same elements immediately from the first composition of nature, and they are called the middles, signified by C — into which vapors all elemented things are resolved, in order to enter into new generations. And you should retain and understand this, because I speak to you in general terms, in order to temper the end of that which is sought. The third is a clear water composed from the said vapors through condensation in nature, into which the said vapors of the four elements come, and that is the thing most closely related to quicksilver, which indeed is found running and flowing upon the earth. And that quicksilver, from every elemented body succeeding in the material of the art, is properly generated. Therefore its weight is exceedingly heavy, through a compact homogeneity, running through motion of its own, through perforated subterranean veins, so that it falls into the end of a sulfurous cavity from hot and dry vapors, and it is signified by D. The fourth is a certain substance proceeding from minerals, and nonetheless more

The fifth extreme or extremity, or rather middle, are vapors immediately generated through the resolution and putrefaction of the said vinous vitrifiable substance, and this is the first and most proximate matter of the generation of all metals, impregnated and apprehended by the natural vivifying heat, in the form and appearance of living water, which all investigators of this art must understand. In the work of nature it is quicksilver, but not such quicksilver as is found upon the earth, nor will it be, until it is first turned into a languid, pasty, and venomous state, and it is signified by K. a better form, just as the generation of flesh happens in the body of an animal, through the digestion of food and drink, as is briefly declared in the magical science and art, which begins thus: "It is manifest concerning generation and corruption in metals," etc. And since it is there described concerning the greatest termination through the literal method, that when the alchemist strives to give to certain metals a form that is similar to another metal, he is like a painter who in the material of wood or stone strives to make the form of a man or beast artificially, which form differs and diverges from the material. Therefore in that case it must be said that such a form is foreign to its material, just as by a barber it is corrupted, and such works are not assimilated to the works of good nature. And concerning this similitude, see the chapter which begins, "Since therefore," etc. For such alchemists and painters in their works do not assimilate their material to the work of nature, because they do not know how to give the informative virtue to the matter containing it, which has the capacity for infusion and sustaining to receive it, when it has been administered to it by the natural informer. But through science and art that is known, that which is abstracted from nature, which is comprehended by the divine evening-light of red genius and the ingenious one, in

--- Page 45 (page 14, Theorica, Cap. III cont.) ---

his work is assimilated to the work of nature in the preservation of the mineral species, and forms with such virtue that it surpasses every course of nature. And through this you can recognize that a natural work can be governed by the science and art of the operator, according to the condition of his proper instrument. And know, son, why men do not know how to use their memory properly, because they must investigate changeable and convertible entities, and choosing according to their will, and leaving behind those things which are against nature, setting them aside. And because they do not know how to apply these powers in their artifices, which are their instruments, and concerning which we shall inform you in our second artificial part, so that you may attain real entities in our magistery, for through the activity of those you will receive habituated science with delight, through which the intellect by means of one species attains another, and in the order of one species attains the order of another species, and thus you will have an ordered delight for understanding the proper and the common. And on account of this, the sciences which are positive and not demonstrative, make the intellect gross, and impede it from understanding the necessities of the most true entities, just as has been declared in the tree of desired philosophy

--- Page 46 (RAYMVNDI LVLLI, Cap. IIII) ---

RAYMVNDI LVLLI

through passion. Wherefore let the succeeding chapter provide another completion of this principled thesis, which has been spoken concerning metals signified by H. And let it be a notice for those who do not have patience in the magistery.

Concerning the principles of this magistery, and from what it is made, and what things are in the mystery, and concerning their conversion.

CAP.  IIII.

The first radical principles in our magistery are three principal ones, namely, living water, and the wise spirit, from which sulfur is created, which is our stone. But since those things are not in their actual state upon the earth, as we would need them, in their arrangement they are earthly, in the form of metal, in which is their potency. Therefore we have accepted them under the extremities of nature through the medium of our art, having recourse to one substance within its minerals, namely D, E, H. And since those are real and contrary through their extremities

of nature

--- Page 47 (page 15, Theorica, Cap. IIII cont.) ---

THEORICA.

the disposition of nature has been chosen from D, as appears through figure A, declared below in the second part of the subsequent chapter, so that through F, D may be converted into E universally into B, and that B through circulation may be reduced into E, from which you extract F in our magistery, in place of living water and the wise spirit, because thus F has the power of converting D and H through the preservation of their forms, since in actuality it will be everything that it was in potency in the work of nature, for the sake of better extremities and by reason of G and H, selected and pure, and strongly digested by the wisdom of the sages' ingenuity. Therefore it is not fitting to remove and separate one part of D and one part of E, and then you will have F in the work of art assimilated to nature in all better modes, through the addition of C and D, which came from F, which descended from H into B, from which F is made, which has been converted into G by the course of nature in our magistery. And that G is the first and most proximate matter, from which we create and make our perfect medicine, which is the ferment of the elixir.

Concerning

Concerning the approximation of temperament with respect to nature, concerning the genus according to the rule of science, concerning the extremities and the middles, and concerning their division in the essential principles.

CAP.  V.

On account of which you can consider that natural things are those which are necessary for the disposition of temperament, according to the reason of their genus, which have had their descent from their extremities and middles, and therefore they are called things without which certain bodies cannot naturally be tempered. For when the thing whose head is red, feet white, and eyes black appears, then the magistery exists, since from this thing the temperament consists. For although a medicinal body can be tempered without such a humor, namely an unnatural one, it cannot absolutely be without such a humor. Therefore it is necessary that every middle should feel the nature of its extremities for the sake and reason of its temperament, and through this the elements are reciprocal to each other, so that their primary

Nor is there any medicine for this without moisture or heat or kind, because without such or such a humor it has no instance or power in its operations. And therefore the work or operation ought not to be undertaken by the practitioner solely through the due action of the elixir, or through its property by temperament, but genera-

Then the action of its sustaining of ignition in fusibility is called a natural operation, notwithstanding that there is a cessation or, as it were, a rest of the sensible animal from action, which comes about in its transmutation through the virtue of the elixir. And thus the temperament of cleansed and transmuted bodies is not without natural operation. And you must attend to this matter in the supreme medicine, and that it be wholly turned and changed. But because, through excessively diffuse and fanciful imagination, you could err accidentally according to your understanding of the three aforesaid things pertaining to theory, which contains in itself the principles touched upon above and generally conveyed, therefore we shall specially instruct ourselves in the remote terms of the three necessary principles of this science and divine art. For when we have told you that through the intent consideration of these natural things, non-natural things, and things contrary to nature, you must have material and essential knowledge of the temperament through all its substantial parts, and also its accidental parts, so that you may know how to govern yourself in our said magistery, with the said principles

thus comprehended by you. You must, however, understand that we speak not of many or numerous things, but of three only, standing by themselves individually, as you can understand from the aforesaid chapter on the principles themselves in our magistery, through which we comprehend this temperament. And by this we say that there are three genera of natural things, namely: animal, vegetable, and mineral. The animal genus in its species is multiplied and divided into three differences, namely: in the active sperm, which is the natural sperm; in the passive sperm, and that is the non-natural; and in the menstrual blood, and that is contrary to nature. The genus of vegetables is in the sowing-seeds and roots, which are natural, containing non-natural things, and through this their complexion is Hermaphrodite, masculine and feminine conjoined, according to the humor and moisture of the earth by the menstrual rain through the air containing water, which things are contrary to nature. The mineral genus, however, is divided into three differences, although all metals exist and have their origin from one matter, and thus are of Hermaphrodite complexion. The first difference is natural, such as gold and silver, since quicksilver [argentum vivum] contains within itself its own proper sul-

--- Page 52 (page 17 verso, THEORICA, Cap. V cont.) ---

RAYMVNDI LVLLI

fur, which is congealed by itself, half into gold and the other half into silver, after its conversion into sulfur, in white and in red. On account of this there are two natural sperms, namely gold and silver, taking the term broadly. When we say broadly, this is to distinguish it from that which perfects our elixir. And the non-natural sperm is like lead, and of the non-natural and impure metals, such as lead, tin, copper, iron, sulfur, and quicksilver, and of things that are contrary to nature, such as vitriol [atramentum], salts, alums, tutty [zinc oxide powder], marcasites, and arsenic, and all things that descend from other genera of vegetables and animals, from which a menstrual moisture can be made, tending more or less toward perfection or imperfection. And therefore the mineral genus alone has been added by the figure of similitude generally among these natural, non-natural, and contrary-to-nature things of the two genera, namely animal and vegetable, because from their nature they receive a more proximate alteration in luminous clarity. And through this you can understand the true temperament, preserving the more proximate nature of its own genus, according to what common

--- Page 53 (page 18, THEORICA, Cap. V-VI) ---

THEORICA.

experience will teach you in the earth of lunaria [a silver-bearing mineral], whose nature is the rectification of our stone, because for this reason it has been liberally given to us.

Concerning from what thing the suitable and most proximate matter must be extracted for performing the magistery, on the conversion of one element into another.

CAP. VI.

Since therefore it is clear and manifest that the property of temperament in a metallic body is of a nature most proximate to metal, as experience, which does not lie, affirms and demonstrates by the effect of noble resistance in good fusion and permanent fusion in ignition -- and this is the proper form in nature, as has been said, and most proximate to its mineral nature, just as from its minerality it descended into the individual parts of its own proper species -- from which you will draw the matter suitable to its form, which is owed to it as the cause of the species of those individuals without contrariety or extremity causing division. But in perfect sweet love they meet one another through real union, looking toward the point of extremity and contrariety

--- Page 54 (page 18 verso, THEORICA, Cap. VI cont.) ---

RAYMVNDI LVLLI

causing diversity, so long as by the course of nature they can preserve themselves from their noble species, from which the true form emerged, which indeed in the great power of its property goes against the sun, which nature created. And therefore I have told you, my son, that you should take the nature of the said genus, in which the two luminaries [i.e., gold and silver] and their clarities are lodged and remain permanent, which do not cease to radiate upon the earth, and they are the sun and the moon, which by their rays obscure fire. Note therefore, my son, that he who does not receive this matter in his work resembles a painter who paints a known form in remote and distant matter, as we said above in the chapter in which we treated of the lesser form. And among the non-natural bodies you will receive the volatile body, that is, quicksilver, which contains its nature within the concavity of its belly, which so adheres to it that no one can possess it except through a certain friendly concord, which nature has faithfully taught us through a certain attraction of love, for which we are very greatly indebted to it, by which bond it is strongly detained and arrested by its love. And therefore it is placed among the non-natural things, yet not entirely as something non-natural. For then it would be numbered among those things that are contrary to nature,

--- Page 55 (page 19, THEORICA, Cap. VI cont.) ---

THEORICA.

contrary to nature. And therefore it is called a mean, participating with natural things and with things contrary to nature, because from within it retains its natural body and pure substance, since it is the proper bond and restraint of that body, and through its property from without it is cast inward with those things that are contrary to nature. When therefore you wish to see nature operate, cast it in and join the natural thing to it with the bond of its love; the lover will be wrathful. This name "non-natural," however, we receive broadly in this art for all things containing within the center of their body passive natures with respect to active ones. And we have said above that in the center of the earth there is virgin earth, a true element, and that it was created by nature; therefore nature is in the center of every thing, as is apparent from the said chapter and from the preceding chapter which makes mention of the first form of the world, and how the Lord our God established the world and formed nature, so that their natures might be better maintained through our theory according to their genus, so that you may know what you should take in the royal magistery and what you should set aside. For it is known that every thing that proceeds to the generation of its genus through nature, each one adheres

--- Page 56 (page 19 verso, THEORICA, Cap. VI cont.) ---

RAYMVNDI LVLLI

to its species, just as animal feminine sperm is joined to the masculine through the addition of its heat. And through this you can understand that the virtues of non-natural species are remote and proximate to the mean, which participates in the extremities of the natural species and of those contrary to nature, according to more and less, according as the whole nature of that genus differs and recedes, or accords with the species. For nature ought not to be discordant through genus, but only through species, with the closeness of love that is born from its own property, just as wax from fire and from water, in which is their median property, just as is the container of natural things and of things contrary to nature. When we say "the whole of nature," we say this to distinguish natural things, non-natural things, and things contrary to nature, which are all of one genus. And yet if we have said that the nature of a genus in its generation ought not to be discordant, you must understand this immediately in relation to receiving the form of any living thing. How brute nature through alteration reverts with human operation -- all this you can see, as with vegetables. But animals never receive the form of humans, until their genus is turned into hu-

--- Page 57 (folio 20r, Theorica) ---
THEORICA.
in living things. Then I say that the seeds come to what has been described, with the multiplicative virtue, which is the spirit, from which individual things are of the kind already mentioned, whence human beings are created through the virtue of the spirit, and this was instituted by the Creator, so that each one through man might render Him honor, or a lesser nature is turned toward a greater one, wherefore it is exalted with very great value. Therefore, if you wish to assimilate our metals to the nature of plants or of beasts through the entity of real natures, you shall make their kind by measure, and then they will be able to receive species and true forms, if you cause them to remain strongly and firmly in the fire through fermentation. And this we shall tell you in the second part, yet keep it secret for a long time, because the whole thing is done by art and knowledge. And you shall tell no one, unless you wish to accuse the majesty of the philosophers and betray their secrets. And I swear to you upon my soul that if you reveal these things, you are damned. For from God all good proceeds, and to Him alone it is owed. Therefore you shall preserve and keep secret that which is owed to Him to reveal, and you shall affirm that you withdraw by right propriety what is owed to His honor. Because if you were to reveal in brief words that which He formed over a long time, on the day of great judgment you would be condemned, as one who existed as a perpetrator against the injured majesty of God, nor would the case of injured

--- Page 58 (folio 20v, Raymundi Lulli) ---
RAYMVNDI LVLLI
majesty be remitted to you. For the revelation of such things pertains to God and not to another. And among those things which are against nature, taking a certain liquor, which is the corruption and separation of the spoil of the body of the said volatile — that is, of quicksilver — which is the cause of the continuity of the parts of that liquor in the said two luminaries and brightnesses without their division in their kind, but remotely and proximately it participates, just as the menstrual matter, from which, when it has been fermented and whitened in the moisture of its seeds, it causes itself to be nourished, as a child under the breast, because by the virtue of the moisture of the two seeds the menstrual blood by virtue of its nature is turned into radical moisture. And when we say "remotely," this is to differentiate from mineral things, and when we say "proximately," this is to differentiate from things which are against nature.
From these three things, my son, you shall extract the great dragon, which is the radical and principal beginning of firm alteration. And in this you shall establish your long and continuous consideration, through the instrument of natural reason. And we shall declare these things better to you in the subsequent practical chapters.

--- Page 59 (folio 21r, Theorica, Cap. VII) ---
THEORICA.
How there is only one philosophers' stone, and concerning the advisement of its purification.
CAP. VII.
We say that there is only one philosophers' stone, extracted from the abovesaid things by our mastery. And therefore when it comes newly into the world, you must not add to it another powder, or another water, nor any extraneous thing, but only that which is born in it, of its own proper radical nature, and its mother, which nourishes it, is sulfur, which formed its stone in celestial heat. But before you extract it entirely, purge it and cleanse it from all its infirmities which are against its nature — the phlegmatic, terrestrial, and corruptible — because they are its death, by which it is surrounded, which mortify its vivifying spirit, which has the power of raising it from death to perennial life against the war of fire. It cannot, however, show its virtue until the motion of the high and honorable mobile nature in great

--- Page 60 (folio 21v, Raymundi Lulli, Cap. VIII) ---
RAYMVNDI LVLLI
purity comes to rest without any corruption. And therefore, my son, unless that spirit is separated from its corruptible death, its dark body cannot shine forth, nor can a marriage be made from it between body and spirit, that is, between sulfur and quicksilver.
How the stone is named, by reason of its property, and its nature.
CAP. VIII.
This spirit we call in our testament a stony and lapideous spirit, because it has the power of retaining stones against fire, and of preserving and vivifying the dead body from which it came forth, whose virtue and property can be found in no other spirit. And therefore when its body has passed from death to life, it is pure, clean, white, and clear, shining in great subtlety, by which you must cause it to overcome the subtlety of the fermentable spirit with its strength and skill. Because the grossness of the fermentable spirit must surround the subtlety of the body and float above it, and this through the property of entering, which

--- Page 61 (folio 22r, Theorica, Cap. IX) ---
THEORICA.
it carries in itself, which is the penetrative property. And therefore no grossness of body ought to or can remove its great and penetrative property, by an impediment coming from its contrary. And in this way the body remains and shall remain clothed outwardly with beautiful brightness, because its spirit has been carried, and the body has entered the spirit which raised it up, and terminated it in the form of a very subtle powder, flowing and melting by the instrument of fire with a gentle fusion without smoke, which is like a burning fire when a flame touches it, with a permanent alteration that has been made by the friendly embrace of nature, retaining its own nature by perennial alleviation, and that is done easily with the practice which we have found from the theory, which instructed us beautifully and easily with these words.
Concerning the preparation of the stone.
CAP. IX.
The preparation of the stony spirit and the fermentable is that you receive from the juice of lunaria [a plant used in alchemy], and extract its sweat with a small and gentle fire, and you shall have in your possession one of our quicksilvers in liquid form and in the form of white water, which is the washing and purification of our stone and of its entire

--- Page 62 (folio 22v, Raymundi Lulli, Cap. X) ---
RAYMVNDI LVLLI
nature. And this is one of the principal secrets, and it is the first gate, as you can understand from the reasons stated before. In this liquor the great dragon is rectified, and it is cast out from the great desert of Arabia, because it would immediately be suffocated by thirst and would perish in the Dead Sea, because you can already know that great coldness extinguishes small heat. Turn it therefore and place it in the kingdom of Ethiopia, whence it is naturally native. Because we say that unless it is turned and placed in its own land, it will depart and enter another region. Therefore know for certain that every other climate or region brings death to our stone — veiled from those who do not know and are ignorant, but known by us.
Of what thing the stone is made, and what it is called, and how it is of the four elements, and in what place it is found, and words against liars and scoffers.
CAP. X.
Therefore you must know clearly that our spirit carries with it the property of its

--- Page 63 (folio 23r, Theorica) ---
THEORICA.
region. And for this reason it is necessary to speak allegorically, that the great dragon is of the four elements, and not in the literal sense, as it is written, as though it were earth, air, water, or fire, but it is only one nature, which contains in itself the nature and property of the four elements, and this property comes complexionally in the said substance of nature, through the action of the primary elemented qualities, now transformed into the property and species of metal, tolerating fire in its perfections. And thus the said primary qualities are cut off and broken apart through the change of their mixture, which was made through alteration, until they are in this substantial medium in which is the species of the Sun or the Moon, or of another which ought to and can be constituted by nature: because the aforesaid medium in which the said species must be preserved has as much breadth and length as can be the likeness of individual parts of this species. And therefore since it is of the property of that nature to sustain fire, it cannot, nor does it have anything, nor does it make any profit, until the entire motion of that uncorrupted mobile is arrested. But notwithstanding, when it has already become of the property of the most excellent

--- Page 64 (folio 23v, Raymundi Lulli) ---
RAYMVNDI LVLLI
fire, it can tolerate the corruption of the harshness of fire. And therefore your speculation can understand the manner through which nature shows us its operation, which is by the example of this science, saying that there is no passage from the first to the third except through the second, which participates with them according to the nature of the will. And through this we have acquired our desire, passing through the intermediaries which are the preservatives and retainers of its species against the war of fire. And through these preambles you can understand the right and true time limited with respect to nature, which you must have and preserve in a certain latitude, until you grow the ruler and principal medium, in which the species of the ultimate things must be preserved, because the medium is the material subject or real kind in which the container infuses its virtue, and the beginnings of generation its inception, in which the determined elements are to be fixed by celestial virtue. For all the aforesaid things have been manifested before our eyes through experience, a clear science shown to us by nature, by the precept of divine predestination. And moreover we tell you securely that from the nature of plants we have created a medium, preservative of the mineral species, and its transmuta-

--- Page 65 (p. 24, Theorica Cap. X) ---
THEORICA.
the fire, into a metallic color, in the transformation of the mineral species. Dearest son, understand that the stone is nothing but one nature, divided into four parts, resulting according to the property of its elements through the property of their concordances, so that it may be a bond and binding in the power of all four elements, as will be declared to you at length. For in no other way can we bind the spirit through its body, unless it is detained and arrested by its own proper nature, which is the property of their binding. And therefore attend diligently, lest the property of the spirit, which has departed from the body, be devoured by excessive heat, because it could not reduce its body to the medium, in which the second spirit infuses its virtue, namely the species of the sun or the moon. And that is our quicksilver, which aptly and suitably receives the tincture of our sulfur, in which our stone is dissolved and resolved in such water, as we said above. And in such water it appeared to us, not in fountain water or rainwater. Wherefore, if you have worked with a great fire, the property of the said spirit, which participates between life and death, will separate itself, and the soul will withdraw to the region of its sphere. Again we

--- Page 66 (Theorica Cap. X) ---
RAYMVNDI LVLLI
say to you, and more clearly, that through the creation of this fifth salt of ours, the stone is generated, and captured in those branches, and found there — false, flavorful, and beautiful — and it dwells in rivers, and there has great power. And add nothing foreign from the world to it, giving it that which exits and is born from it. And truly that came forth from the dragon and from the fetid belly. And in the second part we shall tell you the whole meaning. Guard it therefore from the giants and tyrants of the world, as we said above. For they are placed in the waves of the water, from which we greatly fear. Because nothing suffices them, but that they inflict such evil and martyrdom on the people of peace, whereby the nation is confounded, and they put the whole world into confusion. Wherefore this secret has been given to us by the Superior, as if to be revealed by a lord to a deaf man, until it be made manifest to the sons through divine prudence, through their noble intellect. And therefore sometimes the chattering Clerics, Lawyers, Masters of Arts, or worldly Physicians, and many others of whom we dare not speak, who believe they can make the living water — and the whole thing is not worth a single dried fig — because they believe they can plunder our philosophy, and they find themselves plundered, and therefore such men lose their life thereby.

--- Page 67 (p. 25, Theorica Cap. X / Cap. XI) ---
THEORICA.
Guard therefore, sons, the fire in the region of the spirit, and see that it does not vanish from excessive heat. Because if the soul has departed, the spirit will not be able to vivify the body, since it cannot give what is not in it.

On the division and resolution of the
stone for converting it.

CAP. XI.

In our entire magisterium three principal spirits are necessary, which cannot be manifested without the completion of their resolution. And they are named by another name the three living mercuries. In which our stone is dissolved and turned, through the transformation of its nature, through contrary and diverse digestion, which occurs through elemental circulation, through reciprocation, and these are the primordial beginnings of generation. And because the resolution is the entire disposition of the first gate of our magisterium, which we wish to declare according to the requirement of the reduction and conversion of its stone in three spirits, each of which you must preserve according to the proper property of the nature of each of

--- Page 68 (Theorica Cap. XI) ---
RAYMVNDI LVLLI
them, according to what is required in the ultimate perfection of our magisterium, through the said resolution divided into three principal parts. The first is corporeal, and is called in the Oritan language Recfage; the second is spiritual, and is called Agazoph; the third, however, is spiritual and corporeal, and is called Vbidrugal. And in all of these our dragon rests. Recfage is a moist dissolution in the body, and dry in the spirit. Agazoph is divided into two parts, into Periminel and Adulphur, and Vbidrugal is the consummation of our dissolved earth, and it is a dissolution that cannot be had or known without labor. The first part of the dissolution, Recfage, is nothing other than the diminution of gross parts into the most minute and most subtle parts, or the reduction of the gross into the simple, in the transformation of the specific form into a contrary and corrupted form, standing in its body and not in the form of water falling from the clouds, nor in the form of metal constituted in diverse pieces, but in the form of watery earth and subtle mineral water, standing in the lowest depths of hell. In whose belly its nature has been changed from its previous lights into dark shadows, which indeed

--- Page 69 (p. 26, Theorica Cap. XI) ---
THEORICA.
earth, through the exercises of its washings, into diverse very subtle and stinking parts — like sulfur — may flee higher than all the elements into the region of fire. These you must guard very diligently, if you wish to enter through this little gate. For you need to know how to govern well, and to have a cap or hood, so that you can enter and exit, if you desire to pass to the second dissolution, Agazoph. Therefore attend here, that the menstrual is condensed from the spermatic, from its airiness into gross wateriness, through the resolution of the feminine sperm, which is in the menstrual with the embrace of the Mercurial vapor of the body, against nature, which is in the form of water, which is of a more crude vapor. And that which is more cooked with what is more containing by nature, descends deeply in the form of a black crow in the air, whose head is red, feet white, and eyes black, and of its own making. And this whole thing is nothing other than air and radical moisture tending toward the father, which generated heat, and is the tincture of the highest metal. And the other crude nature, which is the cause of the condensation of the menstrual, which remains below,

--- Page 70 (Theorica Cap. XI) ---
RAYMVNDI LVLLI
is the cause and the conjoining matter and the gathering together of the royal stone, which has been turned into a cold nature, because it lost its hot nature through resolution. Wherefore you shall define the said dissolution thus. It is defined as follows: Dissolution is the action and passion of contrary qualities, through which the virtues of the elements are moved among themselves through the smallest parts. Or it is defined thus: It is a motion that makes a comminution of insensible parts, diverse in species and similar in genus, conjoined in a confused and liquid form, and through the corruption of the subject. For from the contrary qualities, raised by means of excitation into the black fire, without extreme division, the germinating seeks separation from the parts through generation. In another way dissolution is defined: Dissolution is a subtilizing instrument, made with the exercise of heat conjoined with continuous motion, through which the contrary qualities working together touch each other and are mixed, corrupting the individual parts under the preservation of their species for the work of generation. Son, this first dissolution is nothing other than a simple unbinding of subtle parts descending

--- Page 71 (p. 27, Theorica Cap. XI) ---
THEORICA.
from the first nature into the germ, in which natural heat has worked, which is the instrument with which the informing virtue must work, which is introduced into the matter without actual separations of the germinous parts, one from another. This separation requires that it be done with the application of the love of nature in the second chamber, as we shall declare through the benefit of the second resolution, Agazoph, in the second part of its four figures. But before we pass to that, it is necessary that between Recfage and Agazoph there be preserved a peptic digestion [ripening digestion] for the space of its time, as we shall declare in our practice, because by peptic heat our germ putrefies at the appointed term of nature, and afterwards makes it grow and bear fruit. But first it is turned into the nature of sulfur and quicksilver, the germ of quicksilver having been deposited, which will be changed into a stone whiter than snow. And therefore let the first form of the bodies be dissolved into a Mercurial germ through the solemn operation, and immediately through peptic inhumation [burial for ripening] in its belly another form enters through the corruption of the form of the germ, and with the elements mixed and corrupted together in the form of a black body. Because the elements, converted by the virtue of natural heat, which is in the

This corruption is the generation of our quicksilver, as we shall declare in the second part and the wheel of the circle. Because there we shall determine concerning its principal colors, and concerning flavors, odors, grossness, simplicity, subtlety, and weight. And this is the solemn preparation, which is the head and foundation of the creation of our dear infant. And the manner of this creation is as follows. Cast off the moisture in which the dragon is submerged, until the body is made minute from the celestial spirit. And we call this the fire, which God granted to the holy and wise philosophers through revelation before we were born, and afterwards to us, so that we may serve him without want. Because by showing us examples through the course of nature, how we are bound to him, and therefore we must pray to him, honor, serve, praise, bless, adore, glorify, and love him without any filth, vice, or sin. On account of this, because he shows us to truly know his power, and he loved us, after he made us see these things.

CAP. XII.

By thus philosophizing you can have knowledge that the first and principal intention in our magisterium is to dissolve the stone through the transformation of its nature into quicksilver, and that is the first element in its mineral nature by drawing near, and through this it is the root and first element composed of all liquefiable bodies and the father of the two luminaries. Therefore in this alone they are converted through solemn resolution, and afterwards they are converted into sulfur. Because quicksilver by nature contains its own proper sulfur through vapor, by which it congeals itself into the philosophical stone, which afterwards has the power of congealing

--- Page 74 (Theorica Cap. XII) ---
RAYMVNDI LVLLI
all other quicksilver, through our magisterium. And this stone is called sulfur, and matter perfectly approaching the nature of metal, through which nature is reputed noble and most worthy among the wise, from which the philosophers make a strong medicine, and most solemn, and this they call the elixir of life, because in the human body it is the superlative and ultimate consolation. And with this intention of its composition we shall give knowledge in the second part of this book, through which we shall conclude the entire course of the said medicine while preserving our discourse through a middle mode, so that we may be better understood, through the mode and middle way of nature. And therefore it appears that the intellect is directed instrumentally and led by reason through a middle form in this present art and science. And therefore it is not fitting, nor is it necessary, that it be led too subtly, nor to be too subtle in discerning real entities, because it would turn into fantastical matters, of which art or nature takes no care. If indeed the stone, which we have described to you, is red, bright, and in it there is the power of simple fire that does not burn, thus you shall make many things, from which you will be able to compose an elixir for gold, which is the medicine preserving all

--- Page 75 (p. 29, Theorica Cap. XII-XIII) ---
THEORICA.
activities in human nature and restoring diminished powers from a defect of nature. And briefly, if you have this medicine in this world, you have an everlasting treasure.

CAP. XIII.

You can understand, son, that from one extremity to another no passage can be made except through a middle, and we have elucidated by reason of nature that the extremities of our stone in the first degree are quicksilver, and the second is the perfect elixir. But the media of these two extremities are unguents and oils, of which some are more cooked, more pure, and more digested than

--- Page 76 (Theorica Cap. XIII) ---
RAYMVNDI LVLLI
others, and these we call the perfecting medicine and the ferment of the elixir. You shall know the preparation of those oils and unguents in the second part of this work. Through this, the good operator and son of doctrine must make from black quicksilver a white sulfur for silver, and afterwards a red sulfur for gold. Because no sulfur can be made red unless it is first white. Because nature does not permit that passage can be made from the first to the third unless the passage is made through the second, as appears through the division of the elements, which distinguishes that earth is primarily black, then water, and that air is more brightly intense, and that fire is red. Wherefore it appears that earth cannot be converted into air unless it is first turned and changed into water, nor can water be converted into fire unless first one of them is converted into air. Therefore, son, you must greatly note within yourself, with philosophical consideration, the nature of the coloring media in the transformation of mineral nature. Because in them there is much knowledge of those who fortify themselves to transmute metals from species to species. And beyond this we have told you, if you have understood us, that it is fitting for you first to transmute bodies

--- Page 77 (p. 30, Theorica Cap. XIII) ---
THEORICA.
with natural reduction into that medium through artificial alteration, following nature, in which the species of gold or silver can be preserved without the distemperament of individuals that are of one genus. And this is the power of near things that more greatly aids the bond of nature by reason of the first conjunction, by which the stone is resolved through the alteration of its cooked nature into a crude nature, and very weak, which is the conversion of the body into quicksilver, and through the subsequent reduction it is then converted into white sulfur, and then into red sulfur. And thus from degree to degree the matter having fallen gradually approaches the matter and nature most proximate to metal through alterative resolution, through which it can more readily and better receive the species of gold or silver through multiplied science and art, as we shall say below in the second part, in the chapter "We say through the theorica speaking."

CAP. XIIII.

After this we say, son, that our stone is varied and changed by three gradual mutations before it is matter for co-creating the elixir. And these mutations are made through a third digestion. One into a liquid, in the manner of a homogeneous chyle. And just as this occurs in the concavity of the heart, so the first digestion occurs in its own proper vessel by the action of heat, which stands united to the matter. The other is into the four humors, that is, when the luminative bodies are subject to flux and corruption. Because it is necessary for their nature that their bad alteration be converted into good, and the bad is made good, and that consequently they may be able to counteract the corruption of all weak bodies. Blessed be that sweet and just Jesus Christ, the Son of God, who came to earth for sinners, and because it

--- Page 79 (p. 31, Theorica Cap. XIIII) ---
THEORICA.
pleased him to sustain death and pour out his blood high upon the cross, and to be exalted thereon for us and other sinners, and to redeem us from the death of hell and from its torments. By this figure I admonish you that the presence of the four humors of those two luminaries was necessary, from which they were made, and the royal medicine was constituted. And just as this digestion occurs in animals in the concavity of the structure of the epapantic liver: so the second occurs in the head of the alembic, descending into the receiver, namely in the manner of water with an airy liquid, in all the virtues of the mineral elements. The third digestion occurs through the transformation of the said humors into pure sulfur, just as in animals the ptisanary heat and moisture, which is converted into blood, then into the essence and substance of the members, according to the ordination of nature. Therefore you must understand, son, that the first digestion occurs when the conjunction and coitus occurs by the love of nature, which is a libidinous and desired luxury, then the first mixture occurs through the conjunction and congregation between body and spirit, so that the whole together may arrive at unity and a loving and united concord with the bond of their quali-

--- Page 80 (Theorica Cap. XIIII) ---
RAYMVNDI LVLLI
ties, now miscible and composed, and of their elemented virtues, one with another, by the virtue of the conception of two in one. And if you never make this conjunction and coitus, you will never make a conception. And unless you make a conception, you will never have a pregnancy, and if no pregnancy, you will not have fruit. When therefore your matter has conceived, the birth still remains, and when it has given birth, you must have patience in the child being nourished, until you have placed the portion [?], and then you can make a free projection from it, because the first digestion has been made. And therefore in the second digestion there occurs an exuberance of blood, namely of spirit, which carries virtue in itself with the virtual approximation of nature, so that it may be more pleasing to it through the decoction of one degree, when its substance is subtilized according to the intention of natural intellect. With this substance you shall impregnate the stone, which is not yet made, as we shall tell you in the second part. Through this, in the third digestion it is nourished with perfection, and augmented in growing, and the stone is strengthened for performing its actions in restoring the lost thing, in the white earth.

Furthermore understand, son, that the final term of our reincrudation and transmutation of the cooked into the crude — namely that to which the Artist naturally strives — is the substance of non-burning sulfur, which when it has been converted, is the most proximate matter of our stone, the first and most proximate nature of natural and pure metal, yet altered by the heat of its body, namely in that alteration through which it may better and more closely receive the form and species of those metals, by the will and agreement of its mineral nature. Son, the moisture of this matter was at one time nothing other than quicksilver projected from liquefied bodies, both within and without. And this is the quicksilver in which the qualities of sulfur have been admixed through the first and second digestion. And therefore, as nature requires, it is altered by the heat of itself, by reason of its overabundance, through the conversion of its nature, and it is congealed and converted into pure sulfur. For you can know through nature that no other moisture can be more readily converted into the substance of sulfur than that in which the qualities of its own sulfur have been sufficiently introduced through natural artifice skillfully performed, which moisture is quicksilver in the form of

clear water, like milk in the breasts. And sulfur is like sperm in the genitals. This quicksilver has no differentiating contrariety arising from its pure complexion, because it is necessary that such quicksilver be very well tempered with respect to the complexion of its body. And by this we make known that the matter of this is not common quicksilver throughout its entire gross, earthy, turbid, and phlegmatic substance, but only in its middle substance, which must be well guarded, and its property must be defended from fire above all things. Nor is it quicksilver in its whole nature, because it has come to the term of digestion through which it has been changed from nature into nature. But this is quicksilver from quicksilver, projected into our true spirit and placed outside of its contrary extremities, and changed through purification and conversion of that into the virgin's milk.
