CAP. XVI.

Therefore, dearest son, you can be assured that without digestions and dilations or decoctions our stone cannot be amended in its own proper nature. And in truth we discovered the art by which we follow nature, that which nature in its digestions could not multiply, informing the matter as the digestion of nature will teach you. The first digestion we call peptic [ripening], which we wish to signify by H. The second is Hepatic [liver-like], which we call Eptesis, because it is made by the heat of the moist body, and this is signified by I. And this is divided into two digestions. The first of these is bound in Eptesis, and in this we draw the dissolution Periminel from potency into act, and this is signified by K. The other we call Optesis, which is between Scooptesis and Scatesis. And through this the heat better desiccates the spirit by resolving, and we call this resolution Adulphuch, and it is signified by L. The third, however, which is the last of the consummation of our earth or of the third dissolution, is a digestion made when the quicksilver is converted into the essence of pure sulfur, which we call Scoaptesis, because

it is

--- Page 85 (p. 34, Theorica Cap. XVI cont.) ---
THEORICA.
it is between Optesis and Escatesis, and is signified by M. And with that and N we make the decoction of our said medicine, which we call Escatesis. Through the first digestion we extract the elements and bring them into concordance. And through the second we dissolve the spirit, informing its vessel, and where it must join itself with the heat of optesis after the digestion of optesis. And in the third we create and form our stone from that very matter immediately after the spirit has departed from the vessel and the soul from the body, which is the perfection of the stone. And in the fourth we create our royal and true elixir.
H Peptic digestion, or stomachic.
I Hepatic or Ephesis, that is, the bath.
K Solution of Periminel, that is, ashes.
L Optesis or Adulphuc, that is, ashes, sand.
M Optesis, that is, flame.
N Escathesis, that is, the secret furnace.

CAP. XVII.

Through the doctrine already declared it must be noted that three principal things are necessary to us in this part, namely the body, signified to us by N, and the spirit, which we signify by O, and the soul, which we wish to be signified by P. And yet, son, in the mutation of the stone, understand three principal colors. The first color therefore is black, and it begins to appear at the end of digestion L and lasts until the end of M. And when our quicksilver is congealed into sulfur, then in it appears a white color, subtle, whiter than snow, and this is the second color. The third, however, will appear in a very red heat, like blood, with the continuation of M upon the white stone. Son, when the blackness appears to you, it will be a sign of very strong and noble corruption, because no thing is animated through nature without putrefaction having occurred. Therefore make it pass through K and L until O is well white, so that N may become white through M. After this you shall rubify it with the fire of the stone in a successive digestion of N. Yet all practitioners must work patiently in this whitening

--- Page 87 (p. 35, Theorica Cap. XVII cont.) ---
THEORICA.
until the black is whitened perfectly, as we shall show through the property of the elements in this first book, chapter 22 and 23. Because from the black, citrine cannot be made until it is well white. Because citrine-ness is caused by a most white thing and a little clear red essentially. Nor similarly can a passage from citrine to white be made except through black. Nor can gold be made into medicinal silver unless it is first destroyed, and made black and corrupted. Nor can the better be made worse except through its own corruption. Because the generation of one thing is the corruption of another, and conversely. Therefore experienced practitioners, who know how to convert gold into silver, know how to transmute silver into gold likewise. Because that white sulfur which can be quickly made into red sulfur for gold through the digestion of N at the direction of silver, and this is to be understood with the fire of the stone alone, excited through M. Because in the substance of this is the natural heat of goodness and magnitude and of the other parts of its noble substance. And because in it there is a substantial majority, which is of its own proper essence, therefore it has a natural fire and the property of multiplying its species with its heat digesting itself into the other elements,

which

--- Page 88 (p. 35 verso, Theorica Cap. XVII cont.) ---
RAYMVNDI LVLLI
which are heatable, so that fire may avail itself of its heat, and multiply in them through the way of composition and generation with the continuation of M. Because the heat of M is stronger than the digestion of L. And therefore, son, you shall know that this rubification is nothing other than a complete digestion. For when I rise in the morning and see my urine white and undigested, it is a sign of little sleep. And therefore I rest and sleep, and I find my urine almost citrine, and I perceive that sleep is perfect. But when the morning urine is citrine, then the heat of natural fire has been diffused through all the urinal parts, through which nature manifestly teaches and shows that the citrination is completed through complete digestion. And therefore you can understand that white and red sulfur comes from one single matter of metals, namely fine silver with the fire of fine gold. But first it is necessary that the said matter be strongly purified and cooked through a differential manner, as is declared in the following chapter and without intermediary concerning its mixtures. Son, such sulfur cannot be found upon the earth, except that which is in these two bodies. Nor is any sulfur white, or red, or perfect for the magisterium, except that which is in the said bodies. And therefore the

bodies

--- Page 89 (page 36, Theorica Cap. XVII cont.) ---

these bodies must be nobly prepared, so that sulfur and quicksilver may be obtained upon the earth such as those from which gold and silver were created beneath the earth. With these bodies, quicksilver is mixed in a subtle manner. And therefore if you know how to mix quicksilver with these bodies, then you will arrive at the great secret, which we call white sulfur, or the blessed stone, which is afterwards made red, and is called the royal crown, and is the completed stone. From these two bodies, then, the sulfurs have been extracted for white and red, since in these two bodies are found the best and purest substance of sulfur, purified in the highest degree through the ingenuity of nature. And this substance is better, and its earth purer, and more subtle, than could be attained through art and knowledge by following nature, and through which one may best be made like unto nature in all things. For if art wished to begin in the first nature or matter in the likeness of nature, it would be exceedingly long and troublesome, and would be without end, nor would there be anything more base, and many infinite miseries and expenses would befall. And on account of this, art also, by purifying the first matter, would be more base and weaker than nature, and thus could never follow her with all its powers, for many other reasons contained in the tree of questions

of this science. And therefore this science chose gold for the father and silver for the mother. Therefore the tincture is of redness, and capable of restoring, and resplendent, tinging and transforming every body to perfect clarity. Silver, however, is the tincture of whiteness, tinging every body with imperfect clarity. With these bodies mix Mercury, yet not such as is common, as we shall say in the second part of this book. Wherefore you should know that quicksilver in its own nature is not miscible with bodies.

How the Artist must be virtuous in inquiring into the first knowledge of the stone, and from what matter it is made, and firmly constant in pursuing the second intention through the experience of the art known in the matter of nature.

CAP. XVIII.

Son, you who are a seeker of this science, it is necessary that you be firm, and not seek after this thing or that, since in many substances this art is never perfected. And therefore we say to you that there is nothing but one single stone, namely sulfur, and one single medicine, namely the composition of sulfur, to which you must add nothing, but only remove the superfluous earthy and phlegmatic parts. For these are and must be separable and separated from our quicksilver, which is more common to men than common quicksilver, and is of greater price, merit, and stronger union of nature, from which and from its first forms everything that is not of the harmonious succession of metals must be separated through known and recognized degrees of separation, until it approaches and is near to the first thing, namely the nearest of its own kind, insofar as it will be possible with real concordance, before it is incorporated with fire and earth. And then it is found multiplied with many noble virtues, and very weighty. No one should ask of nature what it does not have, nor will ever have. For all buttery sulfur,

--- Page 92 (Raymundi Lulli, Cap. XVIII cont.) ---

RAYMVNDI LVLLI

burning and combustible, is foreign to our quicksilver, because it is combustible in itself and corruptible, and alien to our quicksilver. And therefore when such sulfur is found upon the earth, it is fitting that it be composed through artifice, since vulgar sulfur is combustible in itself. Therefore, in the contrary sense, the thing is not foreign to our quicksilver into which it must be converted through the magisterium, namely into gold and silver. Note that nothing suits the stone unless it is of a matter close to its own nature, because from a man nothing is generated but a man, and other animals generate nothing but their like. And thus every thing that conceives can generate its like. And for this reason you must work with noble matter. And you should know where you will find it. I say to you that it is found in desert and depopulated lands. And when you have found it yourself and begun to work, do not put water in it, nor powder, nor any other thing, except that which has come forth from its own proper nature. For if you do the contrary, it will immediately deteriorate and be corrupted, nor will you arrive at your intention. And because in things that share a common quality the transition is easier, therefore in all corporeal things, and those most suitable and most similar to the metallic nature,

--- Page 93 (page 38, Theorica Cap. XVIII-XIX) ---

THEORICA.

it is necessary for you to receive the medicine of diminished bodies. Since it is necessary to have in their nature a greater suitability to them, so that it may more fully and more profoundly embrace them, with a permanent inherence and perpetual association, so that every body diminished in its own nature may also be more perfectly perfected and completed in its own nature in abundant fullness, and beyond that to the end, that Mercury may be naturally mixed with these same metals through the smallest parts, before it takes flight. And therefore a more subtle substance and one of lighter fusion in liquidity is necessary for you for composing our medicine than the bodies of metals are, and of stronger fixative retention than natural or composite quicksilver is in its own nature.

On the first knowledge of the stone, and from what matter it is made, and on the virtue of patience, which the Artist must have in practice.

CAP. XIX.

Dearest son, you can see manifestly that no body standing in its own nature can congeal

--- Page 94 (Raymundi Lulli, Cap. XIX) ---

RAYMVNDI LVLLI

Mercury, nor firmly adhere to it, nor similarly can the said Mercury in its own specific nature give a medicine by which it could heal infirm bodies and perfect those diminished from perfection, nor can it be altered from its nature into the specification of another form, without it happening that it separates itself from them. On account of this, we choose two luminary bodies, which fix everything that is not fixed. And therefore it is necessary that those luminary bodies be reduced through elemental retrogradation to their first nature, namely into sulfur and quicksilver, through contrary operations, which are dissolution, subtilization, and sublimation, and congelation — not vulgar, however, but philosophical. For we say to you that nothing in this science is secret except the mode of the work and of working, which we shall show you clearly in practice in the second part of this book, provided you wish to understand our theory, which admonishes you that you should be willing to be somewhat vexed in the said operations. For the bodies are of strong composition, and therefore they require a long separation and contrary operation, namely that first they be philosophically calcined, and afterwards also philosophically

--- Page 95 (page 39, Theorica Cap. XIX cont.) ---

THEORICA.

dissolved, as nature requires under the preservation that looks toward generation. For when they are calcined, they are more easily dissolved, because the heat of fire penetrates the body, and by penetrating makes its parts allow water to enter after it. And thus the body is more susceptible of dissolution. And when it is well dissolved, the spirit is congealed; and when the spirit is congealed, the body is dissolved. For without the congelation of the spirit, there is no dissolution of the body, nor dissolution of the body without congelation of the spirit. In this work, son, be long, slow, gentle, and mild, and do not hasten in anything. Permit all things to be perfected by a slow fire. For the first error in this art is to cook too much, because fire burns everything and carries it to the region of perdition. And understand that all delay in this art and likewise slowness are chosen by working patiently, and do not undertake the journey without patience. When it befalls you to know many things through study, retain patience, always working directly, and thus you will have philosophy as a habit, and grant virtue — patience is given to the work. For patience of character is always the greatest, and the greatest thing in our great and secret work, which indeed

--- Page 96 (Raymundi Lulli, Cap. XIX cont.) ---

RAYMVNDI LVLLI

causes one to penetrate and to know the great secret, yet through experience with the instrument of belief which nature has shown, directing and rightly leading the intellect to philosophy. And therefore take heed that you do not make the fire too great at the beginning of the mixture, because corruption would immediately come upon the tincture. Therefore in their first calcination, in which the tincture must be preserved, you shall proceed to the digestion of L, and from it you shall safely extract F from P in the manner that we shall tell you in the practice. And afterwards you shall place D joined with H in F in the digestion of I, and there they will immediately be calcined with the form preserved in them, and there you shall leave them for the space of three years in their own proper nature, and no more, lest the spirit suffer, which appointed term you will find in the second part. After the said term, you will find the body entirely black in intense color. Then you shall carry it to the digestion of K, because that will give N, O, and P, with which you shall mix nothing else either crude or cooked. After this, make O pass seven times through the digestion of I, then make P pass through K, which must be drawn from N with L up to the seventh exuberance. And thus you will have a very beautiful suitable matter for creating

--- Page 97 (p. 40, Theorica Cap. XIX / Cap. XX) ---
THEORICA.
our stone, in which natural heat is contained. And this is the reason why nature, once separated, joins its parts into one, so that the united virtue may be stronger than the dispersed.

On the creation of the stone, which occurs through the virtue of a certain digestion, and on its properties.

CAP. XX.

The creation of our stone is made solely from N. and O. through the digestion of M. until the black body is turned into white. Because then N. approaches as much as it can to its first nature, which is more through I. Therefore in the calcination, in which are the causes of the tincture, by preserving the Laccedes of digestion, and in their proximate nature to H. — because they were not D. nor E. nor F. And therefore it is not necessary that N. return to G., because it is closer to the nature of H. And E. in O., because it is closer to the nature of N., from which P. will come forth, which is the complement of our entire aforesaid magisterium. Nevertheless, keep this secret.

--- Page 98 (Theorica Cap. XX / Cap. XXI) ---
RAYMVNDI LVLLI

How the species of metals transmute themselves. And concerning the fire of elemental nature as its cause, because in no way are they converted. And therefore when there appears from them a species that is in the being of truth, goodness, and magnitude.

CAP. XXI.

After these things let the question be posed, whether metals or their species can be transmuted? Solution: That which is effectively seen by proof needs no other. In truth there is one special metallifactive [metal-making], metallifiable, and metallifacted in mineral nature. And from these concrete and concretized species there is the species of truth, which is the colorifactive and colorifying species. And those two species are in being and in essence. And therefore the species in being and the species in essence, insofar as they are, are not converted; but their individuals, in which through

--- Page 99 (p. 41, Theorica Cap. XXI) ---
THEORICA.
our work their simple mineral elements are bound together — through which binding this species is in act — are most readily converted. And through this the principal means are named in our magisterium, because they can be changed, cleansed, and purified under the preservation of their species, in such a way that the first species, which was in manifest act, is concrete in the essence of the concreted things, which are in created beings, which are not converted. And since from those there consists the species that is in the being of truth, and of goodness, and of magnitudes — which are in created beings — they are not converted, because if they were converted, real beings would be confused, and art and science could not follow the path by which they know, nor could nature multiply any thing to the likeness of the preceding genus. And therefore it was placed in the power of nature by the Creator that only the individuals in which real beings are contained, drawn from their species, without corruption or separation of them in the genus of any species, under their preservation, are transmuted for the purpose of multiplication. And from this our fire is created, and leaps, and from it comes forth light and brightness. And the light is our heat, which is multiplied through fire into the natural and against nature. And this in

--- Page 100 (Theorica Cap. XXI) ---
RAYMVNDI LVLLI
the individual parts of mineral nature, as experience will be made manifest to you, if through art in this practice you can be imbued. Because the figurative heat of our red stone, which has the fire of simple ignition, is a part of the created fire, and from it we draw our living ar. [argentum vivum, i.e., quicksilver] until it becomes a fire of greater composition. And therefore we call it composite fire, because subtly fixed within it is the heat of the simple fire, which comes from the unnatural and against nature through its colorative property, which is ignifactive, ignifiable, and ignifacted. And thus fire in its individual passive aspects moves potency to multiply its effect, action, complement, and end — not, however, that potency insofar as it is passive moves the active potency, but they are known likenesses to real beings toward the end, so that they may be penetrated, cooked, and transmuted into a better nature — namely from the nature of air into the nature of fire: thus it moves potency, and its objects toward completing and finishing, just as fire moves oil to give light and to illuminate the place in which it is. Hold, however, son, as a general rule of nature, that always at every time the

--- Page 101 (p. 42, Theorica Cap. XXI / Cap. XXII) ---
THEORICA.
object insofar as it is desirable moves the potency to attract to itself its desirable thing, just as a thing that desires to be perfected from its own and not from another, so that according to its complement it may have rest. And because nature, which always works, is not infinite — since it is composed of universal elemental parts — therefore in its work it is not infinite, nor is its work infinite, nor does nature make it infinite. Therefore you can understand that all the other elements show their potency in our stone according to the greater and lesser degree of their dominion. Because if air in the generation of the stone dominates over fire — fire being what terminates it — our stone will appear transparent over congealed water. And if water dominates, the element will show whiteness; if earth, it will manifest blackness.

How the entire making of the stone consists in the knowledge of the elements, and in their conversion.

CAP. XXII.

If you understand these elements and know how to convert them through certain operations, you will have the making and understanding of the stone, because according to their proportion we shall

--- Page 102 (Theorica Cap. XXII) ---
RAYMVNDI LVLLI
have their forms appearing in diverse species, and through diverse proportions of the mixture, a diverse form is given to the generated mixable things. And diverse proportion of mixture is in metals, as appears from their forms. And from this a part of the secret is moved, namely that unless you know the stones and their virtues in all their natures, we counsel you not to approach our stone. Son, form and species manifest to you the goodness of the material, or its impurity, and their baseness, and grossness, and simplicity. For a certain form and species according to its own property is given to its own proper matter, and according to the flux of the matter, forms are influxed, as gold and lead make manifest to you. And insofar as the matter is nobler, so much the nobler is the form of any mixture. And insofar as the form of the pure element is nobler, so much the nobler is the form of the body that has been purified from all corruption.

How the elements came forth from one sole matter which contains within itself the mineral species.

--- Page 103 (p. 43, Theorica Cap. XXIII) ---
THEORICA.

CAP. XXIII.

Our elements, son, came forth from one sole nature, into which the mineral species was influxed potentially, which afterward through the work of nature in manifest act showed itself at the end of their elemental circulation. And this work is not in act but in the rationale of power, through which nature with its intelligence places it in its own proper act. And for this reason our work is likened to nature, and exists only in rationale, because without it the course of natural operation would never find its end, unless it passes through by intellectual reason. And therefore few are those who know how to put our work into action. Because almost no one goes by the straight path with respect to nature, by which the elements must be changed under the preservation and multiplication of the species infused in them from potency to act. And thus it appears that our science is not in being, nor in action, but in potency, which we have drawn through intellectual reason, and placed from potency into action.

--- Page 104 (Theorica Cap. XXIII / Cap. XXIIII) ---
RAYMVNDI LVLLI

How the artist must know nature, so that he can assimilate himself to it in certain cases. And concerning the error of very many operators, and their admonishment. And how the instruments of nature are equated to the instruments of art, in which it is governed in its formation.

CAP. XXIIII.

When therefore you wish to make our stone, know first how and in what manner nature works and conducts itself. And follow this path and this method, and you will have from the said elements as much as you wish, because nature must work — and not you — to form, cleanse, and purify. And therefore it is necessary for you to know how to lead it through the right road, through the straight path, and to inform the matter in which nature is introduced, because if you know well how to guard it, what remains for you will give you more than you will know how to ask for. Afterward know specifically

--- Page 105 (p. 44, Theorica Cap. XXIIII) ---
THEORICA.
how many ways the foolish Alchemists err through their senses, uninstructed and inexperienced in three principal things and experiments, of which two return to one, namely, because they do not know how to regulate the fire, which is the entire master of the work, because it is that which congeals and dissolves at the same time, and therefore they do not have the power of understanding this, because they are all steeped in their foolish ignorance. The third cause is that they do not know how to prepare the matter in such a way that the informing virtue is given to it, through which the instrument that is in the matter is directed toward forming, by its own motion, that which we seek. And it is a venerable nature hidden in its workshop, which, son, if you know how to govern well, will appear dear and clear to you, as it appeared to us in clear light, in the form of white earth, subtle, foliated, and fusible, and it was close to the nature of fine silver, clear and precious. Wherefore in our magisterium do not employ an uncertain art in place of the informing virtue. Do not work with a burning fire in place of the instrument. The informing virtue is governed by a certain mode of working, which is given in a certain scientific reason, and this you must have before your eyes at the entrance of our magisterium. And before you do anything, see

--- Page 106 (Theorica Cap. XXIIII) ---
RAYMVNDI LVLLI
what you wish to do, and work wisely. From the gross make the simple, and from the heavy make the light, soften the rough, and make the hard soft, and the bitter sweet. And then you will have completeness in the knowledge of the instrument, which is directed by the informing virtue, moving the matter toward the form, of which it is the active former through the power that is infused into it from the higher part. And it is both place and that which is placed, because through infusion it has been generated in its place. And it is a place, because it generates another thing placed there through the properties of heaven, which have flowed into its matter through the rays of the sun and the stars. Yet that which the elemental and celestial virtues accomplish in their natural vessels, the very same they accomplish in their artificial vessels, if these are formed after the manner of natural vessels. And what the nature of the sun and the stars does through heat, this same thing it does through the heat of fire, provided it is so tempered that it does not overcome the motive and informing virtue that is in the matter, infused from above. Because in all things that are, and have been corrupted and putrefied, we have seen the virtues of the stars flow in, receiving determination in that thing toward which the matter has a natural affinity. Because the celestial virtue is strong

--- Page 107 (p. 45, Theorica Cap. XXIIII) ---
THEORICA.
and most universal, and receives its determination and end through the virtues of its subject in mixed things. And therefore when a mineral nature is infused into the matter, it immediately receives the determination of the mineral nature and virtue. And so it is with vegetable things and animal things, according to their material kind. And therefore, son, you must know the elements of our stone, which are of the matter of mineral nature, because in them the multiplicative mineral virtue is infused, which looks toward the perfection of mineral action through the determination that it received from mineral matter, not from vegetable or animal matter. Nevertheless, when you wish to have the mineral elements, do not take from the first or the last for creating our stone, because the first are very much, indeed too simple, and the last are too gross. When hunger comes to you, take bread and leave the flour. When you wish to make bread, take flour and leave the grain. And this philosophical saying is given to you about the elements figuratively, in which elements there are real mineral entities, from which the celestial instrumental virtue is moved. And note that the informing virtue moves the instrument, and the instrument

--- Page 108 (Theorica Cap. XXIIII / Cap. XXV) ---
RAYMVNDI LVLLI
moves the hidden matter, and the matter is moved from hidden potency into manifest act. And the informing virtue in the matter is introduced through science by the artificer in his natural operation.

On the informing virtue through which our
stone receives creation and multiplication
in its instruments. And on the sulfurous
nature of the elements.
And on the place of generation.

CAP. XXV.

After these things, son, through the preceding you can understand and discern what the virtue is through which our stone can and has the power to multiply itself, and whence it comes forth. And we say that it is a mineral virtue, which descends from heaven into the mineral nature, through which, according to the specification of its own nature, it received mineral strength and virtue, which has a regard toward the perfection of all its matter, insofar as it can extend itself, according to the individuals of the matter, which individuals are indeed enclosed in the universality of mineral nature, and into which by virtue of influence there descends and is infused

--- Page 109 (p. 46, Theorica Cap. XXV) ---
THEORICA.
the formative virtue of our stone, just as in the seed of a brute animal generated from the putrid superfluity of nourishment, the formative virtue of the animal descends from the testicular vessels, which virtue is afterwards, through the diligence of the specific nature, informed as individuals in the species of the preceding animal. And it is like a master carpenter, who informs his apprentice by working through his art. So indeed in the matter of our stone there is an informing virtue, which looks toward its own perfection and the perfection of another. And therefore we say that there is one mineral virtue, which forms stones and metals and all things that are intermediate between them. And further we say that it is the common virtue of mineral nature, and through it they are formed by the determination that it receives from the terrestrial water of their generation, and in that water it stands fixed. See then whether it is a common thing, since it is in stones that you tread upon daily. And note that from this the doctrine is clear, in which earth the soul must be terminated and Mercury must be conjoined. And on this passage let the epistle of Alexander be consulted, which speaks of those who say that the stones which are found are useless and of no value. Whence the rustics marvel, when they are told that our quick-

--- Page 110 (Theorica Cap. XXV) ---
RAYMVNDI LVLLI
silver and gold are of the nature of stones. Furthermore, son, you can better understand the formative virtue through this similitude. For in the gummy liquors that come forth from trees, such as frankincense, mastic, turpentine, pitch, and the like, you can understand that they are moistures mixed in the smallest parts with the subtle dry parts that exist in their trees by virtue and mixture of natural heat, which caused the moist to be acted upon by the dry, and the dry by the moist. Because when such liquors come forth from trees, immediately the moist manifests its dry nature. Because the natural heat dispersed throughout the moist is constrained by the dry that is tempered with the moist, or by the airy cold surrounding it, and so it can no longer move itself in the moist to form it into the figure and form of fruit, since it is outside the place of its vessel of generation. See therefore that you do not open the vessel until all the moisture is terminated and finished, according to how it would exclude its dew-falls and exhalations. This is understood concerning the earth that is not separated from the vessel of the earth of earth. And of this you have the example of a grain of wheat and of terminated things, when

--- Page 111 (p. 47, Theorica Cap. XXV) ---
THEORICA.
through airy cold, being constricted, they congeal. These gums congeal in this way when they are outside the place of their generation, in which place the formative virtue exists, namely heat, which forms those moistures through its two instruments into leaves and flowers and fruits according to their nature, when they were within their trees and were under the said virtue, namely the informing virtue, which was in the natural heat. And through this you can understand that just as the matter of the dry terrestrial substance, which has been acted upon through viscous moisture, together with the matter of the moist substance, which has been acted upon through fire and dry earth, is prepared for becoming a stone, and is generated into a stone through the infusion of the virtues of the stars and of the place, as we said above concerning the formative virtue of the stone — so a similar virtue is generated through infusion in the seed of the genital organs, namely when the seed has descended through the entire sponginess of the body and has been drawn into the seminal vessels, in such a way that the said virtue receives the determination of the place where it has been infused, and through this it receives the power of forming diverse figures on account of the difference in the quality of the place of generation. Because when the informing virtue is infused into mineral matter, it receives matter that is very dry in

--- Page 112 (Theorica Cap. XXV) ---
RAYMVNDI LVLLI
complexion, tending toward minerality. Therefore it is necessary for it that the influence be strong, namely that the heat be sufficient to direct the matter of the metal for the first digestion, or that the matter be sufficiently rarefied so that simple heat can move itself through the whole, until a stronger heat is generated in a stronger composite matter, so that it can direct the stronger matter. And therefore it is necessary that it follow the mineral matter, informing it according to the quality of the place in which nature is joined according to its own strength. In like manner it is in the nature of animals, which is complexioned with a hot and moist complexion, because their proper place is complexioned with the very same complexion, just as the testicles of animals are hot with heat and moist and wet, more or less strongly spongy. And so the virtue forms the quality of nature, guarding and specifying it according to the understanding of nature toward the species of the genus from which it came forth. And therefore we say that in every matter according to its species there is its own infused virtue. And this is what the text of the chronicle of Plato says: according to the merits of the materials, the celestial virtues have been infused, which work and form the things of nature in vegeta-

--- Page 113 (p. 48, Theorica Cap. XXV cont.) ---
THEORICA.
vegetables and minerals. And note that the influence of natural heat descends from the stars in summer, and then the air, containing all things, grows warm, and once warmed it heats bodies and dissolves them. Finally comes the influence of cold, compressing the heat of the air into the center of the body, and then vegetables begin to grow and germinate, and so do minerals. For this reason it is said concerning things that you should leave to the stars the matter for constraining heat into the depths. And from this it is established that natural heat must be accompanied by the heat of the place. And these we call the celestial virtue: the multiplicative, the vegetative, the mutative. Wherefore it is declared that our stone is formed by celestial virtue, and grows by multiplicative virtue, and sprouts and is generated by vegetative virtue, and is changed by mutative virtue, and is corrupted by corruptive virtue, and is generated by generative virtue from corruption into generation. And therefore the celestial virtue is called multiplicative, because it multiplies; vegetative, because it grows and bears fruit; mutative, because it changes; corruptive, because it corrupts; generative, because it generates. And these are six virtues, which receive their determination from the first, which desires to be formed. And formation cannot

--- Page 114 (Theorica Cap. XXV cont.) ---
RAYMVNDI LVLLI
exist without change and alteration of matter. Therefore it is fitting and necessary that this first virtue alone clothe itself in the virtues of all the other virtues, according to the property of the alterable and passive matter. Wherefore it appears that it cannot be formed without change of matter, nor changed without corruption, nor generated, multiplied, or vegetated without alteration of that matter. By which it appears that the celestial virtue, for the sake of formation — namely, so that it may perfect and form matter — does all the aforesaid things, and thus it is declared how nature passes through its intermediaries to its extremes. It is also declared through this that no matter can be perfected unless it suffers for a long time. And therefore it is necessary that in your matter this virtue be infused, which is the soul of the stone, because the soul of our stone is from stones, just as the vegetable soul is from plants, and the vital soul from beasts — not, however, that you should understand it to be vegetable in the same way as plants, but only in following the order, according to its potency, and according to what its matter can sustain of the power of its most noble celestial virtue, and insofar as its matter is more subtle,

--- Page 115 (p. 49, Theorica Cap. XXV cont. / Cap. XXVI) ---
THEORICA.
it works more or less upon it at its will. Not that it is like the vital soul, which is the action of organic and philosophical members, having in itself the power of life, because matter of a terrestrial, hard, and dry nature does not permit this; but rather like an art, with which the artisan works in his craft. And therefore it is necessary for you to know that every such virtue, which forms matter according to the genus of nature, requires two proper instruments, through which it may form its work according to the diversity of its matter, concerning which instruments we shall speak in the second part of the second chapter. Because here we still wish to instruct you about the said virtues, and to declare them through examples, by means of the oft-mentioned vegetables.

On the death of the stone, and on certain causes
and things impeding generation and multiplication,
and how they may be removed through the
demonstration of an example with respect
to common nature.

CAP. XXVI.

In our stone, son, the said virtue is dead, which does not have the power of multiplying,

--- Page 116 (Theorica Cap. XXVI cont.) ---
RAYMVNDI LVLLI
by reason of its hard and overly compacted matter, since through it the heat is bound and cannot move itself — the heat by which the said virtue ought to govern itself in informing our stone. And from this we give you the example of a grain of wheat and all other seeds, because a grain of wheat existing by itself alone in compacted and dry matter does not have the power of growing, according to the course of nature, nor of multiplying itself, except through a certain dissolution and attenuation through the simplification of its hard substance, in which hard substance the heat existing within is congealed and enclosed. This substance is terminated and mortified by reason of dryness and hardness, in which the matter is terminated, in which the heat of nature is retained. For excessive dryness and coldness cause a mortifying quality. And through this the farmer can recognize that such dryness causing hardness and mortification needs airy moistening, so that the natural heat may be resuscitated and extended in the simple matter of air, which is its material nourishment. But to reduce this density of gross parts, or hardness, to simplicity is not given to nature except through the subtilization of its dry parts,

--- Page 117 (p. 50, Theorica Cap. XXVI cont.) ---
THEORICA.
accomplished by dissolution, which is made through the re-crudation [return to rawness] of the moist and humid material of the grain, which had been terminated into a cooked state in perfect digestion, in the manner of a white subtle substance, from which is made the bread that you eat. And the re-crudation of the said seminal moisture, terminated in dryness, is caused by the subtle, cold, and crude moisture which it receives in its dissolution. Therefore it is necessary that that dry substance be terminated or converted into airy moisture, which is the friend and nourishment of fire, which by its virtue forms growing things. But since dry and moist are opposite qualities through the difference of their contrariety, therefore it is necessary that through the difference of concordance the elements be transmuted from quality to quality, and one quality into another — namely, dry into cold, and cold into moist, and moist into hot — so that the matter may be made suitable and joined through fire, through this difference of concordance. This is to say that the dry matter of the grain is converted into material moisture, in which there is natural heat, through the difference of concordance, which has the power to receive the informative virtue of heaven from it. Wherefore

--- Page 118 (Theorica Cap. XXVI cont.) ---
RAYMVNDI LVLLI
it appears that nature does not pass from one contrary to another without an intermediary, through which the difference of concordance is caused, by which matter is transmuted from nature to nature. And this is the special difference that must be guarded. For water is the friend of air through humidity, and neighbor of earth through coldness. From which, through rarefaction, alleviation, and subtilization, comes re-crudation in things that are and have been cooked, condensed, and terminated by nature and art. And earth is neighbor to fire on account of its dryness, and fire is neighbor to air on account of its heat. From which earth, through rarefaction and alleviation or subtilization, comes re-crudation in things that are and have been cooked, condensed, and terminated by art and nature. Son, condensation and rarefaction are the original path in the transmutation of elements, in the degrees of things. And this is the common doctrine, by which sagacious nature converts its elements. And therefore the grain of wheat, when it has been loosened, dissolved, and re-crudated, has been transmuted from its nature into another work. There coldness and humidity operate, which is crude water. And therefore the heat, in which the vegetable virtue resides

--- Page 119 (p. 51, Theorica Cap. XXVI cont.) ---
THEORICA.
potentially, still feels the formative virtue of the second instrument, which is moved by heaven to inform natural heat through another information. And through this it cannot have the manner of actually penetrating, nor can it move itself there in the parts of its material moisture. For it is as if dulled in its material principle, by reason of the cold and moist that entered into its material through the dissolution of the said dry substance, with the superfluity of much great cold moisture. But afterwards from the second instrument there descends the celestial virtue through the heat of the sun, dissolving and diminishing from the said cold moisture as much as is needed for the intention of nature and no more, but only to the point of excitation and acceleration of natural heat. For this matter loses the superfluity of coldness that held it mortified. And when this natural heat, which has been excited and accelerated by the heat of the sun, through the continuation of the instrumentation of that heat, begins to move itself in its moist and humid material, by which it was bound, then in moving itself through it, it penetrates, and by penetrating it expands, and by expanding it forms, and converts its material moisture into its own

--- Page 120 (Theorica Cap. XXVI cont.) ---
RAYMVNDI LVLLI
proper species, and into its own proper being and existence. And thus the said heat does not cease to work within its matter, vegetating, sprouting, growing, and augmenting, extending and stretching out, and also multiplying, so much and until it arrives at the limit where there is no more humid matter. And then the moisture begins to be terminated and thickened by the informative virtue carrying from others through concordance, and the heat is mortified through dryness, which the grain of wheat receives in its termination through compaction and hardness, until another time it returns again into dissolution and putrefaction. Wherefore the wheel of elements by nature turns whole and round in a circuit, and will do so until it arrives at its own terminated point. And thus through evident experience nature shows you that the virtue which is in potency in the seed of grain is at no time brought into effect and productive and vegetative action, except after the corruption and putrefaction of its own proper matter, which is the cause of alleviation and subtilization and rarefaction, until it arrives at the point of generation, which we call

--- Page 121 (p. 52, Theorica) ---
THEORICA.
we call multiplication, from one grain into a hundred, and from a hundred into ten thousand, we name it so. And therefore it is fitting to believe the farmers, that if a grain of wheat is not cast into the earth, at no time will its virtue be multiplied, because its dry nature does not wish to suffer that, on account of its termination, made through the conversion of the subtle into the thick. And if it is cast into the earth, it will lighten itself through dissolution, and its moisture will increase through putrefaction, through which the power of its soul proceeds — not so much that it makes it multiply in celestial heat by ascending upward, as if it were better there than here below, but just as it was. See therefore how the pure and clean is separated from the vile and impure and muddy, viscous matter through elementation, which received putrefaction through rarefaction, through which it is exalted and called wheat. We say therefore that wheat is things exalted on high, which are desired by all the peoples of the world and so named. Wherefore we say that through the labors of farmers the celestial virtues are aided, namely through plowing, cultivation, sowing, and the enriching of the earth. And in such a way farmers help nature, so that

--- Page 122 (Theorica) ---
RAYMVNDI LVLLI
the fruits may be more quickly produced and improved. In a similar way we say to you concerning our magisterium, because first it is necessary for us that the substance, which is the pure nature of our stone, be dissolved through the subtilization of its gross substance. Afterwards let it putrefy, and let the moisture which is cold and unnatural be well separated by solar heat, which works against fire. Afterwards let its proper food be administered to it, which the virtue already mentioned has the power to transmute within itself. And in this way the mineral matter will be aided through the industry of the Alchemist. In such form that before every man it will manifest its effects in clear light, and its powers will need no logical proof. Because it has been well concluded for your understanding of this natural work, in what form the artificer of this natural work ought to be inclined and directed through instrumental or rational consideration, and not sophistical, together with the principles of natural philosophy. For notwithstanding, although the logician may have a profound and argumentative or natural wit concerning extrinsic things, never with all of this through any reasoning that comes to the senses will he be able directly to know

--- Page 123 (p. 53, Theorica) ---
THEORICA.
or judge by what nature or virtue, through intrinsic strength, his multiplication must grow upon the earth, except by way of likeness, as if simply believing our magisterium to exist or not to exist, nor how it can come about that our seed sprouts in its earth, grows, and gathers its fruits and its harvests. This you will never know unless through the teaching of experience you have first entered into natural philosophy, and not the sophistical kind, which is born through diverse fantastical presumptions infused into the heads of those who, with elevated prognostications against the force and will of reason and nature, make many to err obstinately in the sophistication of the mind. Wherefore I conclude through the reasons stated above, that if through natural industry, with clear and evident experiments, you have not obtained right knowledge of the thing already investigated, you will never be invited by nature to so precious a feast. He therefore who does not know the virtue of stones and their property, which bring bodies from potency into effect and into act, as has been known experimentally, will not taste of our precious food. It is therefore necessary for you to learn the nature of our stones

--- Page 124 (Theorica) ---
RAYMVNDI LVLLI
through experience, by way of practice, with the aid of theory and demonstrable teaching, or through the revelation of the secret. Because theory forms little unless you have experience through practice, and through experience practice is formed, and also the clear truth that illuminates the human intellect. Son, therefore you ought to know well the process that will show you the nature of stones and the virtue which through its power converts that which no other thing can do, except it through its own likeness. And that is a thing secret to the ignorant, yet common to all the wise, like the light of the world, knowledge of which you will have through the way of experiments, and not without them, or some of them. Because from experience itself the truth of the thing has come forth, through which the tincture is multiplied with the aid of the instruction of our natural magisterium.

On the corruption and putrefaction of all
things. And how every thing is born, and
receives life and a natural soul from
putrefaction, as from the putrid
belly of its mother, through the
transmutation of the elements.

--- Page 125 (p. 54, Theorica Cap. XXVII) ---
THEORICA.
CAP. XXVII.

Here you can understand that no thing can be animated, born, or created except after its corruption and putrefaction and mortification. Because then the nature of the matter is changed from nature into nature, like a fragile thing and matter that has not had the complement of its perfection. Therefore draw the form from its matter through the corruption of that matter, so that the matter itself, separated from its corruption, may be perfected in its sublimation. Son, in all matter there is a certain active potency, as has been said — if you have understood us — which is called an incomplete form, and by reason of its imperfection it is common to two contraries, which are called, as it were, the informing agents of that matter, according to its instinct and disposition. For when this potency is led by the informing virtue from potency into the act of some form, it immediately has an appetite to stand under the contrary form, just as this potency which is commonly found in the cold and moist. For existing under one body, it immediately desires to be under the contrary form, that is, a contrary quality. Because no form of a corrupti-

--- Page 126 (Theorica Cap. XXVII) ---
RAYMVNDI LVLLI
ble or generable body can ever sufficiently complete the active potency of its matter, since it desires to be completed under another form. Nor can this form (since it is desperate) introduce that into the matter except after the corruption of the first form. And therefore, insofar as the active virtue of the form to be introduced corrupts the present form, so is the passive potency excited and accelerated toward the contrary form, and it gives gradually that it may be more and better completed toward a greater complement, until it is brought under the complete action of that form, and without its proper qualities it cannot exist, nor is it the whole matter. Therefore, son, when you wish to corrupt our stone, do this through dry fire, so that the dry may be converted into cold. And when you wish to approach generation, cause the moist to be converted into heat through coldness. And when you are at the point of generation, place the cold moist with the hot dry of its nature. And when you wish to cool water with heat, heat it more. And when you wish to kindle fire, let it be bathed within with cold water. And if you wish to dissolve snow through the coldness of P., congeal it better, and thus

--- Page 127 (p. 55, Theorica Cap. XXVII) ---
THEORICA.
they are reversed, and you will find the gradual mutations of nature, according to the degrees of the scale of nature, which is round, and rounds the elements, as has been said. If therefore you convert the dry into cold, the cold into moist, and the moist into hot, you will have the magisterium. Because in such a way nature exits from bad nature and is converted into good, and the good into better. And therefore in this art we capture nature in a double manner. First we receive for the retained, as is the fire of nature. Second we take for the container of it, as is air, in which fire has the power to move to form the things of nature. And because air cannot exist unless it has the nature of fire, nor can anything form any material species in its own likeness except in a proximate nature, it is clear that our stone is composed of the matter of air. Because unless the tincture were material, it could not manifest fire in effect or action, nor could fire burn without the food of air, nor would it give brightness by itself without air. Because in every composite there is the fire of nature, and that fire is moved by certain information, and by moving itself it moves matter, at all times terminating it and always completing it. And it is necessary that by moving it lead to some

--- Page 128 (Theorica Cap. XXVII-XXVIII) ---
RAYMVNDI LVLLI
species, according to how the former knows how to inform it well, namely toward that to which the matter will have a suitability according to the course of nature. On account of which it has been declared that you should not work except with the noblest matter you can obtain, because a thing does not exist except according to nature and is commended by it.

On the moisture of our stone, and how
it is permanent water, and on the
diverse actions of fire, and on
the subtilization of gross
elements, and on
blackness.

CAP. XXVIII.

Our moisture, son, is permanent water, because it does not lose its radical moisture, nor is it burned by fire, nor can the fire of our dead stone consume it, but rather it seems to be changed from one heat into another, and from more into better — indeed it seems to be augmented, improved, and to grow in its property. For fire is its proper nourishment, and

--- Page 129 (page 56, Theorica Cap. XXVIII cont.) ---
THEORICA.
the cause of its noble increase. Therefore, when you wish to begin the preparation of our magisterium, take water proportioned in quality according to the nature of the body that you wish to dissolve, in such a manner that the force of fire against nature does not overcome the natural heat. Because bodies are not of the same quality in measure, although they were once all of one virtue, in measure in the Lord our God, yet now they have participation in the difference of concordance. For some of them are not of such strong composition, nor is their sulfur so subtle or tempered. And therefore they can unbind themselves with a more fragile virtue. Dearest son, the fire that warms a man makes a lion cold, and the fire that warms a lion burns and consumes a man. By this, moreover, you must know of what strengths the virtues of our stones are, to the end that the quicksilver may be tempered with respect to the power of the sulfur of nature through a certain knowledge of gradation in its first resolution, because therein lies the whole danger. And therefore I make known to you that you should have sulfurs destroyed by the heat of simple quicksilvers in

--- Page 130 (Theorica Cap. XXVIII cont.) ---
RAYMVNDI LVLLI
such a form that their active property is not destroyed by extraneous heat, and that it cannot be separated from its moist subject, which appears entirely black and full of noble spirit. Thus the blackness demonstrates the sign of the first gate, for entering our magisterium. And without it nothing can be done, because it is the fire of nature, which must create the stone, which cannot be manifested without the corruption of its body. And unless it is manifested, it will never be in action, through which it could grow or generate. Since moreover it has been revealed that the beginning of our operation is in the form of the head of a crow, and the preceding operations without the beginning of our operation are all erroneous, until the whole composition manifests itself as black: therefore, son, do not think that our blackness comes suddenly or quickly, but gently and gradually, because it is necessary that the heats pass through their means just as the elements do. Because the fire of nature, since it is enclosed in the deepest part of its body, cannot show itself immediately, until it has been opened. For in the gross elements the fire is enclosed and hidden. And therefore it is necessary to subtilize the elements, namely, the earth and wa-

--- Page 131 (page 57, Theorica Cap. XXVIII cont.) ---
THEORICA.
ter, through gentle digestion, from which greenness is caused, which is the first color, whose sign gives to know that the action of the released heat is weak and slow, and consequently insufficient to dissolve and rarefy the terrestrial parts. Therefore on the skin and surface, and everywhere, a green color manifests itself, undigested and terrestrial in the belly, whose natural heat multiplies, which on account of the exceeding moisture that holds it dulled, cannot manifest itself. But as was said before, the natural heat is gradually strengthened by the aid of the heat of fire, administered by the Artist and operator, who well knows how to dispose, inform, and cook his matter with his understanding, until it appears clothed in black above. Because then the natural fire fights against the moisture, from which the blackness is created. When therefore it begins to appear, increase it and multiply it through the natural information, by which the natural fire is governed, which begins to digest the matter, as is apparent from the blackness. And from this it has been declared that at the point of blackness our fire begins to operate, and not otherwise. And therefore it is called the beginning of our magisterium, because then the heat of our

--- Page 132 (Theorica Cap. XXVIII cont.) ---
RAYMVNDI LVLLI
matter has been excited and accelerated, which has the power to putrefy all its elements, up to the end of this digestion, which is the precursor of the heat of our natural art, until the matter is completed, because the sharp and terrestrial moisture, altered by its contrary and aided by its like, suitably and aptly digests itself. And then in the completed matter, through the degree of the said digestion, the black color multiplies, not only on the surface or on the skin, but through all the subtle parts, and separated throughout the whole matter through multiplication, which is generated by natural heat in the radical moisture. Whence our white tincture becomes like snow, and then we say that the body is fused and liquefied within and without, and not by the power or action of common fire, but by the power of the fire that is natural heat. Because this fire seizes, impregnates, and thickens our stone with a stronger heat than that of elemental fire. And thus we see the powers and virtues of things, and know them through their effects. Keep this thing secret, son: that the natural heat, before the diminution of the moist, captures all the gross substance of the earthly matter, which it could not previously dissolve well. And afterwards through its stron-

--- Page 133 (page 58, Theorica Cap. XXVIII-XXIX) ---
THEORICA.
ger action it resolves and digests so much that it comes to the ultimate point of its action in the first digestion. And when the matter is thus digested, putrefied, and optimally cooked through the said digestion, nature separates the subtle from the thick, and the gross is cast toward the parts placed behind and removed through the action of the second digestion. And as much as is necessary, it converts from the subtle into the substance of sulfur, created from quicksilver, according as this conversion has been made through the information given to the heat of nature in the third digestion.

On the manner of philosophizing. And how
this science and art has been given only to
philosophers. And that the congelation of quick-

on the patience of the operator,

CAP.  XXIX.

The books of this science speak through tacit words to be strongly noted, to the end that the principal things contained in them — and that this secret may not be manifested except to philosophers and sons of

--- Page 134 (Theorica Cap. XXIX cont.) ---
RAYMVNDI LVLLI
our philosophy, who are investigators and seekers of truth, and lovers of it, and not to sophists full of lies, nor to the avaricious, nor to worldly lovers, such as usurers, merchants, and renewers of coinage, money-changers, and hoarders of treasures, condemned and enchanted by the devil, who do not wish to labor or inquire except into palpable things, because they are steeped and poisoned in worldly affairs, nor can they open their understanding to comprehend our good doctrines, through which they could have an enduring treasure and salvation. Therefore we shall say to you, son, that you wisely consider the doctrines that we shall give you in our books, and note them, and gloss them strongly through many considerations and reiterated speculations, if you desire to understand us. And when you understand us, seize it with both hands, covered with your shirt, the garment of philosophy, and thus leave the doctrines to others, just as our fathers left them to us and not otherwise. And if you do the contrary, you will be accursed, like one who participates in the confusion of the world. And do not reveal it by the living voice, because no one should possess the art without labor,

--- Page 135 (page 59, Theorica Cap. XXIX cont.) ---
THEORICA.
nor actually manifest it to anyone without consideration, except through the covering of philosophy, and to those who are illuminated and elevated through their understanding, and who have instruments rectified for penetrating the causes and secret things of nature. And you who desire to be our son, and nourished in philosophy, must search through the revelation of your noble understanding for that which we say to be true in our magisterium. Namely, that the fume of our fermented gold, after reduction and fixation in its earth, will appear visibly in the fire of the philosophers. And therefore, son, many considerations are necessary before you proceed to the formation of our stone, and that you be a searcher through the revelation of the noble understanding of those things which we say to be true in our magisterium, namely, how the fume of our fermented gold after fixation and reduction into its earth appears visibly in the philosophical fire. Know therefore that the earth in which our gold must be sown and planted is first white before it can manifest the sight of our fire. Yet you shall have patience, son, in the whitening, because there is great delay there. Therefore make your earth be whit-

--- Page 136 (Theorica Cap. XXIX cont.) ---
RAYMVNDI LVLLI
ened, and nourish it, if you wish it to be reborn and to sustain you in your necessities. This earth is our magnesia, in which lies our secret. And our final secret is the congelation of our quicksilver, without which no certainty of our science can be had, which at the beginning of our magisterium is the congelation of quicksilver in our magnesia through skill and a certain regimen. The spirit of the fifth essence is in our magnesia. Our magnesia is the quicksilver of which we have told you. And from this came forth the fume of our gold and silver, through which every color comes. And if you know how to cast it out of the bodies, you will have half the secret. And if you make it return, you will have its magnesia colored like snow. For just as it is cast out from the bodies, so it returns into them, namely, both from the mineral means and from the metals with quicksilver. For since quicksilver is the medium of conjoining tinctures in a pure subject, so it will be the medium of separating them from the impure, which is a thing against nature. And by this Mercury understand the water in which the tincture is carried. Because fire, when it is separated from one subject, is immediately introduced into another, unless it is impeded by extraneous fire.

--- Page 137 (page 60, Theorica Cap. XXIX cont. / Cap. XXX) ---
THEORICA.
since it is incorruptible. And therefore it is necessary, since it cannot be had in essential form, that it be had in substantial form, namely in the matter of Mercury, which is our water. And know, son, that our fire is drawn from the body little by little, and afterwards is turned into a body through its noble heat. Son, this is the necessary seed, with which our tincture is fixed in the body, when it is drawn from it with the water of fire. Son, therefore impregnate the body with fire, and multiply its combustion, and you will have a strong tincture. And understand the secret. For the beginning is also the completion of the whole, as we shall say in the second part of the practice.

Whence the proper tincture of the philosophers
is extracted, which tints congealed quick-
