And therefore it is necessary for you to read, to examine, and to study with many rational considerations this regimen, because here lies the entire danger and the entire benefit, and the entire mastery — namely, in forming our child and breathing the soul into its body with the moisture and heat of the true and clear fifth light. For then you will see the matter transmuted from color to color. And do not understand that this should be done by force, but through ingenuity and subtlety, which you must learn from nature, for through her own wisdom nature takes great care to bring matter to its perfection. Therefore, if at all times you desire to discover the work through nature, always let nature arrange her matter with full knowledge in the fire, according to what nature requires and demands in arranging it. For then

--- Page 203 (page 93, Theorica Cap. XLIX cont.) ---
THEORICA.
she will perform her own proper duty, because she forms, places, and transmutes the matter — not you — when she finds that the matter has been established and prepared through art with respect to natural principles. Then nature, through diligence and intelligence, perfects all the matter in obedient matter. And just as you have nothing to do except minister to nature, since no form except the soul moves nature according to its action, and without motion a thing cannot be generated. And therefore the entire effect proceeds from the soul. Not that you should understand this as though it were an organic philosophical body possessing vital and animal power, but rather as an operator in its artifice. And therefore, son, this soul is not a sixth virtue, but is the fifth virtue of the four elements, which arises above them and from the purer essence of the four elements. And therefore it rests in the sphere of fire and dwells there through continuous motion, and the cause of its motion is its natural heat, excited by simple elemental fire, as if in the same degree. Guard it therefore lest it escape through excessive heat, and hold it in love.

--- Page 204 (Theorica Cap. L) ---
RAYMVNDI LVLLI

How the elemental natures proceed from the male and the female to bring about generation.

CAP. L.

Now, son, since of the four natures spoken of above, two depend on the male and the other two on the female, it is necessary that together they undergo change in their natures, until the aforesaid fifth virtue places them in true concord and binds them with such a bond that never, to the end of the term of their mixture, either by itself or by accident, shall it be wholly separated, dissolved, or unbound from the whole composite. And then the elements have no power to hold themselves together, but remain separated and unbound elementally one from another, when their bond — that is, the aforesaid fifth virtue — has been separated and dissolved from them, like a confused and desolate thing that has lost its governor. O you children, sons of the golden doctrine, take note of this most noble virtue, if you wish to bind the four elements together through unity, because without it neither a true mixture nor a union of aurigraphy can be made of them. And since this is of such a temperament that it is born from noble con-

--- Page 205 (page 94, Theorica Cap. L-LI) ---
THEORICA.
cord with its own measure descending from a higher nature — which nature possesses in itself the philosophy and science of the seven liberal arts by which it operates. For it carries in itself every geometric form, and terminates every thing by virtue of its arithmetic through the equality of a certain number, and through rhetorical knowledge it brings its understanding from potency into action. You therefore, sons of the doctrine, must know this instrument, if you wish to know and to see through certain experience the virtues infused and to be reinfused, as much by the wisdom of nature as by the sagacity of art, in its composites.

On the intention by which the union of the four elements is made. And how it can be done, and when, and why it was done.

CAP. LI.

Before you make the union of the spiritual elements, son, it is necessary to know that through the intention of fixing them you wish to unite them to the end that this unity may be fixed until it can resist the war of fire. Son, fire is of such a property that it cannot allow any thing having corruption to endure —

--- Page 206 (Theorica Cap. LI cont.) ---
RAYMVNDI LVLLI
indeed, it altogether annihilates such a thing according to its power, since its property wills that no corruption remain after it, on account of the great difference of contrariety that exists between the property of fire and the impurity of the other elements. Therefore, son, it is necessary for you first to prepare the elements with philosophical purgation, until you have that pure nature which is appropriate to the pure element and to the first matter extracted from their center in a clear and resplendent crystalline form. And this — that is, that element — is called fire, which is incorruptible, through which the philosophers say that bodies are dissolved into quicksilver, which it is wisdom to extract. For we have already told you in the chapter on the greater form that the earth which we tread upon is not a pure element, but is made elemental by the true fifth element, because in its center a virgin and a true virginal element was found, and so you should understand it of the other elements as well. Here again we tell you that everything which is not of the pure nature of the elements will be burned and utterly annihilated, and then the pure, clear, and clean elements themselves will be revealed, and an earth more resplendent than crys-

--- Page 207 (page 95, Theorica Cap. LI cont.) ---
THEORICA.
tal. And the supreme creator will effect this purgation until the great world is converted into its first matter — namely, all elements into their first essence — which afterward will not fear the fire of heaven, because then the motion of the supreme nature will remain in itself without any corruption. So, son, you must make your work resemble this work, which is called the lesser world, in the purgation of the great world, until its motion remains without any corruption. And then it will no longer fear fire, but all the elements together will rest in great love. See therefore how much you are indebted to the supreme creator, who gave you being in order to save you, and demonstrated such things to you if you wish to see or possess them. Therefore, son, know that since no thing imbued with corruption can endure or tolerate fire, nor can fire endure corruption in itself, it is necessary that the stone be drawn out clean and masterfully, separated from all impurities and corruptible dregs, before you presume to fix it, until it is a pure nature, which is a thing appropriate to the pure element and to the first matter.

--- Page 208 (Theorica Cap. LII) ---
RAYMVNDI LVLLI

How the whole substance of the stone is impregnated with the fire of the spirit of the fifth essence. And on the gum of the philosophers.

CAP. LII.

Know, son, that the substance of our stone is entirely fatty and impregnated with fire, in which it moves, and the spirit of the fifth essence is hidden therein and dwells there — namely, in the sphere of fire — since our stone, which is the fifth spirit, is entirely full of fire, and exists in all four elements whether divided or undivided, and in all of them dwells the dragon — namely, fire — in which is our aerial stone. This property exists in all composites of the world. And therefore it is said that our stone is in all composites of the world, and throughout the whole world, by those who understand well. But the difference between stone and stone is this: for the temperament cannot be achieved except through ferments, which must necessarily fix the stone after it has been turned into the nature of metal — namely, sulfur and quicksilver — and the ferment does not come from this or that, but from the sun and the moon only. Since we seek nothing except that the stone be converted into its su-

--- Page 209 (page 96, Theorica Cap. LII cont.) ---
THEORICA.
a similar thing, because from these the entire temperament is made, nor is there ferment before the said bodies are converted into their first matter. For it is fitting that the ferment be composed from oil and very subtle earth, and likewise the medicine through natural inceration, dissolution, and induration, until it becomes like a single gummy substance. And this gum has the power of attracting to itself every other thing to which it is joined. Son, this gum is our composite ferment, and likewise a medicine, which has the power of converting after the supreme medicine, which has afterwards been fermented with the said composite ferment. And our said simple ferments are our first oils, from which we make our composite ferments, which are medicines of great value. Therefore, unless you know how to reduce two perfect bodies to their first nature, you will not be able to have any composite ferments.

On the universal virtue that separates the elements, and that conjoins them, and whence it was created, in universal knowledge.

--- Page 210 (Theorica Cap. LIII) ---
RAYMVNDI LVLLI

CAP. LIII.

It is certain, son, that a certain influxed virtue exists in all elemented bodies, because without it they could not exist; but in homogeneous things that have reached their terminus, the said virtue remains in perpetual minerality, and it is that which must endure all heat, by reason of its perfect decoction in the form of liquorous moisture. And therefore it is necessary that you draw it from the perfect metals, because from them comes the termination of our perfection and the clarity of the fifth light. This virtue, therefore, through a similar virtue that exists in crude things not terminated to the extreme but terminated only to a mediocrity — from which you will separate water, fire, air, and earth, and these four crude elements are impregnated with a similar virtue, but more crude on account of the material of their vessels and by reason of the potency of their crude nature — you must attract the nature that is more cooked, hot, moist, and aerially subtle, which by the ingenuity of nature has been congealed into the species and nature of metal, influxed in its own proper mine. For this is more proximate to nature than the first matter, which more proximately descends from the form of

--- Page 211 (page 97, Theorica Cap. LIII cont.) ---
THEORICA.
forms, which at no time joins itself to nor is converted into the form of metal, nor will it do so, until it has converted the crude, astringent, and bitter metal into its own nature, and until that crude thing has been acted upon by the cooked, and conversely the cooked by the crude. And in such a manner, son, you should wish to receive the ancient form with all its substantial parts, because this is the true species and pure nature of true clarity, and through the new you should wish to strengthen it, or to augment its powers through the administration of celestial influence. For it is necessary that nature be aided by nature, and the inferior needs the superior, just as the matter of the female needs strengthening, and that it be perfected by the matter of its male, as we have said: that the virtue of the male, tempered in heat and moisture, tempers and strengthens the passive virtue, to retain the germinative virtue, and breaks and drives away all its excessive coldnesses to a remote region through its appetite in the celestial virtue, which is called the form and mother of forms, descending in virtue conjoined with the more proximate nature of the metal, with its virtuous and subtle influence, filling the intermediate forms, according to the genus and form of the mat-

--- Page 212 (Theorica Cap. LIII cont.) ---
RAYMVNDI LVLLI
ter, with the complexion of truest love. Note therefore, son, these three virtues. For the first descends from the prime mover, or from the stellate motion and its figures. And this in our testament we name the first form of forms. This is the first form, through which inferior matters are transmuted from form to form solely through the course and institution of nature. And of this form nature has enough to form and bring from potency into act its first composites through the materials of the elements. And this form cannot be obedient to art. The second virtue is the virtue infused in composite elemental matters, which it is necessary for you to draw from those matters in which it exists, by the method of converting its kind to its first nature, or of approximating as much as possible to its first thing; and this is sufficient for art, because it is material. And on this point art cannot be likened to nature, because it cannot give substantial form to things, as nature does through the ingenuity of its noble operation and intention. And therefore it is necessary for art to have recourse to nature, to obtain from its substantial forms, which it has already created, in order to for-

--- Page 213 (page 98, Theorica Cap. LIII cont.) ---
THEORICA.
m one body with such potency, which lies dead in the earth: and to teach you how you may draw that body from its created composites, and to show you its creation, how it converts matter from potency into the act of generation. The subtle artist, therefore, son, must carefully observe how nature operates, so that through art he may be able to liken himself to it, and follow it as much as possible, and that which is not possible, he may know how to supply through intellectual ingenuity with respect to nature, if he desires our thing, which is perfected by nature, and that thing simply arises from the third virtue, which you can understand through the second. And although we have said that there are three forms, there is nevertheless only one, because it does not receive the end of termination except through the nature of its kind, into which through the course of nature it has been influxed and captured by that nature into substantial form, without which no thing in the world can live. And to make this understood, we fortify ourselves to open the human intellect, so that we may know that the said virtue is influxed into the radical moistures, which are imbued in the essential parts of composite Mercu-

--- Page 214 (Theorica Cap. LIII cont.) ---
RAYMVNDI LVLLI
rial things, and of pure and impure elements, because from the said moisture all the essential parts of all Mercuries have been made, and of all things that are in the world. And through this, all things that have growth and nourishment grow and are nourished. Son, we speak to you generally about every thing, and if you understand what will be revealed to you, keep it secret. For such is this virtue that without it you cannot bind, unbind, form, transmute, wash, dry, heat, moisten, nor place the soul into the body; through it, however, the elements are bound. And unless, son, through the theorica you understand the natures, at no time through the practica will you know how to correct your work, nor understand their measures, but rather you will fall into error, deviating through a contrary point, which lies in chance occurrence.

On the nature of the fetid menstrual, in which is the fire against nature. And on the thing that corrupts all nature.

--- Page 215 (page 99, Theorica Cap. LIIII) ---
THEORICA.

CAP. LIIII.

After this, son, we wish to reveal to you the nature of the fetid menstrual, in which is the fire against nature, which transmutes our entire stone into a certain dragon of a thick, dripping sort, which thickens and impregnates its own mother. And we say that its nature in its first birth is in a cold and dry complexion in the fourth degree, and its substance is astringent. But through its digestions and decoctions it is changed from nature to nature, and from flavor to flavor. For since matter is the subject of transmutation, immediately it receives contrariety in itself by its own proper name. And if it has a mover, and is moved according to this into contrariety, it has and possesses the contrariety of transmutation according to the contrary proximate to that being from which the contrariety is moved. No thing corrupts another unless it overcomes the other in a contrary quality, just as heat and coldness do. And it should not be of the same kind, just as tolerable heat and intolerable heat that destroys the natural sense are not the same. And therefore a small flame is quickly devastated by a greater one, by reason of its excelling great-

--- Page 216 (Theorica Cap. LIIII cont.) ---
RAYMVNDI LVLLI
ness, which it has against that small flame. For the moisture that would nourish its small flame for a long time is burned and consumed in a short time by a great flame, and then the small one is extinguished. And thus let it be revealed to you that all things of nature were made in one moment and corrupted in one moment. And afterwards in another moment they are established and exist, since all things have contrariety, and in that contrariety some things are excellent, and all are of one kind, and some others are entirely contrary, and yet in all things they are overcome by virtues. And therefore they operate continuously upon those things and continuously they suffer, wherefore you can understand the corruption of our stone through the abbreviation of our life, and its length, and its duration, according to the complexion of the containing air. In this magisterium, son, you need two airs: one is of tempered complexion, and through this our stone is generated, because in it is the vivifying virtue, which is like the instrument of the soul to the body. The other air is of untempered complexion, as the sharp and bitter terrestrial astringency certifies to you. Therefore let it be revealed to you,

--- Page 217 (page 100, Theorica Cap. LIII cont.) ---
THEORICA.

salt, which is cold in nature in the fourth degree and hot in the first. Coldness is the cause of tartness. Heat, however, is the cause of bitterness and sharpness, through which sharpness it stinks and stings greatly, and it has an opening and cutting virtue, as is apparent through the power of its action. For that which stings and penetrates causes one thing to be separated from another, and it breaks down and corrodes. And therefore you can know that sharp things among other flavors that are caused by heat are more hot than others, because they can produce no impression such as that which proceeds from excellent heat. And therefore such a thing, so complexioned by its heat, can always dissolve and resolve the moist, and convert it into vapor, and cause it to putrefy by the virtue of natural heat or by its impression, which indeed comes from our quicksilver. Upon Mercury descends from the nature of stones that which is close to sharpness, because it gnaws and stings like a sharp tartness, but not so deeply. And therefore it is considered to be of gross substance, as is otherwise shown by experience. For when the hot sharp, incorporated with the bitter,

--- Page 218 (page 100 verso, Theorica Cap. LIII cont.) ---
RAYMVNDI LVLLI

in the form of moist water has entered into the deeper parts of the body, it dissolves, and when the body is continuous in its moist parts, it separates the gross parts of the salt, which cannot enter the body with the hot sharp. And therefore, since it has been sequestered from its place, it could not have moisture in which it might continue, but begins to congeal in the depths of its vessel, and the metal remains dissolved and impregnated by the hot sharp in the form of water, whence our quicksilver comes forth. The reason why salt is added is to attract the fire against nature, which corrupts our stone. Another reason is that it is cleansing and purifying of unctuosity. You must, however, preserve the measure, because it is not necessary that the bitter exceed the hot sharp, lest it impede the putrefaction of our stone. For no animal can be generated through the unfitness and inability of its putrefaction. The nature of the animal that is in our menstruum is such that it mortifies and obscures every thing, and overshadows and blackens all our airs, and corrupts every animated thing, and desiccates green things and natural moist things that have been generated or are in the process of genera-

--- Page 219 (page 101, Theorica Cap. LIII cont.) ---
THEORICA.

tion. It makes sweet things acrid, and congeals dissolved things, and makes cooked and finished things raw again, and makes the hot cold, the dry wet, the hard soft, the heavy light, and the gross it makes delicate and subtle. And all these things it does from its own first nature through certain operations. Likewise it has other and better powers, because it vivifies everything dead, whitens everything black, generates everything animate, moistens dry things, preserves these from combustion, sweetens and makes mild the harsh things, softens moist and cold things, mollifies dry, bitter, and hot things, and brings them to a temperate complexion, and leads what has been made raw again to greater decoction. Of cold it makes hot, of dry it makes temperate moist, of light it makes heavy with moderation, of subtle it makes gross. That which participates with the simple, it condenses what is simple. It rarefies the thick, and thus it has contrary operations, which are the operations of nature. For when what is congealed is congealed, when what is dissolved is dissolved, it dissolves the congealed. It is no wonder, therefore, son, if the virtue of such a menstruum is placed in your heart and diligently sought after, since it is the generatrix and governess of all nature. And this is the vile thing that by itself is turned

--- Page 220 (page 101 verso, Theorica Cap. LIII cont.) ---
RAYMVNDI LVLLI

into a most noble, admirable, and most precious thing, which crude thing we would not have known through our books, had we not seen its effect. Note that this virtue receives animation and purpose from its element. And on this see the chapter in our Practica, when we shall speak of body and spirit, at the same time giving an example of human generation. And of this we shall speak further in chapter 27 and elsewhere in chapters 48 and 49, before "But the secret spirit." For there is understood the property of the hardening of the menstruum, which participates between two operations, namely between hardening and softening. And if you understand this well, you will know that the mineral virtue, whether it is carried and transported in earth or in water, and consider well its power, because it receives no determination except from its own proper matter, and remember that it can be terrestrial or celestial through the introduced virtue, which must not be suffocated in anything by an external agent. Therefore sublimate the Mercury in which this virtue resides, because sublimated by its virtue it will congeal Mercury into a high medicine. But this will not happen unless it is sublimated. Whence Avicenna says that first our Mercury must be sublimated, and the first thing that must be done in the work is that you sublimate Mercury.

--- Page 221 (page 102, Theorica Cap. LV) ---
THEORICA.

How the Artist and operator must habituate himself to finding the said menstruum, and to understanding its nature.

CAP. LV.

After seeking the menstruum, son, without which nothing can be done naturally, it is necessary to return to the material principles, to those, namely, in which nature begins to work immediately, which material principles are very adherent in the course of nature to their own proper substances, just as are the aforesaid radical moistures, which through their natural heat by the excitation of the fire of the sun are terminated and finished in the substance of the metal. This could never have been possible unless it participated in the nature of the metal. Wherefore we say that this radical moisture is nothing other than quicksilver, a substance impregnated with the heat of sulfur. And this heat of sulfur is the cause of the congelation of the radical Mercurial moisture and of its termination into a perfect metal.

--- Page 222 (page 102 verso, Theorica Cap. LV cont.) ---
RAYMVNDI LVLLI

Therefore it is clear that this metallic moisture, already terminated in the form of a metal, actually has the power and nature of the menstruum, and therefore it has been called the first extremity of our stone. It is apparent, then, since our stone comes from its own nature, that it is dissolved and resolved into that without prejudice to its nature and its growth, since the first regimen and governance of our stone is its resolution and termination into quicksilver. And therefore it is declared to you in what form our stone is drawn from both bodies and from quicksilver. For the nature of the fetid menstruum was, and still is, containing them with the nature of quicksilver, since through the heat of that it has been congealed into the nature of metal. Because the heat of that has been multiplied into the substance of quicksilver, it is everywhere in the moist substance of quicksilver mixed vaporously, by virtue of the first mixture or homogeneous continuation in the form of a liquor or clear water. And by the likeness of a natural principle we say that you should cause the stone to be dissolved until it has been made into water, which common quicksilver cannot do on account of a defect of heat and of humors, in which it is necessary that the stone be continued in the form of a li-

--- Page 223 (page 103, Theorica Cap. LV cont.) ---
THEORICA.

quor, since there is no passage from one extreme to the other except through a medium. Therefore it appears, if you have understood us well, that the stone without the menstruum cannot be dissolved into the moist, which is the fetid spirit from which the living water has been extracted, which appears dry to us. For quicksilver commonly understood, existing in its pure nature, cannot change any bodies unless it is in the nature into which it is changed, for it turns a body when it is changed. If it is clear water, it changes into clear water. And unless it is changed, it cannot change, except into its own nature. And this change happens for the reasons of the preceding chapter. And this is because it is impregnated with such a virtue, which always receives determination in matter, whether the matter be earth or water. And therefore many have been deceived who, contrary to reason, believe that quicksilver commonly understood could, through a nature that is not in it, transmute a body into living water, but all of that is not worth one rotten fig. Therefore understand as we have said before, for so understood, namely commonly, it does not transmute the bodies of the sun and moon into humorous water, except according to its own nature with a slight alteration,

--- Page 224 (page 103 verso, Theorica Cap. LV cont.) ---
RAYMVNDI LVLLI

which we properly call combustion and loss of the real elements, and confusion of the spirit of the soul and the body. The reason is that it does not have the thing that participates between body and spirit, namely the aforesaid quicksilver and the perfect metal. And this thing is that medium which has the power of performing contrary operations, namely of unbinding the elements and of binding them, and of preserving them from all combustion and combustibility against the war of fire. And yet it is necessary that the exciting fire be moderated for it, since just as lead aids silver when it is assayed and guards it from combustion against the assault of fire, and the lead is there placed as a medium between the silver and the fire, so that medium defends the elements from combustion and aids them, and joins, by the property of its own nature, body with spirit and spirit with body. And therefore we call it a medium between body and spirit. But there is yet another medium, when body is conjoined with spirit, and conversely, and that is most proximate to the metallic genus. Whence through mediums a passage is made to the perfect extremes, through which it is clear that no passage is made from one ex-

--- Page 225 (page 104, Theorica Cap. LV cont.) ---
THEORICA.

contrary into another except through a middle disposition, as will be declared to you through the figure of the practical tree. Son, that disposition is the middle which participates in the softness of quicksilver in its first being, and in the hardness of metal on the other side. Since from its own substance its nature has placed from potency to act, and in fact does place it, therefore let it be revealed to you that the immediate extremity in the work of nature and the first being is quicksilver, yet not such as we know how to make through art. And therefore we take that which is menstrual in the real, and on the other side are all the metals, because in them is found the end of the circularity of the perfect operation of nature. This middle disposition of the two extremities is quicksilver extracted from the said first extremity. Which indeed we call the menstrual in the work of art, in the form of clear water made by the ingenuity of nature, which is congealed in sulfur. Wherefore it has been called dry water, not the water of clouds or phlegmatic water, but choleric water, hotter than fire. But the true principles radically born, from which nature works that which was said above, are truly sulfur and quicksilver. Whence come forth two extremities, which are very

--- Page 226 (Theorica Cap. LV cont. / Cap. LVI) ---
RAYMVNDI LVLLI

far removed together with their own middle, which it is necessary that you know, if you wish to begin and commence with respect to nature and science, how they are named in common speech.

On the confused natural principles. And how they are generated through the virtue of successive operation in the higher natures.

CAP. LVI.

The confused natural principles in the work of nature are fetid spirits and living water, which is called dry water, and then it is called the real middle. The fetid spirits are the second middles, which participate between body and spirit. And in another way they are named sulfur and fire. And they are like masculine sperm, which is the active middle between the menstrual and the effective fire. And the living water is quicksilver congealed into sulfur, just as the menstrual, which is congealed into the substance of the embryo. From this matter, successively in its own mineral vessel, there is generated and created, namely in the bowels of its own earth,

--- Page 227 (page 105, Theorica Cap. LVI cont.) ---
THEORICA.

or mineral veins, a certain smoky substance, very subtle, which joins together vaporously into one, and there it is cooked and greatly purified. Afterwards upon it comes a mineral sulfurous virtue in the form of a dry vapor, which penetrates it and congeals it with such a bond of love that they will never be separated. Provided, however, that the virtue of the male overcomes the virtue of the female through the coequality of the fifth thing, then it remains in one fixed thing, by the temperament of the natural decoction that the matter had in its mineral form, so that the Mercurial moisture cannot be separated from the sulfurous dryness, nor the sulfurous dryness from the Mercurial moisture at any time, since they are united through action and passion to that material middle in which the form of metal is plainly constituted — not that it is quicksilver in its entire nature, since part of its nature has already been altered through the coercion or constriction which its subtle parts received by way of the binding of the sulfurous vapor, nor through this is it sulfur in its entire nature, since if it were sulfur, it would burn the substance of quicksilver through the eminence of its dominating action. And therefore, since the virtue of sulfur does not dominate over

--- Page 228 (page 105 verso, Theorica Cap. LVI cont. / Cap. LVII) ---
RAYMVNDI LVLLI

the virtue of quicksilver, nor can it consume its specific metallic clarity, but only congeals it and fixes it up to the degree of the power of its virtue under the conservation of the species of metal, which quicksilver holds materially according to its nature, and such a proportion of sulfur and quicksilver specially constitutes the form and species of such a metal, from which the vapor of sulfur and quicksilver will be the body of gold or silver or another metal. And all of that comes from the diverse purification of the said vapor by digestion or decoction, which it received in its own mine. And therefore the good Artist takes metals as middles in the work of the magisterium, and especially the sun and the moon. This is because these two came into a temperate and greatly purified coequality of the substance of sulfur and quicksilver, purely cooked and well digested by the ingenuity of nature, to which proportion the Artist would vex himself in vain if he wished to begin from natural principles or real middles for the purpose of making.

On the matter of proportioned sulfur, and on its equipollence to the operation of nature.

CAP. LVII.

--- Page 229 (page 106, Theorica Cap. LVII) ---
THEORICA.

Son, this is the metallic matter in which our proportioned sulfur is, which sagacious nature has prepared for us as we have said, by means of which we accomplish our entire secret magisterium. Therefore do not be ignorant that what has been called the second extremity in the work of nature, we call the middle in our philosophical work. For it is necessary that through artifice there be supplied that which nature left behind, for she does not have the power to pass beyond the generation of metal by her motion, because she has completed her circulation. After this it ought to shine forth and come to mind manifestly for you, the cause and the perfecting thing, manifested by all experiments, that the moisture of metals in their calcinations cannot be consumed nor separated from them, by reason of the homogeneity of the union of elements, which are gradually bound and conjoined together. But the moisture of stones is separated, as is apparent in their calcination, solely by fire without medicinal application. And this is because the moisture in the gross terrestrial substance of stones is not bound by the dry in the smallest parts of one and the other, but in the gross and larger parts of the moist and the dry. The contrary

--- Page 230 (page 106 verso, Theorica Cap. LVII cont.) ---
RAYMVNDI LVLLI

is the case in the matter of glass, through which matter the art of glassmaking is subalternated to this art. Wherefore the philosopher says that glass should be an exemplar for you in our magisterium. And this is the reason why the moistures of stones and middle minerals are inks, vitriols, azoquea, marcasite, alums, salts, and borax, which do not have their moisture fixed by nature through the material dry, but it is volatile and fleeing from fire; however, the moisture of metals is fixed and permanent in fire. Therefore with such water we fix the little birds flying in the air by the virtue of our stone, which was created from their own substance. It appears therefore that our quicksilver is the material substance and the radical moisture of all liquefiable bodies. Therefore make the bodies into the water of liquefaction, by making and melting them into quicksilver. For since it is the principal element of the said liquefiable bodies, when they are liquefied they are resolved into it, and sulfur is the stone. And therefore it has been called the middle in the work of nature, but quicksilver and metals have been called the extremities, because they are the matter of the beginning and the matter of the end. And therefore it can appear to you that, although we are bound to assimilate our magisterium

--- Page 231 (page 107, Theorica Cap. LVII cont. / Cap. LVIII) ---
THEORICA.

as much as possible to the operation of nature, we cannot however properly follow her nature except through the mode of operation, and not by taking crude matter as she does in her primordial beginning, although without that matter we cannot begin, since it is a necessary thing, and since it is necessary that the cooked metal, which is the sulfur and quicksilver in it, must be re-crudified, following the likeness of the work of nature in our magisterium with the nearest materials, as it is fitting for every wise Artist to know.

How art corrects and supplies the strengths which it finds in the matter of nature, through the mutation of that matter into the action of pure form.

CAP. LVIII.

Son, let the discourse and teaching that we shall give not weary you nor displease you, because through known art we correct and supply the strengths and virtues of nature. In her assistance, we through art begin

--- Page 232 (page 107 verso, Theorica Cap. LVIII cont.) ---
RAYMVNDI LVLLI

to work where nature leaves off the work. Since it has been seen that metals cannot tinge except insofar as their nature is extended through the power of their first circulation or sulfurous nature. And therefore the work is made certain, in the generation of our stone, so that their tincture may increase more than it was in its nature or operation. If you know, son, our lavatory, you will be able to wash the nature of quicksilver in such a way, to which nature at no time was able to arrive, so that that nature of quicksilver may be a complete elixir. But since in nature and in its work, quicksilver and metals are extremes and extremities, and they do not have the power of remaining and tinging together except through the virtue of the middle disposition, which is between the softness of quicksilver and the hardness of metal, because through this middle disposition there consists a natural obedience, which is the cause of conjunction between body and spirit, as is the case in every generated and generable thing. Note that this virtue is twofold: operative virtue and material virtue, and each of these has its middle disposition — namely, the operative, which depends upon the learned Artist, and the other, the material, which depends principally upon the instrument, although

--- Page 233 (page 108, Theorica Cap. LVIII cont.) ---
THEORICA.

receives its determination and end from the whole composite already subtilized, and this in three ways before it is perfected. Likewise in nature there are many media, of which two are purer and less viscous than others, such as azoquea, green vitriols with a stony nature, which is salt and the nature of stones. Son, by the aid of the nature of this vile matter our stone is procreated, which we have sought so greatly, because it is the medium in the work of nature between spirit and body, and properly the material subject. And therefore nature, by means of it, converts quicksilver into perfect metal, which metal in our magisterium, by reason of its depuration, is called the real medium between body and spirit. It is called body when it is burnt earth. It is called spirit when it has been drawn from the body in the form of clear water, that is, quicksilver. You should understand that in the work of nature there are two proper media elected above all others, namely the aforesaid azoc, and the salt of nature, which converts quicksilver into a liquor of fire, from which sulfur is created, which congeals quicksilver. Son, in like manner we have in our magisterium two things, which nature has given us and depurated from its confused media, and they are gold and quicksilver, which by reason of their pure nature participate in greater perfection. Wherefore the extremities of our stone

--- Page 234 (Theorica Cap. LVIII-LIX) ---
RAYMVNDI LVLLI

in its creation is the quicksilver extracted from bodies in its first being. And in the second it is the completed elixir, and the medium is the stone, when it has been created from the substance of the said quicksilver. And immediately without any other procedure it becomes the completed elixir, which is the ultimate extreme of the stone, and the complement of our magisterium. Son, we shall show you through certain degrees of extremities and media, down to the first roots of nature, so that you may understand the media through which nature passes from degree to degree, going to the extremes, according to perfection, and more and less. For in this lies the knowledge of the entire work of nature and of all its secrets, and consequently of all other science.

On the nature of the extremities of our quicksilver.

CAP. LIX.

You have therefore the extremes of our stone. Now we shall speak of the extremities of our quicksilver. And we say that its extremes on the first side, through its entire substance, are the waters of the green lion conjoined with metal, and on the second side, the stone that has been created, and their medium is the sun and the moon,

--- Page 235 (page 109, Theorica Cap. LIX cont.) ---
THEORICA.

and the moon, from which comes forth our quicksilver, which is a liquefied putrefied body, from which the stone is created when it has been purged from its original nature. Son, the azoquean lion, which is called vitriol, has been made by nature from the proper substance of common quicksilver, which is the natural root from which metal is created in its proper mine. Wherefore in our magisterium we need naturally three principal roots, just as the work of nature does, namely, the two extremities and the medium. Hence it is necessary for you to know these things with great patience, and to perform the work according to what sagacious and wise nature wishes to teach you, with such intention that you must have and must create our first quicksilver, so that you may have the suitable matter from which the stone can be created, because the media of our quicksilver are two proper metals, and their nature has the power to convert quicksilver into a stone of virtue, since nature did not have the power to compose the said metals into another digestion, so that through another circulation it might have made the completed elixir, and this by reason of the thickness, hardness, and compactness of the Mercurial moisture termi-

--- Page 236 (Theorica Cap. LIX cont.) ---
RAYMVNDI LVLLI

nated, and inspissated through the vapor of its sulfur and natural heat in the form of metal, just as it created the sun and the moon from the subtle substance of their own proper media by virtue of its first circular work. Therefore in this case we supply the impotencies of nature, namely by making hard bodies soft, and gross ones fine, with the aid of nature, which shares its substance with us. And we liquefy and melt through putrefaction, until they have returned to the nature of quicksilver through the subtilization of the gross into the simple, and the reduction of the cooked into the raw, and the resolution and dissolution of the dry into the moist. And all these things it is necessary to do completely, so that the simple matter may better and more obey the digestive nature. For it is now manifestly established to every wise person, as was shown in the natural principles at the beginning, that a thing of subtle substance similar to its whole, like a simple homogeneous body, is more quickly changeable, purified, and generated by virtue of its nature, than another thing of hard, gross, and thick substance. And therefore we dissolve gold and silver in the radical things of their own kind, namely in a liquor, and through the liquor of our quicksilvers, of which the first is the fetid menstruum, which enters into the first matter of the two aforesaid luminaries

--- Page 237 (page 110, Theorica Cap. LIX cont.) ---
THEORICA.

as you can understand through the natural principles in its proper mine. And therefore we tell you that into it they are immediately resolved and dissolved, and in that from which they were made they are resolved with respect to it, and are converted into the fetid menstruum. Nor would it have action and passion in any other way, except through the concord and agreement that exists from one to the other. Likewise remember that with the menstruum you should place nothing except a thing that was born from it in the beginning of your commixtion. For if you place an extraneous thing, it will immediately be corrupted by the extraneous nature, nor will you ever have that which you wish to have. Gold and silver and Mercury are dissolved in our menstruum, because they participate with them in the proximity and vicinity of the first nature. And from them you shall extract the white fume, which is our sulfur, and the green lion, which is our unguent, and the fetid water, which is our second quicksilver. Afterwards it is necessary that the green lion be dissolved in the fetid water by a solemn dissolution, before you can have the said white fume, which is our sulfur, which sulfur also dissolves bodies, congeal-

--- Page 238 (Theorica Cap. LIX-LX) ---
RAYMVNDI LVLLI

ing spirits in the form of a dry water, which we call the stone, and the supreme medium of our entire work, because it is the connection and aggregation of all natures, namely of body and spirit. Son, this water is called the water of fire, because it burns and incinerates gold and silver better than elemental fire can do. And because it contains within itself a natural heat, knowing and breaking, which without force dissolves what common fire cannot do. Wherefore we command you that from the hottest things that you can receive in nature, you make the magisterium, and you shall have a hot water that dissolves all things.

On the nature of strong waters, and on their approbation.

CAP. LX.

Many say and affirm that strong waters are the corruptions of our stone, and they do not lie. Because he who does not understand its corruption will find it difficult to understand its generation, since it cannot happen without the corruption and transmu-

--- Page 239 (page 111, Theorica Cap. LX cont.) ---
THEORICA.

tation of individuals, which are real material beings of our stone in their proper nature, which indeed must be corrupted, and ought to be corrupted through the said menstruum, with pious love without their total destruction, because a pious mother never wishes to kill the son whom she has nourished. Son, everything that you place that is not of its kind will refuse its dissolution. But this putrefies, because it is a thing common to receiving the entire nature with which it is appropriated. See therefore that you do not mix with our water any other thing, unless it be of its own proper nature. For the thing that best and most quickly putrefies a perfect body is our water, by reason of its bitter acidity, and crude nature, and this is what burns, inflames, and corrupts everything that is combustible and corruptible through the annihilation of its own proper burning and combustive nature. And if an oleaginous, impure, and unctuous moisture is in its composite, that which must be corrupted by its property and operation reduces all things to nothing. And that which is pure and clean, and better dissolved, remains whole, and defends it from fire, because it itself is the pure nature, and the proper essential matter of our stone,

--- Page 240 (Theorica Cap. LX cont.) ---
RAYMVNDI LVLLI

by virtue of the aforesaid dissolved water, which can neither be burned nor set aflame, nor consumed, but its moisture increases when it is in the fire. And that this water has the power of burning and consuming everything that is extraneous to it, we have proven and seen by certain experience. We dissolved common buttery sulfur in this water by artifice. And afterwards we sublimated the moisture of that which was more pure in nature through distillation. Then underneath we placed vivified quicksilver, and immediately it was congealed in the form of a congealed, black, very soft plate: because the dry vaporous sulfureity did not overcome the softness of the quicksilver. Then we wished to test its lucidity, and therefore we mixed the water of the said sulfur and the water of arsenic. After this we congealed the silver and made it melt, and we found it black and earthy, and we purged it through cupellation, and it was good as before, but more than half had been destroyed: on account of which it is manifest that the entire moisture drawn from the sulfur was combustible and still strong, when the water did not have the power to overcome the impurity and malice of the sulfur. And after this again we mixed

--- Page 241 (page 112, Theorica Cap. LX-LXI) ---
THEORICA.

we made new water with the water of sulfur, and we distilled the water, and we found that the entire moist substance of the sulfur was burned and consumed, because it operated in no way against Mercury. And therefore let it be revealed to you that the virtue of our water has the power of gathering together things that are purely homogeneous, and of separating and dividing heterogeneous things, through the burning and consumption of those things which are not of the same kind.

How the Artist must understand good and bad spirits, and recognize them through the virtues of strong waters, and through the nature of those waters. And on the elemental fire, which elementates the elements.

CAP. LXI.

And in a similar way we tested the nature of common arsenic, which we found to be no less foul-smelling, nor less combustible. And likewise concerning the other spirits understood in a rustic manner, which are all full of the imperfection of whitening. Therefore, as to quicksilver, we know that we found it guarded from all combustion by the said natural experiment. Therefore, son, let it be revealed to you that through our water you can visibly recognize which

--- Page 242 (Theorica Cap. LXI cont.) ---
RAYMVNDI LVLLI

spirit is better in its nature, and nearer to our work, since according to the truth of the matter, quicksilver is purged by it without consumption of its pure substance, as is seen through nature, and as we and others have seen through art, that nature transmutes it into pure metal, and through art into pure medicine, which could not be done unless its pure substance were perpetual against fire, which desires only pure things; and because its substance is pure, it rests in fire without termination of a consuming end. And thus it appears how through water you will be able to recognize a good spirit, and through the spirit the water, which is the cause of its rectification. Son, the elemental fire, which elementates the elements, cannot draw the soul from bodies, but the fetid water draws it well by virtue of the love that participates between them through the medium of their preparations. Because through those preparations their souls and strengths are drawn forth, which are our magisterium, by distilling and redistilling, dissolving and congealing. And thus, son, our water makes the entire body into spirit, and turns dead silver into living substance. Afterwards it mortifies and revivifies it in its own proper nature.

--- Page 243 (page 113, Theorica Cap. LXI cont.) ---
THEORICA.

And afterwards, when it is congealed, it is called our magnesia, which magnesia is nothing other than water composed and congealed, which resists fire, nor after this does it fear fire, because it is itself fire, and made of the nature of fire. It is preserved in fire, and its regimen and nourishment are in fire. How therefore could a father kill or slaughter his own son whom he begot, or a loving mother the infant whom she nourished? Truly, even though both wished to kill him, yet at no time can they destroy him; indeed they can be extinguished by the heat of labor, because their son is clothed in such heat that fire can no longer corrupt him, since he is fortified with greater heat than the element of fire. And therefore we have magnified for you our water after the congelation of its ferment. And if you understand our waters, you will also understand the living silvers, which come successively, and always succeed it with greater power of fermenting than the preceding one. And so it is with the last, which has greater power than all the preceding ones. Therefore, son, if you understand our living silvers and their strengths, you will understand the power of our ferments, and from this much philosophy, which

--- Page 244 (Theorica Cap. LXI-LXII) ---
RAYMVNDI LVLLI

can show everything through visible experience, ordered well and cautiously with patience, and anticipates the delay of time, for obtaining such a medicine, which is not always found. He who has this has an eternal and incomparable treasure above all others.

On the nature of ferments, and on their waters, and on our living silvers, and on their earths. On the magisterium, and on the knowledge of strong water, and on the things that must be joined to them through the concordance of contrariety.

CAP. LXII.

After this, son, we intend in summary to speak of the nature of ferments, and of their waters, and of our living silvers, which are all one, since there is but one single nature, and of our earths. And we say that this whole is nothing but one thing, which varies in the number of three through its operations, and varying through one decoction it is one thing of one simple power, and after this, gradually passing on to information through another digestion, it will be another thing, which we call living silver, earth,

--- Page 245 (page 114, Theorica Cap. LXII cont.) ---
THEORICA.

water, and ferment, gum, and our second falsature, bitter and harsh, which dissolves all bodies by composite virtue and by the property acquired through the second digestion. And after, through another digestion, it has greater power. And thus you can understand that in our magisterium there are three proper earths, three waters, and three proper ferments, three proper gums, three proper falsatures, and three proper living silvers that congeal, as you can understand through the operation of the triangular figure, through which are constituted the three principal reductions, which are the complement of our entire magisterium, as we shall declare in our practice. Therefore, son, guard the living silver, and let it be congealed in the mines of its earth. For that is our living silver, our gum, our earth, our falsature, bitter, which is sweetened by virtue of the third reduction. And it is our ferment, our elixir, and our medicine for all infirmities. This is our water, not the common mercurial water of phlegmatic things, but it is that which we have spoken of, which is of a much hotter nature than fire. Wherefore Hippocrates, Avicenna, Averroes, Galen, and other philosophers called the urine of choleric young men, namely, strong, sharp water,

--- Page 246 (Theorica Cap. LXII cont.) ---
RAYMVNDI LVLLI

sharp water. And from those things which we have spoken of above, drawn, converted, and congealed into the water of ferment as we have said. And know, son, that the choleric thing is nothing but fire, and this, son, is the water of our first and last exposition. Dissipate therefore the thing, namely, the corporeal thing, captured through the binding of nature, or the union, first through the thing, namely the water, namely the corporeal thing dissolved and carried in an aqueous medium, sufficiently apt. On this, see elsewhere in chapter five of the third book of Albert's On Minerals, on the cause of the essence of metals, upon that gloss which touches upon solution. And this water is the thing spoken of above. Gently extract the mass thus made and grind it. Gently extracted, namely, the corporeal thing well dissolved and carried in an aqueous medium, thus grind the mass thus made, namely by repeating the work of solution, grinding, imbibition, and roasting upon the mass which remained undissolved, until it is dissolved up to the sign known to you, namely, through the fire of water or the aqueous fire. And also through the excitation of sufficient fire, since nature in its acts needs information according to its work. This not hastily, but in the order of time, without which. The fury of urine disposes the members of ruin, that is, the fire of water disposes

--- Page 247 (page 115, Theorica Cap. LXII cont.) ---
THEORICA.

the members of ruin, that is, the individual parts of bodies in which the species are dissolved. Because there is nothing in the whole world so good for tincture except the water that we hold in memory. Know, son, that through art and nature, quicksilver is congealed through sharp water. Therefore understand philosophically, because if it were not sharp, it would not penetrate, which is the first action in dissolution; after which, in dissolution it is turned into the blood of tumors through the change of its nature into another. Son, there are two things that must adhere through the concordance of contrariety: one pure, the other impure. The impure departs, since it is the enemy of fire by reason of its corruption. The other remains in fire, by reason of its purity transmuted into blood, and this is our living silver, and our entire secret, clothed in a threefold garment, namely, black, white, and red. And this alone we require for the intention of our magisterium, since it is the living silver containing everything that we need for the fifth substance. In Mercury is whatever the wise seek, for under its shadow lies hidden the fifth substance: for its substance is pure and incombustible. And everything that

--- Page 248 (Theorica Cap. LXII cont.) ---
RAYMVNDI LVLLI

is nothing but gold and silver, liquefied and fused inside and outside, namely in a corruptive thing, which is subordinated to the fire of the stone, since it is against nature; which bodies are liquefied and fused inside and outside by the virtue of menstrual fire, and afterwards purified and separated from it, and from all its original stain and blemish. For gold, which is incombustible, remains fused and liquefied, and is gold-making in the said Mercury, because the substance of sulfur is universally dispersed throughout its entire substance. And Mercury is in the seminal thing, entirely fermented on account of the sweetness of gold, which nourishes the seed, moves and multiplies it, as you will be able to see in the things of nature. Understand therefore, son, our Mercury and its property, and the manner of its copulation, and in what place it must be administered, and how well the living silver extracted from silver joins itself to gold. Do not therefore seek to operate except with Mercury and the moon for silver, and Mercury and the sun for gold. For gold is neither resolvable nor reducible except with its own proper nature. Wherefore you must well discern what are the materials that are so named, and in what figure and matter they exist.

THEORICA.

On the preparation of the stone through its division into the four elements after its putrefaction.

CAP. LXIII.

The intention of nature, son, in performing our magisterium is that the stone be divided into the four elements, to the end that each one by itself may be better prepared according to the requirement of its own nature. And this is the true purification preceding the operation of perfect preparation, and it is properly called the separation of the elements and their washing. For this operation divides all impure parts from the pure parts, so that through those pure, light, and clean parts our perfect preparation may be made successively through natural conjunction, which is accomplished by the reduction of the moist into the dry, prepared immediately and directly after the benefit of the separation of the elements. In no other way would the moist have matrimony with the dry, nor the body with the spirit, unless they were first optimally washed and cleansed of their infections through distillations, calcinations, dissolutions, and congelations. Son, after the separation of the elements, the body remains burned by the

--- Page 250 (Theorica Cap. LXIII cont.) ---
RAYMVNDI LVLLI
corruption of the burning and ardent sulfurousness, which comes in the stain and original blemish of our menstrual, which is the corruption of our stone. And therefore the earth of the body is calcined after the extraction of the air, to the end that it may be well purged and cleansed of its false and combustible sulfurousnesses. It appears, therefore, that if such combustible matter had not been introduced through the corruption of the stone at its beginning, it would never be necessary to purge it. And since this is so, it is necessary that after the corruption of the stone, the composite be cleansed of all its impurities after its separation into the four elements through distillation and calcination, before Mercury is conjoined with the body to begin the real preparation, which is the creation of our stone, namely the preparation that nature performs through the dissolution of the body and the congelation of the spirit, to which nature the simple artificer must first minister the matter in the manner that nature requires in its operations. And therefore it is necessary that the stone be divided into the four elements, and this division is a solution that is made to the end that the substance of the stone may be made most clean, and that the whole

--- Page 251 (page 117, Theorica Cap. LXIII cont.) ---
THEORICA.
of it be nothing but pure Mercury, which carries in its property the nature of fixing and fusing. When, therefore, you wish the body to be dissolved after its calcination, dissolve it with the quicksilver that came forth from it, well purged and cleansed through sevenfold distillation, because then the body is greatly disposed to dissolution, and the quicksilver is very efficacious in penetrating it and in entering through the sponginess of its body, in which the spirit is congealed by the benefit of the dissolution of the fixed nature of the body and of its mixing with quicksilver. And son, if you understand us, this is our solemn dissolution, which is accomplished through the congelation of the spirit, and not without it. Likewise, understand us that the separation of the elements cannot be done except after their putrefaction, through which the spirit is mixed with the body and with its elements, and that putrefaction makes them volatile, as appears in their separations. Therefore the elements are digested and putrefied in their dregs, so that what is well digested will serve to satisfy the will of nature, so that it may be well separated and afterwards buried. And cause the separated things to be buried, so that they may again be better separated, and become quicksilver

--- Page 252 (Theorica Cap. LXIII-LXIIII) ---
RAYMVNDI LVLLI
in the manner of clear water, unless you overcome the stone, the fifth substance, through the four elements. Know that the moist elements cannot naturally be joined with the dry body unless each of them partakes of the nature of the fifth essence. For it is the commingling of the four elements and their reduction into one pure and clean substance, and that the said elements be optimally purged and cleansed of their corruption. We tell you that the combustive and corruptible sulfurousnesses are separated and utterly annihilated by the benefit of calcination, and by fire and from fire the earth of the body is ground fine and greatly subtilized through such digestion, and the waters are purified of their impurities through distillation, and are subtilized, and are turned into the nature of air through the lightening of their levity. After this you can make the conjunction of the male and the female, to the end that they may through art generate the son of fire, who is so greatly beloved by all the philosophers. If you have well understood us, herein lies the whole of philosophy.

On the effect of the entire art in universal summary.

--- Page 253 (page 118, Theorica Cap. LXIIII) ---
THEORICA.
And that the effect of the entire magisterium is nothing other than the multiplication of substantial virtues into fifth essences.

CAP. LXIIII.

We lead and bring back to memory that all of what we determine in our books is that the effect of the entire magisterium is nothing other, in total summary, than the multiplication of the adhering tincture in the nature of all genera of metals. And the metallic tincture in universal summary is nothing but sulfur, from which multiplication is made through its own proper natural heat in the substance of quicksilver, digesting it and concurring into the substance of sulfur through a certain mode of digestion. Therefore the multiplication of the tincture is nothing other than through the augmentation and multiplication of natural heat, which is sulfur neither burning nor ardent, in the matter of quicksilver. Since it is certain that quicksilver is the matter of metals and their substance, in which their clarity is fixed, because metals need nothing other than a substantial tincture, in which tincture sulfur is fixed and held. And it is necessary that it be of those metals,

--- Page 254 (Theorica Cap. LXIIII cont.) ---
RAYMVNDI LVLLI
preserved and guarded from all combustion, and effective of all fusion, and lovable and pleasing to all metals. And therefore it is necessary that the tincture of sulfur be infused into such a substance, and that it be placed therein, if you wish it to be the tincture of metals, and that the said substance be such that it can be fixed without the consumption of its moisture, without the reversion or regression of it into earth, and without the cremation and combustion of its own substance, and all this is found in quicksilver. Wherefore it must be noted that quicksilver is the cause of perfection for the necessity of metals, because it is sufficient in every degree of fusions with ignition, as gold and silver, iron and copper demonstrate, or without ignition, as is evident in lead and tin. And this is through the good adherence of all its parts in the strength of its noble mixture. Therefore, son, if you could in any way thicken it by fire while preserving its moisture, it would at no time permit itself to be corrupted, nor would it ever recede into fume through a furious flame, on account of the heavy weight that it has in itself and the absence of all combustibility that it possesses. However, this thicken-

--- Page 255 (page 119, Theorica Cap. LXIIII cont.) ---
THEORICA.
ing we cannot accomplish with common quicksilver, but with our philosophical quicksilver, since in its effect it has the substance of thickening, and it is through the work of the second or third degree. If therefore it is manifestly perfected in all its parts, let us not care about any test, since visible experience will have demonstrated it to us. For when it is fixed, so great a tincture of overflowing refreshment comes from it, and in such splendor, that it could scarcely be expressed. It is the medium of joining tinctures, for it is mixed with them through the smallest parts, and it adheres naturally in their deepest interior, because it is of the nature of those metals, and especially of the sun and the moon, which are the tincture of all other imperfect metals. And therefore we leave you in instruction that it is necessary that the tincture of sulfur and fire, which is in the matter of complexional quicksilver, be co-fixed in the proper substance of the said quicksilver, since it is the matter of metals, to the end that the tincture, by means of the matter in which it is bound, may through the commonality of its nature be added and joined to the depths of metals by reason of the quicksilver in which the said tincture is fixed, and may adhere without separation. And

--- Page 256 (Theorica Cap. LXIIII cont.) ---
RAYMVNDI LVLLI
if you believe you can fix sulfur in another matter, it will not be friendly to metals, nor consequently conjunctive, on account of the foreignness of its nature. And if you fix it in a perfect matter close to quicksilver, it will be for the perfection and life of metals, because from quicksilver all things are created, and in it all things are dissolved and resolved, and are converted into sulfur through the multiplication of tincture added to the said dissolved matter and congealed by virtue of a certain retrogradation close to nature, and through the understanding of the thing the truth is made clear. For the tincture of the wise Alchemists is greater than that of nature. Since the magnitude of the tincture has grown in the substance of medicinal quicksilver from pure nature through the art of the magisterium, this magnitude multiplied in many modes and united in tinctures has been constituted and made of many powers, and this through the power of the commingling of matter, which is made by virtue of the said natural heat and its motion in the said matter of metal, which are afterwards placed and constituted in sulfur, which is the substance of quicksilver, in which are united, materially added, many tinctures of sulfur of quicksilver, white and red, by lapidifying virtue essentially infused in the matter of quicksilver through the sulfurous quality.

THEORICA.

Wherefore, son, it is evident to every experienced Alchemist that that superiority and magnitude of the tincture is greater than in every other tincture of a stone created by nature, as we have named more broadly in the questionary treatise, question 22, and in the mixture of ferments in the second part.

What thing is the matter of quicksilver and sulfur.

CAP. LXV.

Son, we have told you that the radical moisture, in which the natural heat is lodged and resides, is very unctuous, and therefore is separated with difficulty, and consequently so is its equality and desiccability, because if it were not so, nature could not provide for its necessities, which are solidity, hardness, permanent fusibility, etc. And therefore nature desires to exist perpetually in its individuals under the preservation of its species: wherefore sagacious nature ordained this Mercurial moisture, very subtle and unctuous, which holds in itself the matter of earth united with the nourishment of natural heat, and through the separation of such perma-

--- Page 258 (page 120, Theorica Cap. LXV cont.) ---

Son, the unctuous moisture is the nearest and most proximate matter of our philosophical quicksilver, and the substance of its unctuosity is the proper essential matter of sulfur. Wherefore you see that one cannot be fixed without the fixation of the other. Since it is so that one does not exist without the other, just as matter does not exist without some form, therefore sulfur is as it were the complexional heat in the substance of quicksilver, and is to you like the virtue of male sperm, which is in the seed of the female, or of another generative virtue, which is in seeds. Therefore understand that the matter of such sulfur is very shining, strongly clean, and brought to purity by the virtue of strong and most sharp washings, in such a way that it has in itself no combustible or burning unctuosity, nor any vaporizable or phlegmatic moisture. And since it is so that each of them has been subtly subtilized, they are carried into the form of the superior elements, and as it appears, mixed together through the heat and disposition of the uterine vessel, suffocating and reflecting the va-

por in itself against the lodging of its conversion, they are bound together by the strong bond of nature into one single pure and mineral thing.

On the knowledge of natural heat. And on the place of its disposition for creating sulfur.

CAP. LXVI.

Son, the moisture of quicksilver cannot be congealed or converted into sulfur except through the virtue of its vessel, which is of its own matter, near to it, entirely porous and open. In the belly of which is that subtle nature which congeals quicksilver into the substance of sulfur, in which a portion of natural heat is contained. And therefore through its pores the quicksilver has entry, and through the dissolution of the said natural heat, it and the moisture are congealed into pure sulfur, and unless the body were open and spongy, quicksilver could not enter in any other way. For in nature, which is the teacher and exemplar of all art and science, it is never found that anything could nourish and grow, or augment any thing, or multiply in species and dissol-

--- Page 260 (page 122, Theorica Cap. LXVI cont.) ---
RAYMVNDI LVLLI

ved matter that is compact and closed, as appears in metals remaining in their species. And therefore we say that he who wishes to change metal into metal must convert it to its first species proximate to the genus. Because those metals, remaining permanent in their own species, cannot be transmuted. For if they are to be transmuted, it is necessary that they have a good transmuter and perfector. And if they have a transmuter, they will have to suffer. But this suffering is twofold, according to the nature of the converter and the thing to be converted. For either the transmuter will be simple quicksilver, yet understanding philosophically, or it will be sulfur carried into a medicine. If it is sulfur carried into a medicine, then the body will have to suffer through the conversion of one into the other, just as food is converted into the substance of an animal under the preservation of its species. If it is quicksilver, then the body, through the dissolution of its nature and its conversion by the virtue of quicksilver into the substance of sulfur, must necessarily suffer even to the final step of its death. And the reason is that such sulfur cannot be found in any place except in the deepest part of its belly. And since that is their pu-

--- Page 261 (page 123, Theorica Cap. LXVI cont.) ---
THEORICA.

re nature, through which it is held in its action, it is necessary that we must extract it through the corruption of its substance, since without it we could not create a perfect medicine. And therefore Aristotle and Plato, and all the other philosophers, left it in their writings that the species of metals cannot be transmuted into the specific form and species of a metal, and under the preservation of its metallic form, unless they are first reduced to their prime nature, namely into sulfur and quicksilver, from which afterwards the medicine is created, and not before, and it does not proceed further. And understand this concerning perfect bodies, because those must revert and convert imperfect metals to perfection, just as the lesser is rectified by the greater. Wherefore, son, since such sulfur cannot be had and multiplied, as nature teaches, until the bodies have been made apt and spongy through the operation of alleviation, certain and most true, of all the elements from which they are composed.
