norant do not understand, and the foolish question whether our magisterium is true. Since they are utterly ignorant of the opening and aperture of pores and closed foramina, and the stimulation of the active and multiplicative virtue. For they believe that what they do not know is impossible, but they lie. Since the thing is not impossible, as it exists in real actuality and is done through art by the work of a certain practice, which if we conceal from you, you will not be able to perform it. For you will be able to see the work of distillation and redistillation, reversion and reiteration, but at no time will you know how to understand the magisterium, even supposing that you have seen the work completed before you, unless you understand the matter by which transmutation must be accomplished. Son, there is but one work, which operates in diverse ways according to the intention of the transmutation, understood from its pure nature by the operator with all its workings. Likewise, unless you have an understanding of the proper mode of nature's work, and you set yourself to distilling, and believe you are doing well with that, you would first consume the entire time of your life, even if you were to live for a thousand years, and always distilled, before you would properly bring the four elements to good result, unless having first changed them and purified them and their

--- Page 263 (page 125, Theorica Cap. LXVI cont.) ---
THEORICA.

virtues you do not understand, and afterwards all their signs, if you have well recognized them in their changes, which cannot be made without proper instruments, which we shall show you when you know how to retain them. Already we have shown you the four regimens, if you have understanding, which are called and named the converters of the four elements, obeying the will of nature as instruments. Wherefore you may wish to turn them after you have completed them, for another time I shall grant them freely. And they are called in our magisterium: first, solution; then, depuration; next, reduction, before fixation. And through ignorance of these, the works of nature are not understood. And the lying rural rustics and the entire ignorant clergy hold and believe the aforesaid, that our said magisterium cannot be likened to nature in any way. But we, son, from certain knowledge, as faithful friends of nature, who know by visible certification and proven experiment, and without deceit, that nature properly works in her own proper places that are dry, hot, and porous. Therefore we in this case and step say that nature wished to demonstrate to us that everything that grows and multiplies must be found and lodged within the concavity and porosity of that

--- Page 264 (page 126, Theorica Cap. LXVI cont.) ---
RAYMVNDI LVLLI

which grows and is multiplied. And therefore Hermes, the father of philosophers and Alchemists, by providing for the works of nature, and following and imitating her in her multiplication, teaching the investigations thereof insofar as is possible in true, costly, and useful transmutations, admonishing us, taught, and we have found by proven and certain experiment, that every dissolved and dissolved metal necessarily needs a porous lodging, which is of its own proper matter, containing the pure nature of the said dissolved and dissolved metal, which is the said quicksilver. In which porous lodging everything that nature multiplies has to grow and be multiplied, just as she does with every other thing that is in the action of multiplication. Therefore let the body be porous, so that in growing it may be dilated, and in it be suffocated, enveloped, cleaned, and purified, and converted into the nature of that which is the converter and multiplier. And when the body is spongy, it receives that which it lost, more by virtue of the motion of its natural heat, which motion is in the attraction of nourishment, and the nourishment moves in the parts of that which is nourished, just as it is moved and drawn by some virtue of the place. Son, the cause of the attraction of nourishment

--- Page 265 (page 124, Theorica Cap. LXVI cont.) ---
THEORICA.

of nourishment insofar as it is in the place to which the nutriment is attracted, there is strength and generative virtue, which we call by another name natural and philosophical heat in our secrets, without which no thing is created, since it is the virtue and power of nature. And therefore porosity requires natural acidity, which is the subject and proper retainer of the said heat, which acidity indeed is manifested in the black heat, when the elements are well mixed and the body is burned and scorched. And if the elements have not been well mixed, you will not see the blackness, which is the manifestation of heat. This is because the blackness shows that the body does not remain deprived nor driven from its active virtue. The active virtue, moreover, is its heat elementally and instrumentally. And this is what causes the blackness when it operates in its moisture. And when it has operated in its moisture, it demonstrates that the elements are strongly mixed when the water has penetrated well into the deeper part of the body, and has resolved and dissolved the color that is bound to the radical moisture in the black heat. Therefore when the radical moisture, which is the subject of fire, is separated from the body in its calcination, then the natural heat is suffocated, because its subject fails it, and the body is manifested in a red heat, which is a work

--- Page 266 (Theorica Cap. LXVI cont.) ---
RAYMVNDI LVLLI

without profit, since the natural heat has departed, and the body remains without life. Son, the moisture of which we have spoken to you is the matter of the spirit, in which the natural heat is retained and lodged, and in heating it operates against its own moisture, and generates blackness. For since it is separated from true temperament, it is necessary that it must operate insofar as it is fire. And since it has no other subject to restrain its action through a contrary quality, as water and earth first did, in which it was bound and through which it was tempered, because it itself is hot it burns greatly and scorches its own proper subject, in which it is retained, and makes everything black like charcoal. Therefore, son, take heed that such moisture in which the natural heat is lodged should not be entirely separated from the body by excessive extraneous fire, when you are engaged in its natural and profitable calcinations. For afterward it could neither attract nor convert the nutriment of the stone, because it would not be the principal agent; indeed the multiplication would perish entirely. Therefore attraction cannot occur except through heat and moisture, from which the natural heat has been caused and made.

--- Page 267 (page 125, Theorica Cap. LXVII) ---
THEORICA.

On the effect of fire, and how we name it in our mastery.

CAP. LXVII.

From the reasons stated above you can understand and clearly discern whether such heat is the cause of everything generative. But since it has been seen that every generative thing has contrary operations within itself, the said heat, taken in the aforesaid manner as a natural heat and instrument, is not entirely one in form and in virtue; rather it is of many forms and virtues according to the force and power that it receives specifically and in particular through the determination of the subject in which it resides. And therefore nothing defends or prohibits it from having many and marvelous operations, and notable forms, each of which is proper and natural to it. Son, by the force of reason we have drawn out the power of nature, which was hidden and concealed, into the manifest, and we have sought out the reason through the virtue of sense and intellect: for these are the first to know, and are the knowers of substances and ope-

--- Page 268 (Theorica Cap. LXVII cont.) ---
RAYMVNDI LVLLI

rations of nature. And all this has been directed and known by the instrument of experience, by whose virtue we make manifest to you, as we have seen, that each of the said diverse operations is of diverse measures, either stronger or weaker. But this diversity is caused by the diversity of the mixture of elements. Of which mixture there is nothing but one act in its genus, namely to distill and redistill, and to dissolve and congeal, and because of this reiteration our mastery has been considered by the ignorant of the world to be a game of children or women, or a monster lacking head and tail. But he who perceives little knows little, and that is all they know. We say further, supposing that the said mixture is terminated or not, the hotter it shall be with natural heat, the more calefactive and desiccative it will be of the moisture of Mercury, and the more it will impress its effects upon the said Mercury, until it itself is converted into its like by reason of the conquering virtue overcoming the virtue of the moisture which is called Mercury, although sometimes the natural heat, insofar as it is scant and fixed in gross matter, cannot accomplish this unless it is informed or assisted by the heat of an extrinsic agent wi-

--- Page 269 (page 126, Theorica Cap. LXVII cont.) ---
THEORICA.

sely administered by the natural operator, as has been seen in the creation of sulfur by its multiplication. And this is what the works of nature are done in contrariety with respect to the effectual virtues that are in the elements, and according to the matter or nature thereof the said virtues take their determination. For the active virtue, when it is in exhalative and volatile moisture, quickly penetrates all condensed moisture without the aid of extrinsic heat. And since the moisture in which the said exhalative virtue resided is separated and leaves its virtue in the densified body, when afterward it is empty of virtue, it remains without any impression. Therefore we tell you that the moisture is quickly recovered, and then immediately perishes. But in the dry the cause is contrary, and this through the contrariety of moist and dry. For if the moist is of some operation, the operation of the dry is contrary. The contrariety is this: that it receives an impression with difficulty and relinquishes it with difficulty. Likewise the moist does this without accidental fire, and the dry does not. And therefore, since the dry needs fire to complete the impression, we make that dry as subtle as we can, and mix it in the moist until

--- Page 270 (Theorica Cap. LXVII cont.) ---
RAYMVNDI LVLLI

it becomes a temperate medium, and moderately subtilized, so that with the aid of a small amount of heat it may make a noble impression entirely in its body. For this purpose, however, the heat of common exciting fire suffices for you, and the common heat of operating nature, of which we have said so much that you can understand us. Son, it is needful for you to plant and place the said heat in the most subtle earth, so that the gross terrestrial may not be able to impede its impression. And take an example of this in the subtlety of the moist. Son, the said heat is like the principal effective cause of all that you seek, since that which is only in potency it brings into manifest act with manifest experience by reason of the adherence bound by heat and the instinct of nature. Fire, insofar as it is fire, at no time can do this. For the matter would sooner be incinerated by it, because it would not operate as an organic instrument, as does the innate heat in the composite, infused into it by the sun and stars, and not by common fire, as many believe through their great ignorance: for when we say that the stone is generated by fire, they see no other fire, nor believe in any other fi-

--- Page 271 (page 127, Theorica Cap. LXVII cont.) ---
THEORICA.

re bodily than common fire, nor any other sulfur, nor any other quicksilver, unless it be the vulgar kind. Therefore they remain deceived by their blind estimations, inferring that we are the cause of their deception, and that we gave them to understand one thing for another. But this is not true, saving their peace, as we have proven through those things which the philosophers set down in their writings. For many have become sons of Hermes, and we likewise, as has been declared in many words and places of our volumes, and especially in the compendious art of our mastery for illuminating the evidence and certification of all sons of the doctrine, namely, that we call the sun fire, and we have called its vicar the natural heat, because it brings the strengths of the sun with the aid of common heat, as is clear from its effect. For that which the heat of the sun does in the mines of metals in a thousand years, the natural heat itself does in one hour upon the earth. Likewise we and many others call it the son of the sun. For first by the influence of the sun it was generated by nature without the aid of science or art. And therefore Aristotle called the sun the father and the earth the mother of all vegetable things,

--- Page 272 (Theorica Cap. LXVII cont.) ---
RAYMVNDI LVLLI

because the sun impregnates the earth with vivifying heat. And afterward it converts it into natural heat, which is multiplied with the aid of the heat of fire in our mastery. Whence afterward it was called by the philosophers themselves the son of fire, as has been said and shown above. Therefore it is now manifest that the philosophers gave it in great clarity, or if they concealed it with great obscurity. But to you who have seen nothing, nor searched in philosophy, how can you think that I can give you better? See and consider, or let it suffice for you what we now wish to give without any riddle: this is the natural fire, which is generated by the sun and multiplied with the aid of common fire and philosophical disposition. For if any of the three fires were lacking in the art, everything would be worth nothing. Therefore it appears that it is the son of the sun, since it was naturally begotten by it. And it becomes afterward the son of fire, since through fire it grew and was multiplied in the virtue of innate fire. And again it is the son of the philosopher, since through him it was directed and governed to the end of its perfection. And thus it has been shown that it has three fathers. The first is the cause of its generation. The second is the cause of its multiplication. And the third is the cause of its perfection. Therefore, son, we say that you should take our earth impre-

--- Page 273 (page 128, Theorica Cap. LXVII cont.) ---
THEORICA.

impregnated by the sun, and you shall nourish the child well through subtle nourishment. For he must stand in the place of the sun, having within himself the gates and strengths of his father, and the father has granted his son power. This is our infused gold, or our sun, since it is our soul, and not in any other. Afterwards it is our thing, in which the said sun is, and it is a leprous and black earth, in which common gold is corrupted. Therefore honor its nature, and have knowledge of its virtue, because the stone is honored, found in deserted lodgings, and it is enclosed within like a great secret and hidden treasure. Likewise, son, the matter of our medicine must be well fortified with four things from the four elements. The first is the proportional mixture of the four elements, in such a way that it is necessary for you that the natural fire be aided by digesting heat, and the water by the flow of the dry which has been turned into moist, and the air to the end that the said moisture may be breathed into the earth, and the earth on account of the consistency of its substance and the retention of the figure of celestial virtue, which form was and is in the heat and in the spirit. And in this it is necessary that they be proportionally mixed according to measure, which is called geometric measure, and not afterwards equally in quantity, but as much as is required for the virtue and species, or of sulfu-

--- Page 274 (page 128v, Theorica Cap. LXVII cont. / Cap. LXVIII) ---
RAYMVNDI LVLLI

sulfur or medicine, and this mixture is the first and most principal thing necessary, which all matter of generation requires. The second is that the matter have a good power of lapidification, or of vegetation, before it can be called fortified by the vapor of sulfur, and not as the vegetable soul is in plants, but as was said at the end of the chapter on the formative virtue, and the third and fourth are as we shall say plainly in the following chapter on strengths and virtues.

How the Artist must prepare himself for measuring the imbibitions, having regard to the strength of fire, which is called nature's instrument.

CAP. LXVIII.

Yet, son, we give you for memory and premeditation, that in the imbibitions you conduct yourself temperately according to the quality of the virtue of nature's fire, because it is necessary that the virtue of fire should fight against the water, just as a victor by his strength and forti-

--- Page 275 (page 129, Theorica Cap. LXVIII cont.) ---
THEORICA.

tude overcomes his equal in battle. Therefore it is necessary that the virtue of water not separate the natural heat, because through even a small amount, the vegetable virtue itself would be dissolved and dissipated, and would be converted into the nature of water, but in greater heat. And nature gives an example of this thing, that the stronger overcomes the weaker on account of lesser reinforcement. Wherefore consider, lest that lesser reinforcement should prevail, since you wish to act with understanding. For it is necessary that just as water overcomes sulfur and turns it through dissolution to its own nature, in such a way it is not necessary that the sulfur have the power of overcoming quicksilver. For first it conquered it, and turned it through congelation to the property of its noble nature, and of pursuing it, and of terminating it into the proper nature of fine, clear, and precious silver. And this conversion is made through the reversion of the difference, which is moved by concordance, as natural reason shows, and experience has certified to us by strengthening the virtue of the stone and granting it so much reinforcement, and until it has the power of converting our quicksilver, which indeed first converts it to its own likeness by its virtues. Son, since of all

--- Page 276 (page 129v, Theorica Cap. LXVIII cont.) ---
RAYMVNDI LVLLI

all bodies the measure and temperament was through the strengthening of their members. Therefore it is necessary to know precisely, not only what kind of thing it is, but in how many ways, and by what means it can and ought to operate. And through that power the effect is visibly shown to the ignorant, through which he has knowledge of the potency, since in general things the wise and the ignorant share in knowledge. And this is for recognizing a thing which has been manifestly shown before them. And of this manifest thing the fool will have knowledge equally as well as the wise man, because one and the other will have this knowledge, that the matter is of such or such a kind. But when the virtue of that is strengthened, and how it can most quickly act and demonstrate its operation, one knows and not the other. Do you not know, son, that one thing is before all other things and is general, which does not weaken or strengthen any matter, and any body, and any member in its virtue, except by the love of the work, which the said virtue must pursue and attain, and if afterwards it has been achieved, by one, that work will immediately be recognized by the ignorant as by the wise, whether it will be perfect or imperfect? But on this account the ignorant will not have greater science

--- Page 277 (page 130, Theorica Cap. LXVIII cont.) ---
THEORICA.

or knowledge of the disposition of matter, except insofar as he who has seen a man and says, this man suffers an infirmity, or has a bad disposition in his body, and this is the general cause of speaking to the ignorant as to the wise, and of knowing equally well through one as through the other. But already on this account the ignorant will not know the property of the virtuous disposition, through which the body or matter attains or does not attain its good or bad operations, and unless he knows that, he will never have knowledge of weakening or strengthening. Therefore we tell you that all this cannot be accomplished except through the discretion of natural reason informed by instruments, which is moved by the intellect with respect to evident experiments that are not sophistical, on account of which every ignorant person is as it were an exile and banished from our magisterium through such ignorant sophistical persons full of lies. The philosopher testifies this to the sons of truth through an evident example, that the physician is bestial and knows nothing, who only says, this body suffers from weakness of virtue, and does not judge the disposition of the body any better than a rural rustic, who says neither more nor less: indeed that saying will be said and multi-

--- Page 278 (page 130v, Theorica Cap. LXVIII cont. / Cap. LXIX) ---
RAYMVNDI LVLLI

plied in the mouth of the rustic. Wherefore it is manifestly clear that unless by evident and discrete reason the disposition of the proprietary virtue is known through the considered art, one will never know how to trust that virtue, which has been increased through the way of strengthening and weakening. For the strengthening of one cannot be done without the weakening of the other, as has been manifestly evident at all times, and will appear in the disposition of our stone.

How the weakening of one is the strengthening of the other. And how through the strengthening of mineral virtue our stone is generated.

CAP. LXIX.

Son, every weakening of a body is dissolution, and every dissolution of it is the instability of its temperament. And every temperament is the restoration of a thing that was lost, and therefore it is fitting, since our stone must be weakened through dissolution, division, and corruption, that the virtue of the water in which the individuals of nature flee in all

--- Page 279 (page 131, Theorica Cap. LXIX cont.) ---
THEORICA.

all their species and elemental virtues should strengthen itself to make the conjunction and union of altered things mixed in a new generation, so that through the reinforcement of this strengthening, the virtue of the strengthened waters may more readily and acceptably be appropriated against the advent of weakness, and by good abundance obtain the power of overcoming the virtues of our weakened stone, to convert it into itself and its own quality and nature. And through this conversion so made through the weakening of one and the strengthening of the other, our stone is compared to a man submerged in the depths of a great gulf of water. By this opposite mode, with evident reason, wise nature shows us that the weakened virtue of the stone can be strengthened through the weakness of the virtue of the water strengthened by fire, in such a way that it will have the power of converting the water to its own nature, namely, that what was of the moist nature will revert to the dry nature by way of temperament, and that what was of the cold nature will be converted into the hot. But before that, the dry nature is converted into the moist: for in every transmutation of e-

--- Page 280 (page 131v, Theorica Cap. LXIX cont.) ---
RAYMVNDI LVLLI

lements there is such tolerance, and the suffering precedes the transmutation of substance, namely before the substance can be perfectly transmuted, the virtue of the transmuted element must obtain in its power the parts of the element to be transmuted, and it must be fixed in that before the transmutation of the substance, and by mixing itself in such a way that that element will have matter in some other element. And therefore the reason is assigned, what difference there is between an element and the virtue of another element, namely, if in the three noted and notable doctrines of our magisterium, which say that the work and rest of our stone must sweetly and with great ingenuity ascend from earth to heaven. And elsewhere it is necessary that it descend from heaven to earth, and there receive the wind of rest, which fixes the stone carried in the belly of the flying wind. However it may be, son, understand it thus, because our stone through the suffering and tolerance of its nature must needs pass through the qualities of all other elements before it becomes a perfect medicine. And therefore if the stony dry has been turned into the stony cold, there it must be turned. And if the stony cold which has suffered is turned into the moist, there also it must

--- Page 281 (page 132, Theorica Cap. LXIX cont. / Cap. LXX) ---
THEORICA.

it must suffer, until it is perfectly moist. And if the stony moisture is turned into heat, our fire is kindled inextinguishably, nor will it ever fail, and our medicine has been brought to perfect completion. Because noble nature, by refining and rotating through all its elements, has passed through where it sustained heats and calidities, which is why it is so noble.

That the conversion of the stone into quicksilver is nothing but the conversion of the elements of one single nature.

CAP. LXX.

Therefore, son, let the teaching of the philosopher be made clear to you, who says that the conversion of the stone into quicksilver is nothing but the mutation of natures. Do not understand, however, that the substance of our stone consists of several or diverse natures, since it is but one single substance converted entirely into

--- Page 282 (page 132 verso, Theorica Cap. LXX cont.) ---
RAYMVNDI LVLLI

the whole of itself, transmuting into another element or the quality of another. And therefore we then carry it into an elementary division into four parts according to the instinct and condition of its pure nature, which desires to be separated with all its powers from corruption, or from the thing that is corrupt in it, in which it suffers greatly, to the point that it comes to death, and each of its parts toward its own proper element. Because no part of nature can exist without its proper element of the quality with which it is clothed, until it arrives at the stripping away of that quality by the virtue of another quality which nature desires, nor can the whole nature remain in the element of coldness alone from without, until it is terminated by the element of fire, because then it is of a more heated nature. Son, attend: when we say "elemented," this means the element which, through its nature, which is one part of our whole stony nature, contains one elemental element. And we say "elemental" by way of distinction, because one single elemental element delays the corruption that would occur, and delays generation in itself. And therefore the simples of the elements operate and are profitable in this art, because in them there are no primary essences of the ele-

--- Page 283 (page 133, Theorica Cap. LXX cont. / Cap. LXXI) ---
THEORICA.

ments, in which are the virtues that purify, generate, and corrupt. And therefore know that the simple elements are beyond corruption and generation, and the reason is that they are not obedient to noble nature, which causes all things to be born. And therefore we and all the preceding philosophers say that our magisterium cannot be accomplished naturally except from the four elements, which we call composite both conjointly and separately. And in them the nature that is homogeneous to metals rests integrally and is sustained.

How our stone changes itself in all elemental qualities.

CAP. LXXI.

This is the nature of our stone, which can be changed into all elemental qualities, into whichever a good practitioner of the art wishes to change it. And these said elements are composite qualities, which have the power of converting this nature from quality to quality: first into dry, second into moist, third into cold,

--- Page 284 (page 133 verso, Theorica Cap. LXXI cont.) ---
RAYMVNDI LVLLI

fourth into hot, according as the part of nature through division, or the whole through conjunction, has been strengthened or weakened toward the complexion of its element. Son, our stone can never be made except from pure nature, as every wise Artist can know by two means, namely, by speculative knowledge moderated with intellect, and by visible experience of perfect lucidity and great clarity. And from this single nature alone is our medicine made, and not without it, as we propose to declare in the second part of this book. To which you should wish to mix nothing, if you intend to see its power, except only those things that are of its own proper nature, from which it must be strengthened and nourished, until the fortitude of its nature, which had been weakened by those things, can be increased through perfect nourishment against the battle of fire. Son, since this matter is of subtle substance, and immediately enters and penetrates the qualities of the elements, and is converted into the quality of that element which it finds more abundant in that thing: therefore when our stone is rotated, and has ruminated all the elements according to the will of nature, and has entered pure and clean into the region of its own fire, then the fire of philo-

--- Page 285 (page 134, Theorica Cap. LXXI cont.) ---
THEORICA.

sophy is perpetually kindled, for as long as the elements can endure in the world and the world shall fail. Because our stone is like the matter of the elements, in which they place their form and in which they are retained, just as nature demonstrates in every generation. And because our stone is of subtle substance, the qualities of the pure elements descend into it, and are condensed and captured, as in a fine net. For the elements are what give so many virtues to that same substance, as many as it can penetrate, intoxicate, and impregnate, and produce wondrous operations through the power which it received and acquired in the great battle of high and low virtues and powers. And therefore it conquers everything above and below with its great strength, and it is in nature a most secret thing and very wondrous, which the simple-minded hold and regard as a miracle, or an enchantment, or magic art, since their memory cannot enable them to understand our magisterium, which is done through nature and in nature and from nature.

RAYMVNDI LVLLI

That the entire substance of our stone is essential.

CAP. LXXII.

You children of learning, understand that if this nature were not of entirely subtle substance, the whole essential essentiality of the pure elements could not be imprinted upon it, nor could it dominate over the substance. And if the other elements, each according to its own nature, did not dominate the base elements in quality, the substance could not enter or penetrate, nor have ingress. And unless it has ingress, it could not color, nor consequently dominate, nor give other special differences according to the fulfillment of the action of our intellect, which our medicine must have according to its igneous property, through which it is necessary that fire dominate and be housed in the above-said substance. Likewise, if this nature were not of subtle substance, it could not have uniform fusion, nor continuous sweetening, nor would one part well follow another, and through this property it is necessary that air dominate in the said substance, that is, that our stone be generated from an airy nature. For in no other way could the property of air dominate in it, unless it were generated from its true substance. In like manner, by opposition, it is the same with the other two elements: but for the aforesaid reason their substance has been converted into the substance of air. Wherefore let it be revealed to you that the substance of air issued from the substance of water, just as the substance of water issued from the substance of earth. Wherefore it is clear that from the earth issued the whole wondrous substance of our stone. Since if its nature were not in some substantial being, neither the dryness nor the coldness, which must be in the temperament of fire and air in the said nature, could be bound together, as is necessary for perfect fixation. Therefore it is necessary for you to have a subtle earth, and not that which you tread with your feet, but that which flies above your head, which take and embrace and hold dear on account of its property. Because without it no fixed temperament can be made. Concerning the nature of this temperament, the philosopher speaking says that it is not hot, nor cold, nor moist, nor dry, and yet it is both. We say that it holds no more

--- Page 287 (page 135, Theorica Cap. LXXII cont.) ---
THEORICA.

of one quantity than of another, except as much as is needed for the temperament of its property, which has been made through the concordance of the special difference of our elements. And whoever arrives at such a temperament shall be worthy to be placed at the table of the twelve peers.

That the medicine must be of subtle substance.

CAP. LXXIII.

Therefore you, sons of intellect, can recognize that our medicine must be of pure and subtle substance. Because thus the philosopher left it for us in his writings through teaching proven in the truth of clear experience, saying: Wherefore we tell you that it is necessary that our medicine be of stronger and more subtle substance, and of more liquifiable fusion, than are the bodies of metals, and of more fixed retention than quicksilver in its nature. Therefore the philosopher Hermes tells you to separate the subtle from the thick, and the pure from the impure, in order to obtain the said substance, and the simple from the gross, so that from the lighter and better pure material our medicine can be made to the end of perfection. But in making that separation there is no small science. For there are many to whom

--- Page 288 (page 135 verso, Theorica Cap. LXXIII cont.) ---
RAYMVNDI LVLLI

on account of blindness of intellect this separation is hidden. And when they do not understand it, neither do they understand the least thing in which presumption they are puffed up, so that they esteem they have an understanding that is open, and that they possess it totally and enclosed, just as this art can manifest to them in clear experience, which is in truth. Therefore let this science be for you a mirror of true intellect, if you receive the right way in other sciences, because this one shows you by reasoning touching upon truth through clear experience, which has no other proof, nor reasons, nor logical arguments, which are distant and remote from clear intellect. And there are many others who cannot understand, except what four things they can corporally see, namely, everyday and worldly things, and in those through excessive attention their intellect is

--- Page 289 (page 136, Theorica Cap. LXXIII cont.) ---
THEORICA.

from whom, on account of the blindness of their intellect, this separation is hidden. And when they do not understand that, they also do not understand the thing in which, puffed up with presumption, they suppose themselves to have an open understanding, yet have it entirely locked and closed, as this art can demonstrate to them in clear experience, which is in truth. Therefore let this science be a mirror of truth for you, if you desire to have the right way in other sciences, because it will show you a demonstration touching upon truth through clear experience, for it does not suffer any proof, nor reasons, nor logical arguments, which are far off and remote from clear understanding. And there are many others who cannot understand except insofar as they can see corporally, namely everyday and worldly things, and in those, through excessive frequentation, their intellect is joined and arrested there. And therefore, since that is opposed and contrary without any middle ground to our right and true understanding, and for that reason they are cast out and far removed from our natural magisterium, like a people expelled and condemned from our supreme secret. But to you, son, who understand us, we have divided to the elect in our art our testa-

--- Page 290 (page 136 verso, Theorica Cap. LXXIII cont. / Cap. LXXIIII) ---
RAYMVNDI LVLLI

ment, in which you will find a perpetual treasure, if it can come into your hands and be obtained by you. Hear what we say, for neither honeycomb, nor sugar, nor aromatic balsam will cause you such delight as our confection will cause, if you taste but once a small savor of it; I swear to you that you will have a greater hunger to eat of it than before.

On the recapitulation: how everything that is sought is from one single nature.

CAP. LXXIIII.

Recapitulating, we say, son, that this thing, since it is of one single nature, and is made only from that which all who do not have it, or who are able, or who are givers, and also those who have it, and who consider and demand riches, desire and seek. Wherefore this nature, through amorous concord, and without violence or combustible fortification, and without trituration of hands or softening, through the heat of itself and without the sensibility of the property of its nature, is changed from one qua-

--- Page 291 (page 137, Theorica Cap. LXXIIII cont.) ---
THEORICA.

lity into another. For while it exists in its first nature or quality it is not worth so much as a wisp of smoke, but when it has passed through the wheel of the circle, then it is an incomparable and enduring treasure. Son, we further tell you that before this nature can pass through the circular wheel of the elements, it is necessary that it be divided into four parts, so that it may pass through the four elementary qualities, namely from dry into cold, and from gross into simple, from cold into moist, and from heavy into light, and from moist into hot, and from rough into smooth, or sweet, or soft and placid, through the sweetening of the bitter. But this division cannot be made through the change of nature without loss of property, unless the nature which is in a hard and dry mass with all its parts returns to the likeness of that first nature in which the world was first constituted by divine power in the manner of a confused form, in which all things were confusedly mixed, without which nature could not complete its operations, when the divine Holy Spirit was carried above the waters, which occupied the parts of all the other elements, as God and our Lord

--- Page 292 (page 137 verso, Theorica Cap. LXXIIII cont.) ---
RAYMVNDI LVLLI

our supreme creator of nature had ordained in the same moment without the occupation of time, before it pleased Him to make their wondrous separation or division. This operation our Redeemer performed, showing to prime nature how it ought to work through noble intelligence. And let this not be understood except of all nature, which was first created from nothing, and afterwards divided into three parts with a certain measure. And from the first and purest He created the angels and the imperial heaven; through this lucidity and purity, pure souls participate with the angels in nature, by which participation a true union was made between them in great glorification. And from the second part of nature, less pure, God made the firmament, as we have declared above in the chapter on the greater form: also the sun, the stars, and the moon, and He gave to each its virtue, which in nature participates with inferior things. And from the third part, less pure, God made this world and composed the things that are in it, as is written in this present testament in the manner told to you. Thus God our Lord made nature diverse, so that from each part there might be made according to His will a proper word and edict for

--- Page 293 (page 138, Theorica Cap. LXXIIII cont. / Cap. LXXV title) ---
THEORICA.

its own proper certitude: for from the purer He made the better thing, and from the less pure, the lesser. And as it is written in the reckoning of nature, the better a thing is, the higher the nature from which it proceeds. Wherefore it is evident that inferior nature, less perfect in purity, needs the high in all its works before it can arrive at the perfection of its operation. If therefore the superior nature gives to it of its purity, so much the more as the inferior nature better approaches it and its clarity, lucidity, purity, and light, through the attraction of the superior nature, which was made on account of the proximity and the strong and uniform bond of conjunction before their division, and this on account of the association of the quintessences and the fifth essence declared in the book, under the consideration delivered to you, son, through the sacred spirit. Wherefore it appears that at first the whole was one nature.

How the magisterium ought to be understood with respect to the constitution of the world, namely of this great world made by God.

--- Page 294 (page 138 verso, Theorica Cap. LXXV) ---
RAYMVNDI LVLLI

CAP. LXXV.

Now we shall speak of the third part, that is, of the base nature. This is the nature that makes so many diversities in this world. And of this I have said above that you must understand strictly, otherwise you may fall into error. And therefore I do not wish to tell you more, except only what may suffice for you to understand the secret that has been given to us. Wherefore, son, understand the constitution of this great world, which God almighty, after the creation of nature, made in confused form, namely that all the elements were mixed in such a way that neither fire, nor earth, nor air appeared except in the confused form of water, which reached up to the lunar circle, so that fire had no heat, nor air moisture, nor earth dryness; rather the whole had turned to coldness on account of the confusion of their natures. For the more the nature of water was cold, the more it pressed the hot nature, namely of fire, to the depths of the other elements, and consequently the purer nature of air, since air is the nourishment and matter of fire. When the wondrous division of these was made through divine power,

--- Page 295 (page 139, Theorica Cap. LXXV cont.) ---
THEORICA.

and each of them was associated and lodged in its place according to the due of nature, it was afterwards found that in the earth there remained of fire and the other elements that thing which participates with conjoined things. As if a glass full of water becomes clear and shines in the middle of the air above a round surface, and you seek in vain through the substance and savor of water, and the water full of the fifth substance ascending in its high nature is approximated, since to do this is the due of nature. Let this be revealed to you by a figure, for you must know that nature does not operate except through its own will, because it looks to the will of its master, God our Lord, so that it may satisfy Him, because He created it, and through that will God made and makes through nature all things that we see, and not through miracle, as at the time of the flood, when through amicable concord the supreme God withdrew the virtue of the stars which drew the waters from the seas, which hang on high above the region of air, and afterwards they fell down below in rains or hailstones, covering the whole earth, because they were diverted from the part of that climate into another, until to their place

--- Page 296 (page 139 verso, Theorica Cap. LXXV cont. / Cap. LXXVI title) ---
RAYMVNDI LVLLI

they returned to fulfill the divine will. Son, if God wished that two mountains, one from the east and another from the west, should be gathered together in the middle of the earth and be one mountain, it would be done at once, and not through miracle, but through the open and able virtuous instruments that God placed in nature through the wisdom of their entire concord, through which they perform their actions. And thus the whole work of nature is not hidden from God, but from worldly men. Therefore, son, if you wish to do anything at any time, do it through nature, and thereby know the concord of nature, because without it you will never be able to do anything. For no thing in the world that is to be done is outside the limits or pathways of nature, but through it and from it and in it is it to be done.

How the earth is full of intelligence, and how it operates. And on the creation of Adam and Eve. And how our magisterium is likened to the work of the creation of man.

CAP. LXXVI.

Since

--- Page 297 (page 141, Theorica Cap. LXXVI cont.) ---
THEORICA.

Since, son, you can understand that the whole earth is full of intelligence inclined to the operation of nature, which intelligence is moved by a superior nature. So that the inferior intellectual nature, insofar as it is assimilated to the superior nature. For through intelligence it is aided by its like through conversion and attractive power. Then after that natural wonderfulness God created Adam from the slime of the elements, namely from the slime of earth, water, air, and fire, and vivified him from the sun, blood, and spirit, and from light, and clarity and the brightness of the world. And from all these there resulted in him a mixture of the fifth thing in his composite, but more specially he was created from the terrestrial element, as is evident from his being made from clay. And thus from that slime God, the creator of all things, constituted and made a homogeneous nature, from which Adam was formed, from whom He successively created Eve. Which being done, He commanded nature that it should multiply, and it multiplied their species, taking instruction from the mass of the confused world, and through the noble intelligence, which was first confused, and afterwards divided. For this is the general

--- Page 298 (Theorica Cap. LXXVI cont.) ---
RAYMVNDI LVLLI

rule of operating, which God and our Lord gave to nature, and showed to us through a figure. And he who has ears to hear will have everything in his understanding: because by following this teaching nature performs all its works. Wherefore it is evident that he who is ignorant of this divine teaching is outside the works of nature. Our stone is assimilated in operation to the operations of animal nature and of vegetable nature, and of mineral nature, and better still. Because it is assimilated and ought to be assimilated to the creation of man, who was made from the slime of the earth. Therefore, son, if you wish to perform our operation through nature, follow the operation of the teaching of nature. For it has not been given to it that it could operate through any other teaching, except through that which it has from God its master, commanded and taught through perfect intelligence, through which its operations are moved, and do not hasten. And from this nature becomes your mirror, which does not form its things in a short time, but in a certain time the operation constituted for assimilating the magisterium to it.

How the operator ought to order his things for assimilating the magisterium to the operation of universal creation in creating the confused mass which contains all four elements.

--- Page 299 (page 141, Theorica Cap. LXXVII) ---
THEORICA.

CAP. LXXVII.

After these things, son, it is time that you put in order the nature of that which you need for right knowledge, namely for the reduction, which was made through retrogradation of that thing into a confused form with respect to the mass of this world, until it has been turned into the quality of great coldness, in which it first was. And when it has been so turned you can note and recognize that it is nothing but one composite made through art with respect to nature, as if without the aid of art it had been made by nature to achieve the end of its understanding, which is to create perfect metal, or a perfect medicine with respect to nature, which is aided through art, and in this composite are contained the four elements of mineral nature, simple and composite, through which result the four elemental qualities. And through this first conversion our stone is turned into a col-

--- Page 300 (Theorica Cap. LXXVII cont.) ---
RAYMVNDI LVLLI

der nature. And therefore it is necessary that water should dominate therein, and contain in itself the other elements, from which nature through division has afterwards to turn itself from quality into quality. And this is our thing, which contains in itself all that you need in our magisterium. And therefore the reason written philosophically by Hermes gives to understand that just as all things were made from one single thing through the supreme sementific power, and they came forth from the confused globular mass, by miraculous divine will: so our stone was born and came forth from one compact confused mass, in which all its four elements are contained, created by nature, from which afterwards through the will of God and the operation of nature our stone is born.

How the philosophers name the confused mass, and on the division of it, and on the nature of its elements.

CAP. LXXVIII.

For naming this confused mass, son, the philosophers similarly nomi-

--- Page 301 (page 142, Theorica Cap. LXXVIII cont.) ---
THEORICA.

named it the whole world. For in it are four confused elements, which can be separated, and each divided to its own part, impregnated with the fifth nature, and through which they are separated and have been separated. Therefore, son, separate the elements from that mass by sagacious industry, which is moved by a noble intelligence, and you will find the nature which is in them divided into four parts, and each part of that nature will be adorned and clothed with the quality of its element. Because the part that has remained for earth will be complexioned in dryness. And that which has remained for water, more pure and more subtle, is converted into the cold quality. And that which has remained for air will be found in the humid quality neighboring the quality of fire. See therefore, son, the nature which was in the whole composite integrally, first cold, and afterwards was returned into the principal elemental qualities, namely into dryness, coldness, and humidity, and heat through the division of the elements. And just as each element has participation with the one nearest to it, so each part of this stone or nature has greater affinity with the one nearest to it than it has with that which is

--- Page 302 (Theorica Cap. LXXVIII cont.) ---
RAYMVNDI LVLLI

more remote from it. And this is on account of the power of the elements and their qualities, in which the said fifth nature, which we so greatly love, is diffused in a vaporizable manner. Son, these elemental qualities are said to be appropriated to the elements on account of the grossness and simplicity of the said parts, in which the pure nature lies through great corruption, from which corruption if you know how to remove it, you have our magisterium certified through certain preparation. For our stone is never found except in the belly of corruptions, and from there we extract it. The substance from which corruption moves is very fatty, muddy, and strongly unctuous and airy, in the belly of which is the fire that we seek, and in it we kindle it. And therefore Morienus the sagacious Roman revealed to you through the goodness of subtlety, to whom no other was similar in surety through the goodness of preparation, fearing God, faithful, benign, giving all his counsel, saying that the sciences of men are manifestly conjoined, the dwelling of which is manifested in flesh, and blood, and in the deeper part of the belly. And elsewhere he revealed to you that the secret of our stone is in the hips and warm thighs, that is in the deeper

--- Page 303 (page 143, Theorica Cap. LXXVIII cont.) ---
THEORICA.

part of the composite elements, from which the elements exist through those things which have been said above. And therefore it has been said above and is to be added: the nearer things to the nearer things, and the kindred of their kindred. For you know that the dry nature or the part of it, which is of its terrestrial element, does not have the power to turn itself into the humid nature, nor to bind itself to the humid part, which is of the nature of air, for two contrary reasons. One is that earth and air are contraries, and discrepant in qualities. Since earth by the nature that it has is dry and cold, and air by the part of nature that it holds is humid and hot. And therefore the dry nature cannot be converted into the humid, nor the cold into the hot, unless first each of them passes through its intermediaries, which are the kindred of the extremities. Since it is written for reforming nature that contraries existing each of them in its own nature cannot be well bound together, unless they are first changed from their contrary quality into the middle quality, which participates with the true extremities through affinity of nature. And therefore the nature of earth cannot be converted into the nature of air, unless it is first turned into the nature of water: nor can the nature of fire be con-

--- Page 304 (Theorica Cap. LXXVIII cont.) ---
RAYMVNDI LVLLI

verted into the nature of water, unless it is first turned into the nature of air. Since no passage can be made from one extreme to the other, unless it first passes through the kindred intermediaries. For when we wish to make a passage from the first extremity to the third, it is necessary to pass through the middle degrees. Wherefore, son, water and air are the kindred elemental intermediaries of the round ladder, through which nature from the elemental extremes must pass before it can complete its operations in coming to perfection. For water is cold and humid, and through coldness it participates with air, and through its moisture it rarefies earth by lightening it. And through its coldness it condenses air by constricting it. So that when water converts earth to its own nature, afterwards it can convert it into air through the concordance of humidity, which earth received from water, and of coldness, which air received from water that constricted it. And so consequently air is the intermediary of fire and water, since air is humid and participates with water, and since it is hot it participates with fire, and through its moisture it condenses fire and turns it into the nature of air through thickening, and through its heat it attenuates and softens

--- Page 305 (page 144, Theorica Cap. LXXVIII cont. / Cap. LXXIX) ---
THEORICA.

generates water and converts it into air through levigation. And therefore when water has been converted into the nature of air, air converts it into the nature of fire. Or when fire has been converted into a humid aerial nature, moisture and coldness convert it into the nature of water according to what lies in the intention of nature, or of a good and perfect artificer. For just as water is the matter of earth, so air is the matter of fire and water. And one is the matter of the other.

How the elements are subtilized and thickened through the circular wheel, which the philosophers of this art have called the Golden Chain.

CAP. LXXIX.

Therefore, son, we tell you, if you wish to understand us, that when earth is subtilized through its contrary it is converted into the nature of water on account of the quality, namely coldness, which partakes between dryness and moisture. And when that water is further subtilized by fire it is converted into the nature of air. And when that air is further

--- Page 306 (page 144v, Theorica Cap. LXXIX cont.) ---
RAYMVNDI LVLLI

further subtilized, it is converted into the nature of fire, and consequently by retrogradation it is so that when the nature of fire is thickened it is converted into the nature of humid air, and then fire loses as much of its heat as moisture can oppose it through the blunting of its thickening. And the more this humid nature is condensed through inspissation, the better it will be converted into the nature of water, which through its condensation has mortified the greater part of its heat. And it loses a great part of its moisture through the approximation of hardening to the nature of air toward terrestrial qualities, and when this water is further thickened, then it is converted into the nature of earth, and on account of its coldness it loses part of its moisture, revolving itself and carrying it into dryness. Wherefore if you wish to kindle our fire, administer to it light and tender food: because its blunting and mortification comes only from the crudity and grossness of the matter, as is clear from the foregoing declaration. When therefore you wish to make fire from earth, subtilize the earth as much as you can, and when you wish to make earth from fire, condense the fire strongly. We say that air and water are the cause of the thicken-

--- Page 307 (page 145, Theorica Cap. LXXIX cont.) ---
THEORICA.

ing of fire, and air better than water, and water better than air. And earth less than water, and water less than air. But earth with all its powers thickens water by converting it to its nature of earth, and water, by condensing itself and converting to the nature of earth, thickens air and turns it to its own proper nature of water. And air, by converting itself to the nature of water through inspissation, condenses fire and causes it to be converted into air through the thickening of its subtlety. Similarly fire with all its powers strives to convert air to its own nature of fire through the subtilization of its pure substance: and this air converted to the nature of fire subtilizes water. And therefore it turns it to its own nature of air through the rarefaction of alleviation. And this water turned into the nature of air rarefies earth and causes it to be converted into its own nature of water through the subtilization of its gross substance. And through this last order the secret of the composition of our water shines forth to the sons of understanding, since it vividly appears that a dry nature cannot be converted into a humid one until it is converted into a cold nature. Son, we openly open to you the gate of nature for entering

--- Page 308 (page 145v, Theorica Cap. LXXIX cont.) ---
RAYMVNDI LVLLI

all its works, if you understand. Because the superior elements are the cause of the subtilization of the inferior ones. And subtilization is the cause of vivification and animation. The inferior elements indeed are the cause of the thickening of the higher elements. And this by reason of the attraction of the nearer consonance, which lies in their natures, as was just declared above through earth and through fire, and thickening is the cause of mortification and blunting of animal heat. And we swear in good faith that subtilization does not exist without thickening, nor thickening without subtilization, nor dissolution of earth without congelation of water, nor congelation of water without dissolution of earth. Son, in the spirit the congealed body is dissolved, and through the body the congealed spirit is constrained, because by congealing the spirit we dissolve the body, and by dissolving the body we congeal the spirit. Son, see and behold the multiplication of the true tincture, which God gave to nature for making transmutation. Because from one handful of earth and nine of air we make ten of water. And therefore from one handful of this water and nine of fire we make ten of air, ascending by the ladder of vivification to the subtilization of gross nature into the nature

--- Page 309 (page 146, Theorica Cap. LXXIX cont. / Cap. LXXX) ---
THEORICA.

of the simple nature. In the superior likewise from one handful of fire and nine of water are made ten of dead air, which is living water. And from one handful of this dead air and from nine handfuls of earth are made ten of glorious water. Understand, son, these words, for this is the doctrine through which every measure and every temperament are made. And it is necessary that they be made in our magisterium, without which it could not be done. This is the golden chain and the circular wheel of the entire world, through which sagacious nature governs all its instruments by rotating and circulating, passing around in a circuit, which the supreme Creator wished and wills to preserve in His divine marvels and infinite powers.

How the Artist must know by heart the circular wheel, and the nature of the conversion of the elements with respect to the first and second quality.

--- Page 310 (page 146v, Theorica Cap. LXXX) ---
RAYMVNDI LVLLI

CAP. LXXX.

It is necessary for you, son, to know this wheel perfectly by heart, if you wish to attain our magisterium. Because we have told you that the elements by the nature already described go rotating through its circuit successively, until they have completed their circuit and circle, which is the perfection of their operation, and through the knowledge of their circularity that which we said above through the first reason appears clearly. Another reason why the dry nature that is in earth cannot be converted into the nature of air except through its medium is the diversity of grossness and subtlety, or of heaviness and lightness, through the contrariety of the parts of nature, or of that matter, just as with the aforesaid qualities already complexioned. Because all other things that can be considered in these are reduced to the greater, namely, fluidity and solidity are reduced to humidity and dryness, and lightness and heaviness are reduced to subtlety and grossness. And let the first degree be noted here. Therefore you must understand that the part of nature which is in earth is gross and heavy, cold and dry. And therefore coldness, gross-

--- Page 311 (page 147, Theorica Cap. LXXX cont.) ---
THEORICA.

ness, and heaviness with dryness are caused by the nature which is in earth, nobly caused, and the nature which is in water is moderately gross and heavy, moderately subtle and light, humid and cold, by this second degree, and therefore are caused by them heaviness with coldness, and lightness with humidity. And the nature which is in air is subtle and light, hot and humid. And therefore are caused by it lightness with humidity, and gentleness through heat. And let the third degree be noted here. And the nature which is in fire is very subtle and light, and very strongly hot and dry, and therefore are caused by it lightness with dryness and acute heat. Through this doctrine, son, you must understand that in our natural magisterium, the elements must not be defined according as they are the principle of motion from place to place, but according as they are the beginning of alteration. Physicians have followed and follow this same path. And therefore they say that earth is the element through which every body is brought to a healable body, and through which the healable body partakes of coldness and dryness in substance, by its nature very gross, and water partakes of coldness and humid-

--- Page 312 (page 147v, Theorica Cap. LXXX cont. / Cap. LXXXI) ---
RAYMVNDI LVLLI

ity in a moderately subtle substance. And air partakes of humidity and heat in moderately subtle things by reason of its nature. But fire partakes of heat and dryness in a very subtle substance, and strongly active, by reason of its simple nature. Son, this last chapter opens the gate for you, and afterwards closes it. Because we told you above that from one handful of fire and nine of water are made ten of dead air, which is living water.

On the cause and reason why the elements cannot be converted, and also the cause by which they can well be converted.

CAP. LXXXI.

Through this description the second reason has been elucidated, why the dry nature cannot be converted into the humid without its medium. And by passing through that medium it has been openly declared in the foregoing description that the elements are the causes of their own mutation and alteration, as this can be manifestly proved by visible and true experience in our natural magisterium, without any other logical proof. Son, take of our air and a little of earth, and per-

--- Page 313 (folio 148r, Theorica Cap. LXXXI cont.) ---
THEORICA.

and pierce its side with a sharp lance, entirely hot, and you will see from its belly come forth so much of burnt and combusted black choler that it will have the power of intoxicating and poisoning the entire world. Therefore retain it, lest it depart from you, because it will be good for you against your enemies. Afterwards pierce its other side with the sting of a great serpent, only until all the phlegm has come out, and it no longer has any within its belly. Then burn and combust it until it is near death, and until the soul has departed, and the body has been reduced to dust clothed with black eyes. Know that its nature is slack, gross, and heavy, cold and dry, through the property that is in its terrestrial element, contrary to air, in the whole of its nature and property. Wherefore if you wish to convert it into the nature of air, add to it a new part of the red head. And the nature of this red head is a substance very subtle and very light, and in complexion very hot, and dry and sharp. After this add to them two parts of the white feet. The nature of those white feet is a substance moderately gross, and in quality moderately cold. And on account of this property the black eyes participate with it, and beyond that

--- Page 314 (folio 148v, Theorica Cap. LXXXI cont. / Cap. LXXXII) ---
RAYMVNDI LVLLI

in a substance moderately subtle through its humid and hot quality. And through this property it participates in the substance of air. And therefore let the definition of water and air in their dispositions be revealed to you, as a friend of the contrarieties which are in this composite, namely of fire and water, earth, and air, there is born one pure medium, toward the union of the mixtures of the altered qualities of those things in the form of a white congealed earth, very subtle, and transparent according to the perspicuity of airy water, now congealed through the vapor of the said terrestrial matter, now strengthened through sharp heat.

De symbolo, retinaculo elementorum.

CAP. LXXXII.

Son, in this change, the grossness and heaviness of the substance of the terrestrial nature of the inferior has the power of retaining water, through its whole nature, by the savor of cold homogeneity, which is a heavy quality participating in those two elements, which are of its nature: but be assured that this whole thing dominates in the reten-

--- Page 315 (folio 149r, Theorica Cap. LXXXII cont.) ---
THEORICA.

tion of the attraction of air, which was made through the strengthening of sharp heat, by the savor of moisture, which is the matter of fire, and of the whole nature. Therefore, son, place some of the high heat in the inferior nature, to strengthen its dryness, and to mortify its coldness, through a temperament known by the reason of nature. For know that the greatest coldness is the cause of the mortification of fire, and of hardening, and of the grosser nature of attraction caused by heat and moisture. Son, the heat of nature must be tempered through the radical moisture, so that the hot vapor through its excessive action does not burn or scorch the matter of our dear infant, which temperance is made only through the addition of the nature of its moisture, until it comes to the degree of that tempered sharpness, through which it can penetrate invisibly all parts of the humid nature, and place its form upon it, and figure it in the matter of air. You must see, son, how this temperament must be made, in what manner, and in what place it must be created. And for the sake of the matter we tell you, revealing the power of nature, that through the subtraction of the radical moisture it happens to such an extent that the heat of nature, excited and freed through the stimulation of our no-

--- Page 316 (folio 149v, Theorica Cap. LXXXII cont.) ---
RAYMVNDI LVLLI

ble work, shows the said action. Therefore it is necessary for you not to pass beyond it, lest the composite find itself exalted, or separated from its whole nature. Because afterwards you could not see so readily, nor properly fix the lost nature in the body without a part of itself. And if this part were entirely removed from the aforesaid body, and afterwards you wished to restore it to its beginning by way of correcting the defect, it would be very difficult for you, on account of the cause of delay, and as we have said before, its nature would not be so well radically, and through its properties, fixed in its body through this art, unless it were done by this strong and subtle ingenuity of this magisterium, which is very difficult to accomplish, as if through the air. If nature, which greatly adheres to all its intelligences, in order to complete and finish its operations completely, did not minister, because through art it is not understood by God that the first virtues hidden in their first corruptions, and in the corruption of their objects, should be weakened. Therefore, son, it is necessary for you to take heed in the first corruption, and to understand well the ordering of this present final conclusion.

THEORICA.

De qualitatibus accidentalibus. Et de vnione naturæ.

CAP. LXXXIII.

After this it is necessary for you to know that the sharpness of the aforesaid hot vapor is created from the fire itself, which departs from that nature, which fire indeed is the fourth part of the whole nature divided into four parts, and therefore by penetrating the moisture through its sharpness, the fire digests and assists the gross nature with the simple nature through the continuation and coupling of its minerals, and of the parts of the minerals joined together uniformly in the manner of a homogeneous nature, so that the dry and gross nature through its strengthening retains the humid and subtle nature, through the thickening of the simple nature by coagulation. And the simple nature through conversion rarefies the gross through subtle union. Thus it appears manifestly that insofar as the gross nature has retained of the simple through the attraction of the hot and humid nature, to that extent it is nearer to airy subtlety than it would be in its own nature, and has greater perfection; insofar also as the simple nature will be more assi-

--- Page 318 (folio 150v, Theorica Cap. LXXXIII cont.) ---
RAYMVNDI LVLLI

sted, or added with the gross nature through condensation, to that extent it will be nearer to heaviness and ponderosity, and of lesser perfection. And thus, son, you can understand the multiplication or diminution of the operative virtues. For the philosopher in the book of Meteorology says that the terrestrial nature in the place of the generation of metals contains in itself celestial virtues, through which its individual nature is converted into the form, nature, and species of metals, with the aid of the virtue of terrestrial nature. In another way, if the virtue of the terrestrial nature should surpass in potency the virtue of the celestial nature, which was in the specification of the metal, the great dryness and great coldness of the gross nature would condense the moisture, which should receive the form of metal, into the nature and conformity of earth, and not into the form of metal, on account of the defect of celestial virtues, which are not in the said nature. Wherefore the sons of truth can understand that the form of metal comes and is from celestial virtue, infused into the deeper part of their matter, and therefore it is said by Meteorological reasoning that the soul is in the seed of nature, just as the master is in his craft. Son, such celestial virtue is in the proper place of ge-

--- Page 319 (folio 151r, Theorica Cap. LXXXIII cont.) ---
THEORICA.

neration of metals, and therefore is called the form of metal. Therefore make it so, son, that in the place of generation or conversion of such a kind, there be a celestial potency which can transform the moisture of the individual under the preservation of the action, desiring this from the terrestrial nature, into a transparent and most fine form and species, through the congelation of the terrestrial dry; this will be a thing of great virtue. Because the earthy virtue, through tempered cold and through complexioned dry, has operated by congealing, thickening, and retaining. And the celestial formative virtue infused into the virtue of the earth forms the species in celestial heat, when the matter congeals, so that in congealing the species is transformed, and in informing it the matter congeals. Wherefore, son, scripture commands that you must wash quicksilver so many times until it turns into a clear celestial color. And this is the earth in which we sow our souls, and in it is our congelation and not in any other place, and through it the simple fugitive fifth spirits are retained, which have to form, and must form, the clear earth in the virtue and form of the elixir, through a special difference, which is moved by the noble concordance of the fif-

--- Page 320 (folio 151v, Theorica Cap. LXXXIII cont. / Cap. LXXXIIII) ---
RAYMVNDI LVLLI

th things, as we shall clearly note in the second part of this book, in our said practice.

Quomodo tota intentio operatoris debet esse in præparando materiam lapidis, & in suo temperamento.

CAP. LXXXIIII.

Therefore your intellect can perceive by reasonable consideration that the more the matter will be properly prepared and subtilized, the more it will obtain the celestial virtues of the superior nature, and will retain them in itself, and the less, the less. Son, therefore see and take heed how water is changed into air, and air into fire, if you wish to have the preparation of nature. If you wish to make fire from the matter of earth, it is necessary for you from the beginning to subtilize it well, and that it be transmuted into the nature of water and air, and when you wish to operate in the contrary direction, you will change the elements in the contrary by the intention of nature. Nevertheless, do your work from the simpler thing, because in it the celestial virtue is appropriated as the form of the simple thi-

--- Page 321 (page 152, Theorica Cap. LXXXIV cont. / Cap. LXXXV) ---
THEORICA.

of a simpler thing, and this is according to the action of matter and the course of nature. And hold to this saying of the philosopher in the seventh book of the Metaphysics: That according to the property of matter, a certain form will be appropriated to it, and a proper one according to its action. Wherefore if the matter is simple, it will appropriate to itself a most noble and very pure form, by reason of its simplicity and likeness, which is born from the property of a superior simple nature. Therefore when you wish to temper the substance of the stone, place it in the place of generation, or of conversion to the qualities, by which you wish through certain knowledge that your stone be changed, namely, in moist heat. Because this is the quality through which sweetness is caused in a bitter stone, through the preservation of the moisture of the stone in its fire, until it can endure eternal fire.

On the demonstration of the preparation of the stone through an exemplifying difference in the operation of nature in her mines.

CAP. LXXXV.

--- Page 322 (page 152 verso, Theorica Cap. LXXXV) ---
RAYMVNDI LVLLI

In such a manner nature uses tempering in the mines through successive operation, although by fortuitous accident she errs greatly. For if the matter of sulfur and quicksilver, purified and brought to splendor, finds the place of its retention and congelation to be dry, earthy, dark, and unclean, or cold without any temperance, the generation thereof will be converted into an intemperate, dark, earthy, crude, harsh, and bitter complexion, and it will take on a specific form according to the species to which the crude earthy virtue and the complexion of the place moves the matter of sulfur and quicksilver, toward the form according to its action, as is manifestly seen in all genera concerning the extremities and other metallic means. Because their matter is nothing else simply but sulfur and quicksilver mixed together in a vaporizable manner, and specified into an earthy atramentous species, and a gross earthy vapor, which is born from the transmutable place of generation. Son, therefore return the gross matter to simplicity, until it puts on the celestial virtue, which has the brightness to form in our stone, on ac-

--- Page 323 (page 153, Theorica Cap. LXXXV cont.) ---
THEORICA.

count of this, because you can do this well in the elemental revolution, if you understand the convertibility of the elements. Son, the gross atramentous vapor, from which atrament was created, is very sharp and harsh, and therefore it penetrates the parts of purified sulfur and quicksilver, and by penetrating it fixes and implants that purified matter, congealing it into the species of that atramentous and muddy earthy vapor which is mixed with them. Wherefore what we have said is evident, which is the greater door, namely, that the earthy virtues should not prevail over the celestial ones, but conversely, and you will have the thing sought. Through this mixture it appears that there is a mean between stone and metal, since the said mixture was made in the matter of a stone and in the matter of metals and fusible bodies. Because a mean is everything that in some way participates with stone on one side, and in some way with metal on the other side, and moreover still more the property of stone is the inability to be melted. And the property of metal, which it holds from its matter, is the ability to be melted by hot and dry. Therefore through these things you can note that stones are in the genus of dry earth, and metals are in the genus of moist water. The mean therefore

--- Page 324 (page 154, Theorica Cap. LXXXV cont. / Cap. LXXXVI) ---
RAYMVNDI LVLLI

is called everything that participates with the two extremities of those. But some of them are those that participate more with dryness, and those contain hot and dry, and consist of hot and dry, such as Marcasite, Antimony, etc. But some of those means are those that better participate in the degree of moisture, and from those water is distilled through heat and dryness, such as rock salt. Through this, son, the doctrine can shine before your eyes, declared through this: that which is of the property of metal is the watery moisture, and the quicksilver separated through the art of the magisterium revealed to you. Have therefore, son, knowledge of the nature of the means, because from them we draw our entire science, and the power of placing ar. vi. in the work, and we draw ar. vi. from its vitreous caverns, and with those the stone is carried to its first nature, which is the supreme mean purged from its original stain.

On the extremities of our stone, and what they are, and whence they are extracted.
