of our stone, and in the first degree and in the second it is a complete elixir, and in such a manner through our artifice subtly devised, and by natural ingenuity we draw the extremities from the aforesaid means, which participate with our stone, just as wise nature has given us to know: for she has openly shown us the mean, how nature transmutes the nature of the means from quicksilver, namely the nature of the means from quicksilver into perfect metal; this is, son, the common mean for drawing and placing the supreme mean from potency into act through the supreme mode. Yet, son, you must understand in your operation metals in place of means, which are the extremities of nature. For the more the means are of a nobler nature, the more the extremities are nobler and worthier in potency. And therefore from those means we separate the extremities, namely the living ar. of quicksilvers, and the perfect elixir, which is the medicine of medicines. Since in all means, in some manner according to confused forms, there are extremities. Likewise nature manifests to us, as experience certifies, that the nature of those things crushes quicksilver, and mortifies it by constricting and congealing, and after its congelation it is miscible with a body, and then it operates, and not before, giving every color that we need, and by virtue of its dry earthy nature it is retained, and cannot flee from the bodies with which it is mixed. It appears therefore, and it is without doubt, that the vapor of the means is the cause of the constriction of ar. vi. both in nature and in our magisterium, which could not be, unless the agreement of one and the disagreement of another had against itself through the difference of concordance, as appears in the preservation of its heat through preservative agreement. Therefore this substance has been called the green lion, and the green serpent the greyhound, and quicksilver, and the pasture of the philosophical basilisk. But we call that vapor, on account of its present potency, more properly the menstrual humor, which humor indeed is derived from quicksilver, although it has been altered through corruption and earthy fragility which has come upon it, and this quicksilver with the body

How the menstrual is the cause of the death of quicksilver.

CAP. LXXXVII.

Through this menstrual humor we declare what we say, that quicksilver is the cause of its own death, because it kills itself, and afterwards kills its father and mother, and extracts the souls from bodies, and drinks up all their moistures, and makes them become black as charcoal. Then it resuscitates them with such great splendor, that through that resuscitation its father and mother live with it, nor shall they ever die. And for certain, in the presence and by the will of certain companions, we congealed quicksilver through its menstrual, and on another occasion for one of our companions, in whose company we expressly were, nearly two leagues from Naples, in which place in the presence

--- Page 328 (page 155 verso, Theorica Cap. LXXXVII cont.) ---

of the physician of the king and of one brother of Saint John of Rhodes, and of Bernard de la Bret, and of others, we caused quicksilver to be congealed through its menstrual nature. And although they had seen this and manifestly touched it, yet they could not know what it was, except simply only in a rustic manner, saving the royal majesty. And if they could have really and philosophically known through the speculation of the intellectual virtue the said menstrual and its virtues, they would without doubt have had the art and science, as the said companions, who through us understood very well, manifestly, and possessed it. And then they would have abandoned common quicksilver for creating the stone, and for enriching its moisture, and therefore reason compels us to keep ignorant those who are ignorant. And from you, son, we have not concealed nor do we wish to conceal, to whom and when we said that you should take the vile thing and make it embrace its parents, until they are utterly broken and as if dead, and take from their belly the quicksilver, from which you form the stone, and afterwards the medicine, which is not to be conferred upon all, and thus it will be turned from little into good, and from good into better. Blessed be God

God, the supreme creator and our redeemer, who from so vile a thing created something so precious, which holds in itself the property and virtue, and the possession of virtues and properties, which is not in any other.

That the quicksilver of the philosophers is not common quicksilver, and why the stone is so rarely found upon the earth.

CAP. LXXXVIII.

After these things you can understand that our quicksilver is not the common one. Indeed our quicksilver is a water of another nature, which cannot be found upon the earth, since it cannot come into act through nature without the aid of ingenuity and the operations of human hands with respect to nature. And therefore, since nature alone cannot draw it into act from the things in which it was in potency, except through a dark dissolution, and therefore it is necessary for us, if we wish to create our medicine, that from

--- Page 330 (page 156 verso, Theorica Cap. LXXXVIII cont.) ---
RAYMVNDI LVLLI

from those things in which it was potentially, we draw it from potency into act with respect to nature through the reasonable industry of a good master and operator, by the way of generation made through premeditated corruption, which philosophy has granted to us and given us to know. Wherefore the generation of that thing pertains to us, following the virtue and potency of nature, which virtue we have found in our genuine philosophy to be natural, and not by rustic virtue, which is against nature. And for this reason that quicksilver is ours, and not of the rustics, who are impotent to metallify mineral nature. Since therefore it cannot be generated upon the earth by the sun, the artificer of nature, except with the magisterial aid of our artifice, discovered by us philosophically, without any common rusticity, therefore in its generation it is necessary that we subtly introduce the action of metallic nature into the matter in which it must be generated, for which task to be accomplished every common rustic is utterly absurd and blind, because he lacks our philosophical reasons, which guide the intellect to direction without deviation through the A. B. C. D. of our punctual magisterium.

--- Page 331 (page 157, Theorica Cap. LXXXVIII cont.) ---
THEORICA.

Wherefore it is evident that our quicksilver is not of the common sort, since nature cannot create it upon the earth, nor can the rustic likewise; indeed it is ours, and entirely of our philosophy, just as a son is of the father who begot him. Therefore know, son, that the reason why our stone is so seldom found upon the earth is this: because it is not in the power of nature, granted by the superior, that she can generate it without the ingenuity and operation of human hands. And because men are ignorant of nature, and of its natural mixture and operation, therefore it is rare and scarce in manifest act, because they cannot aid nature, by reason of their ignorance, until they have passed through the universal and desired philosophy, which reveals to them the things that were hidden and concealed from them, and unknown.

How this magisterium is through nature, and through the operation of art and science, and not by miracle, and on its demonstration.

CAP. LXXXIX.

Do not believe however, son, that this happens by some miracle, nor by confused art, as some fools believe: indeed it happens according to the course of nature through a known art, of which we have a certain operation, which has brought us our desire through preparation, because it cleanses and purifies the vile and unclean nature, and places and converts it into a noble nature, and the dry into moist, and the hot into cold. If this change were not earthy, the terrestrial nature would dominate the superior nature, and would convert the individual homogeneous nature to its gross and terrestrial nature, and that this is true appears through incontrovertible experience. For when quicksilver is placed in a dry atramentous vapor, such as sharp water, it is immediately dissolved through incision and penetration, which is moved by strong sharpness openly, and in dissolving it converts itself into the nature of terrestrial atrament, not taking on a metallic form, nor a clear form, nor a celestial one, as appears after the evaporation of the said water, and its congelation, in the form of saffron-colored little stones, which

--- Page 333 (page 158, Theorica Cap. LXXXIX cont.) ---
THEORICA.

which saffron color proceeds from the pontic and sulfurous earthiness, which was beyond measure in the said water mixed in small pieces with the universality and homogeneous simplicity, which simplicity was seized and bound by the said earthiness, by the change of light and clarity and lucidity into obscurity. Likewise through a middle operation with a contrary form, quicksilver mixed with atraments is turned through the washing of water and fire, which devours and consumes all its gross vapor; indeed the mixture of that is made with quicksilver, which through the simple contrariety of gross nature is turned into simplicity, and by means of moderate sharpness is congealed, and is mixed with those things which more participate in its nature in crystalline lucidity, as appears after its sublimation. And from this the cause arises through which we perceive the temperance of fire in the sublimation of ar. vi. until the sharpness, which by excessive fire carries gross earthiness, which gives infection to ar. vi., is completely evacuated or evaporated. Therefore because this through the expression of dry fire carries its nature in its belly, as appears in the confections of strong waters,

of which nature we do not need, because it is the corruption of our magisterium. But we wish you to understand us well by this method. Take therefore the simple aereity, and wash it very well, and make water, and prepare it with philosophical preparation, until it has passed through the five planets, and afterwards it arrives at the moon, and when it has passed through six, it comes to the sun, which will clarify and illuminate all things. Son, what has been said notifies to us the multitude of preparations through which you must go, and in each preparation through the virtue fixed in it is the complexion of each planet, and therefore, if the individual nature finds a fit and suitable place for its generation in the form of a metal, either that place of generation is complexioned by nature of lead according to its planet, or of Jupiter, or of Mars, Venus, Mercury, the Sun, or the Moon. If it is complexioned to the nature of Saturn on account of its weak digestion, it will transmute the individual into the form and species of crude lead, muddy and unclean. And if to the nature of Jupiter, Mars, or of the others, the individual will be converted into the nature of the place to which it is added on account of its infected digestion. And beyond

And therefore it cannot convert, but only thicken, and from the nature of air it converts into the nature of water through condensation, which the air takes from its small terrestrial parts slightly digested, which are mixed with it in a vaporizable manner, through defect of constrictive virtue, and therefore it is thickened into a crude thing, re-cruding itself through the action of uniform heat and unctuous, laxative, tardaric moisture, just as milk does in the breasts.

On the abortive nature of imperfect bodies, and on the temperance of the vessel of nature.

CAP. XC.

Therefore, son, let it be manifest to you that the above-said bodies are nothing but abortive things, which could not reach the terminus of their perfection, just as those who are born and are born in menstruous places, undigested and corrupt, suffering the infirmity of leprosy

--- Page 336 (page 159 verso, Theorica Cap. XC cont.) ---

and corruption in their bodies, and nature does this on account of the defect of due preparation and natural heat, which cannot well digest the menstrual, which is the matter of metal, either through its excessive excitation, or stimulation of burning, ardent, and choleric heat, either of the individual, or of its excessively changeable place, in which very many accidents can often occur, since it is the container of all the matter of every generation. Wherefore if the place of change of the individual is such that it is tempered in such a way that through its digestion, or good temperance, it has more power to purify with the preservative of its clear and purified germ through transmutative conservation, it will verisimilarly congeal and transmute that into fine and pure moon by the virtue of sulfur already purged from its original stain of leprosity, which is called the place of the said transmutation, or into perfect sun according to the good temperance which the individual moisture will take from the place of its generation and complete or incomplete digestion. fire dominates with a very noble temperament, and in the place of the generation of the moon, air dominates with celestial virtue. And therefore it is clarified and demonstrated that the fume or menstrual terrestrial vapor, and of quicksilver, ought not to exceed nor surpass the weight of the pure metal, unless equally to it; otherwise in the preparations one must be warned. Wherefore it is clear that if there is more of the virtue of earth in the place of generation than it has of light and of nobility, which come through the illumination of the planets in the said place, the congealed substance will take on and obtain darkness, duskiness, muddy heaviness, and coldness, like lead. Wherefore it is clear that in lead the terrestrial element dominates, just as in gold the high element dominates with perfect temperament. And if the said place participated more in celestial virtues and less in terrestrial ones, it would be shining and resplendent without any corruption, firm and solid through the constriction of its parts, which the gravity and heaviness demonstrate by necessity of relaxation. As much as pertains to the contrary of this, the said proportion constitutes the species of gold, to which proportion the purified matter comes in that

How the Artist ought to note the mineral virtues nearest to perfection. And of the abbreviation of the work through fermentation.

CAP. XCI.

Wherefore concerning all things which we have told you, be warned both collectively and individually, noting greatly the mineral virtues near to perfection. For we tell you that purified quicksilver can revert to the nature of those things to which it is joined, and understand this both in the first part, which is the purging and creation of the stone, and in the second, which is the fermentation and change of it into a fine and true

When therefore you transfer to the second part of the second degree, add the now-cured sulfur with washed and cleansed ferment, which has been called the ointment, so that the whole may be converted into ferment; therefore the whole ointment will be. For it is known and recognized that he who gives form agrees with that which receives it. Likewise it appears, and has been set forth above, that all generation ought to be from things agreeable to the nature which carries forms and celestial virtues in itself, and this is the reason why our preparation cannot be completed except perhaps by virtue of length of time through many errors. In the first part the ferment supplies and completes through its well-tempered and very well-digested virtues, through brevity of time without great impediment. If you know how to do this in the second part, then in this we surpass the course of nature, because it finishes its operation by its one sole digestion. And we accomplish through the ferment that which nature has already completed by its digestion. And therefore have within yourself known preparations, and patience in their long delay. For all prolongation of time is in the work of nature, because it operates only by a little. And the preparations

How the Artist ought to understand when the stone loses its nature and diminishes, in order to amend it.

CAP. XCII.

Through these things, son, we tell you that it is necessary for you to know how to amend the stone in the first part, which loses some of its first tempered virtue in the whole regimen of preparation: for in that preparation it needs to revert and pass from that to a greater one through the part of the second degree. Therefore, son, we tell you that the more it is removed from its temperament through the mortifications of the body made in the first part, the more it loses of its first nature, and it is a body that has suffered like a man existing in a great infirmity through the disturbance of his spiritual virtues separated and removed from him through the dissolution of those same virtues which it has continually suffered. And this instabil-

--- Page 341 (page 162, Theorica Cap. XCII cont.) ---
THEORICA.

instability we proclaim when the body is removed from its noble temperament, or from its complexional equality insofar as it suffers, because it lacks that which would strengthen it. And insofar as it has received a portion of its removal, so much the better does it approach through the way of strengthening by natural reduction, or attraction to natural temperament; and the more you extend the wheel in carrying the body to longer corruption and mortification under some preservation, the greater will be its life and perfection, if afterwards through the strengthening of its lost spirits it is reduced to its temperament. And this is by reason of that which it will receive in its first attraction made in the regimen of reduction, by which it will surpass in improvement what it had lost in its mortification. Since temperament does not occur except through the restoration of the lost thing, namely the lost vivifying spirits, and restoration cannot happen without natural attraction, nor attraction without evacuation, nor any evacuation without a certain mortality, which occurs through the burning and combustion of the body and calcinat-

--- Page 342 (page 162 verso, Theorica Cap. XCII cont. / Cap. XCIII) ---

ion, and the evacuation of the radical moisture.

To know how the stone is more remote from its temperament.

CAP XCIII.

You ought to know at what time the stone is more remote from its temperament: when the body is burned and empty of its spirit and inanimate, then it is more remote than it can be from its temperament, and it has completed the wheel of corruption so as to be the proper place of generation. And the more superfluous moisture it lost in its dissolution, the more it will attract to itself the said exuberant moisture in its reduction. Example: if the body loses B and C in its dissolution and corruption through the extended wheel, it will re-enter and attract D and E, which are equivalent to the loss of B and C. And beyond that, additionally by nature in the regimen of reduction, it will attract F and G, which are exuberant through D and E on account of the fermentation of B and of C. And if the body loses only B through a lesser wheel, nothing can be recovered in its place except C, and afterwards D exuberant from B, proceeding to greater temperament, because its loss does not permit it, nor the course of nature; and insofar as the tempera-

--- Page 343 (page 163, Theorica Cap. XCIII cont.) ---
THEORICA.

ment of the lesser wheel is less perfect on account of its lesser corruption, so much greater is the temperament of the extended wheel unto double perfection, which is born from a greater attraction of moisture by deeper reason of its stronger calcination, dissolution, and mortification. And through this you can understand our whole intention, namely, that our whole mastery is nothing but the binding together of dissolved moisture, or the dissolution of one and the congelation of another, or the mortification of one which is spiritual, and the vivification of another which is corporeal. Since in whatever manner the stone is to be dissolved, in that same manner it must be composed. And this binding together is nothing other than the multiplication of exuberant and fixed quicksilver. Through whose multiplication our stone comes to greater perfection and temperament.

In this, son, is the completion of the diminution of imperfect bodies, which fall short by a certain small fixed moisture and perfect thickening. Son, consider all that which we have shown you, because we manifest to you the first causes of our whole secret, which are the real entities of our mastery, and without them it can never be accomplished. bring near what you can bring near, and that which you can move far from it, move far away, and you will come to the corruption of the first form, by the truest steps, and to the generation of the second form, which is of noble generation. Son, the regimens of our mastery are interlinked and chained together. For the first is aided by the third and by the second, and the third by the first and by the second, and the second by the first and third. And when you are in the first, mediate it with the second and third. And when you are in the second, guide it with the first and third. tion. And none of these can be completed without the aid of the other, since they must come about through contrary operations, as you can perceive through the dissolution of bodies, which is the cause of the congelation of the spirit, and the congelation of the spirit is the cause of the dissolution of the body. And thus each looks toward its own differing act by reason of discordance. This is the erratic conversion of the elements, which you must understand well, and if you understand well, your intellect will shine abundantly and fully, as will the strengthening and weakening that are made in our supreme secret, and in every body of nature. For through them is every temperament or distemperament of our mastery, which is the latitude of complexion.

On the latitude of complexion, and how it is comprehended from two limits.

CAP. XCV.

Every

--- Page 347 (page 165, Theorica Cap. XCV) ---
THEORICA.

Every latitude of complexion, son, of whatever species it may be, turns toward temperament or distemperament. And therefore the first differences of complexion are only two, namely, temperate complexion and distempered complexion. Temperate is that in which the primary qualities are broken up through mixture until they come to that limit through which the better disposition of the composite results, according to that mode which is owed to its species. For in any species absolutely there is a temperament, which indeed is such for its species in respect of its individuality. And distempered complexion is that in which the solar body recedes from the better disposition through the domination of certain primary qualities. Therefore it appears that since in no complexion can any quality dominate except simply or compositely, therefore every distempered complexion will be either simple or composite. Simple is that in which one quality alone dominates, as in our stone dryness dominates with a simple quality. Nor should you understand that we call it so simple that it does not participate with others in some degree: for this would be against the nature of complexion, since it is a qua-

--- Page 348 (page 165 verso, Theorica Cap. XCV cont.) ---
RAYMVNDI LVLLI

lity composed of the four primary ones. But because through the judgment of evident knowledge, which has been granted to us through manifest sense, we see and know that one of those four greatly dominates over its contrary. And therefore we wish to say that when the stone is reduced, it must be turned from great humidity into great dryness, and into lesser humidity, and lesser coldness, and lesser heat. Therefore it appears that since through this dryness the earthy element determines our humid stone, it is naturally converted into the nature of airy earth through the regimen of reduction. For this reason some wished to say that our dry stone was in the ultimate degree and most remote from its perfect temperament, already having suffered by reason of its dryness and sharpness. Therefore they wished to draw that food outward to its temperament with nourishing fermentable humidity: but this was seen to be against stable reason, through the wisdom of nature, and a great inconvenience to their understanding, since our stone through the regimen of reduction has already been informed through opposite differential qualities through concordances, through which information every thing tempers itself to its temperament and

--- Page 349 (page 166, Theorica Cap. XCV cont.) ---
THEORICA.

approaches it. For when our stone had been through the second regimen killed, dead, and submerged in the gulf of great Satalia, and its pasture and nurse had been entirely cremated, burned, and consumed, failing from the perfect life that is the cause of the approach to noble temperament, therefore it was seen that our stone is more remote than it could be from its temperament while existing in the second regimen, standing within that limit in which there are two complexions most extreme and distant, because it is necessary that the matter not be cast to the contrary beyond the latitude of said limit, but that it participate in some way in some of the metalline complexions that are nearest to the extremity. And that the matter not pass beyond the extreme complexions that participate in death. Otherwise, if intensification or remission were to proceed against nature from strong and opposite qualities, said matter would utterly fail from the complexion of metal, to such an extent that afterward the individual of the metalline species could not be preserved under it, as appears in the combustibility of matter, when through excessive fire it is corrupted, it is entirely separated from its nature through defect of natural information. Son, see

--- Page 350 (page 166 verso, Theorica Cap. XCV cont. / Cap. XCVI) ---
RAYMVNDI LVLLI

that your body does not entirely recede from its nature, that is, from its particular ultimate complexions, which are contained under the latitude of temperament. And with the matter existing at such a point we say that it is most remote from its temperament: but if you wished on this account to say that our mastery should be begun through the third regimen, you would fail in the understanding of our work, since we do not yet have the subject or convenient matter in which and through which the elements are to be fixed. Therefore it is necessary that through the third regimen of water and earth our stone is procreated.

How the substance of sulfur exits from the extremities when the stone is created.

CAP. XCVI.

We say therefore that our stone is first created so that the subtle hot and sulfurous substance may be extracted from its extremes, and that it may be tempered with moisture, and with heat, which is the third perfective instrument of nature, and the medium participating immediately in said substance, through which it receives the tincture of metal, which is in said instrument. Thus, son, we draw

--- Page 351 (page 167, Theorica Cap. XCVI) ---
THEORICA.

our stone from the extremities, in which are confused media, so that it is neither one nor the other: but so that it is a medium, or a neutral to said extremities, that is, between those from which it materially issued, which neutral we call it, according to the consideration expressed in the treatise on the intention of the wise and subtle Alchemists: not that it is neutral through the negation of the extremities, but through the participation of one and the other. And all its extremities are the body of said thing and the spirit, from which bodies neutrals are procreated in all media, and have been procreated. Since therefore such a neutral has been procreated from its certain and known mineral extremities, without doubt it participates indistinctly in their natures, according to the judgment of the wise and of experience. And consequently, it will thus have and possess certain subtle and mixed and absolutely indistinct extremities. And therefore it has been called neutral, as there are the healthy-sick. And therefore it must work in them indifferently through its neutral property, and render sick bodies neither healthy nor sick, until the virtue of health is integrated in it. And therefore it is clear that this is on account of the imper-

--- Page 352 (page 167 verso, Theorica Cap. XCVI cont. -- end of Theorica) ---
RAY. LVL. THEORICA.

fect cause, which comes from its infirmity and sickness. Afterward, make from that neutrality a perfect extremity, as it is imperfect. You will add to it another time something of its homogeneous nature, from which it was created in part, namely simply, so that from the perfection of health which is in the air, it may take away the infirmity and sickness of the neutral, and so that the virtue of one may be strengthened through the virtue of the other, and so that the infirmity and sickness of said neutral, which comes from the part of earth and water, may be commensurably alienated through moisture and heat, and then you will find it approached to the perfect and tempered extremity, and it holds only half a part of sickness and two of health. Another time make that imperfect extreme, and add to it two operative virtues, namely natural air and fire, and you will find it returned from the imperfect extremities and media to the extremity full of health and every temperament. Understand, son, for we have said all these things after the consideration of chapter 168, which begins, Our stone remains in all its elements, etc. Praise, honor, and glory to Jesus.

End of the Theorica of the Testament.

Here begins

--- Page 353 (page 168, Practica, Incipit / Cap. I) ---
HERE BEGINS THE PRACTICA OF MASTER RAYMOND LULL UPON THE PHILOSOPHERS' STONE. And first the definition: what thing it is, or what science or art Alchemy is.

CAP. I.

Alchemy is a part of natural celestial philosophy, most necessary, which constitutes and makes one art and science, which is not known to all, and teaches how to cleanse and purify all precious stones, not perfected but cut off, and to bring them to true temperament, and to restore all human bodies that have fallen and are infirm, and to reduce them to true temperament and optimal health, and also to transmute all metallic bodies into true luna, and afterwards into true sol, through one medicinal body, to which all particular medicines have been reduced, and have

--- Page 354 (page 168 verso, Practica Cap. I cont.) ---
RAYMVNDI LVLLI

been. And through which, integrated and made through one manual regimen, it has been revealed to the perfect sons of philosophy, by means of the heat of fire, with respect to the six latitudes of heat, which are principally reduced to three. The first is called Dulled, and this prohibits and prevents the integral motion of nature. And therefore it leads and induces putrefaction in mixed things, without which putrefaction animation cannot come about in elemented things. The second heat is excited heat with the tolerable heat of vivification, and this heat leads and induces generation and multiplication. The third is excessive and deadly heat, which destroys all senses and instruments, instincts, and natural appetites, through which corruptions, generations, and multiplications occur. Son, this doctrine is called the Royal Flower, through which the human intellect is rectified by the force of experience, with respect to the eye and true knowledge, since its experiences cannot suffer any phantastic proofs, but it shows the intellect how to enter vigorously into all other sciences, how it must penetrate the divine virtues, which are to be veiled: also through nature we understand that which is, whence many foolish and infamous people be-

--- Page 355 (page 169, Practica Cap. I cont. / Cap. II) ---
PRACTICA.

lieve that it is nothing: but we know whether this thing exists or not. And therefore this secret will not be known to the ignorant. For through such science, the intellect is expelled of superfluities which remove it from all truth, and in another way this science is called the standard and philosophical instrument, through which the good philosophers and ancient Poets directed their sense toward any science whatsoever, to enter through this into every experience, to be made according to the course of nature through real knowledge.

How the practice is transmitted through a double instrument. And what are the instruments that it gives.

CAP. II.

Son, in this second practical part it is, and it is of the intention of our practice to transmit, and the mode of practicing by a double instrument. The first instrument is the memorative practice, or recordative, or recollective. The second is the operative practice, the whole mixed together, yet successively, made in the mode and form of an Alphabet, according to the de-

--- Page 356 (page 168 [sic] verso, Practica Cap. II cont.) ---
RAYMVNDI LVLLI

termination of each regimen, and this so that you may better understand the royal magisterium, and form it through art, for making and achieving the practical result which is certain, since before your eyes we shall demonstrate it to you by an action known through clear experience. And because the virtue of the beginning cannot run, nor course through, nor flow to the end, except by means of a middle virtue, the copulative of the extremities, this proceeds from reason, which says that we shall form through the memorative art the aforesaid instrument, especially since it is so as the dictation of real nature will show us, that the understood theory ought not to be separated by one step, nor distanced from known practice. For the one forms the other, and corrects its defects. And therefore we have placed the memorative practice according to this science in a middle form, which naturally issued from its own proper extremities, and its proper extremities are the operative practice on the second side, and on the first its sister, namely, the theory. And these are the principles of true instruments, which integrate and perfect the whole science and art, which is formed through the first. Because there will never be an art or science, unless it is first begun and initi-

--- Page 357 (page 170, Practica Cap. III / Cap. IIII) ---
PRACTICA.

ated from its certain principles.

On the second division of this part through the memorative instrument.

CAP III.

This book, which is called and named the second part, is divided and partitioned into four principal operations. The first is to dissolve, the second to wash, the third to congeal, the fourth is to fix the matter. The first and principal operation is divided into two solutive parts, as we touched upon in our theory. Through the first we make unity from plurality, through the second plurality from unity, as will be shown to you through honest and beautiful doctrine. Son, all these operations have been given to you to be understood through certain and determined alphabetical distinctions, namely through the mode of the Alphabet A.B.C. and of figures, and definitions, mixtures and applications.

On the first distinction, which is of the A.B.C.

CAP IIII.

--- Page 358 (page 170 verso, Practica Cap. IIII) ---
RAYMVNDI LVLLI

For the first distinction, which is the A.B.C., in the first operation: A. signifies God our Lord, who created all things, and from whom everything proceeds. B. signifies quicksilver, which is the common substance, standing in every corruptible body, as appears through its property, because it has the action of corrupting and generating, conjoining, dividing, thickening, grossifying, rarefying, hardening, softening, augmenting, diminishing, dissolving, congealing, calcining, mortifying, vivifying, crudifying, maturifying, washing, desiccating, humidifying, heating, cooling, softening, soothing, roughening, sweetening, embittering, strengthening, weakening, entering, making ingression, tinging, giving weight, incerating, feeding, impregnating, engrossing, sublimating, and making the prime matter, espousing, and making the first marriage, fixing body and spirit, obscuring, and kindling. For through this quicksilver, let all sons of doctrine know how to discern the works of nature through its properties, and many and numerous of those

--- Page 359 (page 171, Practica Cap. IIII cont.) ---
PRACTICA.

to reduce from potency into action through its property, and to regulate and order its operations according to what the instinct and institution of nature wills. And therefore we say to you, that since it is the father of all liquefiable things, on account of the agreement and commonality of its own proper nature, hold it dear and secret, and do not defame it. This is the matter of nature, without which no corporeal thing of nature is generated, which does not have its beginning from it, from one slime in which nature is, which operates, strikes, and always fabricates, without the passing of its measure. C. signifies rock salt, which has a common nature similar to quicksilver through the property of its strong nature. D. signifies azoquean vitriol, which corrupts and confounds everything that is of the nature and being of common quicksilver. E. signifies the menstrual, which contains the natures of the three aforesaid altogether. F. signifies fine, pure, and resplendent silver. G. signifies Mercury, which we know. H. signifies gold, which is so greatly honored. The question is why we placed A. in this art: this is because without God our Lord nothing was created or generated, nor was any work begun, nor without him con-

--- Page 360 (page 171 verso, Practica Cap. IIII cont. / Cap. V) ---
RAYMVNDI LVLLI

summated, and because he is the principal and final efficient cause of all creatures and operations, worthy and true reason shows us that he should be placed before and set first, as the former and creator of everything created, and that through him and his immensity the Artist may be illuminated in this work, who indeed ought to have before his eyes the good, true, strong, and principal memory of God our creator.

On the second distinction, which is of the figures of the first solutive part.

CAP. V.

This figure teaches that by means of A. the six aforesaid figures are composed for the first solutive part, namely three triangular and three circular. The first circular figure is signified by E. and is made from B.C.D. The second circle, signified by I., is made from the second triangle, composed of E.F.G. The third circle is signified by K., made from the third triangle, composed of E.G.H. And these figures pertain only to the first solutive part, which is more properly called the liquefactive.

C. by heart, you will not be able to practice, nor to begin anything, and thus you will be impeded by God, so that you will not know what to do: because you will be ignorant of the receptions of the species, which the letters signify, nor will you know how to mix them in such a manner as nature requires, nor how to make your mixtures, which it is necessary for you and every practical and practicing Artist to know how to make, so that the said mixtures may be operations of nature, and that the said letters, or their signified things, may be the proper matter, and what it demands.

On the second practical instrument in the first. And on the first solutive part.

A practical instrument means as much as to say putting theory into operative practice, by means of an instrument, which is memory, which has to show you how the said letters are figured for the first

On the first triangular figure, which demonstrates the beginning of the practice, and on the triangular intention.

CAP. VIII.

Upon this following figure, which is the first triangular, we show

Since therefore, son, this is the first distinct beginning and the first entrance into our magisterium, apply yourself to the weights, from which the temperament comes rendered in balanced proportion, as, son, we shall say without delay.

On the first disposition, beginning the work in the form of the practice.

CAP. IX.

You,

--- Page 367 (page 175, Practica Cap. IX) ---
PRACTICA.

in the virtue and honor of A., shall first take one part of D. and a half part of C., which shall be ground together most thoroughly and sifted, finely pulverized upon marble, and well mixed. And you shall place them in a glass cucurbit, situated in a furnace, and with an alembic placed above, in which through resolution the spirits are seconded and condensed, and you shall seal the joints of the vessels with linen cloth, pasted and moistened in a lute made from flour and egg whites tempered together, so that the properties of the three Mercuries united, namely the saline, the vitreous, and the aqueous, joined together and placed as one, may not be lost, or that we may not lose them. And take care that the said powders placed in the cucurbit do not exceed or surpass the weight of eight ounces, and to shorten the time, you shall place in two other cucurbits an equal powder according to the weight of eight ounces in each cucurbit, and place those upon long furnaces, in the manner which we shall declare in the chapter on furnaces, nor place more than three cucurbits upon one furnace: for the fire could not minister to more of them equally, as

--- Page 368 (page 175 verso, Practica Cap. IX cont.) ---
RAYMVNDI LVLLI

the mixture of nature requires, and let the said cucurbits be placed one from another at a distance of five or six fingers' breadth. And let the bottoms of the cucurbits be sealed with clay earth, strongly beaten with animal hairs, as far as those bottoms will enter the furnace through three openings at the top of the said furnace, and to the thickness of the material: and place clean ashes, well sifted, pressed down well beneath, to the thickness of five fingers, and place at the nose of each alembic a glass phial with a long neck, to the end that the container of those phials may not feel the heat of the furnace, nor may the water of the phials be able to flow back, nor the spirits to recede or flee. Afterwards have sawdust of wood, of which you should have a great supply, and take two parts and a half part of the marc of the wine press, or of small debris, or of dry tanbark, and mix it with the said sawdust. And fill your furnace with such a composition, then light your fire at both ends, and let it burn. For no other fire is to be made by you, until you see six drops of water distill, or 10, or 15, or 20. And when it has distilled to twenty, make a fire with small

--- Page 369 (page 176, Practica Cap. IX cont.) ---
PRACTICA.

small pieces of wood, or bundles of dry wood, and so gradually begin to make a flame fire directly beneath the material. And observe, when it distills, that the water is clear, and when it reaches fifteen points, and the water is clear, and the vapors are subtle, continue that fire equally. And if you see that from fifteen points it returns to twelve, or less, strengthen the fire, and continue according to the rate of its distillation, and afterward strengthen your fire a third time by one point beyond, and continue until nothing more distills, and then stop making fire, and close your furnace, and allow it to cool, and if the water is fine and clear without any turbid color, or without turbidity, take this and preserve it, and seal the flask with warm wax, lest anything escape, nor let air enter, because the spirits, which are subtle, would immediately be corrupted by the air, which enters full of impurity and foreign nature. Remember, when you begin to make fire with dry wood, that your vessels must be prepared with the paste described above, and wrapped around with linen cloths, and the flasks well sealed to the noses of the alembics, with the same lute placing one straw between the nose of the alembic and the band

--- Page 370 (page 176 verso, Practica Cap. IX cont.) ---
RAYMVNDI LVLLI

in a line. For very often while the fire works, the air wishes to escape and breathe, when it does not have a container that can retain it. For it is hot and full of heat, and the vessel that holds it cannot endure the excessive heat. And therefore it needs some place where it can breathe. Therefore open a hole for it when you hear it blowing. O father, how did you make the practice so long? Son, so that you may be practiced in all things small and great, and so that you may have knowledge of fires both specifically and generally, and of other operations, through the present chapter, and also of all sealings: because it is not our intention to say more about them, since to one who is wise, observant, and understanding by means of the aforesaid, nothing therein is obscure. And because afterward you can say that the fetid menstrual is at your command, which is a vile thing, through which all bodies are briefly converted into their first nature, and it is the pure and proper origin of a marvelous and most useful thing, but you must know how to recognize it with a clear intellect. Son, this menstrual, made elemental, is one organized and watery body, through a high virtue, which is the fourth element, that is, hot fire, which

--- Page 371 (page 177, Practica Cap. IX cont.) ---
PRACTICA.

mixes all things uniformly, to which mixture man cannot attain, unless he is a master of fire, who from three things has made one through his reduction, as nature wills, which mixes this uniformly with the moisture, which is the principle of nature's greater operation. Question: Father, what is the third? You did not mention it. Son, form cannot stand without matter, nor can any thing be made if form is lacking, nor can D. exist without B., which has the power to form the things that are in the world, that is, quicksilver, and hence it has its name, as was said in the theorica. So we say of C. that it is formed from B., because A. so wills it, on account of which it is proven that there is more of B. there than of C. or of D. And this is the reason why we call it by its proper name "menstrual," and this is because in simple unity it placed it with great power, which it gained from three proper substances conjoined into one, which is within, common to any body and likewise to any stone, and is subalternated to the vegetable kingdom, as was handed down to you in the property-book of our present art. Wherefore we tell you to well recognize its virtue, and its potency: for

On the first solvent part through the form of practice. And on liquefaction.

CAP. X.

Son, the light of the world, or part of it, you must now dissolve through liquefaction of all its parts, which are substantial and accidental, to be preserved in the trituration made by the motion of fire against nature, whence the foolish deceivers are ignorant of its measure, and therefore you shall do it in this manner, without touching the material with feet or hands. You, in the virtue of A., take one ounce of F. well purified through the cupel-

cupel, or through a strong cinerarium. And cut that, well beaten, into small discs, into small, short, and narrow pieces. Then divide it into two equal parts, and take two glass vessels, called solutories: but more properly they are called and named liquefactories, whose form and measure will be shown to you in this regimen in the chapter on vessels, and in each of these two put one and a half ounces of E., namely, the eighth part of the whole menstrual, and cast in immediately each part of F. and without delay carefully seal the mouth of the vessel with its cover, and seal well the joint with the lute already mentioned, or with clear and clean wax, which being done, place in a hot balneum Mariae for three days, and may God grant you a good day.

On the liquefaction of G., which is the Mercury of the philosophers.

CAP. XI.

Son, take one ounce of G. and place it in a glass flask with a long neck, in which you have previously put three ounces of E., and immediately seal it, so that nothing can escape, with its cover with common wax, and seal well, and

--- Page 374 (page 178 verso, Practica Cap. XI cont. / Cap. XII) ---
RAYMVNDI LVLLI

place in a hot balneum Mariae for two natural days, and after two days you will find it dissolved in clear water.

On the conjunction and marriage of the said liquefactions.

CAP. XII.

Then take F. and strain the water into another clean glass flask, and incline the vessel very well and carefully, lest the earth of F. be able to strain through with the water, nor let the water become turbid, and seal the said flask, where you have placed the water of F., as was said above concerning the others, and set it aside. Then immediately upon the solutory of F. which must be dissolved, place its alembic, which shall be joined strictly and wisely with the lute described above, after this let it be situated upon sifted ashes, and placed thereupon on the small furnace, and make the first fire, and cause it to distill, and collect the moisture in a glass flask, and make the fire from sawdust of wood, and when the moisture has been almost entirely distilled, strengthen the fire with coals gradually, according as it appears need-

--- Page 375 (page 179, Practica Cap. XII cont.) ---
PRACTICA.

ful for precisely calcining the earth, but guard it from excessive heat, for we have seen that it is done by the heat of the sun. Understand well, unless you wish to become a fool, and give this heat continuously for eleven hours, then seal the small furnace, and go to sleep, and in the morning take your calcinatory (because it is so named on account of the operation of its property, which the material action offers to you) and within cast the oft-mentioned menstrual, in which G. was dissolved, and you will see it work, and the furnace rise up high, and place the calcined metal in liquefaction, but seal it better than before with its own cover, which goes inside, and take care not to place it in any other heat, until it has worked by its own proper virtue, and after it has worked and come to rest, seal well the joint with common wax, and thus sealed place it in a hot balneum Mariae for three natural days, as you have done on other occasions, because it is expedient to do so, then hide the water and distill the moisture, and calcine the earth, as was said above, and repeat so many times that all the earth is dissolved through this regimen in the form of a liquid, and always set the liquid aside, or the oil dissolved by art, which is the substance of the body purified

--- Page 376 (page 179 verso, Practica Cap. XII cont. / Cap. XIII / Cap. XIIII) ---
RAYMVNDI LVLLI

through water, and carried in the belly of the wind. And when all will have been dissolved, conjoin them together, then place it for a month and a half for putrefaction in temperate heat, where the vessel is optimally sealed with its said cover, and thus the figure of I. must be made.

On the conjunction of the red.

CAP XIII.

After we have spoken of the white, we shall now speak of the red. Son, the body of the sun is wholly essential with respect to silver. Therefore you must be advised how you should transmute it, through what, and with what: for all its operations agree in the essence of one operation, if you know how to consider that which we principally say philosophically concerning the corruptible water, which by its great virtue liquefies and melts the sun and carries it upward into the air, which is our secret, and that of every other philosophy.

On the corruptible water.

CAP. XIIII.

You

--- Page 377 (page 180, Practica Cap. XIV cont. / Cap. XV) ---
PRACTICA.

You, in the virtue of A, take one ounce of water of composite silver distilled through the alembic, and into it cast one dram of vegetable G, then place inside some fine gold, according to the weight of G, then place it in the balneum Mariae for three or four days. And within that period you will find it black as charcoal; then place within twelve parts of E, afterwards put it to putrefaction for one complete month.

On another corruptible water.

CAP. XV.

Take two drams of vegetable G, and extract its moisture through the alembic with two or three ounces of simply rectified water, which is living water, and cast into it your sun which you wish to dissolve. This dissolution is under the preservation of its form. Therefore the wise Physicians make from it a potable water, separating through the alembic, then within they cast as much juice or sap of lunaria as they wish, and they see that the gold is entirely dissolved in that vegetable water into the color of the sun, and thus from three things we form a circular figure, signified by K.

--- Page 378 (page 180v, Practica Cap. XVI) ---
RAYMVNDI LVLLI

On the second part of the solution, which is the solution and separation of the elements.

CAP. XVI.

Son, when your white composite has been putrefied for a month and a half, then it will be well animated, and of great virtue and well esteemed, and from it you shall separate the elements by the method that follows. You shall remove from the vessel, in which your matter is, the cover, and place above it the alembic, seal it with lute most carefully, and place it in a hot balneum Mariae, as best and most discreetly you know how, and as will be shown through our teaching. And first you shall receive the water that will come out through distillation by a gentle fire equally continued, until nothing more can be distilled; then cease from the fire and allow it to cool. Afterwards take the vessel and place it upon ashes in an aludel, and gradually strengthening the fire in the furnace, distill all the air mixed with its fire, and receive it in a glass phial, which seal well, as has been said, and set aside. That which remains at the bottom of the cucur-

--- Page 379 (page 181, Practica Cap. XVI cont.) ---
PRACTICA.

cucurbit, burnt and scorched, will be like dry and black earth. Father, why is the distillation of water done in the hot and moist, while air is distilled in the dry and hot? Son, the reason is that the substance of water shows itself to be of a subtle nature, wherefore it is necessary to extract that water through a moist substance and a very subtle heat, since artificially there could not be a more subtle separation than the balneum Mariae, in all that has been seen, because water standing in its own nature does not endure ignition, and therefore it is necessary that it be separated with fire corrupted through a medium. But air and fire, which endure ignition, are distilled over ashes, because the gross parts and terrestrial heats are separated, and ascend upward to the air. Thus you shall divide and separate the three elements, which pertain to the white, signified by L, M, and N, because for the white there is no fire, but for the red there are all four elements signified by O, P, Q, R.

How the Artist ought to have the fixed earth.

--- Page 380 (page 181v, Practica Cap. XVII) ---
RAYMVNDI LVLLI

CAP. XVII.

Son, after this separation you will have the fixed earth, and nothing further can be sublimated from it, and this after the rectification of the four elements, through which the second regimen is established, which is one of the four principal regimens. In this separation you must strive to overcome the Mercurial property in your mixture. For if you do this, you will be the master of victorious nature. And understand this matter in the aforementioned first mixture, as we told you in our Theorica. Therefore it is necessary for you that the Mercurial properties overcome the properties of the fetid menstrual, if you wish to be the discoverer of the precious perfection of nature. Son, by reason of this word "properties," all things have been given to you for knowing, namely the virtues of materials, and as was declared in the proprietarium of this art. Therefore, because any property, although it is only one common to all minerals, nevertheless receives its determination from its cause, just as visible experience with the clear lumi-

[MARGINAL NOTE: handwritten annotations visible in left margin: "inquire / quis liber / sit propri= / etarius. / aut prophe= / tarius"]

--- Page 381 (page 182, Practica Cap. XVII cont.) ---
PRACTICA.

light clearly and effectually shows us after its division into four elements, and as you can manifestly see in a similar way, because the property that is in the part of earth dries greatly, congeals and fixes. And that which is in water cools greatly, dissolves and washes. And that which is in air moistens, wets, softens, and causes the disconnected parts to flow, and that which is in fire heats and tinges. Furthermore this property receives another determination according to the matter of its element in grossness and subtlety, through variety and variation, on account of which there are four principal fires to be observed with respect to the substance of the properties of the four elements. Therefore, the property that is in earth is of a grosser substance and of a more fixed nature, through which it has a greater tolerance against fire. Therefore a fire proper to it is owed to it, according to the requirement of its nature, and so it is with the other elements. Wherefore it is evident that under the preservation of its property, matter is governed through certain degrees of exciting and stimulating heat. Therefore, when you separate the elements, take care that the matter is not evacuated of its property through too much fire,

--- Page 382 (page 182v, Practica Cap. XVII cont.) ---
RAYMVNDI LVLLI

or excessive fire, nor against nature. For those elements are still disagreeing and mortal enemies of generation, unless the rule of Geometry is observed, with which you will be able to draw through your will the air, and without this rule you cannot. Son, here is the secret that is worth more than all money, for drawing out the nature that is the true Mercury full of property. Son, we have told you the practice, how you can extract it with an appropriate fire. But now we shall tell you our entire instrument, through which you will be able to guard, and rightly lead, and direct the work. When you wish to draw the air from its own proper lodging, it is necessary for you to assimilate your work to nature. Son, in the subject of emptiness there are extremes and middles according to more and less, on account of which one element flees into another, and returns into it, to conquer nature by the necessity of nature, as we shall manifestly exemplify for you through water, in which is dissolved just as much salt as can be continued in the watery moisture with respect to the airiness of the salt. Wherefore we show you that the moist is porous and full of openings, in which porosity the salt is continued, which is a natural stone in the water,

--- Page 383 (page 183, Practica Cap. XVII cont.) ---
PRACTICA.

in which it is carried, because it is assimilated to a stone existing in its seat. Son, its seats are the pores and small openings of its waters, which at first were full of excessively subtle air, and afterwards are filled with a thing that thickens itself. Wherefore it is evident that the more it thickens, the more it flees rarefaction and goes toward density, when the pores and small openings are filled with gross matter, through the evacuation of the simple, because at no time is a vacuum given in nature, for it would rather be destroyed, because it could suffer that. And from this we shall show an example: the Physicians in the science of cupping vessels, and we in the extractions of natural airs. Whence certain of the ancient philosophers wished to say that nature was empty of all entity, when it attracts a thing to itself from its own proper degree, but they do not have the entire depth of nature. Therefore, because although the heat of fire consumes the moist matter of the air and renders it porous, yet the said matter still has the said place full, notwithstanding that it attracts according to the proportion of the natural void from the said place more than it has lost, as appears in those convalescing after great illnesses, whose nature desires more

--- Page 384 (page 183v, Practica Cap. XVII cont.) ---
RAYMVNDI LVLLI

than it can digest, and yet that place properly according to truth is never left empty of all the entities of nature. But only of its matter that is more simple, which participates there according to our science of sensibility between the simple and the most simple, which are the more simple extremities, just as is air, in which we breathe, with respect to its two extremities, namely of simple elemental water and of pure most simple air, which by the learned is called aether, because it properly exists next to fire. And if this were true, no element would have a true sphere of animation and station, and especially that air which is called aether, which cannot be destroyed by fire, nor corrupted by its heat, which is always near to it and joined with it. Therefore it has been declared that emptiness has never been given to nature, for there is no place from the height to the base of the spheres, because fire has its place there, just as its nature is desiring perfection. Otherwise it would be corrupted, and the fire of nature in animated things would remain, which is a certain sphere of one element or another, and nevertheless without virtue and without attraction, and consequently would remain empty of its end, which never was nor ever will be requi-

--- Page 385 (page 184, Practica Cap. XVII cont.) ---
PRACTICA.

required by nature, that it remain without those potencies, wherefore having laid waste the simpler matter, namely, of air, which was moist in this sphere, which was given to it and properly owed to it through the medium of heaviness and lightness, then at all times in every place there remains another simple thing, which fire can neither destroy nor lay waste. But as for this most simple matter, since it is sufficient, or sufficiently occupying, or filling the place of the said sphere, according to its substance, therefore nature, wishing to recover in the said place through its instinct and institution for the restoration of its lost matter with a similar thing or one equal to it, always desiring to attain its perfection, recovers it in an attractive virtue, with great appetite drawing to itself the substance of the other elements through their rarefaction, until nature has recovered what is owed to it, namely, that which it had lost through the heat of fire, and the more the place is emptied, so much the more does the nature of those elements attract through its great appetite, and it has a greater force of greater attraction, as we have told you, just as appears in the con-

--- Page 386 (page 184 verso, Practica Cap. XVII cont.) ---

condensation of air and rains. And as is seen, when the vital nature, strengthening in restoration, rectifies a wounded member, which is one of the parts of its true individual substance, in which it rejoices, and this is the radical reason and power of nature, without whose intention you can do nothing, because through it the elements ascend and descend, are thickened and rarefied, compose and associate, unbind, and subtilize by the force of love. And the simpler fire is with great action in the said place of nature, the stronger is the said containing place in the loss of its own matter, and consequently of stronger attraction. Son, if you understand this matter well, you will know how all things in the world are made through nature, and how you will be able to make them with respect to nature, if you can have the air which causes this through nature. But the rational animation of the quintessence is nothing that any thing knows how to make, unless God creates it. Therefore we have certain things, and many other sciences, for many arts and sciences, of which there is one that is the form for knowing all the others, caused by a subtler subject through nature: for that which nature makes, we can destroy,

and afterwards reform, placing nature below, without which no cure can be made by the physician. And let this be given to you for dividing the four elements, which is the greatest secret, for forming the instrument. And as you did with the white composite, so do with the red, for which reason look at the following figure, which teaches how the elements are elemented, and how one element enters into another circularly through natural artifice. The following figure shows how the elements through artifice constitute one round element: so that the smallest part of one is in the smallest part of another, otherwise there would be neither a true nor a good mixture in determinate bodies, nor consequently would it be good for working gold or silver.

On the operation of the second regimen, which is to wash. And first on water and air, which must generate.

CAP. XVIII.

--- Page 388 (page 185 verso, Practica Cap. XVIII) ---
RAYMVNDI LVLLI

Son, when you have divided the elements of the stone, or the stone through the four elements, it is necessary that you purge them through this regimen. But first it must be known, the substance of the four elements and how to recognize it, whence we tell you that earth and fire are similar in stony substance, and therefore the preparation of calcining fire is necessary. And the other two, namely water and air, are in watery substance and aquatic nature. And therefore it is necessary that you know the preparation ordained according to the requirement of its nature, because air and water need a preparation that is through seven distillations, or more, until they are emptied of all burning, which comes from the part of the menstrual, and then each of them, full of true tincture, will remain without burning or scorching. Son, you shall distill the water and the air to the side, each in its own rectification and in its own vessel, and the dregs of the water you shall place with the earth, which you shall make in each distillation. And after the sixth distillation, place one or two drops upon a hot plate of fine and pure silver, and if it blackens, it is not emptied of all its burning. Therefore place it for a seventh distillation and more, until it leaves the silver without black corruption,

--- Page 389 (page 186, Practica Cap. XVIII cont.) ---
PRACTICA.

and then you will have the water of life, with which you shall wash the earth, and the philosophical Mercury, strengthening and mediating, which makes the marriage of tinctures. And as you did with the water of the moon, so do with the water of the sun, and as you have heard concerning the water, so also understand concerning the air, and that which will remain after the distillation of the air will be fire, full of tincture entirely, and gold and soul, and the ointment of the philosophers, without which the said magisterium cannot be completed. And understand, son, why we say that fire is distilled with air and not with water. Son, water does not have the office of air to perform, but only to wash and not to tinge. For if it tinged, fire would remain without moisture. Because water flees fire, nor would it make a fusible tincture for lack of moisture, therefore we tell you that you should distill fire through air, because in quality they agree, and water flees fire because it is clothed with a quality contrary to fire. Through this, however, they disagree. But air, because it is water tinged by great digestion, is oil, and also a strong tincture, and of great mixture. And fire is its tincture, and a body strongly digested. And the spirit is air, which will lead fire. Consider, son, those things which we

--- Page 390 (page 186 verso, Practica Cap. XVIII cont. / Cap. XIX) ---
RAYMVNDI LVLLI

have told you and shall tell you, namely, that you wish to work the moist stone, which is a muddy substance tending toward water and earth, because from those two pure sulfur will be procreated, and the dry sulfur you wish to work toward fire and air, since it is the tincture and perfection of the entire magisterium. Son, if you mix the fire of the stone with Mercury, immediately without separation it will become red. And therefore when you wish to make a permanent tincture, redden your sulfur in the fire of the stone, and at all times they will love each other.

On the washing of fire and earth.

CAP XIX.

Son, when you wish to wash fire, first you shall separate the air, in which it rests, through distillation, and all the dregs, which are of a color between black and red, you shall place together, into which you shall put their weight of the first water, and afterwards you shall distill the whole, until nothing remains but dry fire, and this you shall repeat seven times, and you will find your red in a subtle powder. Son, do the same with earth, until it is empty of all moisture, and the moisture that you have separated from the earth is white oil, very

--- Page 391 (page 187, Practica Cap. XIX cont. / Cap. XX) ---
PRACTICA.

very precious for the waxing, and because it participates more in the nature of the body, it is more difficultly separated from it, and consequently it is held to be more precious for completing every elixir. And when you see the earth to be between heavy and light in the manner of a powder, place a little of it upon a hot copper plate, and see whether anything evaporates from it, and if not, this shows that it is deprived and cleansed of all its spirits, which held it in life. Guard it very carefully: for it is the magnesia, which will give you what you seek after its resurrection, and when, as we have said, you have purged the four elements, guard them well, each apart in its own glass vessel well sealed with wax, and especially and particularly the oil: for air entering would immediately corrupt and consume it on account of the nearness of nature which is in it, which indeed is airy and very volatile, and let what we have said about washing well all four elements suffice for you.

On the third operation, which is to create the stone from the above-said pre-

--- Page 392 (page 187 verso, Practica Cap. XX) ---
