prepared substance, from the four elements.

CAP. XX.

Son, the third operation of our stone is that you reduce the air, which is signified by M. after its rectification with O. which is reduced with L. because those two are two dry elements of a fixed nature. Therefore add O. from the red composite with L. which is from the white composite, so that the mixture, which is through strengthening, may be a more useful preparation of O. and L. so that they may receive and recover greater moisture through attraction, which they had lost in their calcination, through which it was deprived of all moisture. And therefore when it is dry, and empty of all its moisture, it especially most gladly drinks its watery moisture. And if you understand us, you will understand that which we said in our theorica in the sixty-third chapter, which begins, The intention of nature, son. And upon that which we said concerning perfect preparation, which succeeds the purification of the aforesaid elements. Son, this preparation is worth all gold: for through it stones are fine and precious, and all pearls and gems. Therefore, son, to achieve the said preparation, take M. to achieve

--- Page 393 (page 188, Practica Cap. XX cont.) ---
PRACTICA.

its nature, and its effect, and you shall divide it into twelve parts by weight of O. and L. since more than half of M. is necessary for you to keep, and the other part shall be equal by weight of O. and M. divided into twelve parts, and you shall place each part with it, and with L. everything mixed together within a vessel similar to the others. But we call that by this regimen Ymon. Afterwards you shall seal your vessel with its glass cover with wax, and place it in the furnace, and there you shall cause it to be nourished by feeding with a little water first, and afterwards with more, as nature teaches you in the rearing of small children. And be not slow, nor lazy in imbibing the earth every fifteen days, according to what you will see, that it eats and converts, nor let this work weary you to repeat it many times, and when you find that it is congealed and converted, and dry, place it in a dry fire for one natural day. Afterwards give it more M. and do everything as we have told you: for in this work you cannot err, provided you have patience in the long and tedious nourishment. Son, you will see there so many and such great colors, of which you should take no care until the matter is led and brought to fine white powders,

--- Page 394 (page 188 verso, Practica Cap. XX cont. / Cap. XXI) ---
RAYMVNDI LVLLI

or into a white foliated earth in the color of translucent pearls. And if you see it in the form of the finest white powders, know that it is of greater strength, and of greater mineral sulfurous virtue for congealing Mercury, and transmuting all metallic bodies after their elixation. Son, we do not wish to make you a long speech here, lest your understanding be impeded by words. For our theorica can suffice for you well enough, which you can well convert into true practice through this, which we give you. This is the sought-after thing, the congealable foliated earth, our arsenic, our sulfur, which is completed to whiteness for making the medicine for silver, as we shall tell you, since it is now the complete white sulfur, non-burning, through which the medicine must be made.

De compositione sulfuris rubei.

CAP. XXI.

Son, you shall take the white sulfur, and you shall imbibe it by a rorating imbibition of red water after its dissolution in the said water. And when the water has been con-

--- Page 395 (page 189, Practica Cap. XXI cont.) ---
PRACTICA.

gealed in it to half its weight, all will be white, then strengthen the fire of coals: for nothing else need be done, until you see it red like scarlet, afterwards strengthen the fire with flame, until it sublimes. Son, know that what will be sublimed will be white sulfur, very noble, and that which will remain fixed in the bottom of the vessel will be red sulfur, fixed like blood, red, very bright and most precious, and let it be shown to you here that what was white has been turned into manifest red through ignition and the ignitibility of the water, which was placed therein through the substance of the white sulfur essentially. And of this let the substance of lead be a mirror to you, and for so much let the tradition of the composition of white and red sulfur suffice for you, from which through the artifice of the fourth operation the elixir has been made by its benefit to complete and transmute all imperfect and diminished metallic bodies into true gold, and into true silver.

On the fourth operation, through which the composition of the elixir is made. And first on the com-

--- Page 396 (page 189 verso, Practica Cap. XXII) ---
RAYMVNDI LVLLI

position of the stone, which is the first sulfur.

CAP. XXII.

Son, when you wish to make the medicine, consider first to which perfection of preparation you propose to lead it, and upon what kind of body you wish to make the projection: for from that body upon which you wish to make the projection of the medicine, you must place some in it, but if you intend to lead it to the ultimate preparation philosophically, it is not to be cared what kind of body you add, but only quicksilver, which is the medium and mediator of all things, and if you do not intend but to lead the medicine to a certain simplicity of subtiliation and foliage, you must subtilize the body upon which you wish to make the projection. Let us suppose, therefore, that it is upon tin, then you shall compose your elixir thus: take two and a half parts of the finest silver, most subtly filed, and from it make an amalgam with five parts of common quicksilver, joining them together, and let it be led and formed in the manner of butter, so that the parts of the said silver cannot be felt. Then take half a part

--- Page 397 (page 190, Practica Cap. XXII cont.) ---
PRACTICA.

of Jupiter, and melt it three times, and cast it into distilled vinegar, and amalgamate, and feed with one part of quicksilver, grinding together, until they become one body. Afterwards wash those two amalgams, each apart, with common salt prepared and dissolved in distilled vinegar, until the vinegar comes out clear, pure and clean, then join the two amalgams, and again wash them together with salt and distilled vinegar, grinding, and mixing, and washing, until the vinegar remains clear as above. Afterwards wash the whole in sweet water, so that all the salt comes out, and dry the water in the sun, or at a gentle fire. Son, our magisterium is divided into two parts, namely, in creating the stone, and composing the medicine. In the first part we place quicksilver, such as we said in all our theorica, and placed in the beginning of our magisterium in the practica. In the second part sulfur must be added with the matter of metal. Son, sulfur is its fire, which digests the matter of the metal, and quicksilver, which is named there in the place of a medium, and stands between sulfur and body. And this we tell you, so that you may not understand in the rustic word, nor in the common

--- Page 398 (page 190 verso, Practica Cap. XXII cont.) ---
RAYMVNDI LVLLI

common, but in the philosophical word we shall tell you, that there are other media with differential extremes, because in no other way could we say when the matters have been transmuted philosophically. And therefore, when we speak philosophically, you must understand concerning matters already transmuted through the virtue and work of philosophy. And when we speak rustically, you must understand concerning matters which common nature created for all peoples, taking the similitude from it. Therefore in the philosophical word we tell you, that there are other media without which the tinctures of ferments can neither be added nor mixed with the tinctures of the red ointment. And therefore it is necessary for us that we pass from the same medium to a medium, until we are at the extremes which we seek. Son, the nourishment which we gave you, philosophically speaking, is one of the media between water and spirit, namely, when it has been turned into powder through alteration. And sulfur and air are the extremities: but without the will of philosophy we could tell you, since there is alteration there, that quicksilver, understood vulgarly, by reason of its similitude, is the perfective medium, multiplicative,

--- Page 399 (page 191, Practica Cap. XXII cont.) ---
PRACTICA.

and completive of the elixir, because it is the effecter of all metallic fusion, and of all ingression, by reason of its weight, of which we were speaking above at the will of our philosophy in its first extremity, which it elevated of necessity, and altered in its special difference. The second extremity, without the will of philosophy, we say are the metals for composing the elixir. And the other media and extremities, philosophically speaking, are the matters after their purification, clean, therefore, because between sulfur and quicksilver there is a powder made from body and quicksilver, and between the said powder, in which is sulfur and the end of the magisterium, there are two quicksilvers, which are the cause of the continuity of the parts of the said powder into a fine medicine, and between the water there is calx and air. Son, the entire beginning and end of this work is only through one single operation, which we properly call reduction. Son, understand well the power of nature, which must change everything. Our sulfur is a subtle earth, or dry water, in whose belly the fire of nature has been strongly multiplied, the cause of its dryness is the termination of hot moisture, which it received from the compression

--- Page 400 (page 191 verso, Practica Cap. XXII cont.) ---
RAYMVNDI LVLLI

of fire, which is in its viscera and belly. Son, quicksilver is an overflowing and flowing liquor, preserving fire from combustion and burning, and air is the matter of sulfur, in which fire is compromised and compressed through the ingenuity of the magisterium with respect to nature, until it is terminated into a dry and subtle earth, which is sulfur, and just as air, which is the matter of fire, puts on its form in it, so quicksilver is the matter of sulfur, and is converted into it by reason of the property of its nature. Wherefore, son, do not marvel when you shall see things changed before your eyes through the potency of sulfur, because it is very hot and dry, and calorific. Therefore it is necessary that it have the property of fire, namely of opening the parts of every body, and of quicksilver, and of penetrating and terminating, because through the heat which it has in itself, it naturally opens the closed and locked pores. And since fire is substantially bound and collected in a subtle substance, with all that it also enters vaporously within the whole body, since its substance, through which it is bound and collected, is very dry, it terminates and congeals the moisture into which it has entered, according to its heat's

--- Page 401 (page 192, Practica Cap. XXII cont.) ---
PRACTICA.

of color's formation, which is the formative and digestive instrument of nature. And thus, son, our said sulfur has the virtue of sealing and forming what is similar to itself, and everything that is sought, and of not retaining it in itself. And therefore we say that it is the father and the seed of the male, through ignorance of which many foolish and inexperienced Alchemists have fallen, believing they could fix amalgams without the fire of nature, not keeping the right way, nor the rightness of the proper thing, which should have led and brought them to the perfection of true digestion. For mineral quicksilver is a very crude thing, and of gross substance. Wherefore it is necessary that a strong virtue be administered to it, which may help it to be led to perfection, and of this we have spoken at length in the booklet on the intention of Alchemists. Therefore, son, in the name of that Lord of ours, who suffered a cruel death for sinners, begin to make our philosophical alterations in this manner, first making the sign of the holy cross, lest the Devil harm you, and understand that when we say ar. vi. vulgare, we say this by similitude to that which is made like it, vivified through art: for me-

--- Page 402 (page 192 verso, Practica Cap. XXII cont.) ---
RAYMVNDI LVLLI

tallic bodies receive differentiation in that manner by which quicksilver differs from its similars. And when we say similars, this is to distinguish pure from impure metals, and let this be given to you, son, as a weighty and profound rule to aid you in composing the royal medicine in this manner. Take a glass vessel with a long neck of one palm's length, with a round bottom and a narrow mouth, such that only your middle finger can enter it widely, made in the manner of an ampoule's neck, and let it have a cover that fits under and joins well. Then take the amalgam, and in six parts of it place one part of our stone at the bottom of our said vessel. Then skillfully place your amalgam within, without violence, along the length of the vessel, and let it cover the stone entirely, in such a way that the stone cannot breathe when it feels the heat, except through the medium of the amalgam: for it is a necessary thing that the amalgam receive the vapor of our stone, and retain it in its belly, and be altered through it. Afterwards place your vessel, covered with its cover, in the furnace, among compressed ashes up to the quantity of the material,

--- Page 403 (page 193, Practica Cap. XXIII cont.) ---
PRACTICA.

or slightly less, namely, by the thickness of a knife's back, or less, and do this so that you may be able to see the material being altered, and afterwards light the fire, if you wish, with powdered charcoal, or with wood shavings. And thus let it stand, so that it may begin to be heated gradually, or if you wish, with coals that make fire without smoke. And when it feels the heat, then the heat of the sulfur will begin to move, and that which is in its vapor will breathe into the substance of the quicksilver, and will penetrate, and will congeal the amalgam. First into the form of fine silver: let the fire continue still, because the metallic species will show you that all the sulfur has not yet been raised in vapor, while it has not yet rendered all its effects, nor its virtues, nor have they yet entered the belly of the said amalgam, since you should not be of the intention to create a piece of metal through the said sulfur, in which metal there is little virtue, but to create a medicine, in whose substance many and great virtues are gathered, through which and in which you will afterwards do such things as are considered miraculous by every great and wealthy person, unless he be a philosopher who recognizes its powers and virtues, through which

--- Page 404 (page 193 verso, Practica Cap. XXII cont.) ---
RAYMVNDI LVLLI

it operates, and which we shall tell you, so long as you keep them secret. Son, the effect of the sulfur which it must deliver and render to the said amalgam is that, by reason of the power of its property, it converts it into the finest powders. Therefore permit it to continue in your fire: for you need do nothing else, until you see the said amalgam converted into powder on its surface. And let all this take place in four, or six hours, or a little more or less, according to how well and discreetly the fire has been continued, with respect to the property of the material: but as soon as you see the sign of the said powder, remove the fire from the furnace, and permit it to cool, so that the virtue of our fire may not be weakened. Son, if a little of this sulfur be projected by such an infusion, as we have told you above, upon much of the body, in such a way that it has power over it, it will immediately reduce it to a very subtle powder, whose color will be such as the color of the body in which the whole spirit has been infused by divine virtue. Therefore, son, have a good understanding of multiplying the said material by the virtue of the first sulfur, before you make the medicine.

De multiplicatione nostri secundi

--- Page 405 (page 194, Practica Cap. XXIII) ---
PRACTICA.

De multiplicatione nostri secundi sulfuris, & proprietatibus eius.

CAP. XXIII.

Son, you must not be ignorant of the powers of all your sulfurs, whose powers are found in two ways, according to the number of degrees of their virtues, namely, through signs and experiments, which ratify by clear light the reason of the senses. Son, the virtues are linked and gathered together punctually and gradually through augmentation and strengthening in the substance of our sulfur. Therefore we tell you that the more punctual degrees it has, the greater power its substance has of altering all binary and double weights, each of the ten numbers. And it does this on account of the property of the said virtue, which is in its form, through which the matter has been specified in the substance of sulfur, and if you add more, the succeeding substance will receive greater virtue, on account of the plurality of virtues taken and

--- Page 406 (page 194 verso, Practica Cap. XXIII cont.) ---
RAYMVNDI LVLLI

collected in the preceding substance. Let us pose an example: that the first sulfur in itself has only fifteen punctual degrees in the number of virtues, for which reason experimental force has taught us that sulfur which has only one degree of virtue, the weight of the simple substance being reserved, alters two whole weights, which can be divided into 20 numbers, which are called and named simple weights. We number simple weights 1, 2, 3, 4, 5, and 6, 7, 8, 9 we call simple composite numbers, and 10 we call the whole number, because it receives relation from one, and therefore the gradual virtue of our sulfur corresponds to 10, and so for the others. And therefore understand that through the difference of weights of convertible materials, and through the intention and remission of the virtue of the sulfur, the virtue specifies the said material into a diverse form, as has been declared in the treatise on the degrees of the great medicine. Therefore, son, since one whole virtue punctually congeals 10 of quicksilver into perfect metal by the projection of one weight upon 10 substantially, consequently one weight of 30 virtues con-

--- Page 407 (page 195, Practica Cap. XXIII cont.) ---
PRACTICA.

geals 500 of quicksilver proportionally into perfect metal, because at no time does the virtue receive diminishment on account of the diminishing of its act, without operating according to the proportion of the degree of its action, just as the degree of hot pepper in the fourth degree sensibly operates in human nature, and also its part, on account of the virtue which stands uniformly in the whole and in the entire substance of the pepper, and therefore the part through degrees is likewise in the same degree. For as much as the substance and virtue can continue, so much does it show the proper effect of its operation, which depends on its graduality. And if the weight of one or another be projected upon less than its proportional weight, then it will produce in that metal a greater combustion or ignition, and so much beyond, as the igneity and virtue of its power is extracted by digesting, and beyond by burning and consuming. And insofar as it will be beyond the proportion of the convertible weight, so much more and better will the effect of perfect alteration follow, if the weight of the converting agent be not augmented proportionally to it, as much as the convertible has been augmented in substance beyond the proportion of the right weight.

--- Page 408 (page 195 verso, Practica Cap. XXIII cont.) ---
RAYMVNDI LVLLI

Son, through this intention, if you understand us well, you can have the true weight of the virtues of our stone, until it shows you the power of simple ignition that does not burn, and this is the limit and measure, that is, when it congeals quicksilver into perfect metal, and leaves it in the specific species of gold or silver. And when it leaves it in the special form of sulfur, that is, of a subtle powder, then it shows that it is of great ignition, nor on this account should you esteem it less, but honor it more strongly, since it can be brought to the limit of simple ignition, through the discretion of the operator wise in the doctrine which the art above confers in the multiplicative substance. Therefore many virtues collected in one small subject extend themselves and are enlarged into much and great material terminating it, and bringing it to some degree of perfection, according to the action of the perfective power, and the administration of the weights applied. Afterwards, son, for better understanding part of those things which we have said through visible experience, take one part of the stone, and administer to it ten parts of quicksilver, amalgamated or simple, and see how it will be terminated after its transmutation, whether it into

--- Page 409 (page 196, PRACTICA) ---
PRACTICA.

in the specific form of silver, you can judge what virtue it has. And if it does not congeal perfectly in six, it is of lesser virtue, and then it requires that the weight of the convertible be diminished while increasing the weight of the converting agent. But if it leaves the quicksilver in the form of white earth in one mass, its significative virtue gives you to know that it is of stronger action than it was in the beginning, and therefore of greater value: for the more it holds beyond the specification of the form of perfect metal, the more it can act against other quicksilver, on account of the potency of the virtue of the stone diffused within it. And if it leaves it in the form of a powder, then it shows a sign that it is fortified with the potency of a noble and strong virtue. Take therefore one weight of that powder, and bring it near to ten weights of quicksilver as we have said above. And if it transmutes them into similar powders, it should be judged to be of excellent virtue. Continue therefore, son, this operation, until you arrive at that degree where it ultimately changes not only quicksilver into powder, or perfect metal, but solely into white earth in the manner of a frangible mass. Son, this is

--- Page 410 (page 196 verso, PRACTICA) ---
RAYMVNDI LVLLI

the quicksilver that has been changed into the form of perfect metal, and has the virtue of simple ignition to the equality of its whole number, but when this virtue with respect to the said number in the composition of our medicine does not suffice, since it does not extend itself proportionally except to the termination of the said quicksilver into the form of metal, and not to the termination of its elements, which must be terminated into a medicine, unless this were done through another weight, and through subtle ingenuity in long decoction. Therefore you must not bring it to an end, lest you err, unless you are very skilled in science and art, nor yet in the form of powder, because the ignition would be excessive, and it would burn and consume the simple elements. And when we say simple, this is to distinguish them from the fermentable substance, which we place in the elixir, nor could we show you its temperament: but that which is changed entirely into the form of earth in a frangible mass, that is the temperate medium of simple artificial ignition, for creating a medicine directly, and the preservative of all its elements. Temper therefore your sulfur, until it arrives at the state

--- Page 411 (page 197, PRACTICA) ---
PRACTICA.

of earth, which we call a frangible mass. Son, of however many whole numbers it successively converts, so many whole virtues it will have. When therefore you wish to test its virtues, do not make the experiment with the whole stone, but with the smallest part you can, and set the rest aside, until you have seen and known how many virtues and how great powers it holds. And when you know the powers of its virtues, add to it and give it food according to the proportion of its strength. And because its virtue is of ten weights, you must add to it and administer proportionally 100 weights of quicksilver, and if 15, add 150, and if 100, then above 1000. Now therefore put it into actual practice, after you have known how many whole virtues the stone holds in its smaller part. When we say smaller, this is for the sake of a middle way of speaking, and thus you should understand: if you wish to multiply the stone materially, even if you know with certainty that it holds in itself the power of 50 whole virtues, you shall take the whole stone, and place it in 450 weights of ar. vi. [quicksilver] amalgamated, or not amalgamated, because from each ten the tenth part is subtracted, and we make this subtraction so that the material is not terminated

--- Page 412 (page 197 verso, PRACTICA) ---
RAYMVNDI LVLLI

into perfect metal, and then its ignition would be blunted and diminished. Therefore, by however much less of the substance of quicksilver ascends, by so much more the diffused virtue, which is in its substance, is surpassing in action. And we do this more according to the principle of temperament. From this therefore, son, you shall create a medicine, as we shall say: but first we tell you about the multiplication of the virtue of the said sulfur. Son, we call sulfur the entire substance mentioned above that has been converted into earth by the first virtue of our stone. Take this therefore, and from it take one part, according to the proportion of the weight of the materials that you wish to place in the elixir. For it is necessary for you that a tenth part be of it, and place the whole remainder of the sulfur in an ampoule with a long neck, and preserve it in heat. If you wish its virtue to be multiplied further from month to month, give it of ar. vi. [quicksilver] the fiftieth part of itself in the ampoule, which you must seal firmly; nothing else is necessary for you to do. Thus you shall have in your possession, and in your home, double mines for reigning in the service of God, but know how to keep the secret. Son, we could not tell you the powers of this thing, because they are innumerable, if we could tell you

--- Page 413 (page 198, PRACTICA) ---
PRACTICA.

the term and limits of this world, since its power, or multiplication, is infinite, because its duration will be infinite. We say infinite, not simply, but to distinguish it from the life of man, and the duration of this world.

De fixatione, vel de confectione medicinae.

CAP. XXIIII.

Son, we instruct you to take two and a half parts of fine silver, and make from it, as we have told you, an amalgam in the preceding chapter on the multiplication of the stone. Afterwards add to it one part of the said sulfur, ultimately congealed, and then mix the whole together in a glass mortar by grinding, until the whole becomes one body. Afterwards moisten your elixir with one part of its water, and nourish the whole in febrile heat, as you did in the creation of our stone, continuing this decoction until the water above it is congealed by way of sublimation. Then gradually strengthen your fire, until at last by the force of the fire, everything that is volatile is sublimated.

--- Page 414 (page 198 verso, PRACTICA) ---
RAYMVNDI LVLLI

Know then that the body is made and turned into volatile by the power of the volatile, which gives beyond itself what is fixed. Afterwards, when the vessel is cold, take that which is sublimated, and with one other part of its water place it again upon the dregs, grinding, and imbuing, and roasting according to what nature requires, cook and sublimate. And thus henceforth continue the said regimen, strengthening the fire, until two parts of the water are fixed with the earth, and nothing more of it can be sublimated. Son, the regimen of all these fixations should be in such a vessel as is that in which reductions are made, nor is there a difference between fixation and reduction as to the manner of operation, except as to the intention of fixing, and not merely of hardening. And therefore you must increase your fire in fixation, so that the humid may be fixed in fire through fire, and you must always return that which will be sublimated upon the fixed matter remaining, until the whole is fixed, because the aforesaid sulfur, since it is fixed and congealed through its own property, and coagulated by itself, naturally coagulates its Mercury, and this is through frequent sublimation upon fixed matter repeated, and we do this so that the said sulfur's

--- Page 415 (page 199, PRACTICA) ---
PRACTICA.

parts may be well continuous in the said quicksilver, which is of its own natural moisture. Son, if you know well how to dispose nature from without, it will be sufficient for you and for it to go to the perfection of the whole thing of which it is in need. Because nature itself, since it is very sagacious through its instinct, is very solicitous for the natural appetite toward the inceration of its body, of which solicitude it has no end. And therefore let the motion of nature be made clear to you, which is the instrument through which it does all things. Son, if from without you know how to dispose nature well, it will sufficiently work from within, up to the last step of its perfection. Since its motions, through which it will enter and place and join the dry with the moist, and will continue the moist with the dry, adhering so strongly to it in such order, acted upon with great unity, that the best and most certain man in the world could not, nor would know how to consider, how great is the perfection of nature, by reason and cause of the adhering motions, which are the nearest instruments to its works. Wherefore, son, let it be intellectually revealed to you that by reason of completing all the aforesaid motions, the slowness of preparation must be in our mastery, so that nature may not be able to pass

--- Page 416 (page 199 verso, PRACTICA) ---
RAYMVNDI LVLLI

its motions along a transverse line, which is moved by a contrary impediment, such as completing too quickly, namely, before the motion of nature through the succession of natural order has been continued by the intention of perfecting reason. Therefore, son, we recapitulate that nature must have a certain time for thickening and impregnating, and a certain time for giving birth, a certain time for nourishing, and a certain time for operating. Therefore, son, when you have impregnated the earth, await the birth, and when it has given birth, nourish it like an infant, until it can endure the full fire of ignition. And then from it you will be able to make a very noble projection, for which purpose, to fulfill what has been said above. And Morienus says: Let the creation of man be your mirror.

De fixione aeris.

CAP. XXV.

If you wish, son, to know the end, which is the perfection of our mastery handed down by paternal teaching, this is that you cause the said non-fusible material to return to perfect liquefaction, assimilating your work to the manner of nature through reiteration, and softening, and that the discontinuous parts be conti-

--- Page 417 (page 200, Practica Cap. XXV cont.) ---
PRACTICA.

continued through such a moisture, which above all other moistures has the ability to remain, at the heat of any fire without exhalation or termination of itself, so that you may assimilate yourself, son, in this step to the work of nature, insofar as it is possible for you. We tell you that nature itself, at the root of inceration, does not cease to incerate its body with the moisture of fusible things, namely sulfur and quicksilver: because nature has found no other body more suitable than sulfur. But believe well that the whole proceeds from quicksilver, and this moisture is neither terminative nor consumptive, but rather perfective of the radical, very subtle, in which the natural heat of all fusible or liquefiable things grows warm. Wherefore nature, by reason of its instinct, greatly desires to remain in being and in its species for a long time in its perpetual individual being. Nature itself, by reason of its perfection, has ordained and established in the nourishment of natural heat such a moisture, as has been said above and is set forth below. Therefore, son, by assimilating sagacious nature, you must prepare by the way of exuberation that same or similar moisture. For just as you see that receptive nature in the cre-

--- Page 418 (page 200 verso, Practica Cap. XXV cont.) ---
RAYMVNDI LVLLI

ation of man has formed the menstrual parts, not only with sufficient nourishment for restoring what has been lost, but by reason of carrying and bringing it to greater increase in its own time. Therefore nature formed the said spongy parts, so that they might receive more of nutritive moisture and nourishment than the loss of it would amount to. And therefore, once what was lost has been restored, there will still remain in those spongy parts a great and notable quantity of nourishment, which indeed the fetus receives from the undigested substance through the umbilicus, although that which ascends above to the breasts is nobler beyond comparison, on account of the necessity of the end, namely that it may nourish the infant after childbirth until it is able to eat bread. Therefore, because every living thing requires a very well-tempered substance, for which reason limited nature has at all times been ingenious in doing this in its works, and just as in the first formation of human members a small quantity of the seminal principle suffices, in like manner a small quantity of sulfur is sufficient for creating a medicine, as has been made manifest through our preceding statements. But for growing, augmenting, and well perfec-

--- Page 419 (page 201, Practica Cap. XXV cont.) ---
PRACTICA.

tly nourishing it, it is necessary for you to know how to exuberate the said nutritive moisture, assimilating nature by reason of nourishment, because in doing this lies the mastery and the perfection of the entire high secret of this art and most noble science. And you shall do as we will tell you after the inceration of the elixir. Therefore, son, when the water has been fixed with the earth, imbibe it with a dewy imbibition with one part of its air, and afterwards place it to soften in dung, where there is a peptic and gentle heat. Therefore, with the said earth and water it will be mixed through the motion that nature makes in its grinding. Afterwards place it to sublimate, gradually increasing the fire, until that which is volatile has been sublimated, and the moist is mixed with the dry. After this, place the volatile upon the fixed with another part of air by the mode of dewy imbibition, at first with a gentle fire, and afterwards making it stronger. And thus repeat, because nothing else is necessary for you to do, until through continuous reiteration of sublimation and continual motion the whole matter is fixed in one and a half parts of air. And then for one natural day you shall continue a strong fire, and for the second natural day a stronger one, and for the third the strongest, such as

--- Page 420 (page 201 verso, Practica Cap. XXV cont.) ---
RAYMVNDI LVLLI

is the fire for melting copper. And thus by means of water, the air is fixed in earth, because the nature of earth is lightened and elevated by the nature of air, and the nature of earth contains within itself the nature of air, and water likewise, and earth retains it, because it wishes to be perfected by the hot airy nature, and it teaches the natures of water and earth to wage war against fire through their concordance of love. For they convert into love, and the fleeing into the sluggish, when the air sets out to meet the fleeing one, and the unfeathered bird holds back the feathered one, so that it cannot fly away. Son, apply your spirit to the understanding of what we say: do not drink unless you eat, nor eat unless you drink. We say this to you by reason of the uniform imbibition that you must make of the moist with the dry in successive turns, after the manner of the said creation by the example of nature, for aiding the natural heat of a body that has passed beyond the latitude of temperament or from distemperament, just as an old man, on account of the capacity of his temperament, must not proceed in this way, namely, that he should not drink unless he eats, and conversely: for the dry parts must flow in the moist and be continued in it, and be simplified for strengthening the natural heat,

--- Page 421 (page 202, Practica Cap. XXV cont. / Cap. XXVI) ---
PRACTICA.

and therefore in fixation we mix water into earth gradually, and thus it is converted, and with it is bound and fixed by the power of nature. And then the air is fixed upon it, until through the victory of the conquered the conqueror is overcome, because when the water is dead, then the air is fixed, and not before. For air nourishes fire, just as water nourishes earth, and fire lives from the sliminess of air, and air from the sliminess of water, and water from the sliminess of earth. Fix therefore, son, first the water in earth, so that the air may be fixed in it, because nature so wills. You can do this, and if you have killed the water, all other things are dead and slain. Yet the water without earth is not fixed, and therefore the philosopher speaks these verses: No fruit ever rises without a body. When the seed dies in it, it is brought forth to give fruit. Thus food in the stomach receives nourishment within.

De inceratione, quæ dat fusionem elixiri perfectam fixionem.

CAP. XXVI.

When you have fixed the water upon the earth, and the air upon the water successively, as we have told you, you shall take of

--- Page 422 (page 202 verso, Practica Cap. XXVI) ---
RAYMVNDI LVLLI

the medicine one part, and place it in an earthen crucible over a gentle fire. And immediately, as it feels the heat, drop by drop, drop after drop of its white oil, know that the oil will penetrate all its parts, and will insensibly enter the deeper parts of the body of the elixir, and will place itself there, and will render it fusible, entering, and well penetrating. Then take a small amount of it, and test its fusibility upon an ignited copper plate. If it dissolves like wax without smoke, the medicine has been created. If not and it gives off smoke, place it back for incerating upon it of its oil, dripping drop after drop, until it gives the sign described above in the aforesaid manner. Afterwards allow it to cool: because the elixir is complete, and it is a priceless treasure, which converts every diminished and imperfect body into true and good gold without limit. Cast therefore one weight of it upon 1000 of common quicksilver washed with salt and vinegar, and it will be converted into pure silver better than that from the mine in every approved assay. And likewise, if it is cast upon any of the imperfect bodies, it will immediately be transformed into true ar. [silver] after the radical moisture.

--- Page 423 (page 203, Practica Cap. XXVII) ---
PRACTICA.

De exuberatione humidi nutrientis, vel nutritiui.

CAP. XXVII.

The exuberation of the nutritive and nourishing moisture, son, is nothing other than to make the nutrimental appropriate to the proportional temperament of the qualities of the nourished, so that it be not too cold or too hot, nor too raw, nor too much cooked. Because if it is too cold, it would kill the converting virtues. And if it is too hot, it would suffocate and extinguish the digestive virtue. And therefore it is necessary that everything generative of sulfur and quicksilver have the power of heating and moistening, according to reason and the capacity of nourishment, either by itself or by accident. And those things that heat and moisten by themselves are two things, of which one is, as it were, the efficient principle, namely, the perfect digestion such as nature makes, and that we make in one hour through the retrograding of the excessively cooked into the sufficiently raw, and through the successive grading of the excessively raw into the sufficiently cooked. The other is like a medicinal food, which is apt and suitable to generate choler by heating

--- Page 424 (page 203 verso, Practica Cap. XXVII cont.) ---
RAYMVNDI LVLLI

and drying, and consequently to gather phlegm, which moistens by cooling, unless the fumes are retained. And therefore it is called water against nature. Thus it itself is apt and suitable to generate the nutrimental and nourishing moisture, which is like milk that is converted into blood: for then, according to the exuberance of the exuberated moisture, it heats and moistens the quicksilver by reason of its known mutation, and therefore it has been called unnatural. In such a way it heats and moistens the quicksilver, which is the food of sulfur, and this is to such a degree that it is generative of spiritual blood, because from the substance of blood, which is the exuberated moisture, spirit is generated in the substance of the medicinal body. And from that the aforesaid substance is nourished and invigorated nutrimentally. Wherefore, son, recall within yourself from the above statements that for the multiplication of sulfur and ar. vi. [quicksilver], the perfect exuberation and digestion of these is necessary, with the aptitude of a greater and more abundant generation of sanguine spirit. But those ar. vi. [quicksilvers] that by their nature are not apt nor suitable for generating vivifying spirits absolutely, such as are all kinds of menstrua, through accident mul-

--- Page 425 (page 204, Practica Cap. XXVII cont.) ---
PRACTICA.

multiplying agents, and in others generating and procuring generation, just as the vegetable water of Mercury, which by its own nature is apt and suited to generate phlegm, yet if it accords and delights in heat, and in the dryness of its complexion, it multiplies the vivifying spirit. And therefore this water multiplies sulfur when it is cast into the bellies of choleric males. And when it is cast into the bellies of choleric females, quicksilver is multiplied, very well tempered, and they abound greatly from heat and moisture. Likewise, medicines that have the power to remove everything that hinders our spirit, and procure it to generate, are the fire of the stone. Son, the special ferment, if it has been gradually subtilized and digested, then it is nearer to metal than the stone is. And therefore the stone thus returns to the nature of metal, when it has been totally imbibited with pure blood, and not before. When therefore you wish to have such blood for imbibing our stone through a dewy imbibition, place upon the substance of the body from which you wish to extract the blood so much pure Mercury that it exceeds by five or six fingers in a glass vessel with a long

--- Page 426 (page 204 verso, Practica Cap. XXVII cont.) ---
RAYMVNDI LVLLI

and narrow neck, and make a fire gently beneath, until you see the blood rise above the Mercury. And set aside everything that has been dissolved by liquefaction, and add more Mercury. And continue this operation until you have its blood dissolved in a liquid, then distill it through an alembic. And then you will find the quicksilver to be of greater heat than it was before its exuberation. Likewise, if you seek to exuberate it further, place it in another body, and do as we have said, until it has drawn from it all the vivifying moisture: in such a manner our stone ascends high to the heavens, and is carried in the belly of the wind, that is, of quicksilver. Thus you can make your accidental exuberation, and multiply it always into another body at your will, and if you are ingenious and subtle, do not discard the earths that are black and dry: for with them you can make many things by instinct and instruction, and create many stones. If you are idle, son, and do not know what to do, enter into our mastery, and you will have enough to do, so much that you will not value all the riches of the world at one button: because you will know that all the precious things of the world in full cartloads from one

--- Page 427 (page 205, Practica Cap. XXVII cont.) ---
PRACTICA.

vile thing had their origin. Son, this thing was made from exuberated fermentation by reason of the elongation of the body from its true temperament, and of the water to its menstrual, and of the appropriation of this to the metal. If therefore after the distillation you dissolve the body again, dissolve another body of a new metal, that which is afterwards dissolved will approach a greater fermentation on account of the impregnated and exuberated virtue, and better proportioned by the nature of metal, pure through the radical moisture. And thus through extremes and means, it is necessary for you to make the exuberation of our quicksilvers. Therefore the second is worth more than the first, and the fourth more than the third, and the sixth more than the fifth, provided that each preserves its perfection, according as its nature has been exuberated. And this multiplication is always made perpetually, and this is the cause and reason of the great and excellent mineral virtue that is in our water, through which the stone, which is not a stone, has been brought from hidden potency into manifest action, and from a remote and distant virtue into a proximate virtue through certain degrees of operation, which are within the latitude of tempera-

--- Page 428 (page 205 verso, Practica Cap. XXVII cont. / Cap. XXVIII) ---
RAYMVNDI LVLLI

ment and of distemperament, and thus nature appropriates itself to its perfection. Know therefore, son, that in every form there are degrees through which virtue is strengthened through fermentation and exuberation, because a tempered thing restrains and arrests an untempered thing, and multiplies it in virtue. Our operation, son, is nothing other than to run through the whole latitude of temperament and distemperament. And beyond this the Artist is not required to pass. And if he passes beyond, it will be beyond our mastery.

De compositione elixiris rubei.

CAP. XXVIII.

In entirely this same manner, preserving the practice by which the elixir for the white was made, you shall make the elixir for the red, because there is no difference in the practice so long as all the red elements are present, and just as you multiplied the white sulfur, so you will multiply the red, placing gold instead of silver. And let the water of Mercury be first rubified with the fire of the red stone through decoction alone, and let the medicine be composed of three parts of fer-

--- Page 429 (page 206, Practica Cap. XXVIII cont.) ---
PRACTICA.

ment, three of water, and three of air, and one part with a half of fire. And when you come to the reduction of the air, repeat the sublimation until the whole is fixed together, and afterwards continue the three fires that we described for the white, to the end that the elixir may be better fixed and purified through the digestion of peptic heat. Son, this peptic digestion gives the medicine the potency and action that it must perform, because through it the moisture is terminated and the fire is digested. Afterwards draw out your elixir, and place it, and incerate it within a crucible with a gentle fire without fear of its fleeing, and drench it with its red oil drop by drop, until you see it melt like wax, and without smoke, and like tin upon burning iron, and then it will have been made one: stable, deepening, penetrating, consolidating, tincturing, and permanent, of which cast one weight upon 1000 of luna, or of quicksilver washed with salt and vinegar, and it will be transformed into true and perfect gold at every assay, and better than from the mine in all its properties. Son, we tell you that our gold and our silver are not common gold and silver, because to their tinctures we add

--- Page 430 (page 206 verso, Practica Cap. XXVIII cont. / Cap. XXIX) ---
RAYMVNDI LVLLI

a great fiery addition, and we cause them to be granted and conceded a great perseverance in fire, through the property of our known operations, and of many good and useful things for expelling every infirmity.

De perfectione & melioratione omnium medicinarum.

CAP XXIX.

Son, if after projection you see that the metal has been converted and transmuted into another metal by the medicine, or if your medicine is not of a sufficiently good color, let more of the said medicine be added to the said metal. And if it has too much red color (for the white cannot have too much), do not put so much of the medicine, and if you have put too much, add more metal to be transmuted and converted. And if your medicine does not remain, or does not fully resist against the fire, the defect is one of fixation. Whence its ingression fails, and it re-

--- Page 431 (page 207, Practica Cap. XXIX cont. / Cap. XXX) ---
PRACTICA.

quires assistance, either by reiteration of solution and congelation, or by sublimation of the unfixed part upon the fixed thing, until it takes on resistance against the harshness of fire. And if you see that it cannot be melted except by the violence and severity of fire, the defect is one of poor inceration. Therefore restore and make good through the inceration of the oil of the stone, casting it drop by drop upon a gentle fire in a crucible, as above, until it flows like wax. Son, when you wish to incerate, you shall put more of the hot and moist thing than of the cold and dry thing. But when you work with the intention of fixing, more of the cold and dry thing shall be put than of the hot and moist. Understand what we say, because the perfection of this work is nothing other than the mutation of natures.

Quomodo medicinæ se multiplicant de bono in melius: & de vno in plures.

CAP. XXX.

When you have perfected the said medicines, and have made projection from them, you can artificially multi-

--- Page 432 (page 207 verso, Practica Cap. XXX cont.) ---
RAYMVNDI LVLLI

ply their virtues in two ways. The first way is that you cause them to be dissolved in their white water of Mercury, or red, from which they were procreated, until they have become a clear water, and afterwards in a gentle decoction cause them to congeal, and then incerate them in their oils. And upon the fire often described, until they truly flow upon the plate that we described, and on account of this their virtue will be doubled in tincture and in all perfections, as you will be able to see in their projections: for their weight, which was cast upon 1000, will be cast upon 10,000. And in this multiplication the labor is not great. The second way of multiplying is of greater labor, because if you distill them after they have been dissolved in the water of their Mercury, their virtue will be multiplied a hundredfold, that is, one weight will be worth 100. The method of multiplication, moreover, is that you dissolve each of their kinds individually in its water through inhumation, afterwards by distillation you will separate the elements, receiving first the water, then the air, as has been said, and there will remain at the bottom a clear substance of fixed earth in the form of a powder. Return the water upon it after distilla-

--- Page 433 (page 208, Practica Cap. XXX cont.) ---
PRACTICA.

tion, until it has drunk the whole, and that all that water be fixed with that earth, in dewatering with its oil, and with its tincture, until the whole is fixed together, and the whole melts upon the plate, etc. And of this medicine cast one weight upon whichever of the bodies you wish to convert, and you will assuredly find its tincture multiplied by 100 virtues in potency, in such a manner that if one weight formerly converted 1000, it will convert 10,000. Wherefore if you prepare it a third time, as has been said, from three it will convert 100,000, and so proceeding to infinity. Therefore, son, you should note that the more the medicine has been subtilized, namely dissolved, sublimated, and congealed, the more and more abundantly it works, because in each dissolution it gains and acquires ten weights in each projection, on account of the virtue multiplied in it. Therefore, son, let it not weary you to reiterate solutions, sublimations, and coagulations: for through such operations the medicine is digested, united, and its virtue is multiplied, and it works more perfectly and is fixed. Wherefore, son, if you dissolve well, and know how to congeal as we have said, and if you know the species with which

--- Page 434 (page 208 verso, Practica Cap. XXX cont. / Cap. XXXI) ---
RAYMVNDI LVLLI

you must work, you will arrive at the perfection of our mastery, with a high and brief secret reserved, which no one can reveal, as we have told you. But if you are a good investigator, and a son of subtle doctrine, through those things that we have said and will say in the book of our present testament and codicil and others, you will discover it in full, and you will know the secret. And when you know it, under pain of anathema we forbid you to disclose it to anyone: for he who has disclosed it is cursed by God. And therefore the philosophers have thus concealed it, and by God's will shall conceal it in future times.

De multiplicatione medicinæ in quantitate.

CAP. XXXI.

Son, you shall take one dram of the said medicine multiplied in virtues, upon which you shall cast 100 drams of Mercury, and immediately when the said Mercury begins to be heated in the crucible it will be congealed into a fine medicine, to be used for projection upon other Mercury. Then take one ounce of the said second medicine, and make projection upon another 100 drams of heated Mercu-

--- Page 435 (page 209, Practica Cap. XXXI cont.) ---
PRACTICA.

ry. It will again be converted into a new, true, and pure medicine. Afterwards you must multiply in quantity the first medicine in whole or in part, because from one ounce we obtained 200 ounces, not however of equal virtue as the first that produced for you what has been described, nor will it be, unless you multiply through dissolution and congelation: for as we have said, it can be multiplied from one virtue into infinite virtues, and afterwards in quantity. Therefore cast one weight of the last congealed medicine upon 100 of Mercury washed with salt and vinegar, and heat upon the fire. And when you see it fuming, it will be congealed into pure gold, or into perfect silver, to be submitted to every assay and judgment, according to whether the elixir is white or red, concerning which, son, we have given you the truth in a complete discourse, to which you can arrive without doubt, if our teachings have been labored upon with good understanding concerning this matter. Therefore have diligence through the method of the said regimens in the exercise of practice, and with reasonable considerations of an elevated intellect, and you will see the truth of all these things corporeally, and all that we have told you between your hands

--- Page 436 (page 209 verso, Practica Cap. XXXI cont.) ---
RAYMVNDI LVLLI

you will find in reality and in fact, which thing indeed exists by itself, and not perhaps as if you were saying that the whole were done by miracle, but it is done through art and science, and is completed through continuous operation and the hand ministering to the work of nature. This is the supreme stone, son, kept secret and hidden by all the ancient philosophers from the ignorant and the unworthy, everywhere revealed, which converts every diminished and imperfect body into infinite gold-making or silver-making capacity, according to whether the elixir has been subtilized and prepared. In like and similar manner we tell you that it has an efficacious virtue beyond all number of other medicines, for truly healing every infirmity that exists or can exist in the human body, caused by cold or hot natures. And because it is of the most subtle and most noble nature, it preserves the health of the human body. And it multiplies that health to such an extent that it causes an old man to arrive at the potency and strength of youth, and expels every present and future infirmity from the body through the organic passages, and nullifies and destroys every poison, and moistens and softens arthr-

--- Page 437 (page 210, Practica Cap. XXXI cont.) ---
PRACTICA.

itic conditions and acute gouts from all the members, and dissolves that which is and has been congealed in the lungs, and consolidates, strengthens, and heals a wounded and injured stomach, and purifies the blood, and purges all the spirits, and preserves them in health. And if the infirmity is of one month, this medicine cures it in one day; if of one year, it will heal it purely in twelve days; if the infirmity is of long standing, it will be most purely cured in one month. And therefore do not marvel if this medicine has been sought and desired by mankind above all medicines, since all others are universally reducible to it. If therefore, son, you possess it, you will have a perpetual treasure. And the said medicine has yet another power: for it rectifies every other animal, and vivifies all plants in the time of spring through its great and wonderful heat: for if you dissolve in water a quantity the size of a grain of millet, and place that water around the trunk of a vine in the amount that a hazelnut shell would hold, flowers and leaves will artificially spring forth, and it will fructify and bear ripe clusters of grapes in the month of May. And so with every other plant, which thing when it has been done will be considered a miracle and con-

--- Page 438 (page 210 verso, Practica Cap. XXXI cont.) ---
RAYMVNDI LVLLI

trary to the course of nature, because such people are ignorant of its potency, and believe that such things are done by enchantment. Son, this thing is nothing other than natural heat infixed in its radical moisture. And therefore nature by its own instinct greatly desires to exist in the deeper part of every elemented thing, and it opens and multiplies the natural heat of the body, and into the center of which it enters, because it is common to every body, and has the power of rectifying all parts that carry virtue, and of fixing them in those parts, in the manner that we described in the treatise on gems, and it makes glass malleable, and it does many other great and weighty wonders in the three natures, namely, mineral, vegetable, and animal. And Galen was not unmindful of this medicine in his third book of the Tegni, in that canon which begins: "The usefulness indeed of both." But with such hidden words that today there are not found in the world except certain persons who with firm conscience can believe that some of the philosophers have spoken of the aforesaid medicine.

De re-

--- Page 439 (page 211, Practica Cap. XXXII) ---
PRACTICA.

De recapitulatione magisterij in forma diuisionis.

CAP. XXXII.

Son, this mastery is divided into three degrees. The first is divided into three principal dissolutions; through the first degree you must purify the stone and make the fixed volatile. The second is divided into two principal reductions. And through this middle degree the prepared stone is fixed, until it can rest and withstand the pressure of fire. The third degree is formed through the preceding two. And therefore it is divided into two parts. The first is solution and congelation. The second is fixation, and through this third degree the stone is completed, and its multiplication is accomplished through the operations of the first degree.

Finis secundæ partis testamenti, quæ est de praxi.
