--- Page M1 (printed page 1, Liber Mercuriorum, Caput Primum) ---
RAYMVNDI LVLLI DOCTISSIMI ET CELEBERRIMI PHILOSOPHI Mercuriorum Liber.

CAPVT PRIMVM.

It is necessary that you understand the operations by which the nature of quicksilver is created, and afterward if you have knowledge for recognizing our quicksilver, you will have the complete art: because all the operations are nothing more than a single one, which is done by the method that we shall tell you. Take as much as you wish of the liquor of lunaria, and from it by distillation, separate the elements: but first you will separate the phlegmatic water, in which the spirit remains mortified, and continue the distillation in a gentle bath, until

--- Page M2 (printed page 2, Liber Mercuriorum, Caput Primum cont.) ---
RAYMVNDI LVLLI

you see the animated water distilling, which begins to burn, and let that be distilled to one side, and when that which could be distilled by that heat has departed and the phlegm has been removed, as the signs of burning make manifest, divide it into two parts, and one part you shall keep for creating and with it you shall sublimate the earth of the stone, and from the second part you shall draw the elements without any combustion under the preservation of the property of sulphur, and through the quicksilver, in this manner: place the portion of animated water over the dregs, which will be in the likeness of melted or liquefied pitch at the bottom of the vessel, and immediately place the alembic with its receiver, and kindle the fire of the furnace grate, and seal with paste, and continue that fire until whatever can be distilled is distilled by the aforesaid intensity of fire: afterward place it in a dry, controlled fire and continue to distill the element from the grate, and at the end

--- Page M3 (printed page 3, Liber Mercuriorum, Caput Primum cont.) ---
MERCVRIORVM LIB.

of its distillation, allow the material to cool with the whole vessel, then return the first liquor, which is the water in a mass, over the dregs, and repeat your distillation, as has already been said, until the dregs remain dry and burned, and the unctuous moisture is entirely sublimated, like the soul in the substance of the spirit. Son, the nature of our spirit operates in all things and surpasses every thing, and through it come blackness, whiteness, and redness, and let it observe thus to mix so that the blending of these is as we have told you, because one enters into the other and flees into it, and the rarefied is filled by the condensed, and the condensed is subtilized by the rarefied, and all of this happens through dissolution and heating in a gentle fire: and this fire must be continued until the elements have embraced with the completion of their moisture: and its completion is that every small amount is burned until with no gentle

--- Page M4 (printed page 4, Liber Mercuriorum, Caput Secundum) ---
RAYMVNDI LVLLI

fire it is dried, and know, son, that one burns the other, and strengthens it, and teaches it to fight against fire, and thus by cooking, pursuing the elements at a gentle fire, they are softened and converted into extreme natures: because what is liquefied is entirely diminished, and if it is made non-liquefied, and the moisture is subtle, by this method the body of the spirit is made, and it becomes thin and strong, fighting against fire.

CAPVT SECVNDVM.
De assatione & dissolutione, & de coagulatione ipsorum.

Son, the property of each is to dissolve and whiten its sulphur, and the properties of its sulphur are to coagulate and purify its quicksilver. Thus, son, you see the vapor that coagulates quicksilver is of the nature of its own middle substance, which resides in the dry, subtle, airy earth, mixed

--- Page M5 (printed page 5, Liber Mercuriorum, Caput Secundum cont.) ---
MERCVRIORVM LIB.

mixed and digested and cooked with a gentle fire continuing with it; therefore you must manage so that the dissolved quicksilver is returned over its dry earth little by little, not all at once: because the draconic virtue of sulphur converts a small divided portion more readily than a large divided one, for the sense of the virtue of the dragon does not reckon with a sea of falsehoods: because you must do this with the intention of congealing and not of dissolving. Thus, son, quicksilver first reigns against sulphur, and as is evident through the virtue of the preceding chapter, continue therefore its operation, roasting and imbibition, until first the water has whitened everything, and it is coagulated by the vapor of sulphur: there is no need to do anything else by the contrary operation: because just as quicksilver conquers sulphur entirely, so conversely sulphur, being dry, conquers quicksilver through coagulation and through the repetition

--- Page M6 (printed page 6, Liber Mercuriorum, Caput Tertium) ---
RAYMVNDI LVLLI

of sublimation such as we have described.

CAPVT TERTIVM.
De sublimatione & mortificatione, id est vapore seu sulphuris.

And when through the aforesaid sublimation and frequent roasting you see that the greater part and quantity has been destroyed, repeat with a strong fire the sublimation upon it, until you have purged away more of its wateriness entirely, and when you see that the whole has been elevated like a dead powder whiter than snow against [?] its particles, repeat the sublimation again by itself alone without its dregs, for then it will be perfectly cleansed and sublimated for roasting the tincture to whiteness, the like of which cannot be found.

CAPVT QVARTVM.
De fixione & perfectione ipsius &.

--- Page M7 (printed page 7, Liber Mercuriorum, Caput Quartum) ---
MERCVRIORVM LIB.

When you have sublimated and taken the said pure substance, then you will fix one part of it, and we shall tell you the method of fixation in the greater stone, and when that part has been fixed, it will afterward fix the other: therefore repeat the sublimation of the unfixed part over the unfixed matter until it likewise becomes fixed, to be tested by experiment whether it has produced a good fusion upon the fire, and if it does so, it is accomplished; if not, join to it some exuberated quicksilver, repeating its sublimation until it is fusible, and we shall tell you the method of exuberating any quicksilver if you have understood us in the practice of the greater stone in the chapter that begins, "Son, nutritive exuberation is nothing other than," etc. And in this practice in the chapter that begins, "You shall take," etc. But that one does it from its own quicksilver, and therefore it is simple: if you wish to make it more compound, and so make it from

--- Page M8 (printed page 8, Liber Mercuriorum, Caput Quartum cont.) ---
RAYMVNDI LVLLI

dissolve it in the first water, which is exuberated from the soul of the said substance from which the tincture is made, then separate the water by distillation, and do not repeat distilling over its dregs, until the water has drunk and drawn to itself all the moisture, as the Mercurialist has done. This is the creative moisture, which above all others endures upon the battle of fire: and thus through substance alone we make an excellent medicine of whiteness. And as we say of one, so understand us to say of all: we exclude none, neither the common nor the vulgar, and when we say common, we speak of that which the pious philosophers understand. And when we say vulgar, we speak of that which the rustic understands, which is sold in apothecary shops: but understand well that where the common one is employed, as we know it, who recognize it in its proper virtue.

--- Page M9 (printed page 9, Liber Mercuriorum, Caput V-VI) ---
MERCVRIORVM LIB.

CAPVT QVINTVM.
Exuberatio alia salis de corpore.

And if you wish to exuberate your quicksilver with the moisture that nature has prepared for you, it will also be fixed with its own proper permanence. This exuberation is with the moisture of the body prepared through the ingenuity of nature: because a prepared thing will give you a prepared thing. And such as it shall have been prepared, such will be its perfection: and whatever its perfection shall be, such it will be and ought to be in its projection upon any of the imperfect bodies, and upon common unprepared [metal].

CAPVT SEXTVM.
Ratificatio humoris exuberati per fusionem tincturæ.

When you have exuberated your quicksilver, whether with the body or with the ante-

--- Page M10 (printed page 10, Liber Mercuriorum, Caput VI-VII) ---
RAYMVNDI LVLLI

cedent, before you fix it, you must rectify it through repeated distillation, until the clear liquids have splendor and crystalline clarity, or that of pearls, or the lily of silver, like clear chalk [?]. Then therefore it is apt for performing efficacious operations in the fusibility of quicksilver, for in it the tincture is fixed, as they have told you. And if its form be made more fusible through its perfection, let it be from quicksilver. And beyond this it has many other virtues over common stones, of which we shall tell you something concerning pearls.

CAPVT SEPTIMVM.
Ratificatio parlarum.

The demonstration of the virtue of the said exuberated moisture is that you should have minute pearls, or pearls as clear as glass,

--- Page M11 (printed page 11, Liber Mercuriorum, Caput VII-VIII) ---
MERCVRIORVM LIB.

in powder, and you shall place them inside your glass vessel, which should have a round bottom with its cover, and you shall pour enough of the said exuberated water so that it floats above the pearls to the thickness of one cautious [?] key-stroke, then cover with its glass cover so that neither powder nor water nor anything else disturbs your pearls, and within certain hours they will be in a white paste. Collect to the side the sliminess that floats on the surface of the water, because that is its exuberated and rectified form: the simple water of Mercury, which you must return to it when we tell you.

CAPVT OCTAVVM.
Diuisio speciei, & vnitas illius cum limositate aqua.

When

--- Page M12 (printed page 12, Liber Mercuriorum, Caput VIII cont.) ---
RAYMVNDI LVLLI

you see that the said pearls have been liquefied into the said paste by the virtue of the water, all whole without any other action of heat, you shall then strain the water wisely by inclining the vessel, very limpidly with all its sliminesses that float above, and all of this you can do without any whey or serum, which is called priam [?], and preserve well so that none of the aforesaid paste can impart any color whatsoever to the said water, but only the sliminess which is its own proper form; and cover well, and let all these worthless [materials] be disposed of, and let it be done so that the water of the bath [?] may have this artificially arranged so that it can receive the action of heat through sublimation into vapor of heated water; and if it is done through the stages of repeated processing, in this manner the sliminess of your pearls with the sliminess will agree and will be continued and will be united through all the parts of the water, you [will have it reduced] into a perceptible liquor,

--- Page M13 (printed page 13, Liber Mercuriorum, Caput IX) ---
MERCVRIORVM LIB.

fusible; separate from the bath and set aside.

CAPVT NONVM.
Tertio margaritarum increduli impressus formam en fac.

Afterward take a form or mold of silver, made artfully to the size of the repository of a half-circle [?], for making pearls as you desire. And if it should be gilded on the inside, or let it be made of two concave parts well joined one with the other, so that the parts of the form or mold are conjoined and smoothed round about through the roundness and concavity with which you wish to make the pearls; and let each of the said forms have one part of the form with a passage around it, in such manner that a thread [?] may pass through and correspond to the opening so as to enter, and so that one hole is toward the right of the other, arranged in a straight line, each one simi-

--- Page M14 (printed page 14, Liber Mercuriorum, Caput IX cont.) ---
RAYMVNDI LVLLI

larly, as they make those holes of the quadrant. Then fill the said mold, and with the said paste fill one part of the mold only, until it comes that the half is equally full; afterward place crosswise the said thread of gold, and then join from above with those two gold [wires] at the end, so that the other part of the mold can be properly filled; then close the mold, assembling the parts, and when your pearl is formed, draw it out; and if you see it does not correspond as described, let it be perforated part by part, and pass the thread through again, then close the mold and cast out the excess. Then upon a plate of glass, covered with interior glass, allow it to dry in a shady place, aggregated; in such manner afterward make as many others as you wish, and set these to dry as we have told you.

CAPVT DECIMVM.

--- Page M15 (printed page 15, Liber Mercuriorum, Caput X) ---
MERCVRIORVM LIB.

Modus quo tu debes facere formam perfectam ad margaritas, postquam ipsas iam fuse formatæ.

When you have formed your pearls with the shells, and they have been well dried, you shall take a golden thread, and without touching the said pearls with your hands, you shall industriously place the thread through the holes of each one, and gild your pearls in the manner of a Pater Noster [rosary]. Having the aforesaid proof, take two or three eggs according to how large the string of pearls may be. And so let each broken [egg] with each [shell] receive one insertion [?], and you can apply the ointment at the head of the other through the openings, now under your hand, as we have shown you by this figure. And smear everything that is within, and place your pearls therein.

And plunge them in the said liquor, and let them stand for the duration of one Ave Maria,

--- Page M16 (printed page 16, Liber Mercuriorum, Caput X cont.) ---
RAYMVNDI LVLLI

and afterward place them inside the said eggs and seal the eggs with wax, and place them in open air for eight days, so that the sun does not touch them. And afterward in the sun for three days, turning from one side to the other every three hours in return; and it will be informed by its spirits, finally through the concoction of pearls. Nothing else is necessary for you to do. Open the eggs and break them, and you will find pearls with such clarity or light that nature itself has not made them dissimilar in any respect, nor by the most expert arts of nature will it be judged that it was done by art, if the master knows how to make them. In this way also you can rectify those that have been diminished by the heat of the bath, because when the Lord [?] bathes, he restores them to their nature and beauty, if they be made natural: because artificial ones cannot be corrupted so quickly, as they do on account of the fixed moisture, and the exuberation, which retains clarity just as forests retain souls.

--- Page M17 (printed page 17, Liber Mercuriorum, Cap. X cont.) ---
MERCVRIORVM LIB.

which soul holds the body in life by means of part [of the spirit]. But such a spirit is not one to which you should give heated things, and on account of this, you should know concerning them. And if you wish to rectify the dominant [materials] in clarity, you can rectify two 3 [drachms] of them with the fourth part of one 3 [drachms] of pearls; dissolve them together, forming [them], and thus it will be a dissolvent, neither more nor less in the said liquor. But to the end that you not deceive yourself in purchasing, take one and break it, and if inside you find it white as on the outside, it is good; and if it is rough, it is adulterated, or if around it in the cavity, or in composition [?]. And commonly such ones are false externally, because if it appears that they are not well clear on the outside, do not buy them, nor break them, in hope that it is good. The selection of offerings [for testing] is beyond such indication. And with this they test them with sal ammoniac, which is that with the salt of the vapor of sulfur, as is more fully contained in our Lapidary, where the place is.

--- Page M18 (printed page 18, Liber Mercuriorum, Cap. X cont.) ---
RAYMVNDI LVLLI

concerning certain secret experiments. And if you wish to have the transmutation of rectification, when you have dissolved the pearls by decoction, you shall take their infused species; read our Testament and take a glass tube to the length of two fingers. Inside there place it with the pearls in the same manner that you did with it from the other glass in the [same] manner, and join its head inside the flask in which there is water of pearls for collecting the vapor of the water, and seal well with foaming cement, so that it is not possible that through the glass [comes] its transformation. In a similar manner you can make red ones, and otherwise compose things with the liquor of the Mercuries. And thus concerning all other stones we describe for you the methods in the treatise on the Lapidary, in the second chapter on the rectification of the stone. Thanks be to God. Amen.

--- Page M19 (printed page 19, Liber Mercuriorum, Cap. XI) ---
MERCVRIORVM LIB.

CAPVT VNDECIMVM.
De creatione Mercuriorum rubeorum ad faciendum tincturam rubeam de sua propria substantia ad configurandum spiraculas.

Son, take the ruby liquor, which is more greatly separated by distillation over ashes, and you shall distill it in the bath three times. And after each distillation you shall pour the water back over the dregs, repeating the procedure, and thus you shall do so that it quickly dissolves in the said water. Separate again this water over ashes — let this be done with intention, because the water draws out the fire that is in the tincture — and in order to preserve it, distill that liquor by the method through the bath to the end that the phlegm may be separated from the fire, and apply a tempered fire in part; when it has been distilled, draw the dregs from

--- Page M20 (printed page 20, Liber Mercuriorum, Cap. XI cont.) ---
RAYMVNDI LVLLI

the soul of the earth, with which deed, take care however that the earth is not corrupted, because it would immediately burn the tincture sustaining it otherwise [?], in which it must be evaporated with small fires for some time, and this you will see whenever the earth is broken down and lacking in all moisture. Afterward take it and wash it with distillation and calcination, until the earth is made red, so that it has produced a clean extractive [substance] with heat and moisture, and the other is extracted with dryness and coldness.

CAPVT DVODECIMVM.
De sublificatione, Mercurium sublimationem in fumum signum ad succendum calcem rubeam.

Take sublimated Mercury, [brought] to the red [or] white, as we have told you, and let it be dissolved in the water of Mercury from which you extracted the liquid of silver from the said Mercury, and in this water let the said liquor of the stone be dissolved with this sublima-

--- Page M21 (printed page 21, Liber Mercuriorum, Cap. XII cont.) ---
MERCVRIORVM LIB.

tially as well as essentially: when we say substantially, we mean through the substance of fire; and when we say essentially, it is to distinguish the quality that is taken up in the substance of quantity [and] could have made the water by distillation until it is entirely congealed. And another time reduce the water over the Mercury to the end that, if the thing is greatly subtilized essentially with the water through distillation, so that there is another return, and a third time distill, and afterward gradually strengthen the fire until you see a less strong reddishness. And if there is nothing ignoble [remaining] nearly under the application with the water of the stone, mixed with any water whatsoever, let it be sublimated — thus may the sublimation be done — and thus when what is in the vessel has been reformed upon or fixed [in] its elements, know that we tell you, if you have heard us well, that you will have from the Mercury an elixir in full.

CAPVT DECIMVM TERTIVM.
De

--- Page M22 (printed page 22, Liber Mercuriorum, Cap. XIII) ---
RAYMVNDI LVLLI
De modo fermentorum et eorum compositionum.

You must know that there are two ferments composed of tincture, and ferments composed of liquefaction. The ferments composed of tincture are seven in number, as is shown through the table of ferments and through the triangular figure in the practicals under the alphabetical letters, namely B C D E F G. B signifies simple red gold. C signifies simple red sulfur. D signifies simple dissolved gold. E signifies the water of composition. F signifies composite red sulfur. G signifies composite dissolved gold. And from these are born three triangular figures, which are the exchanges of the said table, as the figures of the significants are signified through the figure. And since from them come forth these figures, one from the exchange of the said table through the fact of experience, namely

--- Page M23 (printed page 23, Liber Mercuriorum, Cap. XIII cont. / Cap. XIV) ---
MERCVRIORVM LIB.

namely in knowing what our philosophy is, and its truth, and hence you can derive many natures, in examples and in experimentation branchwise, without detailed explanations of the said exchanges.

CAPVT DECIMVM
quartum. De modo fermentorum compositionum, et de liquefactione quod sunt tres tantum gummae.

The composite ferments are of liquefaction as many as there are ferments of tinctures, and they are made successively upon those which the flowing [agent] needs to enter into all tinctures through the denomination of the compounding, which is the surface of the ultimate liquor, whether simple or composite; and therefore there shall be no such composition except that

--- Page M24 (printed page 24, Liber Mercuriorum, Cap. XIV cont.) ---
RAYMVNDI LVLLI

which receives the composition of the tincture through the virtue of the liquor of God [?] and of sulfur, and the mixings of true sweetness: wherefore in place of simple red water you shall put simple red air, and in place of composite red water you shall put composite red air. The simple signifying is the type H, and the composite is signified by the letter I. For example, when you wish to have a composite ferment of liquefaction, first make that of tincture, and multiply it with the fire of the stone, dissolving with the water either of simple Mercury, according to the form of the method of congealing the nature of sulfur; afterward congeal, and cause it to be rubified, as we have told you. And again dissolve that rubification in its own water, and afterward again congealing through several repetitions. The extraction itself was of gold to its perfection, [being] part of the rubified sulfur. Afterward mix the waters, and from the small fermentation do not give [more than] the natural [portions]; afterward let it be evaporated with the heat of the simple three times.

--- Page M25 (printed page 25, Liber Mercuriorum, Caput XIV cont. / Caput XV) ---
MERCVRIORVM LIB.

done three times in the bath of Maria continuously, until nothing more can be distilled from it: afterward place the ashes wisely with their cover closed, and leave it thus until the moisture dries and ferments. And thus [you will have] the ferment of your tincture, and you will have completed well through the first chamber. But the ferment is such that when it has been gummed, it attracts all metals into its own proper nature, and converts them into its golden [nature], exceedingly brilliant, and attracts [them] to its pure substance, and liquefies them so that the whole becomes a ferment.

CAPVT DECIMVM QVINTVM.
Fermentum in liquefactione.

When you have made the ferment of the tincture, you shall convert it into liquefaction by joining H to it according to the weight that you know, and [which] sense demonstrates to you through the work of nature, until it is fixed within by means of C5 of the sen-

--- Page M26 (printed page 26, Liber Mercuriorum, Caput XV cont. / Caput XVI) ---
RAYMVNDI LVLLI

sensory [faculties]. Afterward you shall place a certain quantity, and you shall bind it, as if green [?], which through deceit or certain melting, and thus the ferment will have been made by the liquefaction of the first chamber. If you wish, you can multiply it infinitely through secret operations in the mixings made by means of our chambers.

CAPVT DECIMVM SEXTVM.
De multiplicatione fermentorum per viam mixtionum.

Son, if you place B with E, which are two chambers, let the whole be mixed into one reduction of liquefaction with heat, forming [it]. But if with a triple or secret [proportion] in the mixing, that will be better because it is near to the virtue of the true fusible [agent], which have already been altered through the operations spoken of above. Take therefore the ferment of B and that of G, or with [them], and combine the waters, and evaporate them in the bath of Maria, and afterward place [them] upon

--- Page M27 (printed page 27, Liber Mercuriorum, Caput XVI cont.) ---
MERCVRIORVM LIB.

the ashes, and do [this] by the method that we have told you in the preceding chapter. And if you see that it does not flow, continue to increase [the heat] as much as shall be expedient, because the greater the liquefaction, the more water is drawn out through distillation. And through this the fifth separation is revealed to you as one of those displayed: restore to it four [parts] in proportion [?], and more, and thus the ferment of those [things] in composition over others is a composition, and thus masterly you join gum with gum, and ferment with ferment. And if [it is] small, you can multiply it, because once multiplication [has begun] you can never be deprived of decoction. When we do not say better or more, we rather speak through experiences according to the reason of sense, since it is of a certain weight limited by nature. On account of this, conduct the operations through fair reasoning, and thus for you from this, reducing to the end of the operation which nature desires. And this can truly [be done] quickly if a little

--- Page M28 (printed page 28, Liber Mercuriorum, Caput XVI cont.) ---
RAYMVNDI LVLLI

at a time it has drunk, until you see it flow, because there is no other weight: therefore on account of this we say, and more with caution done through your intellect freely in celestial things [?], known through the figure of its nature. And if [so], when it is used with caution, until you see the sign that demonstrates the measure, by which its nature may be brought to fusion, so that you may hold it to be a perfectly corrected body. And when we say rubifying, we say it in distinction from the life of man. And if you wish to multiply the said ferment of composition, and to bring it to a greater multiplication of composition, continuously those said chambers of B P D H [are used]. And if from such multiplication you wish, mix with any of the chambers through the practical method. I and some of my companions emptied all four or eight chambers primarily of tablets and tables over three years with great solicitude. Secondly we made each chamber's [work] annual within three months of ses-

--- Page M29 (printed page 29, Liber Mercuriorum, Caput XVI cont. / Caput XVII) ---
MERCVRIORVM LIB.

sions, and the division had not been suitable to the will of all. Son, if you have entered the art through the practical method, and if you do not see [that] all faith makes [it so]. And if in the entrance [you are] a master, if it can be delayed, and new [things arise]: because in the beginning the separations of elements are long, and the rectifications of them, and the creation of the superior mineral medium, which extends just as much as all the mineral portions, signified in their true order through the ingenuity of the master, and as if what has been said may be so. And if all ferments of D [were] mixed from the vessel [?] of G, or [as] we shall tell you through the practice of the first chambers, or of their multiplication, it will easily demonstrate the ferments of power [?].

CAPVT DECIMVM SEPTIMVM.
De prima parte aquæ, quæ venit ad inquisitionem perfectionis illius.

Now we shall tell of the first operation, which we shall claim in the inquiry into the perfection

--- Page M30 (printed page 30, Liber Mercuriorum, Caput XVII cont.) ---
RAYMVNDI LVLLI

of Mercury. It is thus: first dissolve silver against one water of Mercury, and it will be thus: Separate the water through the bath, and sift through ashes, and place each part separately; reduce the water over the dregs to the end that you may dissolve what can be dissolved, and that which has been distilled, place with its like: then upon a certain [quantity] for the fusion of the water, place the whole to ferment with gentle heat for one natural day, to the end that the said oil may continue with the water through the virtue of heat of the minute elements of myrrh [?], and dissolve; place the separation above by inclining the vessel, and place it within a cucurbit, and upon this you shall place an alembic for distilling the water in the bath, and the air in ashes, as above concerning the Chemistry, and by its method [?] over ashes upon the first dregs, and again another time you shall instill above the water that is separated through ashes. And if you see that the dregs

--- Page M31 (printed page 31, Liber Mercuriorum, Caput XVII cont.) ---
MERCVRIORVM LIB.

are in some way moist, separate the moistures, and as above you have left [them], and [do] so much through the bath: continue, and then dissolve all the second and third dregs all together, and cast away the first, and from those separate the water through the bath, afterward through the vessel [?] through ashes. Son, you should know that this which is most heated in the nature of Mercury, because it is upon its sublimation, and the continuation of it in the fires of Mercury, because in itself it is matter that dissolves more quickly [when] it is hotter. And with the waters separated, distill over the dregs several times: afterward, having mixed the earth with water through watering a little, until it is well dried, place the residue of water through the anterior fermentation, in the bath until, and afterward let it be restored, and afterward [add] water to it, until it sublimes whatever it can, and with the water reduce the sublimate over the dry matter, and make [it] restore until it is dry.

--- Page M32 (printed page 32, Liber Mercuriorum, Caput XVII cont.) ---
RAYMVNDI LVLLI

Let it be dry; [add] water to it at half its weight. And afterward let it be dried of the second water through a fixed sublimation with which they may render [it], until you see it flow, because nothing else remains for you to do: always when you see that it is becoming wine-like [?], you shall give it a small amount of dissolvents, which ceases to fix in it. And on account of this let it be with this [that it] desists in its Sicilian heat [?], just as in it there is made a degree, given, given, it melts into the construction of sulfur. Whereby it is declared to you that perfectly nothing which can be fixed in frozen sulfur, and harshly congealed, nor should such frozen sulfur be placed [so]. Each one has carried [it] to a very vivid ignition, by the smallest parts, until it is [brought to] the composition of heat and desiccation, which is of the nature of sulfur: and the more frozen it is, the more it shows [its] nature to flow. And truly upon quicksilver [it works] over heat and desiccation with slowness of time.

MERCVRIORVM LIB.

Take the water which we have told you of above, which has the power of solids under the confirmation of its form. And distill it by the way of continuation with infusion from the bath of I. in decoction, afterward place the dissolved gold in a glass cucurbit, and distill the water, and separate the whole, and the substance will remain by dissolving the gold. When it is entirely dry, afterward take of our [water], and distill through the alembic, until you see through one day of return its stronger [form], which cannot burn further, then continue the distillation by returning to this, and with the water collect until through the grains of the alembic no trace of the veins appears.

--- Page M34 (printed page 34, Liber Mercuriorum, Caput XVIII-XIX) ---
RAYMVNDI LVLLI

In this water dissolve [?] the substance of gold, and immediately it will be dissolved in water through the evening [?] distillation of Mercury. Afterward rectify its Mercury from the phlegm, until you see that it burns; then mix with the substance of gold, and it is the water of life.

CAPVT DECIMVM NONVM.
De vino & humoribus acuti corporis humani, &

You shall take of the water of life, and you shall separate all its moisture through distillation and the substance of the water, which is in the manner of gold, of vegetable brazilwood. You shall place a third part of pure honey with all its substances, namely with wax, and with honey, and you shall place it to ferment in lightening [?] through the bath of distillation in the bath. And this distillation and fermentation you shall repeat by returning the pure [substance] at each sec-

--- Page M35 (printed page 35, Liber Mercuriorum, Caput XIX-XXII) ---

ond distillation through nine times.

CAPVT VIGESIMVM.
De constitutione secunda aquæ.

Take one old capon, or one plucked hen, and cast out the innards, and with its [parts], and whatever may be in its old belly, and let the feet be cut to pieces, the flesh separated from the bones, and all the flesh shall be pounded; afterward place in an alembic in the bath, distilling all the water, and set it aside.

CAPVT VIGESIMVM PRIMVM.
De constitutione tertia aquæ.

Take the flesh of a hen, or of a capon, and over ashes distill all its moisture, with which medium between the converted [substance] and the tomato [?], and having finished with combining its constitution, and set the moisture aside.

CAPVT VIGESIMVM SECVNDVM.

--- Page M36 (printed page 36, Liber Mercuriorum, Caput XXII-XXIV) ---
RAYMVNDI LVLLI

De constitutione quarta aquæ.

Take of the simple moisture of the said lunaria, and of the first place three parts upon the substance of the said flesh, afterward close the cucurbit with its glass cover, and with common wax, and place the whole upon ashes for three natural days with fire maintaining [its] composition, afterward place the alembic above, and distill all the water through the alembic, and preserve it set aside.

CAPVT VIGESIMVM TERTIVM.
De constitutione quinta aquæ.

Take the said substance of the hen, or of the capon, and over ashes [extract] all the moisture of those species through distillation.

CAPVT VIGESIMVM
quartum. De constitutione sexta aquæ.

Take all the bones of the said capon, or hen, well pounded into small pieces, place in an alembic,

alembic, and over ashes take all its distilled liquor.

De distillatione tertiam aquarum per

Take the third water, and the fourth, and the sixth, and combine them together, afterward distill through the alembic, and set aside.

CAPVT VIGESIMVM SEXTVM.
De modo administrationis.

Take gold, which is a terminated water, and of this kind subtle, and of celestial [nature], and of a moderate citrine color, similar to orpiment, and place half of it in the first water, and immediately it will be dissolved into a glorious water. Of this water take to the quantity of one small ounce of the acidity of the nuptials [?], mix with a large part of white wine in winter, and give to the phlegmatic [patient] in winter. And if [the patient] is choleric, give with simi-

--- Page M38 (printed page 38, Liber Mercuriorum, Caput XXVI cont.) ---
RAYMVNDI LVLLI

ple water. And to the melancholic give with broth of dog, or let it be cooked with mutton. And if [the patient] is sanguine, do not give him of this water, but of that which is figured, for his coldness simply. Take of the water of gold to the quantity of half a spoonful, and they will come forth secure from every illness, and you shall rectify against the quality of the season. And if [the patient] is afflicted, to the phlegmatic give with broth of hen. And if [the patient] is infirm of the law of ill health. And you ought to give this water when the season is stirred in its great action, whether in cold or in heat. And if you wish to give [it] to the infirm, administer in this manner. Take the golden medicine, dissolved [?] in the water of capons; if [the patient] is phlegmatic, give him the medicament with two parts of this water, and do likewise if he is sanguine. And if he is choleric, and in like adjustment [?], give with broth of flesh, and give with almonds; in the same manner you shall do for the melancholic. You need not give nor do [anything] for anyone if within [a number of] days there is no cur-

--- Page M39 (printed page 39, Liber Mercuriorum, Caput XXVI cont.) ---
MERCVRIORVM LIB.

cure, or at least improvement. Nor trouble yourself to know the diseases: because wise nature through its infusion has given virtues to this distillate, both for overcoming all diseases, and for rectifying itself, just as we have said more broadly in the treatise on medicinal waters. By such a method you can administer the medicine of the human body, unless perchance, since the members are thus, and disposed through the industry of so great an art as through the industry of nature, and if at the appropriate time the remedy has been applied, so it is necessary to administer with proportionate waters. But that [medicine] against great diseases is applied: if [the disease] is contrary, administer to it to the quantity of one grain of millet of felt [?] strength, whether it is returned to its own use as broth, or in clarified wine, or its own liquor, or clear, according to the judgment that you shall know [how] to apply as [suits] the appetite. And if this medicine has an affin-

--- Page M40 (printed page 40, Liber Mercuriorum, Caput XXVI-XXVII) ---
RAYMVNDI LVLLI

ity for [working] against hot and cold diseases, both natural and accidental. When you wish to move from one land to another, and you wish to carry the said medicine, whether simple or composite, you shall congeal it. And afterward you can [apply] it in herbs or in other things, as the art of things [requires], and through these there can be made electuaries, through potable liquor, or in every food wherever you may be; it will have such virtue, or so many virtues, which are incredible to those who do not know, whether through devotion of this kind to the Lapidary. And when you wish to administer it to a leper, you shall give [it] with common cold water: and to those suffering from venereal [disease], with white wine.

CAPVT VIGESIMVM SEPTIMVM.
De furno, & vasis. Et primum de furno.

For preparing and composing the said medicine, whether the mother and empress of all other medici-

--- Page M41 (printed page 41, Liber Mercuriorum, Cap. XXVII cont.) ---
MERCVRIORVM LIB.

nes, it is necessary that you have four or five small furnaces: one for calcining, one for distilling upward and downward, a third for imbibition, a fourth for distilling in the bath of Mary, and a fifth prepared for interrupted operations and for correcting defects and amending errors, according to what the teaching of nature shall have shown you. And they ought to be round, of proper [size], with three middle [shelves] in each, with round openings cut in the middle of the bottom, of the size of the full magnitude, or of a chaplain [?], or of the width of four fingers, and with covers that can be raised and opened when the work requires it, with clasps fitted for this purpose. And from one middle [shelf] to the other let there be a space of one broad palm, and let each middle [shelf] have its door, through which it can be opened and accessed, according to its proportion. And these furnaces should be arranged conveniently one next to the other in a row, and in each furnace let there be in each of them

--- Page M42 (printed page 42, Liber Mercuriorum, Cap. XXVII cont.) ---
RAYMVNDI LVLLI

a corner with a conduit, so that it can receive the heat of the others below and above, and let it be made below near the bottoms, and so that the closure may be open or shut as the work requires. And thus you will have good and perpetual furnaces for maintaining the diverse fires which the medicine requires up to the flowering and completion of the work. And if at some time you have tested the small furnaces twice or more according to what the matter requires, whether in the bath or in ashes or in the fire of flame. For in the bath we distill our waters, and over ashes or our figured [substances]. Therefore let the flame be on the outside. And thus with the fire of flame we fix our spirits, because it is required, and thus it is that which gives the medicine its active power and grants to it that which it does. And if during the time of prolonged decoction you require the moderate [fire] of the philosophers, in your practice it is necessary that you have four small furnaces of one me-

--- Page M43 (printed page 43, Liber Mercuriorum, Cap. XXVII-XXVIII) ---
MERCVRIORVM LIB.

asure for this reason: because particular medicines do not require so many diversities of digestion, but only three, which can be carried out in small furnaces, arranged for cooking through diverse and particular branches. And if this is a great disposition of joy for wisely directing the operations of nature, and for having patience in the decoctions of a great medicine. And thus it is for avoiding vice, you can make many [medicines].

CAPVT VIGESIMVM OCTAVVM.
De vasis proprijs habendis.

For composing all medicines you need nothing but one form of vessel, which vessel is indeed threefold, namely one cover, one alembic, and one cucurbit of canna-

--- Page M44 (printed page 44, Liber Mercuriorum, Cap. XXVIII cont.) ---
RAYMVNDI LVLLI

bis [?], which vessel has no difference according to the difference of operations: for when it is used for distilling, it is called a distillatory through its alembic. For dissolving, it is called a dissolvatory; for putrefying, a putrefactory; for calcining it is called a calcinatory or mortificatory; or for congealing, a congelatory, a crematory, which is an informatory; for roasting, a roastatory, an odentatory, and a terminatory. And yet it has no form but that of one vessel only. But each medicine requires having its own nature or its own alembic for this purpose. And through this it is said: when we say, son, "medicine" in this chapter, this is to be understood as a simple [substance] by itself, or a composite by itself — simple, such as earth only, or water, or air, or fire only — not of those already made into composed [substances] that have been administered. And likewise of each of those composites, such as a stone, which is creat-

--- Page M45 (printed page 45, Liber Mercuriorum, Cap. XXVIII-XXIX) ---
MERCVRIORVM LIB.

ed from two simple elements, namely earth and water; or as that which is to be specified of composites from simples and from composites, and of sulphur and of ferments, and of simples such as earth and water. And therefore, son, when the water shall have been prepared with its proportionate judge [?] or process, whether it be through addition, so let the simple medicines be, or accidental [ones]. And likewise of other elements and simple medicines. Wherefore let it be manifest to you that when two things can be done in one time, do not accept delay, or make successive times for the division of future [operations]. Therefore, because sometimes you can do two things at once, and sometimes three or four, what you cannot do, and something may have leave, through its continual cold [processes]. And if, son, it is possible for you to separate in order to compose, and to its regular medicine by dissolution [?].

CAPVT VIGESIMVM NONVM.
Quo-

--- Page M46 (printed page 46, Liber Mercuriorum, Cap. XXIX cont.) ---
RAYMVNDI LVLLI

Quomodo debes intelligere elementa.

You must understand, son, that the elements are all composites, which nature cannot sustain except in the matter of one simple composite from other composite elements of fine matter, and of clear mentality [?], through the variation of elementary qualities, in which it is sustained, and a vegetable virtue has been given, which through our elements, son, are in each of them. And they are such that each is in the form of a circle, and each circle is called a simple mixture. Son, the name of each element we take from the nature of its property in the properties of its [parts]. And that which [pertains to] its coldness, and that which [pertains to] its heat on account of its [nature], and likewise of the others. Furthermore, looking at the terminations of the body of animal, and of vegetable matter, and of mineral, they are administered in each of them. And because bodies are most justly generated naturally from the elements

--- Page M47 (printed page 47, Liber Mercuriorum, Cap. XXIX cont.) ---
MERCVRIORVM LIB.

according to the gradation of their diverse [qualities] implicated in vegetable [things], or in those that are brought together, properly by diverse forms of natures diversely constituted, of which some are clear elements, and especially mineral bodies, and every thing of which one part is through its own [nature], and the relation itself is said to be the medicine of a determined generation of the complexion that it has, notwithstanding whatever may have been made from composite elements, and tempered from contrary qualities. And because one of the composites in the given internal [parts], and may receive for itself through a homogeneous form a lesser nature, which one is said to be the alteration of things, which apparent mixtures, so that they may come to the composition of a lower power, in which the altered thing may be said [to be], but also if repeated in all things, and then the composite is perfected of the spherical composites of the four spherical circles, which [are] quadrangular and perfect when they shall have been divided.

--- Page M48 (printed page 48, Liber Mercuriorum, Cap. XXIX cont.) ---
RAYMVNDI LVLLI

Wherefore, son, you must understand that our elements are composite and elemented, because in our earth there is living fire. And therefore because that is through an appropriated complexion from the parts, and from the same itself, and the same contained in it, and they participate in none according to the greater and lesser properties of their extremities; and likewise it is of the other elements: because in our water there is air, and fire, and earth, and thus it is that from the properties of their [natures] there remained a complaint, that they were made, that is, its earth or air. And therefore we implicate their fire greatly through the heat of others, and we temper the overflowing in the coldness of water, and in the four [elements] we fix them formally, which is the matter of sulphur. Now of humidity and under the genus of our nature, son, oil is the matter of a burning light in a lamp. In such matter, properly appropriated, our fire is to be augmented, since our sulphur, which burns and consumes under the hand [?] —

--- Page M49 (printed page 49, Liber Mercuriorum, Cap. XXIX cont.) ---
MERCVRIORVM LIB.

by which indeed through its sulfurous power, by its own property, which congeals all ar. viui. Receive, son, an experimental example of the reasoning of our philosophy, which will openly show you in our mastery that, just as simple natural heat terminates its simple natural moisture by digesting and congealing it according to the subtilization of well-measured nourishment in proportion to the power of the digesting natural heat, as the first regimen of reductions in the substance of sulphur demonstrates. So likewise the composite multiplied heat, which is in the moisture of the mother, digests and matures the crude matter of common ar. viui composed by the termination of metallic form. Multiply therefore, son, in the subtle substance of our ar. viui, and you will congeal that composite. For nature shows you that a child at the breasts, sucking them, cannot take strong food, unless through natural heat first

--- Page M50 (printed page 50, Liber Mercuriorum, Cap. XXIX cont. / Cap. XXX) ---
RAYMVNDI LVLLI

it is first digested, cooked, and subtilized, and converted into the substance of milk, which is the food and the infant's proper nourishment, and nature requires and generates this thing generally in its first actions, for nourishing its fruit or infant. And that which it could not take in coarse food, let it draw and suck in the substance of milk, until the infant or fetus can be nourished and receive coarser food. And let your mirror be generation, and the first nourishment of the infant in creating the stone, and herein lies the whole regimen of health. To which every good physician ought firmly to adhere through understanding.

CAPVT XXX.
De animatione elementorum, & de cognitione ipsorum.

Our argentum viuum, son, is apt and suitable, or a part of it is of the water

--- Page M51 (printed page 51, Liber Mercuriorum, Cap. XXX cont.) ---
MERCVRIORVM LIB.

which was distilled from its earth. And the earth likewise is also our animated argentum viuum. And the soul is the natural heat, which has been collected in the first essence of the elements of argentum viuum. Wherefore we make known to you that some portion of this heat the water must retain. For otherwise it would not be animated. And to accomplish this we elementate it. Because natural heat elementates and animates the elements in appropriate dissolution and putrefaction, which naturally, through natural heat penetrating all elemental parts, diffuses and spreads through the entire matter essentially, and that once diffused it binds itself into the elemental parts through the smallest and most minute portions, so that in the separation of elements the bound and coupled heat in the essential matter of the elements may be drawn out through distillation. And thus the matter of each element will remain animated by natural heat, which is the unifying heat, from which

--- Page M52 (printed page 52, Liber Mercuriorum, Cap. XXX cont.) ---
RAYMVNDI LVLLI

all elements are enriched. And the more heavily and ponderously the water is distilled, the more it will be coupled and bound to the essential parts of argent. vi. to which natural heat is coupled, as is apparent in oil and other hot liquefiable [substances]. Consider therefore, son, the difference, as it is between water and oil, for sight does not so much show the difference through its species alone, but also through their effect. If you know how to test and approve, if you are a good theorist in our mastery. When therefore one part of the heat of nature is separated from another, as by resolution, then the matter flows and melts, and flees and separates from the greater power, since in flowing it has lost from its natural heat some of its own and connatural moisture. When otherwise through conjunction the body itself receives [it], then the body returns with great appetite through strengthening, and through re-

--- Page M53 (printed page 53, Liber Mercuriorum, Cap. XXX cont.) ---
MERCVRIORVM LIB.

storation is made whole, and thus sulphur is joined with sulphur, and moisture with like moisture. Because the nature of fire, namely, both heats the matter, and the matter is multiplied in the bearing of its essence and nature, and makes the radical moisture entirely new, as easy experience can show you in the stone of great light, especially after the separation of elements, since it always has the appetite to possess its own moisture, in which natural heat resides, and in which it impresses its form and likeness. See that the matter upon which the heat must work and impress its essence is well subtilized, so that the whole can become fire and radical moisture, and that the matter through long decoction is subtilized and completed in contrary regimens, which are the works of nature. Here understand, son, that you need a twofold heat of fire: common

--- Page M54 (printed page 54, Liber Mercuriorum, Cap. XXX cont. / Cap. XXXI) ---
RAYMVNDI LVLLI

namely moist [heat] that collects and conjoins, and through the nourishment of the spirit, and dry [heat] that destroys and dissolves phlegms after the condensation of spirits terminated through moist fire.

CAPVT XXXI.
Quomodo debes intelligere actiones ad temperandum elixir.

You should know, son, for the purpose of tempering your elixir, that every temperament is constituted through the primary qualities, conjoined in the immediate essences of the elements. And therefore it is necessary for you to temper them through strengthening, when nature so wills. But as you can see, the primary qualities cannot be had except under a composite and visible substantial form, therefore it is necessary that the substance of each element be greatly purified and subtilized, so that

--- Page M55 (printed page 55, Liber Mercuriorum, Cap. XXXI cont.) ---
MERCVRIORVM LIB.

the [substance] of one element does not disturb the temperament of the medicine through the quality of another, which is due to a defect of preparation. Wherefore let it be manifest to you that if in the water there is any undigested earth, which is owed to its completion, three [times] after its preparation it will be poorly rectified, which will retain in its belly [something] of dry quality, which is the proper quality of earth. And through that the cold of the stone is disturbed in performing its action. And so with the other elements. See therefore that the defect of preparation does not confound the dry with the cold, nor the cold with the moist, nor the moist with the hot, nor the hot with the moist. For otherwise you could not temper the stone, nor bring it to a certain degree, especially since it is of great necessity for you to know divisibly how much you must add of the four elements. Therefore when you rectify the elements through the degrees of separation easily made, so that the stone may proceed and operate according to its four

--- Page M56 (printed page 56, Liber Mercuriorum, Cap. XXXI cont.) ---
RAYMVNDI LVLLI

elemental qualities. And let each of them be strong according to the quality of its element, insofar as is possible. Therefore it is necessary that you temper the stone from four excellent qualities, and the said four excellent qualities are not found except in simple elements. And the elements are simply prepared so that the quality of one is not disturbed by the quality of another, and from this results and is known the temperament according to weakening or strengthening, which are an easy way of reduction. And thus at another turn the elements are bound, and are made into one composite water, which contains within itself all four prepared elements, which water indeed is fixed through the smallest parts, and in its ferment through mutual reductions is congealed, and by that method the elements are tempered through the mutual action of contrary qualities, and there results a composite of one complexion in temperament

--- Page M57 (printed page 59, Liber Mercuriorum, Caput XXXI cont.) ---
MERCVRIORVM LIB.

of hot and moist, cold and dry qualities after this composition has a temperament through cold moisture, and then it is tempered with respect to cold and moist, and it is now no longer opposed to what is moist and hot, since it moistens and elevates, which it does through this temperament.

CAPVT XXXII.
De virtutis instrumenti sine quibus operatio naturæ
Principijs sine quibus consistit.

In this art you must consider three instrumental subjects, namely the vegetable, which is the truest principal of transformation of all substances, and the elemental, which is the greatest principal of the elements, and the instrumental by reason. Which three cannot be only two, and all of these require that they be joined at least in the matter from which our magnificence is made, because if the vegetable were not present in the matter, the transformation of nature through its effects could not be received in

--- Page M58 (printed page 60, Liber Mercuriorum, Caput XXXII cont.) ---
RAYMVNDI LVLLI

those principal instruments rightly in the instruments of this. Son, natural fire is [one]. And if the elementary [instrument] were not present, the elements could not be well elementated. For that which cannot be well elementated by other elements, and especially cannot be nourished by earth itself, and consequently would not have substance for a false constitution, because if the supreme medium were present, it would nonetheless have substantial powers by which it could effect change. And through this you understand and are able [to see] that in the simple [form] of all three, the elements achieve little, since nature manifestly demonstrates to all that the elements are made instrumentally through nature: because the elements of the three produce marvelous effects through their mixtures. And art likewise, since it follows nature, shows you through principles that the elementary [quality] of this is the perfect body's manifest effect. And they produce miracles in the mixture of elements more than in simple ones, because in those mixed

--- Page M59 (printed page 61, Liber Mercuriorum, Caput XXXII cont.) ---
MERCVRIORVM LIB.

[states] the first essence of the elements [is found], and from those they are nourished and fed by virtue of the said two instruments, and thus they make themselves similar, for the [quality] of each one is greater together with that of the other. And they can bring forth miracles less in isolation, [than] when combined together through their proper qualities. But if the binding and congregation is made from diverse proportions, they produce in themselves what is fitting and capital. And this will be your instruction in the chapter on administration, and in the final chapters, and in that immediately preceding. Wherefore it is clear that it is necessary for you that the matter not be without that instrument by which the elements are elementated. And if the instrument is joined to the administration, it will not have the means to govern its elements for elementating according to nature by empowering itself through the composition of the vegetable instrument as mediator, since by itself it could not be what it then could be

--- Page M60 (printed page 62, Liber Mercuriorum, Caput XXXII cont.) ---
RAYMVNDI LVLLI

through the difference of successive operation. For when we wish to place instrumentality in the matter, we proceed to the regimen of dissolving upon the red [substance]. And if this be so, or if it be a small eliminable [portion] of treasured [matter], to which we wish to gently elementate the elements from the things we desire, and following only that we pass over to the regimen, and if there the abithanases wishes to begin immediately, which is the cause of transition, and it abrades from the elements those essences remaining in the sulphur, through the operation of the three regimens, for which the congelation of nature occurs, that is, sulphur. Son, the matter from which sulphur is created is the substance of the air of gold, from the cistern of the first composition of the mortified [substance], insofar as the part from which the pure matter of the mortified [substance], insofar as the part by virtue of the said operations, by which powers have transmutations from which [?] into something better, [it] is into the perfect. Thus through the mean of natures and ambition. And in the composition of discipline and of another

--- Page M61 (printed page 63, Liber Mercuriorum, Caput XXXII cont.) ---
MERCVRIORVM LIB.

simple [form]. And it is similar with the coldness of water, which is contained through terrestrial dryness, as to its place and is opened, so that it may remain in its state or truly [pass] into the further places of air. And concerning the allocation through the sharpness of fire for penetration: when it is in its body, it is more capable and apt to refrigerate it, and to harden throughout its whole mixture and compound, which [is] much more than if it were simple. And likewise it is with fire in terrestrial earth: it could not change, nor dry or congeal moist bodies without the aid of the other elements of the others, which is [done] through fire, by sublimating it. Therefore. Through this, then, which has been principally said, the cause has been brought to knowledge, and on account of which we say the stone would not [be] what it is created [to be] unless it emanates from the elements, rightly in these instruments of nature, and if it is read with understanding: concerning the other deprived [matters] entirely, just as it would be shown to you by warnings

--- Page M62 (printed page 64, Liber Mercuriorum, Caput XXXII cont.) ---
RAYMVNDI LVLLI

you can stand and [see] clear experiments. And since simple elements would not have [the power] to elicit, the ancient philosophers called the mixed [substance] a binder and a marvelous mixture, through which another virtue beyond the three instruments of practice results, which itself was discerning in the elementated [substance]. And if [you take] the whole element, or in the whole body read all the virtues which are constituted in the composition, and you will know and understand that their number is, namely: the action of each simple element which is not essentially the proposed instrumentality, nor is there another of these instrumental [things]. The second is the virtue of the proportion of the mixture or mixtures. The third is the virtue which aids the mixed action of the said elements, through which combined virtue the instrument is constituted. And this is the elementary [virtue], or the virtue of elementality. The fourth is the celestial virtue. The fifth indeed is [the virtue] of the vegetable instrument. And through these five virtues the

--- Page M63 (printed page 65, Liber Mercuriorum, Caput XXXII cont. / Caput XXXIII) ---
MERCVRIORVM LIB.

action in our medicine is established and formed. And likewise the mineral place of this [is] a helper. Nature, however, does not use [it] as medicine to that extent of sweetness, just as that which it is, however through [?] it may be. Son, when we arrive at the celestial virtue, understand it by the virtue of common fire, which has [the power] to move the vegetable instrument, which is within its matter and form. And if the stellar [virtue] is to be well restored to their [purpose] the common [fire] is to be informed and [made] technical, and the common fire is informed and directed through the practical respect of the wise person with the acceptance of celestial virtue, to such an extent that it is well informed concerning the proportion of vegetable [substances], such fire would have greater appreciation of celestial virtue. And therefore we call it the philosophical common [fire], but what its action shows us in our mastery.

CAPVT XXXIII.
Quomodo subiecta per operationem instrumenti
condescendunt, & qualiter capiuntur
in stare.

--- Page M64 (printed page 66, Liber Mercuriorum, Caput XXXIII) ---
RAYMVNDI LVLLI

Subjects in this art are taken in a twofold manner, namely as instruments, as we have named above, and which are not attainable in the mastery. And the other instrumental subjects pertain to nothing except to single things, which the art cannot receive on account of the rarity of their matter: therefore it cannot avail either concerning imperfect or perfect [substances] in the medicinal matter, unless in them there is the property of two opposites organized through the science of stellar and harmonic nature into instruments of sensuality, so that through art it may arrive discrete and apt through alteration to facilitate the confusion. And to such an extent that through the word we may urge [them] toward certain demonstrated superior impelled virtues, just as stones, words, and herbs do, in which many virtues lie hidden: so no less through the being of the essence of superior nature, the mother of accidents, and of inferior nature through its own

--- Page M65 (printed page 69, Liber Mercuriorum, Caput XXXIII cont.) ---
MERCVRIORVM LIB.

good qualities, and subtle mixtures, whence virtues in simple materials are caused, bound and useless to the gross according to the sense and understanding of the superior natures, which for itself makes worldly things, just as we say all things with good understanding in the volume of sensible things. And you must understand the subjects by another mode of nature in the place where they are through all the instruments, which are without the matter of the transmutation of special things, notwithstanding the difference of its own proper nature and concordance, and of the determination of the said matter. And by another [way] we tell you that you must consider the diverse mode of subjects materially as well as instrumentally in four conditions, so that the habituated understanding may remain [directed] toward the difference of the said work materially and the plain of gross materiality, and if sublimating instrumentally together, yet the instrumental

--- Page M66 (printed page 70, Liber Mercuriorum, Caput XXXIII cont.) ---
RAYMVNDI LVLLI

[qualities] are always with the material [ones]. And secondly, that any subject, whether material or instrumental, is conditioned naturally according to its own proper nature. Therefore, because the material and instrumental subject, which are in the green regime, have one condition between themselves and other conditions in the truo [vessel] wherein it is compelled. In the green regime the material subject dominates over the matter of its gross substance, which enters the instrumental [subject], which is not the work, that it should depart from here through extracted fire, until it be fixed and generated in the subtle substance. And in the congealed truo [vessel] the instrument of rarity dominates and the site of the matter through special acquisition, which receives and retains [it] from flying away by water, in which are its vivifying souls, which wish to vivify, and after it to bring forth so that they may work and generate flowered things, that is, verified [things]. And from this I give you an example in quicksilver sublimated, when vivification of it

--- Page M67 (printed page 71, Liber Mercuriorum, Caput XXXIII cont.) ---
MERCVRIORVM LIB.

and impression occurs. But afterward through sublimation it is lost, unless it returns through reiterated sublimation, which carries life to it through the known operation, and by what mode of work and true merit. Son, the condition that you must have of this science is this: you must be habituated so that from any subject you may draw out its proper virtue, under the conservation of its essence in the proper distinction and formation of nature, from which you can dispose it to the utility of another subject. The second condition is that in practice, considering the difference of subjects both material and instrumental, [one] causes vegetation and composition in the body in its virtues and substance, which is the fume of frozen water and of the gross body and perfect [substance]. The third condition is that [things] are concorded, which is between one sublimate and another with the power [by which something is] impressionally destroyed, just as is the concordance which is between earth and waters,

--- Page M68 (printed page 72, Liber Mercuriorum, Caput XXXIII cont.) ---
RAYMVNDI LVLLI

and air and fire, or between body and spirit. For the spirit is concorded with the corporeal in composition, and that instrumentally through the difference of concordance: and the body with the spirit into spirituality likewise through the difference of concordance, through the concord of unity which is our disposition concerning them. The fourth condition is that in the nobler subject there are attributes, and the elements are adapted and participated to the nobler [forms], and the essence to the nobler principles as they are in the stone, insofar as it was created. Because it is nobler in virtue and through substance than the mineral matter in which it was created, and proof is used [to show this]: that earth more than fire, and fire more than air, and the mixed more than the simple. And through such conditions the understanding of philosophy can immediately become habituated, for whoever wishes to practice in the works of nature, making and composing the treatise which is called, Vade mecum from the hand of the philosophers.

--- Page M69 (printed page 73, Liber Mercuriorum, Caput XXXV) ---
MERCVRIORVM LIB.

CAPVT XXXV.
De temperamento lapidis, & qualiter est in quatuor
elementis cum dispositione temperamenti.

This stone, if it be black, is in the four elements, and in all its elements it is wholly in any one of them after their separation, which is called the mixture of things of mixed nature. For it is wholly in earth, but it is dead without sand or dryness, and is without life through its great dryness. And moreover we tell you that it is wholly in its water. But because there it lies mortified by excessive coldness without life. And moreover we tell you that it is wholly in its air, and in it lies mortified, and does not live, and in it unless there is its heat, because unless the natural heat is suffocated by the excelling humidity. And furthermore, it is wholly in fire, but because in these heats the forces of creating nature's actions do not remain, on account of which it has been revealed to it that in no extremity of

--- Page M70 (printed page 74, Liber Mercuriorum, Caput XXXV cont.) ---
RAYMVNDI LVLLI

excelling qualities the complexion is tempered, through which all the actions of life result, unless it be in mixed [substances]. If therefore the excelling qualities are all equally proportioned to one maximum, namely that the hot stone, the cold stone, the dry stone, and the moist stone are equally separated in likeness, the complexion will be most pure, which carries the nature of true temperament through the excellence of the great primary qualities, as do mineral actions. And therefore let it be manifest to you that no complexion or qualitative complexional [state] is excellent except in the elements, when they are imparted in mixed action through the mean of the material and elemental, augmented: the mediation in one more perfect species of the mixed [substance] can be constituted. Therefore here it appears that the action of life cannot exist without a mean: add earth and water, as the requirements of nature [demand] in the smallest parts, until the stone is regarded, so that there is in it neither in it

--- Page M71 (printed page 75, Liber Mercuriorum, Caput XXXV cont.) ---
MERCVRIORVM LIB.

[excess] from it, and from what has been said, the monstrosity that is by nature incomplete, when the water is congealed in the vapor of its earth, and then the mean of nature will be approximated to air. Nevertheless, take heed that one must swear by nature [?], as you may grasp from the extremity, but it could be tempered through air, and it is heavy on account of having ingress, because it does not yet participate so much in air, insofar as [it departs] from earth. It returns therefore, and begins diversely [to pour] water upon the white earth, and then with it it waits, and the mean of air is more approximated than that of earth, and because the cold compresses so much by the expulsion of humidity, then the individual [substance] will be proportionably veiled in portions and the patience of the nature of air is vibrated toward the waters circularly. Therefore the humidity of air is thus proportionally added to be tempered through water. And the virtue of the extremity being alive above the heat [?], and that the air may be

--- Page M72 (printed page 76, Liber Mercuriorum, Caput XXXV cont.) ---
RAYMVNDI LVLLI

tempered, and the moist through the dry and the dry through the moist, [so that] the whole be desiccated through the proportion of their continuations, as an impression, so that if you make your projection, the said moist [substance] may acquire with the dry proportionally through water upon earth. So that no impression exists except through the moist bound in the belly of the dry, and the dry in the belly of the moist, and from this binding there comes about through the suffering of one and the other through continuous decoction. And it is possible if it were in a calamity, when the moist feels coldness, it hides itself and enters into the belly of the dry, and if the dry finds excelling heat, it will defend itself from that in the belly of the moist, and this can be, so that all things may be considered, and then through the moist there is given to it the impression that is water, and the life of the united [substances]. Son, if such a dry [substance] by itself in the fire should wish to return to a dark [state], and the half-[consumed substance] may repair its work: this the moist calls dry, and in such a manner the battle against the dry fire

--- Page M75 (printed page 77, Liber Mercuriorum, Caput XXXV cont.) ---
MERCVRIORVM LIB.

in its defense it has sustained itself through the humidity of the separations of its parts. And similarly, that through the moist [substance] the dry is denied from the yoke of humidity, and the desiccability [becomes] heavier and corporated through D it can be, or still recede, since the impression has already been made. Therefore, if you strive when the dry vapor has been constructed in its exhalation, and expressed through homology, and constituted in a new redundant body, to open and make for itself a crystal, so that it may fix through the moist, the hot, the waxy, the loosened [parts], through the parts of the dry [substance] the crystallization of light is continued in construction, while effectively, with the flowing ingredients entering, in that proportion to itself of that vaporousness, but solidifying that which was formed from its own virtue. And [it is] the quicksilver and the life of all liquefiable bodies, and commonly of the non-liquefiable absolutes. And if it is thus, it is the [stone] of the philosophers, in the end sought [and] terminated through its own

--- Page M76 (printed page 78, Liber Mercuriorum, Caput XXXV cont.) ---
RAYMVNDI LVLLI

own proper means brought to the perfection of its [qualities] through the entirety which is derived from it. Indeed, concerning the good principles, let its fixation suffice, and soundly in them through its own means let it constitute, just as has already been seen in the projection which comes from the metal and is terminated in the metal through the natural consummation of motions, which are lightning. Consequently again also permutations. Therefore we say that the stone is not made until it has been optimally dissolved four times, and afterward congealed. Because one informed [thing] of the extremes proceeds to the tempered reference of the extreme in its parts. Unless it is thus, nature would never go to its perfection unless it transfers itself through the means of time, which then distemper themselves and contract the qualities of all the elements first, and this residue is composed to its proper nature in the individual [substance], which is moreover the nature and matter of the supreme metals.
