--- Page F1 (printed page 71, Practica de Furnis, end of Liber Primus / start of Liber Secundus) ---
TESTAMEN. NOVISS.

above is descending from the Father of lights, with
whom there is no change, nor shadow of turning.
And he who fears God shall do good. Therefore
you shall not set your mind upon vain things,
nor upon perverse thoughts, while you wish to
attain the grace of God: For the most great God
resists the proud, but gives grace and glory
to the humble. To whom be praise and glory forever. Amen.

TESTAMENTI NO-
VISSIMI RAIMVNDI
LVLLII
DE PRACTICA.
Liber Secundus.

Son, to compose the said medicine, the mother
and Empress of all metallic medicines, you must
have three furnaces: one for calcining and
distilling through ashes, another for burying
in a water bath, and another which is ready
for interrupted operations, namely for correcting
errors according to the sign that nature will
show you. And the furnaces must be round on
the inside, and with three middle sections made
strong from earth, of solar earth which withstands
fire, broken in the middle, all in the round to the
size of a cleric's tonsure, or to the length of four
fingers, in such a manner that it can be covered

--- Page F2 (printed page 72, Practica de Furnis, Liber Secundus cont.) ---
RAIMVNDI LVLLII

and opened: and when it is necessary, with its own
piece, and from one middle section to another let
there be a good half-palm in length, and let each
middle section have its own door, which can be
closed and opened, according to the diversity of
fires, and let them all be joined together, one
after another, in length, and in the furnace in the
middle make one receptacle in each of its middle
sections to the end that it can receive the heat
of the others from above and below, and that
the aforesaid may be enclosed and below against
the floor. And that it can be closed and opened
when necessary, and you will have perpetual
furnaces for maintaining diverse fires, which the
medicine requires for its completion, and when you
wish to do something, adapt your furnaces
according to what the matter requires, whether in
a Bath, or in ashes, or in a flame fire. For in a
Bath we distill waters, in ashes we separate our
airy substances, and we calcine earth in the form
of clear water: and in a flame fire we fix spirits,
because it is optetic. And this is what gives the
medicine its actual power for all that it does. And
if, while the great medicine is pending, you wish
to philosophize in your practice, you must have
small furnaces of only one middle section: because
particular medicines do not require so many
diversities of digestions, but only such things as
can be done in the said furnaces, as we shall say
afterwards. And this is a great dwelling of joy,
for digesting the spirit in all our works, for having
patience in the slowness of the decoction of the
great medicine, because while not standing
idle, you can do many
other things.

--- Page F3 (printed page 73, Practica de Furnis, De Vasis / De Medicina) ---
TESTAMEN. NOVISS.

De Vasis.

Son, you must understand that for composing all
medicines you need only the form of one vessel,
which is of three pieces, namely of one cover, and
of one alembic, and of one cucurbit. But it takes
a different name according to the diversity of its
operation: for when it is for the purpose of
dissolving, it is called a dissolvatory; or for
putrefying, it is called a putrefactory; for
distilling, it is called a distillatory with its
alembic; or for calcining, it is called a calcinatory
and mortificatory; or for congealing, and it is
called a congealatory, and a reductory, a
sublimatory, an adjutory, a vivificatory, a
ceratory, and a burying vessel, a putrefactory,
an attenuatory, a condensatory, etc. And the form
is always only one. But each medicine requires its
own vessel formed, as has been said, of glass with
its cover and with its alembic.

De Medicina.

Son, when we say in this chapter "medicine," we
say this meaning the simple by itself, and the
composite by itself, without conjunction of other
elements, and so of each of the other composite
elements, such as the stone, which is created from
two simple elements, namely from earth and water,
or the elixir, which is made from composites and
simples: from composites, such as from sulphur
and from ferments; and from simples, such as
from water and air. And therefore, son, since each

--- Page F4 (printed page 74, Practica de Furnis, De Medicina cont. / Quomodo debes intelligere elementa) ---
RAIMVNDI LVLLII

is prepared, it needs its own proper vessel made
as was said above, just as a simple medicine, and
air likewise, and so for the other elements and
simple medicines. Wherefore let it be made clear
to you that two things can be done simultaneously
in one vessel at one time. Do not let delay or
lingering or length of time distress you for lack
of vessels, because sometimes you can do two
things at once, sometimes three, which you could
not do if you were in scarcity of vessels, which
are all of one form, and that form suffices for you
to compose and bring to a real conclusion the
medicine.

Quomodo debes intelligere
elementa.

Son, you must understand this: the Elements are
all composite things, because nature must not be
sustained except in the matter of a simple
composite, with other composite elements, of fine
and clear matter elementally through the
elementative virtue, in which the vegetable virtue
is sustained. Wherefore, son, our elements are in
each of them, and each of them is in the form of
a circle, and the simple mixture circulates in
courses. Son, the mode of each element we take
by reason of its determinate property, as water by
its coldness, and fire by its heat, and so of the
rest. For this, with respect to such determination,
we call bodies, animal, vegetable, and mineral
elements, each according to the dominion of the
element which dominates in each of them. And on
account of this, that they are successively
generated naturally according to the graduality

--- Page F5 (printed page 75, Practica de Furnis, Quomodo intelligere debes elementa Caput II) ---
TESTAMEN. NOVISS.

of their diverse simplicity and grossness of
mediocrity, taking diverse forms and diverse
elements. Of which we call elements, and
especially mineral bodies, because every single
part is similar to another in its whole. And on
account of this our medicine is called fire: because
it determinately has the complexion of fire,
notwithstanding that it is composed of composite
elements, and tempered with contrary qualities:
and because one composite is ingressive into
another, it takes on a homogeneous form through
varied mixture, which is called the union of
altered things, which has in itself miscibility,
the cause of coming to composition, with the
power of its altered things, namely elementability
and vegetability. Then therefore the spherical
circle is perfected, with four spherical circles
which make a quadrangle, when they were
diversified, as these figures demonstrate to you
here.

Quomodo intelligere debes elementa.
Caput. II.

Wherefore, son, you must understand that our
elements are composite and elementated: because
in our earth there is illuminated fire, and therefore
it is elementated through the complexion
appropriated to it from the part of fire. And
likewise fire and air are contained in it, and yet
it participates in them according to more and less,
and the property of their extremities. And so it is
with the other elements: because in our water
there is fire, and air in earth. But fire in the
deepest part of all its regions rests more remotely
than does earth and air. And on account of

--- Page F6 (printed page 76, Practica de Furnis, Caput II cont.) ---
RAIMVNDI LVLLII

this, we multiply this fire with the heat of others,
and we temper it with the coldness of water, and
we fix it in the aquatic moisture, which is the
matter of sulphur: because moisture is the matter
of our fire, just as oil is the light that burns in
a lamp, and with such matter our fire is increased
and augmented, until it becomes sulphur: because
the fire of earth, which burns and scorches the
hand, is changed into the substance of mercurial
water, and becomes non-burning sulphur, through
the tempered coldness of incombustible water:
And afterwards it is converted into the substance
of air, when it is placed in fusion. And this is our
fire, which you must drive through certain
regimens of reduction into moist matter, which is
the boundary for leading into the matter of secret
sulphur through the radical moisture: because the
more you conceal it in subtle matter, the more
its radical moisture is multiplied, the more its
radical moisture operates: and the more subtle
its matter is, the more secretly it penetrates, with
firm radiant alteration. On account of this, son,
let this be the stated warning of this natural
operation, which you must govern with long delay.
Wherefore in a plurality of time a plurality of
parts is conjoined into one substance in which it
is united, which is a potent virtue, which is wholly
essentially composed of composite fire, with
radical moisture. And therefore since it is
composite fire from many ignited parts, it has
ignitable power to ignite other parts: and since
those parts are ignitive, they are colorative, which
have colorable power to color and tinge other parts.
And it depen-

--- Page F7 (printed page 77, Practica de Furnis, Caput II cont.) ---
TESTAMEN. NOVISS.

And the colorative depends upon the ignificative,
and from the ignifiable comes the colorifiable, and
from ignifying comes colorifying. And this is what
our tincture is: it is nothing but pure fire composed
of many coessential parts conjoined into one
through a clear and known art: Whence we say
that our fire does in one hour what the Sun and
Stars do in a thousand years. Consider therefore,
son, and understand how and by what way our
fire is nourished and grows, until it comes to that
power by which it shows its sulphurous virtue
through its property, which congeals all
quicksilver. Son, take the extreme spirimentale A
by the reason of our Philosophy, which teaches
potency in our magisterium, that just as simple
natural heat terminates its simple radical moisture
by digesting and congealing it according to the
subtlety limited to you in proportion to the virtue
of natural digesting heat, as the first regimen of
reduction manifests in the substance of sulphur,
just as congealed and multiplied heat in matured
moisture digests and matures and congeals the
arid nature of quicksilver, the conjugated
composite matter in the form of metal. Whence,
son, multiply the fire in the substance of subtle
quicksilver, and you will congeal all that is
composite: because nature demonstrates that a
newborn infant or suckling child cannot take
strong food except through the natural heat of the
womb: it is not digested unless it is first cooked
and made subtle, and converted into the juice of
milk, which is the food and proper nourishment
of the infant. And this nature naturally requires,
son, in its own

--- Page F8 (printed page 78, Practica de Furnis, Caput IV) ---
RAIMVNDI LVLLII

actions for nourishing the fetus brought to term,
that what it could not take as coarse food, it
attracts and takes in the substance of milk, until
the nourished one can take coarse food. Son, let
your mirror be the generation and change of the
human infant, for creating our stone. And herein
lies the whole according to soundness, to which
every good philosopher must direct his
understanding.

Annunciatio elementorum & notitia illorum.
argentum viuum philosophorum.
Cap. IV.

Son, our quicksilver, or a part of it, is water
distilled from its earth, and the earth likewise is
our quicksilver, animated: and the soul is the
natural heat, which stands bound in the first
essence of the elements of quicksilver. Wherefore
we make known to you that the water must have
a small amount of this heat, otherwise it would
not be animated. And in order to do this we have
the elementativity, which elementates and animates
the elements, with the concurrent dissolution and
putrefaction, which naturally, with natural heat
penetrating all elemental parts, diffuses through
the whole nature essentially, and once diffused
is bound with the said essential parts, through
the smallest parts, to the end that in the separation
of elements it is drawn through distillation. And
the matter of each element will remain animated
by the natural fire, which is the vivifying heat,
from which all elements are enriched. And the
more the genuine water has been distilled, the
more it will be bound with the essential parts of
quicksilver, with which it is the bond

--- Page F9 (printed page 79, Practica de Furnis, Cap. IV cont.) ---
TESTAMEN. NOVISS.

joined natural heat, as appears in oil
and in hot liquid things. Therefore, son, consider
the difference that exists between water and oil, for
sight will demonstrate their dissimilarity, not by
its appearance alone, but by its effect, if
you know how to verify through the intellect, if you are
a theoretician in our mastery. When therefore one part
of natural heat is separated from a greater power, since
it is the case that it is lost in its flowing from the natural
heat, and from its own proper and connatural moisture.
But when it is brought back there again, through that
conjunction the body receives it with great appetite,
through strengthening and complete restoration.
And thus conjoin it together with sulphur, and
moisture then with its like moisture: because the power of the
fire of the matter is multiplied in itself, by reason of its
essence and its nature: because it makes the radical moisture
entirely new, as can easily be demonstrated to you
by the experience of the great Lunar stone. Similarly,
after the separation of the elements, since
it always has an appetite for its own moisture,
with which the natural heat is warmed, and in
which it imprints its form and its likeness.
See to it concerning this, that the matter in which the
heat must operate and imprint its essence
be well subtilized to the end that the whole may become
fire and radical moisture, and the matter subtly
through long and gentle decoction, with
contrary regimens, which are operations of
nature. Son, understand here that you must
have a double heat from the common fire, namely
a moist heat binding and conjoining with the nourishment
of spirit, and a dry fire dissolving and destroy-

--- Page F10 (printed page 80, Practica de Furnis, Cap. V) ---
RAIMVNDI LVLLII

ing phlegmatic matter, after the condensation of the
spirits terminated by the moist fire.

How you must understand the acts for tempering
the elixir, and concerning the tempering
of the true elixir. Cap. V.

Son, you must know by intention for tempering
your Elixir, that every temperament
is constituted through the primary qualities
bound in immediate essences: because it is necessary
for you to temper it in them through strengthening:
because thus nature wills. But since you can see
that the proper quantities cannot be had
except under a substantial and visible composite form,
it is expedient for you that the substance of each element
not be disturbed in the time of the medicine by the
quantities of another, which is in it through defect
of preparation, and that the substance of each element
be a medicine subtly purified to the end. Therefore
let it be manifest to you that in water there is something
of undigested earth, which is called the sustentation of
spirit; after its preparation it will be poorly rectified
because of this, since it will have in its belly
a quantity of dry matter, which is the primary quality of
earth. And through that, the cold stone is drawn together
to perform its action: and so it is with the other
elements. See why through defect of preparation
the dry does not confound the dry, nor the cold
the dry, nor the moist the cold, nor the cold
the moist, nor the moist the hot, because otherwise
you will not be able to temper the stone, nor bring it
to a certain specific degree. Since it is
expedient for you divisibly that you know how much

--- Page F11 (printed page 81, Practica de Furnis, Cap. V cont. / Cap. VI) ---
TESTAMEN. NOVISS.

you must put of each element. And therefore,
when the elements are rectified with degrees
of separation, we do this to the end that the
four elemental qualities, and that each of
them be strong according to the quantities of its element
only, as much as is possible: because from the
four excellent qualities it is necessary that
you temper the stone. And the said excellent qualities
are not found except in simple elements, and the
elements are simplified through the force of
preparation to the end that the quantity of one
not be impeded by the quantity of another. And from these
results a temperament according to strengthening
or weakening, which are done through the way
of reduction. And thus the elements are bound again, and
become as it were a composite water, which contains in itself
all four prepared elements, which water
is fixed through minimal parts, and congealed at its
end through the mode of reduction, and by such a
mode the elements are tempered with minimal
action of contrary qualities, and there results one
complexioned composite, in a temperament
of hot and moist, cold and dry. Yet first
with cold and moist, and afterward through that it is
more moist and hot, until the elixir
comes to be with a noble temperament.

Concerning the three instruments, without which the work
cannot be done. Cap. VI.

Son, in this art you must consider three instrumental
subjects, namely the vegetable, which is
the principal agent of change of all substances,
and the elemental, which is the principal agent of mixing

--- Page F12 (printed page 82, Practica de Furnis, Cap. VI cont.) ---
RAIMVNDI LVLLII

of the elements, and of instrumentalities: since
without it the others cannot exist, because all these three
require that they be universally in the matter from
which our mastery is made, because if the vegetative
were not in the matter, it could not be transmuted
as nature requires, according to its organic
instruments. Son, the principal and most noble
effect of this instrument is natural fire, and
if the elementative is not there, the elements cannot
be mixed with good elementation. And if the
water is not elemented from the other elements, and
especially from earth, then it will not be able to be nourished, nor
consequently will it have substance for
construction: nor will the instrument of vegetability
have substance that it may change. And through
this you can understand that in all nourishment
simple elements avail little, since
nature manifestly shows you that the elements which
it mixed with its instrument of elementation
produce natural effects through their mixing.
And similarly, insofar as it follows nature, it
shows you in practice that mixed elements operate
better and faster and produce more effects
than simple ones do, because
mixed elements remain in the primary essences
of the elements, and from them all things are nourished through the
powers of their instruments, and thus they are bound
together, because the greater part of one is bound with the greater
part of another, all together alternating among themselves through
their own qualities. But that binding, son,
occurs in diverse proportions, as we shall tell
you in the following chapter, and we said in the
chapter on its animation, or concerning its anima-

--- Page F13 (printed page 83, Practica de Furnis, Cap. VI cont.) ---
TESTAMEN. NOVISS.

tion, and in the immediately preceding chapter. It is
clear that it is required that the matter not be
lacking such a vegetative instrument, through which
such things must be elemented. And the instrumental is not
for elementing elements to enter
the composition of sulphur by means of the
vegetative instrument. And thus it is made manifest to you that
one cannot exist without the other, always with the
difference of successive operators: since
when we wish to place instrumentality in the
matter, we proceed to the regimen of dissolution
which we gave you above. And here you begin to
see elementality and instrumentality. And
when we wish that it element its own
elements, we proceed to the second regimen of
resolution. And immediately here the vegetative begins to be born,
which is the cause of transmuting and elementing
nourished elements, through elementativity into sulphur,
through the operation of the third regimen, through which
the congelation of nature occurs. Son, since the matter from which
it is created, which is called quicksilver, is
the small and primary matter of metals, in which
matter all three instruments are universally
implanted through the power of the said operations, which
has the power of transmuting one metal
into another, with perfect and mineral transmutation.
And concerning this some Aristotelian
philosophers have said that it is impossible to
transmute the species of metals unless they are first reduced
to their primary matter, or nature,
with all their instruments, and that the said
primary matter diminished in them, and through which the
artist may artificially make his transmutations, with

--- Page F14 (printed page 84, Practica de Furnis, Cap. VII) ---
RAIMVNDI LVLLII

respect to nature. For this reason we have from
nature three noble regimens of operation, through
which we cause all three instruments that
change matters in that which we have said.

The binding occurs in diverse
proportions. Cap. VII.

Son, the binding or mixing of elements occurs
in diverse proportion, according to the property of
each element, in such a manner that
each element has entered through elementativity
into every elemented thing. Always
in action one element is stronger than
another in its mixture: and this is so in all mixtures
and elemented things. Because to element is nothing other
than to mix, while yet preserving the discreteness of its
difference. And these bindings made in diverse
proportions we call numbers: because
through them the elements stand bound in elemented things. And
therefore, the operation which occurs through action sometimes
finds fire elemented too much, too elemented,
too active, because of this: that from the moist
with which it is bound it has adherence, and
with that from which it goes out, which fire would not have
by itself, and through earth or from earth: with
which it is bound, for the continuous downward motion, in
that it corrodes, and through airy moisture it
has nourishment, as we have already said in the chapter and
in many other places. And fire by itself could not have
all this. And therefore inextinguishable fire exists
because it is simple fire. Thus, son, it is with the coldness
of water, which is contained by terrestrial dryness,
when the place is there opened to the end that it

--- Page F15 (printed page 85, Practica de Furnis, Cap. VII cont.) ---
TESTAMEN. NOVISS.

may enter within its belly, in the place of
aerial spirit. Yet it is sharpened by the sharpness
of fire for penetrating, and when it is within the
body, it is more apt for cooling it and
hardening it with the whole mixture. And this simple
water could not do: in such a manner it is with
terrestrial dryness, because it could not enter to
desiccate and congeal a moist body, if
it did not have the adherence and sharpness of
the other elements, as appears through our
sulphurous stone: for this reason let there be given to you
clear knowledge of the cause, why and how the said
stone must be created from four elements rotated
in four spherical circles bound with
the ligaments of gilded chains, as its
action can manifest to you with clear experience.
And because the ancient philosophers called such action
of non-simple elements the said binding,
a mineral mixture, through which
there results a certain power beyond the three instruments
that we spoke of above in the elementary: therefore
we say to the end that you may gather together in yourself
all the powers that are caused in the composite,
that they are five in number, namely: the simple
action of each element, which they live, is
more closely related to it according to nature, and this
is the instrumentality: the second is the power of
the proportion of the mixture. The third is the power that
assists the action of the mixing of elements, through
which with the said elements they constitute a
mixture, and this is called elementativity. The fourth is
celestial power: and the fifth is the power of the
vegetable instrument. Through these five powers, son,

--- Page F16 (printed page 86, Practica de Furnis, Cap. VII cont. / Cap. VIII) ---
RAIMVNDI LVLLII

the action in our medicine is constituted and formed.
And in the same way the place of the mineral vessel with the
aid of that always with its nature does not enter
into the medicine essentially only, as the
said five powers do. Son, when we
speak of celestial power, understand the power
of common fire, which must move the
vegetable instrument, which is within the matter,
formative and sealing, as much as it is
well formed through common fire with
respect to nature: And common fire is
formed and governed through wise practice
with moist respect to celestial power, and thus
we wish philosophically, according as
its action has required of us in our mastery.

How you must consider subjects
with four conditions.
Cap. VIII.

Son, subjects are taken in this art in two ways,
namely as instruments, as we named them above,
through which our mastery is accomplished.
Other instrumental subjects do not pertain
except to living things, which art cannot retain
because of the rarity of their matter: and therefore
to generate them vitally, if the intellect does not supply with
the aid of certain divine seeds,
let it supply through the science of stellar nature
organizing through harmonies with the instrument of
sensuality, which through the art of experience declares
itself entirely, nature assisting from above through
many turns, to such an extent that they caused certain things
to speak and to be moved with certain powers

--- Page F17 (printed page 87, Practica de Furnis, Cap. VIII cont.) ---
TESTAMEN. NOVISS.

infusion and a marvel of supernal influence, just as
stones and words and herbs do, in which much
virtue flows in and is born through the fifth essence
of the supernal mother nature. And the lower natures,
through their good motions and subtle mixtures,
whence virtues are caused in simple matters
bound with gross ones, according to the spiritual
understanding of the supernal nature, which afterward works
miracles: as we shall say to anyone of good understanding
in the volume of sensible things. You must understand
in another manner the subjects in the place of matter,
in which, standing with its said instruments, which are
the transmutation of that thing according to the special
difference of its own and natural concordance,
and the determination of said matter. And therefore,
son, we tell you that you must consider the
manner of these subjects as much materially
as instrumentally, with four conditions
up to the point at which, through those things, the
habituated intellect is moved and conditioned to
discursing said subjects materially and instrumentally,
their elementated things toward gross simplicity
materially, and likewise to sublimate all
instrumental things together with the material ones. And this
according to each subject, both material
and instrumental, stands at its condition through
its essence and through its nature: because the
material and instrumental subject, which is the green
lion, has one condition within itself, and another
in frozen fume. It has a condition in the green
lion: the material subject dominates, by reason
of its gross substance, which impedes the instrumental,
so that it is not necessary that it depart hence through

--- Page F18 (printed page 88, Practica de Furnis, Cap. VIII cont.) ---
RAIMVNDI LVLLII

extraneous fire, so that it may be fixed in the subtle
substance through operation; and in frozen fume the
instrumental dominates by reason of the rarity of its
matter and by its special nature. After that which it receives from
water or from air, in which they exist without a vivified
soul, each of which vivifies it: and afterward, just as a
vivified thing operates with a living thing. And concerning this we
give you the example of sublimated quicksilver,
when it is vivified, which makes an impression,
but afterward it immediately loses it through sublimation, if it
does not afterward receive it through fire, as one who brings
life through an operation known and understood by
an authentic workman. The first condition, more strictly,
which you must have with habituated knowledge,
is that from each subject you extract its
virtuous property with the conservation of its
essence with its proper diffusion, and through the way of
formed practice you draw potency into act to the
end that they differ from another subject. The second
condition is that in practice the difference
of subjects be observed, both material and
instrumental: let the vegetative power of the Green Lion differ
from the vegetative power of sulphur in virtue and
substance, which is fume congealed through
bodily grossness, fixed and perfect. The third
condition is that the concordance which is between
one subject and another, both materially and
instrumentally, not be destroyed, just as the concordance
which is between earth and water, and air, and
fire, or between body and spirit: because spirit
concords with body through corporality, and
instrumentality, with difference and concordance,
through the concordance of unity, which dissolution
made

--- Page F19 (printed page 89, Practica de Furnis, Cap. VIII cont. / Cap. IX) ---
TESTAMEN. NOVISS.

from those two. The fourth condition is that
to the nobler subject be attributed nobler elements
participating in essence with nobler principles,
just as in the stone, when it has been created, it is
nobler in virtue and in substance than its
mine from which it came: in air more than water,
and fire more than air, and the mixed more than the
simple. And through such conditions the intellect of him
who wishes to be a practitioner in the operations
of nature can quickly be habituated, by means of the
perfective secret contained in the compendious treatise which
is called Vade mecum, from the number of the
philosophers.

De Temperamento lapidis, & quomodo stat
in quatuor Elementis, cum distempe-
ramento. Cap. IX.

Son, our stone dwells in all its four
elements, and it is whole in each of them
after its separation, which is called the mixture of
mixed things, because it is entirely in earth, more
dead: just as the sand of gravel stands and lies without life, through
great dryness. Further we tell you more,
that it is entirely in its air. But because here it stands
submerged in its great sea, its natural heat
is suffocated by the exceeding moisture. Further
we say more, that our stone is entirely
in fire, but because it lies soaked through great
heat, it remains unburnt, without seasoning,
all vital action. Wherefore let it be revealed to you
that in none of the extremes, where the excellent qualities
stand, does there stand a tempered complexion, through

--- Page F20 (printed page 90, Practica de Furnis, Cap. IX cont.) ---
RAIMVNDI LVLLII

which all acts of life result, unless in those things
mixed in excellent quantities, all are duly
conjoined together in one mixed thing, namely, that
the hot stone and the moist stone, the cold stone,
the dry stone be equally tempered together, and the
commingling will be a peculiar one, which must have its origin from
a true temperament through manual action, as
much as of the primary excellent ones, which are of the
elements. And through this let it be manifested to you how
our natural complexion is a quantity that is excellent
only in the elements, when they, being mixed, are broken
in the joined action of permutation, until they
arrive at that mean, in which the species of a perfect
mixture can be constituted. Wherefore, son, since it is now
established that a new act of life stands without a mean,
join water with earth, as nature requires,
in the smallest parts, until our stone results,
because it is neither one thing nor the other, and it
shall be called a certain marvel, which is of an incomplete
nature, when the water shall have been frozen by the
vapor of earth, and the mean approximated to the
nature of air, yet it still retains much of the dry nature
of the first extreme, and cannot yet be contemplated
through air, unless heavily an impression were made upon the moist,
since it does not yet participate with this one
as much as it does with earth. Therefore repeatedly bring back
water upon the white earth, to the
end that it be fixed with it, and that the mean be
more approximated to air than to earth, and that
the cold compress the moist to a greater
depth; then the mean will be proportioned in
dryness to the tolerability of the nature of air, through
the virtue of the cold of water. Therefore conjoin the moist air

--- Page F21 (printed page 91, Practica de Furnis, Cap. IX cont.) ---
TESTAMEN. NOVISS.

with the dry, proportionably tempering
through water, to the end that the virtue of one
extreme be exalted over the other, and one temper
the other, and that the dry through the moist, and the
moist through the dry, all be thickened together
through the continuation of the two parts, to the end that,
when you are at your projection, it acquire the moisture
of the dry: because our Impression is not made except
through the moist bound in the belly of the dry, and the dry
in the belly of the moist: and let it be bound through the passion
of the one and the other, through decoction in a tempered
fire. And after such binding, when the
moist feels the cold, then it closes itself in the belly
of the dry. But when the dry finds an excellent
heat, it defends itself from it in the belly of the moist,
and the moist is retained by the dry, to the end
that the whole may be able to endure fire: And through the moist
an impression is given in the dry, which is the water of life
of the dead. Son, thus such dry, being converted by itself into
a new body, together takes on an impression
through the virtue and impression of the living moist
in the dry, in such a manner that in the battle with fire the dry
is defended by the moist from the separation
of its parts: in the same way that the moist
is prevented by the dry from its exhalability,
so that such a body can only slowly and with difficulty lose
the impression now made. Therefore, son, observe
that when the vapor of the dry has been exhaled
in fume, and constricted, and pressed out through the
cold moist, constricted in a new body, shining
and resplendent like crystal through it, in itself through
the moist, or with the hot airy moist that is
laxative. To the end that the parts of the constricted dry

--- Page F22 (printed page 92, Practica de Furnis, Cap. IX cont. / Cap. X) ---
RAIMVNDI LVLLII

are contained with a white crystalline lamina, flowing,
entering, drawing and penetrating without any
vaporability or mortality, but with solidity, which
is the first virtue of radical moisture and of quicksilver,
and the life of all liquefiable bodies: and thus
the question of the fire of the philosophers is terminated
at the end, being led through its own proper means to its
perfect end, through the intentivity of the thing which you
shall pollulate [?]. Whence the true principles look to their
end, and the ends restore to other things through means, just as
has already been seen in the projection, which comes from metal
and is terminated in metal through the conservation of
natural means, where there are the extremes, the summit,
and the principles. And therefore we tell you that the
stone does not exist until it be well dissolved four times
and afterward congealed. Because then the infinite
means of the extremes, already projected into the tempered
extreme, return freely through noble nature from
their midpoint: because as we have told you, nature never
goes to perfection except through the transit
of its first means: because then the proper qualities
of all elements are constricted and broken, until they
come freely to the proper tempered state, and to the
individuals, which are the pure nature of the highest metals.

Quomodo debes intelligere ignem Mercurij, et
quanta est differentia inter ignem vul-
garem & philosophicalem. Cap. X.

Son, by the sublimation of Mercury (by human means) we
understand, and all the philosophers do, the decoction of it,
until some part of it becomes sulphur, and
another part quicksilver, and it is purified from its
unctuosities.

--- Page F23 (printed page 93, Practica de Furnis, Cap. X cont.) ---
TESTAMEN. NOVISS.

Son, if you wish to understand the nature of sublimation,
you must know principally two things, namely
to simplify the gross matter, and to direct and
sublimate it by heat, which is the instrument of nature,
without the separation of its own proper subject. To simplify
and direct the matter is done through diverse decoctions
which it takes from fire, excited against nature
by reason: in such a manner that the heat
of nature, which forms all things, be not separated nor
suffocated from said matter; rather you must
sublimate and exalt and augment it by simplifying
and digesting said matter through long and
gentle decoctions: because the matter itself takes, as
we have said, from fire against nature. And to do
this you must imitate each wise
philosopher, who must look to nature.
Secondarily, son, when the wise philosopher
or physician has seen hard and compact matter,
namely in diuretics, it is necessary that he
use digestive, divisive, and incisive
medicines, to the end that the hard matter be softened
without destruction of its own nature by a slight burning.
Secondarily, It is necessary that he use laxative
medicines, to the end that the digested matter be
expelled and diligently evacuated. Thirdly, It is
necessary that he use certain strengthening agents to
strengthen nature, which through the violence of the
medicine was relaxed and debilitated in its evacuation.
Fourthly, It is necessary that he use certain
restoratives and recuperatives, to the end that what
nature lost in its evacuation be restored
to it. All this similarly the workman must imitate
in our mastery, with the operations above

--- Page F24 (printed page 94, Practica de Furnis, Cap. X cont.) ---
RAIMVNDI LVLLII

described: because through the first operation he must
attend to L. and K., through that one L. and M. and N. O. P. Q.
and R. And through this the third reduction of M. into L.
and O. And through this the fourth, he must understand the reduction
of N. into that which is the entry of L. into
O. And when you wish to have N. through M. from L.,
you must use a sharp evacuation, and with gentleness
and patience, until it is little by little stripped, and
prolonged in respect of N., and that the natural vegetative
property not be separated from L. through M. S. by a great
evacuation, because the evacuation of M. S. is sudden and
precipitous. And thus the repletion, heating, or cooling
will be entirely a fallacy of words, and deceptive,
and inimical to nature. Son, let L. not be evacuated of
N. except on account of this: that it sins or fails in
attracting M. and in the desire to convert everything with L.
into its own nature. But because L. is an
impediment through M. in retaining M., it strips L.
of N., which was impeding the love of L. and M. And
on account of this you can recollect in yourself that
three things are principally necessary in our sublimation.
The first is dissolution, the second
constriction, the third restoration. Dissolution is for
evacuating full things, constriction for hardening
dissolved things, and restoration for refilling
nature. Always, however, son, you must make
the restoration little by little, with appropriate duration, that is, tempered,
because nature in its evacuated vessels is wont to desire greatly:
and therefore it would take more than it could
convert or digest, unless by a wise and
natural workman it is held in the fire of the said thing.
And therefore we told you in our Theory, if you
wished to understand us, that nature would more quickly convert
a part.

--- Page F25 (printed page 95, Practica de Furnis, Cap. I cont.) ---
TESTAMEN. NOVISS.

would make small rather than large, small divided little by
little into parts, rather than making it gross and
undivided all together. And thus our quicksilver
is multiplied and fixed through digestion and
conversion, which the fire of nature makes in its
parts through the four elements. Therefore, son, when
you wish to sublimate our Mercury, first
you shall separate its elements in such a manner that
its instrument in which the property resides cannot
feel the extrinsic fire, but only that
which is sent to it against nature to corrupt
its elements little by little to the end: that
our sparrowhawk or bird may resume the feathers
that it lost, and that it may fly: and it
is cooked by its own fire for the space of time that it
requires in nourishing with its own milk, like an
infant between the breasts of its dear mother. Afterward
make it fly to the sky, until it drinks the air,
which it greatly needs to become strong. And thus it
will recover the power to penetrate any
body whatsoever, and to transmute it into N. or into
F. Son, sublimation is one general thing for all
operations, both particularly and generally: because
according to the works, sometimes sublimation is taken
for all things that are required in the perfection
of the great work. And sometimes it is taken for
any of the said operations, whether they be done through
the moist, as dissolutions: or whether they be done through the dry,
as indurations. Understand that the said operation
with the intention of the perfection of its nature,
as much as can pertain to the regimen
of the said operation. Whence many unfaithful ones were
blinded and deceived, as is evident in their

--- Page F26 (printed page 96, Practica de Furnis, Cap. I cont.) ---
RAIMVNDI LVLLII

noted and condemned writings, namely in the epistle of Demoschilis
the Jew, of Abraham the Rabbi, who understood
our sublimation to be made with extraneous fire in
the dry: and others in moist fire with extraneous food:
and others such as Rasis in M. Idee, and Eitul, who
disapproved of sublimation being made with fire against
nature. Whence we say that generation does not
exist without corruption. And although it be so, that they could
have understood that the natural fire cannot always
be aroused without that which is against nature,
so that it might do anything, it
seems that such men gave an ignorant knowledge,
or one cloaked with obscurities, as did
Kesu, Marabh, Kalitis, Hezodi, Zeber,
the Arabs, and many others who confusedly and with great
corruption made known our sublimation
under extraneous operations, which was never
the custom of philosophy: nor did they wish
to understand it even under a veil, since in no
way did they speak of perfection in the
manner of tradition, except only in the form of
darkness. By which it appears that if such men did not
give it, they easily knew the other operations,
without which it cannot be made or
completed, by which it appears through their extraneous
operations, with which the work can be done. Likewise
that the aforesaid worked through ignorance
is manifested by this, that they did not know how to name
a more perfect thing, nor whose name
it was, which above all other materials prevails
against fire, and multiplied with such
sublimations, as we have said three times, and not
with others, which are destructive of all generation.

--- Page F27 (printed page 97, Practica de Furnis, Cap. I cont. / Cap. II) ---
TESTAMEN. NOVISS.

And in arousing this, it ought not to be hidden
from the Christian multitude, to the end that the glory of the
good may reveal evil things: because it is not credible that
such a people departing from truth, as most especially
was this traitor, iniquitous, reprobate, etc. Of
whom more words and writings by Pagans. But that
you may wish to grasp how you ought to grasp freely,
it will be of God, if you attend to the testament
granted to you, wherever you may be far removed.

On the diversity of fire against nature, according
to the diversity of medicines, in the
form of practice: and how you should
preserve it.
Cap. II.

We tell you, son, that you can work in
bodies only, whether from quicksilver
or from another: but this around, or with a diverse intention
from what you have in your practice, according to what
you have worked, whether from body, or from spirit, or
from both together. Wherefore if you have the intention
of working in the body only our stone,
you can make the stone with such intention
that crude Mercury may be converted into
cooked Mercury commensurate by nature. Son, this
diversity of considerations exists because of the diversity
of the cooked and the crude. Now, having posited that anything
whatsoever must pass through four operative means,
as much as is the making of any of them, since
through that it is converted into the philosophers'
stone. Thus there will be seven operative means, namely
B. C. D. E. F. G. H. By B. is signified the operation
of lique-

--- Page F28 (printed page 98, Practica de Furnis, Cap. II cont.) ---
RAIMVNDI LVLLII

liquefaction, by C. solution and elementation, by
D. elementation and congelation, by E. sublimation,
by F. elementation and congelation, by G. solution
and elementation, by H. liquefaction, and by B. C. D.
E. are understood the works of Mercury, or quicksilver.
And these are successive deccoctions. And by E. F.
G. H. are understood the operations of bodies: and
these are by the retrogradation of incrudations. And
we name the letters of bodies H. G. F. E., of which
each of them ends in E. But inasmuch as
the body is converted from H. into G., Mercury
did not proceed gradually from B. into C. nor from C. into
D. immediately, just as the body is converted in A. G. into F.,
because anything whatsoever is converted more quickly into that
which it was, than into that which it never was,
according to the instinct of nature: and a clean thing is more quickly
soiled than a soiled thing is cleaned. And wherever any
thing is slower, it more quickly fails or is destroyed by reason
of impure parts which require reformation.
And therefore when you have worked with Mercury,
you will always have enough material at the beginnings
of its decoctions. And this by reason of its
heaviness, crudity, and grossness. Because that
cannot be converted into the nature of the elements,
of the perfect means, it needs a double decoction,
to elevate its grossness, until
it comes to the perfection of E. having completed
its decoction, in which P. can be reduced
with the reversion of reincrudation. Therefore, son, understand
that after D. has passed into E., it will pass
into F. and from F. into G. and from G. into H. and from H. into I. and from I.
into K. and from K. into L. These letters, my son, understand: when
E.

--- Page F29 (printed page 99, Practica de Furnis, Cap. II cont.) ---
TESTAMEN. NOVISS.

when E. wishes to be graduated into F. and A. B. into G. etc.
Then you should take F. G. H. by other significations, because
by F. you must understand the liquefaction of
ferments, and G. the solution of solutions: and by
I. multiplication in virtue: and by K. multiplication
in quantity: and by L. understand
the metal of perfect transmutation, through the virtue
of the medicine. And understand all this with a
perfect operation, by dissolving Mercury, or quicksilver.
Always however, son, notwithstanding
what we have told you, that Mercury needs,
on account of its great crudity, doubled
and repeated decoctions. I said this on account of
the thing commensurate with supernal nature, which looks
toward greater perfection to the equipollence
of the virtue of sulphur. Likewise, every perfect
body is converted, or can be converted:
and therefore it needs two letters. But
if you do not have the intention of making such a
commensurate thing, you can well abbreviate
your work by passing immediately in succession
from B. into C. and from C. into D. and from D. into E. But if that
is so, as we have told you, it will not be such nor of as great
virtue as is the sublime commensurate thing in
supernal nature: because its decoctions were
generated from so much. Whence when from materials according
to their requirement virtue and force are subtracted,
because virtues and forces come only because of
the nobilities of the materials from which they go forth, and in the virtue of one thing
which we call the philosophers' stone, and the
medium: because just as a key joining two things makes
them hold together, so nature joins these two into
one virtuous body. Whence it greatly considers
that

--- Page F30 (printed page 100, Practica de Furnis, Cap. II cont.) ---
RAIMVNDI LVLLII

one thing wishes to be cooked, and the other to be incrudated, to
achieve the purpose, with subtle artifice, as
the master wills, by the will of God, whom he
follows. Whence we tell you, son, that just as one thing
demonstrates in its decoctions diverse passions,
so the other in its reincrudations shows
visibly diverse powers, because as we told
you above, Mercury is a gross earthly thing
on account of its great crudity, as its
nature demonstrates vividly in its first
decoctions: so on the contrary the perfect body
shows actions and contrary accidents,
through the noble and great decoction that it
receives over a long time from nature, as its matter
demonstrates by retrogradation and reincrudation.
And just as the crude demonstrates a multitude of
matter, so the cooked through its contrary demonstrates
paucity: and this through its good elementation,
digestion, and subtiliation, the cooked fixed elements
are dissolved and flee from the crude, which
incrudated them by will, if it does not have
a restraint owed to nature, namely either
from the temperament of its own menstrual with
information, or from the first Mercury, which you
must understand with spiritual reason, which nature
requires. And just as the spirit is full of splendor
and subtiliation and rarefaction, and all this cannot
come except through the tolerance of matter, and
tolerance comes to it through the action of fire, which is the
supreme element that burns everything and
purifies everything and brings any thing whatsoever to simplicity. Therefore
as much as your Mercury will correspond
to simplicity, so much will it operate and no more: and
the more

--- Page F31 (printed page 101, Practica de Furnis, Cap. II cont. / Cap. XII) ---
RAIMVNDI LVLLII

pure it is, the more it will work in clear
lucidity. Therefore it is declared to you that from Mercury
alone, or from quicksilver alone, one can make a
perfect or imperfect work: if perfect, it will be done
through the reasoning of long decoctions and
doubled digestions; if imperfect, this will be
on account of the brevity of its decoctions, not
fully completed, which it has from
its volatility. Thus it is, it suffers the reasons of its
proximity and facility, which it has from its
volatility. Thus, son, we draw ourselves from that work
which is nearer and easier, but not more perfect,
except with the aid of its decoctions, or through
the complete mixture of its matter in a perfect
body, as we shall tell you when we have spoken
of the operations of the body.

On the reincrudation of the perfect body, and on
its diversity, and on its accidents,
and on its weight.
Caput XII.

Through the things said above, you can, son, naturally
understand how the reincrudation of the crude, according
to the course of nature, until both separately
are converted, through which the work is improved and
strongly corrected, inasmuch as it has a double
regard, by reason of bodies and spirit:
because just as they are contrary in their actions,
passions, and operations, because the spirit by
its nature always looks toward true fixation in the
true magisterium, and the fixed body in the contrary manner
strives to fly in the form of water, and the fixed body

--- Page F32 (printed page 102, Practica de Furnis, Cap. XII cont.) ---
RAIMVNDI LVLLII

in the contrary manner clearly, and the congealed spirit
remains like white earth, brown like salt or
crystal or thick talc, from which earth you must
separate that precious soul, which more
slowly departs from the body, and this with the water of the
body. And from this understand that the spiritual solution of the
body does not occur without the natural congelation of the spirit,
as we have told you, or elucidate throughout all
philosophy in the compendium of this art. Nor would the
congelation of the spirit occur without the congelation of bodies.
Nevertheless understand thus concerning the solution of the fixed spirit
and the volatile body, through the first spirit before
its constriction, and not of the fixed sulphurous
body clothed in hidden blackness, because it is
dissolved through congelation and constriction of its
quicksilver, which it receives from the fire of nature, and not
against nature. Son, here contrary operations
fight, because just as fire the body appears almost like
sap and dark, afterward when the body is
totally lightened, the spirit is filled with great
heaviness. Whence when you wish to dissolve the body very
well, congeal it from the heavy one, who will
carry it in his hands. First make that
it be converted into H. and from H. into G. through incrudation,
then from G. into F. with retrogradation. And afterward
F. into E. will be converted into a stone through the
virtue of the reduced spirit, which is not common quicksilver
entirely, indeed it is from the body of the first
congregation, and from the virtue of its own proper substance
it is clothed without extraneous heaviness. Therefore
attend to this, that the work of M. looks toward
constricting and remaining below, and that of
bodies toward loosening and dissolving, and the spirit
ascending high-

--- Page F33 (printed page 103, Practica de Furnis, Caput XII cont.) ---
TESTAMEN. NOVISS.

to ascend. By this it is shown to you that if both are well prepared together, you will perform the mineral work without difficulty more quickly than you would if working with one alone, and much better, because then one assists in that which the other has, and one is improved through the aid of the other. Begin therefore your work from these two together, as we have told you regarding the greater stone. And we intend to show you in the branches immediately, when we have told you about the double accident that is caused by the body and the spirit, when they dominate together.

On the double relationship of the body and Mercury, and on their contrary operations.

Cap. XIII.

When the work is done with Mercury and with the body together, then many contrary accidents are caused in the magistery against the matter. The spirit dissolves the fixed body into the water of clouds, and the body constricts the volatile spirit into congealed earth. Thus by the contrary mode, the fire of nature congeals the spirit, dissolves the fixed body into granular earth, and the body of the volatile spirit fixed by fire against nature does not dissolve into the form of the water of clouds, but into the philosophical water. Wherefore the fixed, through the virtue of the fire of nature, has been made volatile, and the body of the spirit becomes spirit of the body, and the moist becomes dry, and the heavy becomes light; just as through the contrary virtue of fire against nature, the volatile will become fixed, and the fixed volatile, and the body spirit, and the spirit body, and the dry moist in the manner of the water of clouds, and heavy and constricted. And therefore, son, understand that the fire against nature

--- Page F34 (printed page 104, Practica de Furnis, Caput XIII cont.) ---
RAIMVNDI LVLLII

is so called because it is by its nature against all natural operations, since it has been seen that what the natural fire composes, this one always disposes and leads to corruption. And therefore everything that is complexioned is corrupted, unless the fire of nature dominates it. Whence we tell you that such a fire or the operation of this is not of the virtue or operation of our magistery; rather it is the fire of the whirlwind, which is purely natural. Whence, son, our sun stands wholly exalted in the sign of Aries when you see the matter standing in confusion. Therefore, son, we tell you that our magistery must begin when it enters into Aries, or is as if exalted in the work by us, because then you can learn to know and truly discern the meaning that the philosopher gives you, when you say that the first degree of sublimation must stand so far above the stone, which I know, until it is exalted and sublimed. And we tell you that it is nothing other than fire, because potency into act, which you must understand, lest you be deceived, because without it there was no work, as he told you. Whence there are many accidents that are caused through such contrary operations, as has been revealed to you in the Proprietarium quite readily. Wherefore, son, since the dissolution of one is never without the congelation of the other, you should not doubt whence you may obtain earth, nor water either, for performing the magistery with the appropriate fire. Whence the earth is from the first substance of quicksilver, constricted or congealed, impregnated with the virtue of the body in the fire of the Sun, by which the quicksilver remains sublimed philosophically as was said before. And the water is from the first substance of the metal, in which sub-

--- Page F35 (printed page 105, Practica de Furnis, Caput XIII cont. / Caput XIIII) ---
TESTAMEN. NOVISS.

stance the aerial moisture of quicksilver is appropriated, which looks toward the perfection of its nature of the first earth impregnated by natural fire, in which is the celestial virtue that vivifies and illuminates the earth of Mercury, which below is dead. Therefore it opens the water itself with the instinct of recovering the lost thing. But when that thing, which is the vivifying moisture, is fermented with the body, having been made to overflow, as we told you, therefore it is considered better than it was before, through the multiplication of cooked moisture. And thus through air the elements are aided, to the end that, conjoined through overflow, they may with greater instinct aid others, because M and P aid Q and R and N, which L and O through the corruption of Z, which is the cause of the dissolution of L and O; and afterward M and P aid L and O to receive Q, R, and N. After the aid of their overflow through the conjunction of Y, which is against Z, as we treat elsewhere, how the elements are mutually aiding in composing and corrupting every body, with the aid of V and Z, which are the instruments of generation and corruption.

Now we shall speak by way of Practice concerning the Branches of the Tincture, in which you may rest and take comfort, son, and first concerning the solar body, with the aid of A.

Caput XIIII.

Son, you wish to know the transmutation of the first lunar body, with a clear practice. You must separate from it the quicksilver that is terminated by

--- Page F36 (printed page 106, Practica de Furnis, Caput XIIII cont.) ---
RAIMVNDI LVLLII

the dry terrestrial, because that is what gives whiteness to silver, and it is nothing other than moisture terminated in the form of silver through the virtue of its sulphur. Therefore you must intend to dissolve that moisture in your reduction, so that it does not retain its condensation, and you will draw it out by this method. Take one part of the best lunar body, and as we told you, liquefy it in six parts of rectified E, yet without reiteration of calcination. Then place the whole to ferment in the heat of moist dung for eight days, and you will find the substance of the body at the bottom of the vessel, black like charcoal. Son, that is the sulphur which must be whitened through the condensation of its moisture in double proportion to nature, as we told you above; which moisture was condensed, before its liquefaction, into the form of the color of the metal, and with the continuation of the parts of the said black sulphur. But now it is separated from it through liquefaction. Place your matter in the bath, and from that liquefied moisture make your elements, elements through the mixing of the instrumentated element. But first you will elementate the water, and then with the elementated water you will elementate the part that you wish to constitute into air, until the third instrument of vegetative power appears. Then therefore reduce the elementated water over the dry earth and over its excited fire, and cook the whole, as we told you, until the moist in relation to nature is well ground, not into metal, but into white sulphur, because there is no passage from one extreme to the other except through its mean. Son, the converted mean of the said moisture is the fire of earth, but the

--- Page F37 (printed page 107, Practica de Furnis, Caput XIIII cont.) ---
TESTAMEN. NOVISS.

mean converting quicksilver into metal is sulphur in the form of a subtle powder, as we told you regarding its multiplication. And let this be made clear to you, that the corruption of quicksilver terminated in the form of metal through the course of nature with our artifice, through the mutation of it into its first matter, is the generation of sulphur. If therefore the quicksilver is not corrupted and divided into the four elements, the generation of sulphur will never occur, because it is necessary that the sulphur be of subtle and most pure matter. Whence the quicksilver that is created must be of the most subtle substance in the form of clear water. And thus it cannot be without the mutation of its nature, nor without the division of its parts. Corrupt therefore the quicksilver, and from its divided, subtle, and sublimed parts, form the stone that you seek, because the magistery must be made and governed, namely: because the magistery lies hidden in corrupting the quicksilver and deviating from its species. And after, with the aid of the virtues of its matter, you will have led and carried it into the form of another, namely sulphur, and thus you will change nature, and nature will form what you seek through the operations of certain successive degrees. Provided, however, that your works are made to resemble those with which nature proceeds and desires to proceed, such as with the purgation of the said sulphur and the creation of it, which are done with such coction and with such sublimation, as we told you above, if you understood us philosophically, as nature requires, and with such operation of its principle of quicksilver, such as the said instrumentated element is, and the purgation of the whole diverse substance through the four elements, and with such good

--- Page F38 (printed page 108, Practica de Furnis, Caput XIIII cont.) ---
RAIMVNDI LVLLII

mixing, as we told you concerning these two with the matter of the fermenting metal. Then therefore, son, truly into that matter through the virtues of sulphur and quicksilver, the form of metal is put on and changed. Nor let anyone call it true gold, nor does he make or will he make true silver, if he does not proceed by that way, since there is but one single nature of limited operation. Therefore it is necessary that the species remain in matter similar to the prior metal, since it is manifestly necessary, as was shown to you above, that its quicksilver be converted into sulphur, and afterward the species that it lost be returned to you in the form of clear water, which we call oil, whence new species are caused through generation, which leads them from potency into act. Wherefore let it be revealed to you: all those who whiten with white things and make yellow with similar species without the corruption of their individuals, truly all these are deceivers, nor with all of this can they make true gold nor quicksilver, if through contrary operations they do not have their white and red sulphur. Because although it be so that they have the art for extracting their quicksilver from bodies with the aid of liquefactions through sublimations, and that all individual things are corrupted from their species, still through such corruption they will not be able to make a true tincture to white nor to red, which through new generation is renewed and converted into a subtle, white, and resplendent body. Then through such renewed white, a true Tincture to white is made, and through the fire of the stone to red, because when the motion of corruption is complete and the said things are renewed, never thereafter can the Tinctures made from them come to corrup-

--- Page F39 (printed page 109, Practica de Furnis, Caput XIIII cont.) ---
TESTAMEN. NOVISS.

tion, because they are purified within and without, and terminated in great lucidity. But few are those who know how to proceed by this way, because they are ignorant of the works of nature. And therefore their gold and silver cannot withstand the fire; rather they are consumed, corrupted, and converted into corrupt earth. And this is because when such men have placed the metal in the motion of corruption, it is necessary that it be totally corrupted and converted into dregs with the loss of its entire species, on account of the fact that they did not follow through the entire course of its corruption; and if they did, they were not provident in the purifications of the corrupted parts; and because those parts remain mixed with its purity, they are burned and go away with their entire species; and this by reason of the combustible sulphur, which through the agreement of the heat of fire burned all those things, because the metal cannot defend itself from fire. And this is only because the parts cannot be joined to the individuals of the noble species. Wherefore, son, let it be gathered for you in clear summary, that from no white thing that nature has formed should you wish to make a tincture, neither white nor red, until its corrupted parts are removed through the art of the magistery. And we give you this indication: if you know the Lunaria created for the moist work in the ferment of the body, because from oil and sulphur the whole composite is made, which tinctures all metals into gold or silver, according to whether it is white or red, as long as its parts are noble. As we saw through the work of a certain companion whom we found going through the world, who made known many beautiful and wondrous things, but afterward was

--- Page F40 (printed page 110, Practica de Furnis, Caput XIIII cont.) ---
RAIMVNDI LVLLII

opposed in his art, since we knew more through sensory experience than he had known, nor understood through art. Whence afterward we instructed him in many other things with the species of influence and of great virtues, until from above we were joined together in the virtuous stone of refining. We were together for years in great loyalty; whence thousands [of gold coins] were the whole of the year's work in [our] undertaking. In the year 1230 the work was completed. Whence afterward the art departed from three of those known to it, and the vessels remained, and the fourth who remains in his kingdom, regenerating as before. And if God has given you anything, hold it in any way secretly, so that the world may know nothing. Likewise we tell you further that you should keep the present Testament secret from all those from whom you may seek the practice, because it is forbidden; because if they should wish to err beyond their ignorance, in teaching they should beware on account of the prohibition that you know, as he through the said Testament, which with reluctance teaches you that you should dissolve the moisture of the terminated silver, with the reiteration of liquefaction; and at the bottom of the vessel black earth, with some small amount of the substance of the said quicksilver, will remain burned. Son, that earth is the belly of our sulphur, from which dissolution is made solemnly, through the resolution of it into its own quicksilver, from which it consists or was generated. And therefore some of the ancients said that sulphur cannot be dissolved except through the benefit of its own quicksilver, namely when it is congealed through its own vapor, then it dissolves it, and it is transmuted into a transparent nature like a spirit, by which it is established that the body has been converted.

into its first matter, a pure nature close to its own kind, namely into sulphur and quicksilver: which is like water. When therefore you have well whitened your body, sublime it, and that which you find elevated above its dregs, into white pieces, imbibe with the oil of these, and fix it, and project one part upon 50 of Venus, and it will convert into true silver without any corruption. Son, that sulphur is wholly fixed, and quickly penetrating through the nature of silver, which is close to copper: and the powders that you will find here sublimed, on the surface of your sublimatory, take those and preserve them, because the sulphur is of one power beyond the other, as you alone can test by experiment.

The Work of Lead.  Cap. XV.

Son, lead contains in its nature a great part of H. with combustible grease: and the defect that is in its substance is that the terrestrial is not well mixed with the dry water, by reason of its gross substance, nor was it well purified. And therefore it is necessary for you to divide it into the said substances, and that each of them be well purified, and extracting the gross sliminess through elementating, which is done with condensation and rarefaction, and moist matter. Son, when you wish to compensate the water with the air, apply fire, and earth, which causes the emptying of a certain essence: and when you wish to condense the air, draw it out, and rarefy the water, with levigation, until you see your body calcined. Then cook your body with water, and afterward sublime it, and when you have sublimed it, it is called the body exalted

--- Page F42 (printed page 112, Practica de Furnis, Cap. XV cont. / Cap. XVI) ---
RAIMVNDI LVLLII

exalted into mineral salt, which we call the stone and sulphur of nature. Incerate this salt of nature with the simple air of the moon, until it is fixed and flowing, and of this salt project one part upon 50 of lead, and it will convert into pure silver. And if you wish to congeal, and perfectly transmute quicksilver, and fixed silver, mortify it with the vapor of the same body, then make the projection upon the same, namely one part upon 50 of the said salt, and it will be fixed by its natural heat into true silver.

On Tin.  Cap. XVI.

In the same manner you shall work with tin as with lead: except that you need not prepare so much, if you do not wish. But when you are at the reduction, conjoin gradually with graduated coctions, because it holds a small sulphurous quantity in its substance. Therefore augment the said virtue with coctions, through the attenuation and sublimation of its substance. We make known to you that the tincture to white is very great; sublime it, when it seems good to you, after its nourishment, until you have the salt of nature; incerate it until it flows with the air of the moon, and project one part upon a hundred parts of tin, and it will be changed into true silver. And if you make the projection of it upon quicksilver, congealed and fixed, it will fix it into true silver with perfect retention.

--- Page F43 (printed page 113, Practica de Furnis, Cap. XVII / Cap. XVIII) ---
TESTAMEN. NOVISS.

The Congelation of Quicksilver, to take on the form of metal.
Cap. XVII.

Take the earth of lead, and with all its four elements well mixed, suspend as much quicksilver as seems good to you by reason of the spirit, according to the proportion of the power of the earth over it, so that by such means it may receive the vapor of sulphur, and light the fire from below, and you will find it congealed within six hours. But since this operation is laborious, and of little utility, in comparison to others, since at your pleasure you cannot congeal so great a quantity for your purpose without repetition of labor, in order to make projection, we advise this transmutation of quicksilver for this purpose: that you may see and know its nature, which is close to all metals, just as it rectifies the treatment of its nature. But by this understand that in the projection of the supreme medicine, it is right to congeal it, just as it is in the branches, because it does not have that power.

On the Work of Mars and Venus.
Cap. XVIII.

In the same manner make the separation of these two metals, as you did with the two leads. However, of these two you should take nothing except the second earth, and with the water of that metal which you wish, cook it until you have its honored salt sufficiently. This salt incerated with the oil of K. and if the salt is of the nature from which one part of it projected upon 50, with light preparation, converts into K.

--- Page F44 (printed page 114, Practica de Furnis, Cap. XVIII cont. / Cap. XIX) ---
RAIMVNDI LVLLII

into K. better than from the mine: and if the salt is of the nature of O, let it be projected upon it in the same amount, and likewise it converts into K. And if you wish to project the salt upon Mercury, pursue the way that we have told you. And if the salt is of O, through dissolution to the end that the body may love the foreign spirit bound with its nature; otherwise it will not love it, nor would it wish to receive it, except through the bond of its love, which is done with dissolution not into common water, but into mercurial water, full of blessed sulphur, which penetrates every body. And as we have told you concerning O or concerning Q, so understand concerning all others. And if you understand this, you will be the master of this entire art, or science, through which the secrets of nature are drawn from potency into act.

On Body and Spirit, working together, through human generation.
Cap. XIX.

Son, when you wish to make or begin any work with the benefit of our magistery, you must at the beginning know that when human nature proceeds according to its process, you likewise must conduct yourself in your work. Son, know that it may be revealed to you as if with a living voice, that the moisture in the first substance of the sperms, we call it wholly the radical moisture. Now therefore, imitating human nature: take the body with union, and natural conjunction with quicksilver, and quicksilver with the body, with the weights of their virtues, make them embrace in such a manner that with a delightful nature, through the virtue of the heat of lustful desire, with some ex-

--- Page F45 (printed page 115, Practica de Furnis, Cap. XIX cont.) ---

tension and softening, the body is continued with the parts of quicksilver, and the quicksilver with the parts of the body, thickening itself, contracts in some small degree, so that from the whole there is one white composite, like soft paste, or white mass, uniformly mixed, through the grinding of heat, with excited motion, adding even more quicksilver, as much as suffices, according to the virtue of the mutuals, in such a manner that these two moistures are mixed together. And when they have been mixed, place the whole in a place of digestion, namely in a pit, which partakes of the nature of the toad, until it has eaten and covered all its heads. Son, the heads of the toad are the two sperms, which are dissolved into the substance of water through the virtue of the venom, when the stinking dragon flies through the air. And afterward, when you wish the said natural heat to be excited, so that the whole has been resolved into water, separate the superfluous moisture, which holds the drunkenness, the natural heat excited from pungency and acuity, which are now the properties of the nature of true sulphur. Son, let this excitation be done with fire, with the information of the caution of wisdom, administering fire gradually, little by little, and increasing in quality according to the measure of reason, until you see that the body is thus made brown; always beware of redness, which comes through excessive fire. Son, if here you are not warned, the spirit will flee from the body, and afterward it cannot retain the soul. See therefore that the body is not completely deprived of this through the stimulation of too great a dissolution, nor through too extended an excita-

--- Page F46 (printed page 116, Practica de Furnis, Cap. XIX cont. / Cap. XX) ---
