tion: because it is the spirit that retains the soul, when the spirit is retained in it, because the soul does not wish to remain in the body except through the mediation of the spirit. And when all three are conjoined, they are never separated: because the spirit retained by the body revives the soul, to the end that the soul may retain the body in triangular life. Son, by spirit we call whatever remains in the body, and whatever is congealed in it. Likewise, by souls we mean whatever is separated from it through sublimation in the radical moisture of nature. And when you thus see the body prepared, give it its menstrual, which stands overflowing through the virtue of the mixture of its own nature, to the end that from this overflow it may altogether recover more than it lost in the flux of dissolution. Afterward conjoin with its own nature, which it must integrate, until it is fixed and flowing; project one part of this matter upon a thousand parts of quicksilver, or of whatever body you wish, and it will transform into true silver. Always, however, son, keep in memory with the operation and closure of that one: dissolve and bind it, because water is the medium for separating the tinctures of all elements, and for conjoining them in a royal crown of triangular form.

On the Conditions of the Elixir.
Cap. XX.

Son, the first condition is that it have the power to separate and segregate all foreign sulphurousness and all unclean terrestriality of sick bodies, in such a manner

--- Page F47 (printed page 117, Practica de Furnis, Cap. XX cont.) ---
TESTAMEN. NOVISS.

that from the whole mixture they are separated by infusion, after the projection. And this condition is acquired immediately through the virtue of the conjunction of the elements, according as they were bound together, with the mixture of the union of altered things, by the virtues of altered, miscible, and terminated things, with the administration of the fire of great sublimation.

The second condition is that the medicine have the power to illuminate and alter every body into the most brilliant whiteness, or resplendent citrine color, according to the intention that you sought with great lucidity, to the end that the diminished body may be perfected with a more perfect perfection in its complement from the whole into the whole. And the medicine acquires this condition through the virtue of the noble preparation of its nature, namely of quicksilver. And in such a manner that it is disposed into elements, and afterward it goes to the deeper part of the alterable body without separation: but this cannot be done unless its substance, namely of the body, is made more subtle through strong dissolutions and sublimations, done with prolonged preparations. And thus the brilliance of its illumination is established. The third condition of the medicine is, principally, that if it by its virtue leads the altered metal, or the reiterated Solar fusion, to the end that the metal be yielding addition to every assay, nor can it be separated from the mixture in the trials of assays: and the medicine acquires these virtues through the operation of induration and fixation into calite, before it is incerated and fixed through the intention of flowing: because when all the extraneous mois-

--- Page F48 (printed page 118, Practica de Furnis, Cap. XX cont.) ---
RAIMVNDI LVLLII

ture, phlegmatic, which cannot endure fire, is separated through the virtue of the optic fire, and totally destroyed, when the spirit is fixed into white calcium with the reiteration of sublimation of the unfixed part over the matter.

The fourth condition is that the medicine have a perpetual virtue, with a firm alteration of impression against the harshness of fire without evacuation. And this comes to it through the virtue of its own fixation, after the preparation of its brilliant substance, with its fusible ferments.

The fifth condition is that the medicine have the virtue to bestow the weight of the aforesaid perfected things to the termination of the weight of perfected metals: and this comes to it only through the property of the metallic substance from which it is composed, as much as pertains to the property of the operation, which made the binding together of plural and minimal parts conjoined into one. Son, do you wish to know what the altering things are? They are quicksilvers, altered, or they are bodies, which we call sulphurous: because when they are altered by the primary altering agents, they themselves are the secondary altering agents of the primary altering agents, and of things to be altered. And as if in those things, especially in one, there is uniformity and densification, and therefore it naturally adheres to bodies, and with these it is bound by reason of its uniformity, and through density it gives it good and correct weight. And therefore it will be necessary for us to know this, which afterward we saw with clear experience, namely when from Mercuries a man must create the perfect medicine, more than from other spirits, with the concordant differ-

--- Page F49 (printed page 119, Practica de Furnis, Cap. XX cont.) ---
TESTAMEN. NOVISS.

concordance according to the degrees of its preparation. Son, if you have knowledge of the preparation, you will be a great philosopher, and full of real philosophy. Son, do you know why modern philosophers and youths fall short in knowledge of the virtues of nature, more in the strengthening of natural virtue than in purging corrupt matter? Because they do not know how to compose strengthening medicines, which pertain more to form than to matter. Nor do they know how to expel medicinal matter from corrupt things, in which there is matter with the entire celestial virtue implanted in it, through the whole course of nature, with the slow coction of the sun and the stars. Because it is necessary that natural virtue, which is heat, and the governor of nature, be aided by the fifth celestial natural virtue, in mixing with medicinal matter subtly drawn from its corruption through the power of good understanding, which every good philosopher must have, if he wishes to understand the operation by which natural metallic matter is drawn out and brought to completion under the preservation of its virtue, through which it must operate. And therefore, since they are ignorant through lack of recognized evidence of the extraction and composition of such medicines, they are deceived by their foolish and blind suppositions. Therefore, because they wholly ignore the measure of the strength of the bond by which the operative virtue is bound in its matter, until it could overcome the purchased degrees of graduated heat, beyond the power of the virtue of the bond. And therefore the said virtue is dissolved, and is renewed from its subject, and flees from the burning fire, which is its mortal enemy, and the matter remains without strengthening virtue

--- Page F50 (printed page 120, Practica de Furnis, Cap. XX cont.) ---
RAIMVNDI LVLLII

strengthening virtue. And the cause why the virtue withdraws from fire is this: because its subject, substance, or its body was not exhaled together with the said virtue, before fire is given to it beyond the measure of the strength of its bond: because the strengthening virtue is carried and drawn with common fire applied together with natural fire, in which there is celestial virtue, which you likewise seek, and by such instruction being informed, so that common fire does not exceed the said measure. And therefore, in aid of all who wish to assist nature through strengthening, we say that they should learn to extract the known virtues, with the preceding doctrine, from vegetable, mineral, and animal bodies, without injury to their subjects. And for doing this we direct the understanding to the water of simple composition. Son, it is necessary that you apply the extracted virtues with subtle matters, to the end that the strengthening power is not disturbed by the grossness of matter. And therefore, son, when you wish to strengthen, see whether the virtue can overcome the matter: and that if the whole will be simple, and mild for performing its actions against ailments, which natural heat by dilating always through the virtue of strengthening medicine expels whatever is contrary to its individuals, just as fire conjoins homogeneous things and segregates heterogeneous things, which are contrary to homogeneous things. And see, son, that the subtle matter in which you dissolve the essential virtues is not of easy resolution into gross parts, namely into the smallest and almost insensible particles, to the end that the virtue of heat

--- Page F51 (printed page 121, Practica de Furnis, Cap. XX cont.) ---

of natural heat, so great accidentally in receiving with great delight the strengthening that acts back and perceptibly against whatever infirmity there may be, is annihilated without injury to the other spirits: because through too great a motion made in such a case, many would be weakened. And this would be a contrary strengthening, inasmuch as the body is weakened through the excitation of great motion in gross substances, so that it might through its own property segregate the heterogeneous things of metals, when through its spirit it is transformed, except through the aid of the ash furnace. And it is by reason of the gross medicinal substance, in which the virtue is bound, which transmits that which hinders the completing of the entire action of its spirit, which is by dividing: this, which belongs to the essence of nature, and conjoin this, which is close to the nature of quicksilver, which afterward is found changed into fine silver, better than that from the mine. And therefore in this case we return to the same thing, for the purpose of dividing that which is extraneous to the pure nature. But do not understand that in your ash furnaces you should place lead, because the medicine will do everything, with the aid of the ashen fire. Son, when such medicines are projected upon bodies, they afterward cannot endure the fire of ignition: rather they immediately melt without determined ignition: and this is because their nature suffers the contrariety of undigested matter, because it makes it melt before its time, until through the ash furnace the undigested matter is consumed, which stands in the place of the phlegmatic vaporizable substance. Son, if you have not understood, repeat any lesson, dividing it into diverse lessons and into diverse chapters: convert each chapter into chapters, and let each chapter be one lesson for you, and with recollective memory and intelligible understanding with armed will, mix one lesson with another: because one understood makes you understand another with some small amount of words, enigmas, as they themselves confess through these following words: Cleanse the stone, purge it, grind it, and at the same time compound it hexagonally, let it be cooked with pure liquids, the finest powder of unslaked lime mixed with it, so that it passes into a mass, and the stone, etc.

XXI. Son, in the sweetening of the said nature, you shall proceed by the regimen of fixation. And if you understand what has been told you concerning nature, how Christ saves us through nature. I ask, son, why do the virtues in the deity make

a triangular form from the divine triangle, and not from others? It is because it is the mother of other material virtues. And because of this you can understand through art that without divine virtues nothing can be formed; wherefore, if you wish to stand stronger than nature, it will be necessary for you to know how to sew the fruit in the hood, as Virgil did when he lost his form. Son, nature can form one thing into its first form, if another way does not move it through the aid of art, just as we have properly seen through this science, when the corrupt thing does not return into a similar body, but rather into dry powder, and humid Elixir, which converts all other things according to its purpose: similarly it is with all of this. Nature cannot perfect, if it does not receive aid from art, which preserves its species from the corruption of individuals, from which it is afterward reformed into the form of whatever body, when it makes powders from metal, and from those the Elixir, from which all metals at once receive health, through the great power of that thing which is called Elixir. Do not understand, however, that it is terminated in the form of metal, as before. And to this we assent, because that which is less is worth more, since from a single grain a hundred thousand are born, more than we have told you concerning the doubled virtue, i.e. and to this body, which it converts and adorns with virtues, such as lead, or tin, or iron, or quicksilver, or copper, white or red, to

How Errors Should Be Corrected.
Cap. XXII.

Son, it is impossible in this science that you can have the fifth spirits without some material change of color into color. In which matters be forewarned that you should have solicitude in such manner that all colors be pleasing to you, except redness, which comes in the matter after the separation of the fifth substances through stimulating fire against nature as its cause. Son, therefore the fifth substances which remain in the dry are burned and their tincture consumed: because they wish to be the virtue of simple humid air, nor can they be drawn out, unless they are conjoined with them artificially, and through their attractive virtue from their attracted and revivified substance, as is clear in the last point of the separation of their waters in the bath. If you do not know how to draw the air from the waters, ask nature, that through its attractive virtue it may wish to teach you: because it will teach you: because through the appetite for that which it lost in its calcination, it separates the air from the middle of the water with restoration, until the medicine of attractive virtues

--- Page F56 (printed page 126, Practica de Furnis, Cap. XXII cont.) ---
RAIMVNDI LVLLII

is almost totally separated. And beware of great fire in the distillation of air, because the body would be reddened, and would suffocate the attractive virtue which the air gives you. Where it would be at the degree of suffocation, it could attract more than you need, except with very subtle air. Therefore extract it through inhumations, because those preserve tinctures from all burning, and restore the lost moisture, by revivifying the attractive virtue. And if you see that the body has also thus been reddened, and its leaves are like those of Talc, which in similar fashion is often changed, cast it into its water, and in that water cook it until it takes on the life of whiteness: and draw out all airs and inhumations without calcinations made with fire extraneous to it. Its three colors are principal through our instruments, which give understanding of what nature wills: and they are blackness, redness, whiteness, as we have told you in the chapter, Always, son, etc. But this we tell you with the intention of drawing airs, and correcting errors, and avoiding them. Always, however, since it is of total whiteness and of much blackness, beware that there be for you only one redness, which must come at the end of the first degree. And if it comes before, it signifies the combustibility of heat, because they wish to redden through haste, which they feel from the inclination of stimulating fire, which has been administered to it through faulty instruction, before its own proper motion has been completed for them. Therefore, the more it is reddened before its time, the more it loses of its Tincture in its final and proper reddening, inasmuch as its reddening essence is collected into the outermost matter, which does not

--- Page F57 (printed page 127, Practica de Furnis, Caput XXII cont.) ---
TESTAMEN. NOVISS.

which cannot endure fire, and this, since it does not have true redness in true preparation. And if you understand this, you will know why the sophisticators fail in making true gold.

On the composition of our Vinegar, which constrains quicksilver to expel its nature.
Caput XXIII.

Son, take good wine, well dry, and with it dissolve D. to the fourth part of dry vinasse, and with as much of C. And if in place of wine you put the juice of lunaria, one bolus will be worth ten. Afterward, take the water by distillation, which is the juice of great exuberance, and proceed to the dissolution of its Mercury, as we told you above in the book of Mercuries, and draw out the clear water with great fire, as we told you at the beginning of our practice, and in that dissolve what is dissolvable of the densified stones and of the liquefiable metals. And if first the body drew the substance of the menstrual knowledge, the mineral virtue will be multiplied. And on account of this let it be revealed to you how the vivifying virtues are infused into materials with conjoined spirits. Son, beware that you should not wish to give anything of such things, and know that this science is not permitted to be read except in generality with other sciences, without naming anything of it, neither publicly nor secretly. And therefore, because one ought not to give motion to a human person to enter by art into natural things, except through one's own motion by the gift of glory, which is the God of heaven, who knows

--- Page F58 (printed page 128, Practica de Furnis, Caput XXIII cont. / Caput XXIIII) ---

The question is why we made the art, and through nature it is completed in A. and that the artist should ascend to high places, and that King Edward might fill his heart with good will against the pagan peoples, through n.a.u.y.z.e.a.u.z.j.u.z. And all these letters with the others from above signify with the declaration in our compendious art, which is called Vade mecum, to the number of the philosophers with the whole integrity of the present Testament.

On the projections of all medicines, both universally and particularly.
Caput XXIIII.

Son, projections are made upon fused bodies according to the greater and lesser virtues which the stones acquire through fused subtlety: because the subtler the stone is, the more you must put of fused metal, and less of the medicine. But there are many who in projections of particular medicines are deceived through ignorance, like those who do not know how to test, nor how to see the alteration of medicines: because when some make a projection of them, namely of a little upon much of the fused body, they do not see anything, neither in penetration nor in alteration, and so they suspect the medicine to be of no virtue, and do not care to proceed further. Wherefore if immediately that metal into which the medicine was poured had passed through the cupel, or through

--- Page F59 (printed page 129, Practica de Furnis, Caput XXIIII cont.) ---

or through a long cupel, they would know and see that whatever is there has been radically transmuted from the body into pure metal. Son, since in your medicines according to greater and lesser degree that subtle spirit is infused, which you seek, which by necessity and the virtue of its property fixes and changes all bodies, just as purgatives purge all leprosy, and is an antidote against every infirmity. And this, by nourishing the natural heat in the bodies of metals, imperceptibly penetrates every body, and in the depth of that spirit which knows, like nature, and the fire of nature, and the radical moisture, which is of exceedingly fixed retention and great alteration, under the conformation and reformation of individual metals. Beyond this, by the appetitive virtue of the leprous metal, because it draws to itself the glorified humid spirit, and draws it to the deepest part of its heart, with the cooperative aid of the impulsive virtue, which is moved by the heat of nature, which impels the most powerful spirit into the body, which is informed when the parts touch, and are conjoined, and are mixed to such a degree that the more fixed parts retain the virtue of the fixed spirit contracted to the deepest part of them in perpetual life, and the metal remains altered, as can be proved through its examinations. And in the same way as you make your projections upon bodies, so likewise you shall make them upon Mercury. And if the medicines with which you wish to make projection upon Mercury are not of subtler substance, by reason of the abbreviation of their confection, they will not have ingress into Mercury before its flight. If you do not congeal Mercury through the vapor of another

--- Page F60 (printed page 130, Practica de Furnis, Caput XXIIII cont.) ---
RAIMVNDI LVLLII

Mercury, whether it be vegetable or mineral, it is transmuted into sulphur: let the congelation of that be according to what we said, in such a long vessel, in which there is multiplication of sulphur. And as we said in the congelation of that, for projecting the medicine. But if you wish to make projection from another medicine, you need not congeal it, because before it is constituted in flight, the Elixir itself penetrates it, on account of its subtlety, because it is a thing perfect in all its virtues. Nevertheless, you can always subtilize the said other particular medicines, or do it through solution and congelation and join with ferment, which likewise will fall upon a thousand thousand parts of them, and converts them all. But the certainty of weight in the projection and sublimation of the said medicines, with the aid of that which we have given you, we leave to the industry and judgment of your greatest mastery, since it has been fully treated in our Codicil. Son, in the projection of our medicines you will find every metal's middle altered to perfection extrinsically, until it is led through the cupel: because tin appears as tin, as before, and is slightly brittle and slightly hardened without manifest brightness, until it is led through the cupel and through it is well purged. And so it is with Saturn, and likewise with Venus: but this one is in the color of Jupiter, yet led through the cupel you will be able to see that it has been changed into purified silver. And these diversities come through the maturity of the virtues of the branches of the stone, according to the greater and lesser degree of their preparations and sublimations. Wherefore the heat of the mutative spirit of these does not have so much virtue [that] its own make those err who through their dark

--- Page F61 (printed page 131, Practica de Furnis, Caput XXIIII cont.) ---
TESTAMEN. NOVISS.

ignorance do not know how to choose the materials of nature by their properties: whence they are deceived and blinded in the art, like people of little wisdom. And to you, son, we say with the true part of its property, that you should never understand any other salt except that of metals: because they are dissolved into it, as you can see with your own eyes through the art. If therefore you know how to sweeten that salt, it will enter into bodies like true nature, which wishes to stand within: and it will transform them from one species into another. Let the King of Metha have an ill pleasure, or the Sultan of Calamia. Because since salts are the pure nature of metals, natured from the metallic property, on account of the friendship of that thing they are all conjoined together, since salt is nothing but fire, nothing but sulphur, nothing but quicksilver converted into a stone of price, which is the matter of altered nature, changed from vileness to nobility: because perfect mixture cannot be made without the alteration of the things of nature, which can be mixed in perfect unity, speaking corporeally. Son, you know what sort of thing is reputed vile; you know that if it were not known, man would make no estimation of it: and on the other part, which is a thing finished according to the nature of its act in complete being, expelled outside the house. And because it is a thing of nature, like a man who is expelled from his own possession and would wish to return there strongly, like a thing which was for him of utility and preservation of his bodies, in which it terminates: Therefore it briefly wishes to be formed, how through its own instinct it distinctly desires to retain its extraneous things: and beyond this, and it is found in every place, since without it no thing can be generated. There is one vile thing found everywhere,

On the method of separating the elements in eight days, for achieving all brief works, with the letters of the Alphabet, and the composition of sulphur, and its confection.
Caput XXV.

When you have made H. project it into B. And afterward make the separation of H. and of B. through Y. And set H. aside. Then separate L. through X., dead and dry. Afterward conjoin H. with B. and place the whole in V. And after three days separate H. with E. through Z. And afterward separate H. from C. through V. And take from B. that which remained to it of H. through X. And you will have the first element, which we call earth. And when you wish to have water, which is the second element, take C. and lead it through X. And afterward conjoin with H. and place the whole in the chamber of V. And after two days separate H. through Y. And from E. take that which remained to it of H. with the benefit of X. And you will have the second element, which we call Mineral Earth. And when you wish to have air, which is the third element, take D. E. that which has been led through X. Conjoin with H. and place in the chamber of V. for one natural day. And afterward we separate E. with H. And again from E. separate H. through Y. And

And you will have fire in the nature of heat. And all four elements from Mercury, or from quicksilver. And if you have understood us, we have already told you the sublimation of that. And when you wish to operate through mixture from the matter of Venus, or of Mars, and of both together, or thus through Ciofrosia, or through Anafrosia of the elements, of those put no more than B. The others are of the most wicked and infirm ore. De fermentis. Cap. XXVI.

Son, with the medicines of ferments you can ferment the nature of all bodies: and because we have not yet spoken of them, we tell you that before you wish to ferment, see first that the

--- Page F66 (printed page 136, Practica de Furnis, Caput XXVI cont.) ---
RAIMVNDI LVLLII

ferment is well prepared. Son, the preparation of this is that it should first be passed through the principal controverting agents of nature, before you make the fermentation from it: because for you it should first be a powder calcined through liquefaction, secondarily a powder resolved through dissolution, and thirdly a powder incerated through coagulation, and fourthly sublimated through separation. Son, in hardening and softening stands our secret: because without them no simple body can be brought into perfect union: and if there is no perfect mixture of altered things with true perfect union, then gold and silver will not be fit for working. Son, the ferment is a perfect body subtilized and altered through the power of the said converting agents together with the stone all at once: because no body would be sublimated, were it not for the benefit of the nature of the stone, which draws it upward, as the magnet draws iron. And the Elixir, which must give relaxation with the addition of humid elements, consists of subtle powders, which stand in the middle of the sublimate. If therefore you have sublimated the sublimation of this body, you understand the reiteration with the mixture of the humid sublimated spirit. As many times as you do this through the excitation of the operation, so many times you will gain a hundred parts in its projection: because the lighter, subtler, and more volatile the body is, the better it is for projecting. One weight upon a hundred, and a hundred upon a thousand, and so to infinity. Conjoin the body with white ferment for the white, or red for the red, and make a marriage of stone and of stone, until one fixed body through the medicine is conjoined with the water of Mercury. And afterward give it fusion with the oil of the stone only. And note if

--- Page F67 (printed page 137, Practica de Furnis, Caput XXVI cont. / Caput XXVII) ---
TESTAMEN. NOVISS.

you wish to know why metals have motion, do everything by way of reduction, and it will be a tincture of exuberant and incomparable price. And as has been said to you concerning the sublimation of bodies with the stone, we still reveal more to you to the end that you may better understand the Wheel which has been given to you: that the stone drawn upward comes to our quicksilver, which draws into its nature the virtue of the diamond: and bodies are full of dross when they are resolved into their first nature through the virtue of the stone, which is sweet.

Aliae reuelationes, et de proprijs naturis, et de proprijs subiectis. Cap. XXVII.

We tell you that the said natures are nothing other than sharp salts, which you cannot have until you pass from the first into the second part: because those which receive tinctures are the matter of nature, and the tinctures are of the first Mercurial essence, of one pure nature. Thus nature must not be conjoined except with its own nature, namely with its own most pure intrinsic matter, expelled and purified by art from the vitriols of the dead, with the aid of the stone, namely from metals. Son, the said matter of nature in virtue is such a thing, and is called the earth of living sulphur, created from quicksilver: and it is called the proper salt of nature, which is its matter: and the tinctures of its proper essence are quicksilver, which likewise cannot be had except through the sciences of the regimen of the first part, which constitutes the first degree of the operation of nature. If therefore you are to be diligent in the said regimens, to the end that you may have animated matter for making medicine of high

--- Page F68 (printed page 138, Practica de Furnis, Caput XXVII cont.) ---
RAIMVNDI LVLLII

price, because it consists of sulphur and quicksilver, or of the salt of nature. Son, this nature is never anything but one common thing, from which nature is natured: because mineralized nature is impregnated by formal nature, and from it nature works in perfecting its renewals against extraneous corruption, which we call destruction. Wherefore, son, if you have understood us, I have already told you that our silver is never without sulphur, as you already know, since it converts it: nor can sulphur stand without the nature of the sun, which is the principal medium in form, through which it passes to making and completing its generations. Son, if then nature had quicksilver in its operation, its parts could not be continued from its matter, nor could it be preserved in the radical moisture. And if it did not have sulphur, it did not have salt, therefore it could not penetrate the parts which must be congealed through a defect of sharpness. And therefore we call it the Honorable Salt, the Animated Salt: because it is a treasure of nature such that it penetrates and expels: and this through the virtue of its heat, by which the matter stands in nature in expelling, penetrates and expels, and in penetrating congeals through its great dryness the moisture of quicksilver in its radicality, and our salt follows the formal mixture, and is the harmonious salt of all elements. Wherefore you should not wonder if we call it harmonious salt: because just as it has an exalted and sublimated name through its property and pure and first matter of nature, so it is the whole of nature, and stands within, through which it is nature, like a smith in his forge, who is apt for forging. For these reasons we also call it the salt of fire: because fire is nothing other than nature captured in na-

--- Page F69 (printed page 139, Practica de Furnis, Caput XXVII cont. / Cantilena) ---
TESTAMEN. NOVISS.

in nature in the art philosophically, with natural heat, standing complexioned according to the merit of its subject. It is also called the Honorable Salt, the sharp water of the sea: it is also called the salt of egg yolks, and the star Diana, and the morning star, and the flying eagle, and the cloud, and fire, bitter salt, salt of food, salt of glass, ash, borax, salt of urine, Athincar, alkali salt, salt of the pit, exalted sulphur, and sublimated Mercury. And all these names.

Incipit Cantilena Raimundi Lullij.

Love made me search, for with corruption you can, because without it you cannot distance generation from its being, and essence makes the fifth operate. If you wish to make unity from this, and I wish to declare more to you, it is necessary to have plural love, and concerning this I will give you this similitude to

know: a parent extended from the simple through concordance. Another thing which is called incense, you will have to know with the dissimilitude of air, it will be congealed. That which love makes by concordance for concordance of the elements, you others with created beings through the fantastical creature, if you wish to have genus, you will have the measure of real beings. Abstract beings are given to be sought from the maker through the making, especially by knowing you will find the natural one; air will be a higher lover. But if nature does not avail you, you will no longer be an advocate, for being ignorant of the art, likewise of the transubstantial essence of humors, and of the accident, with its substantial parts. There must be transmutations, and likewise of the accident, after thus newly renounced, converted by other signs into another being of all its elemental degrees, with an accident of new being: then art cannot serve you, that it not be of another skin, but fails in conception; if you wish to enter through the storm itself, so that you know well how to govern yourself, it will be necessary for you to carry the globe, so that you can enter and exit. Son, you have all this in the Testament; with the will of charity you will find the aggregation: for that which is revealed is sought daily. We can put everything into a dictation, because it is worthy and pleasing. Whence the man who has much is esteemed by seeking after the pagan people, and in power is strengthened with a heart of noble daring, which are
prudence and charity.

ELVCIDATIO TESTAMEN-
ti Raimundi Lullij.

Although we have composed very many books of diverse operations of our philosophical art, nevertheless we prefer this last little book of ours above the rest, which deserves to be entitled by us the Elucidation of the Testament: since what we conceal with immense prolixity in our Testament and Codicil, written in philosophical terms, we show more lucidly in this little book of ours, and elucidate most briefly, so that henceforth there is no need to compose other books. Since the composition of our books is nothing other than the subtlety of a keen intellect for concealing our art, which, copiously displayed in our books, is here led from obscurity into pleasant light -- something no philosopher ever dared to do. And we attach six chapters to this book, in which the entire mystery of this art is elucidated in the clearest terms, of which chapters:

The first is on the matter of our Stone.
The second is on the vessel.
The third, on the furnace.
The fourth, on the fire.

--- Page F75 (printed page 145, Elucidatio cap. 1-2) ---
ELVCIDATIO.

The fifth, on the decoction.
The sixth, on the Tincture and multiplication.

Chapter one, on the matter
of our Stone.

First, then, let us undertake to demonstrate the matter of our Stone. For we have introduced extraneous things into our mastery through their similitudes, though in truth only one single thing constitutes our stone, to which we add nothing extraneous nor diminish anything. We have also described three stones, namely the Mineral, the Animal, and the Vegetable, though there is only one stone of our art. We wish, O son, to signify this composite of three things, namely body, soul, and spirit, which is called mineral because it comes from the mine; animal, because it has a soul; vegetable, because it grows and multiplies. In this lies hidden the entire secret of our mastery, which is the Sun, the Moon, and the water of life. And the water of life is the soul and life of bodies, through which our stone is vivified and supplanted. Therefore we call it heaven, and the fifth essence, and incombustible oil, and by infinitely many other names, since it is nearly as incorruptible as heaven, in the continuous circulation of its own motion. Thus, by lucid demonstration, free of all circumlocution, you have the matter of our stone.

Chapter two, on the vessel.

Now we have resolved to speak of our vessel. Prick up your ears here, O son, so that you may grasp our design. Although many kinds of vessels are written in enigmatic fashion in our volumes, nevertheless our

--- Page F76 (printed page 146, Elucidatio cap. 2-3) ---
RAIMVNDI LVLLII

design employs no other vessel except one alone, which we shall here demonstrate with clear proofs, in which our work is completed from beginning to end of the entire mastery. Our vessel, then, is composite. For there are two vessels with their alembics attached, of the same size and height, where the nose of one enters the belly of the other, so that what rises by the action of heat into the head of the vessel from either part, again descends by the action of cold into the belly. Son, thus you have the Elucidation of our vessel, if you are not stiff-necked.

Chapter three, on the furnace.

We are about to speak of our furnace, but it will be most difficult to disclose the secret of our furnace, which the ancient philosophers concealed. For we have depicted various furnaces in our books, though we use only one furnace, which is called the Athanor, the interpretation of which is "immortal fire," because it provides fire always equally, enduring in the same degree from the beginning to the end of our stone, vivifying and maturing. Son, listen to our words and you will understand that our furnace is composite, made of two parts, well sealed at the closure joint. Its design is as follows: Let the furnace be made large or small according to the quantity of the material, for a large quantity of material requires a large furnace, and a small quantity a small one, in the manner of a distillation furnace with its cover firmly sealed, so that when the furnace has been assembled with its cover, it should have absolutely

--- Page F77 (printed page 147, Elucidatio cap. 3-4) ---
TESTAMEN. NOVISS.

one breathing hole, so that the heat of the kindled fire may breathe; because the nature of fire requires this furnace alone and no other. And the closure of the joint of this furnace of ours is called the seal of Hermes and of the wise, because it is known only to the wise, and the rest has never been expressed by any philosopher, but is reserved in wisdom, which guards it by its own common authority.

Chapter four, on the fire.

Although we have treated of a threefold fire in our books -- namely, natural, connatural, and contrary to nature -- and of other diverse modes of our fire, nevertheless we wish to signify one fire composed of several things, and it is the greatest secret to arrive at the knowledge of this, since it is not a human but an angelic, a celestial gift, to reveal it. Lest, therefore, the execration that the philosophers left for posterity fall upon us, we pray to God that this treasure of our secret fire come only into the hands of the wise. Son, incline your ears to perceive that our fire is composed of two things. For the Creator of all things created two things among the rest suited to this fire, namely horse dung and quicklime, whose composition causes our fire. Its design is as follows: Take the belly of a horse, well-digested horse dung one part, clean quicklime one part. Having composed these and packed them into our furnace, and having placed our vessel containing the matter of the stone in the middle, and the furnace being firmly sealed, you will have the divine fire, without

--- Page F78 (printed page 148, Elucidatio cap. 5-6) ---
RAIMVNDI LVLLII

light and without charcoal, which, situated in its furnace, cannot be otherwise than it must be.

Chapter five, on the Decoction.

There are also manifold modes of preparation of our stone set forth in our Testament and in other volumes of ours, namely: solution, coagulation, sublimation, distillation, calcination, separation, fusion, inceration, imbibition, fixation, etc. The signification of all of these is decoction. Since in our decoction alone all these modes are accomplished. The design of our decoction is that you place the matter of the composite according to its measure into the vessel, furnace, and its fire, continually decocting until it arrives at the fullness of maturity, which will be completed in ten months without any manual operation from beginning to end of the entire mastery. But we wish the assigned modes to signify the sublimity of our art, and that it leads the understanding of the foolish astray under a shadowy veil, so that the unworthy may not reach the summit of the hill of our secret, but may persevere in error, until the Sun and Moon are reduced into one globe -- which is impossible to accomplish except by the command of God.

Chapter six, on the tincture and the multiplication of our Stone.

Lastly, we shall narrate concerning the tincture and the multiplication, which is the end and completion of the entire mastery. For we have shown manifold modes of projection of our tincture in our volumes, though in fact our tinc-

--- Page F79 (printed page 149, Elucidatio cap. 6 conclusion, end of text) ---
ELVCIDATIO.

ture is not different from the multiplication, and neither of these can be accomplished without the other. Because our stone, unless it is first tinged -- and when it is tinged, its quantity is multiplied. And when our stone has been multiplied, whether from white or red, it is tinged. O son, dispel the obfuscation of the understanding, in order to comprehend the secret of secrets, hidden with skillful industry in our books, which here emerges from the depths into the heights. Hear and understand that our multiplication is nothing other than the reiteration of our original composite work. For in the first reiteration, one part of our stone tinges three parts of imperfect body, and grows and multiplies in that same quantity of parts. In the second reiteration, one part tinges 7 parts. In the third, one part tinges fifteen. In the fourth, thirty-one. In the fifth, 63. In the sixth, 127 -- and it always grows and multiplies in that many parts, proceeding to infinity.

Behold, son, we elucidate from our writings in parabolic sayings hitherto kept secret, against the precepts of the philosophers. But we wish to be excused from their rebuke, lest by divine permission we fall under their execration. To almighty God, in whose power are all things, we commit this work of our little book -- He who bestows all knowledge and every gift upon whom He wills, and withdraws it -- so that it may be kept hidden in the power of His divinity, that the unwise and the impious may by no means be permitted to discover it. Now, son of wisdom, give thanks to God, who by His divine illumination closes and opens the human understanding, whose name be blessed,
for ever and ever.
Amen.

RAYMVNDI LVLLI

INCIPIT COMPENDIVM ANIMAE transmutationis artis metallorum Ruperto Anglorum Regi per Raymundum transmissum.

ALREADY many times and more have we spoken, as we recall, in many and diverse ways of practicing upon the mastery of the precious philosophers' stone. Now indeed, that you may weigh the truth by its light, know that three things are principally to be noted. First, know the material from which the philosophers' stone and all other precious stones are composed. Second, know the instruments with which one must work. Third, know how the said material must be worked and practiced with the instrument. Know that around these three things our mastery revolves.

--- Page 441 (page 212, Animae Transmutatio cont.) ---
ANIMAE TRANSMVTATIO.

What is the material of our stone.

First therefore, most illustrious King, know that the material of our stone, or of all the stones of the philosophers and the virtuous, which are generated or composed by artifice, is the metallic soul and our menstruum rectified and sharpened, or celestial lunaria, which among the philosophers is called vegetable Mercury, arising from red or white wine, as is clearly evident and was clearly demonstrated, divinely revealed in the figure of the individuals of the third book of the fifth essence composed for the Abbot of Saint Benedict near Paris. And also in our Codicil sent to you by King Edward. The instruments are two glass cucurbits having seven or eight receptacles, as is demonstrated in our testament sent to you with the codicil, in the chapter that begins, "Son, we intend for you," etc. The method of practice is treated fully in what follows. The present book is divided into two principal parts: The first part indeed concerns the Theory of this art, and remains undivided, and begins there, as immediately follows: "But nevertheless it is necessary, most sere-

--- Page 442 (page 212 verso, Animae Transmutatio cont.) ---
MVNDI LVLLI

ne prince," etc. The second part is about the practice, and is divided into two principal parts. The first is about the composition of the philosophers' stone, and it begins there, "You, in the virtue of A," etc. The second part of this second part is about the composition of precious stones, and begins, "The work of pearls you shall prepare thus," etc.

First part.

But nevertheless it is necessary, most serene prince, first to extract our menstruum by mastery from its mother, that is, from the dregs of wine and phlegm, through the office of the alembic, and it is to be sharpened in distillation with the pertinent vegetable substances, which are: wild celery, squill, nightshade, thistle, muscatel, oleander, black pepper, euphorbium, vicitella or flamula, and pellitory, equal parts of all, and pulverized. On the other hand, the menstruum itself is to be rotated in a vessel of circulation continuously for the space of ten days in a warm dung-bed, wine-bath, or bain-marie. Likewise it is necessary, dearest son, to have the prime matter of the elements and the sulphur of nature. And because quicksilver is the existing genus of metals,

--- Page 443 (page 213, Animae Transmutatio cont.) ---
ANIMAE TRANSMVTATIO.

which we draw by artifice and extract by the art of mastery from metals through the division of the elements, and their purification, and the fixation of those with the agents through rarefaction and circulation on the one part: and on the other part through the rectification of the elements of metals. This genus indeed, of which we understand the philosophizing, is in our codicil in the chapter that begins, "Through this division the second reason is elucidated," etc. And this indeed is what we understand by the first Mercury through the limes around this chapter, etc. Son, take equal weight of air and earth, a little less or more, and this also is of the nature from which we understand it to be made, and we say that the unctuous moisture is the nearer or more proper material of our stone, in chapter 36, which begins, "We told you that insofar as the radical moisture," etc., through which moisture the common people understand sulphur to be common sulphur. Remember, O King, what we said in our codicil that begins, "Son, I intend to tell you the material of the ferments," for when we say that through one decoction, we understand another rectification of the menstruum. It has a simple

--- Page 444 (page 213 verso, Animae Transmutatio cont.) ---
MVNDI LVLLI

power; we understand the first rectification of the menstruum. And when we say that it is gradually transferred through another coction, we understand another rectification of the menstruum up to the third or fourth. Likewise you should also understand in the chapter other decoctions of the stone, as will be evident in the following practice. Afterwards, son, this water rectified four times we call vegetable quicksilver or water of Mercury. And thus whatever we say through that chapter, we understand of that. This also is the material of which we understand in that codicil in the chapter that begins, "Of this material," and this is that moisture, called permanent water, which we understand in our codicil in the chapter that begins, "Our moisture is water throughout." This indeed, O King, is more clearly evident in the second book of the fifth essence, which I do not believe is hidden from you. Consider therefore, O King, the nature of our menstruum, how virtuous it is, and understand the things that have been said about it in the aforementioned chapters. For through it the mastery of the stone is ennobled to such an extent that its abundance can in no way be expressed. The present reasoning is evident through those who corrupt metals

--- Page 445 (page 214, Animae Transmutatio cont.) ---
ANIMAE TRANSMVTATIO.

with waters outside of nature, because they are ignorant of our menstruum, because it happens thus that its dissolution somewhat opposes nature; however, if they were to strengthen the metal dissolved with our menstruum through inhumations of twelve or sixteen days, and were to wash it from the blackness, they would strengthen the water against nature. But they do not know how to strengthen it with a natural thing. Therefore their practice is ultimately deprived of effect, and they blame the art for the defect that is theirs. And what we have said about the knowledge of the menstruum, which is one of those things without which nothing can be done in the mastery of this art: let this suffice for you concerning it.

On the sperm of metals, which is the material of our stone.

Now you should know, most serene Prince, that the spirit of metals is the other part and the material of our stone, which you must evacuate from the bodies of all metals. And this evacuation is done in two ways, as the practice below will show, because in two perfect bodies it is divided through putrefaction and the division of the elements and their fixation. But in imper-

--- Page 446 (page 214 verso, Animae Transmutatio cont.) ---
MVNDI LVLLI

fect metals it is divided through the purification and sublimation of simple unity, and in this step the ignorant err, thinking that the bodies of metals can be reduced to their prime matter with strong common waters, as I said above, with waters against nature. And if they had read our books, they would surely know that such liquors oppose the intention of the philosophers. And what we have said is clearly demonstrated in our codicil that begins, "Therefore gold and silver are dissolved in the radical things of their own genus." This indeed is the water with which incurable infirmities are cured, under the conservation of its nature, as is evident in the codicil that begins, "Son, the sublimation," etc. And also in the chapter that begins N. D. E., etc. Also, most serene prince, it clearly appears that those strong liquors are not of the intention of the philosophers, as is evident clearly and without metaphor in the said codicil, in the chapter that begins, "Through this distinction." The second reason is elucidated in the chapter that begins, "Just as the matrix generates through the operation of the aqueous form," near the end of the chapter: for there is nothing that conjoins the dissolutions of metals except our men-

--- Page 447 (page 215, Animae Transmutatio cont.) ---
ANIMAE TRANSMVTATIO.

struum, because it is the water with which the dissolution of metallic bodies is accomplished with the conservation of their species, as is evident in our testament of the codicil, in the chapter that begins, "But to work with strong waters is corruption," and in the chapter, "Because this has the power of burning all foreign things." Also concerning this menstruum we understand in the chapter that begins, "Concerning that menstruum in which there is a dry fire against nature." And likewise this menstrual water is that with which quicksilver is dissolved for the work of pearls, which is done through the reduction of these into prime matter, as is demonstrated below in the material on precious stones. Wherefore, most serene prince, metals cannot be dissolved in any other way unless they are animated with the vegetable menstruum, whose power is resolution in dissolvable things.

On the instrument of the stones.

We have told you, most serene prince, the thing from which the philosophers' stone is composed. Now we shall tell you the instruments with which the stone is composed. They are vessels, such as the furnace, the dung-bed or wine-press, with the arti-

--- Page 448 (page 215 verso, Animae Transmutatio cont.) ---
MVNDI LVLLI

ficer's diligence. But before you come to anything, you should know that the mastery is divided into three principal parts, namely dissolution, separation, and union. Through this it is demonstrated to you that first you must dissolve the bodies in our menstruum, then you must divide the elements from the luminaries, and purify and fix them, and extract the sulphur of nature from the perfect bodies. Third, you must unite elements with elements, or lime with lime, or elements with the sulphur of nature. And this union is divided into as many parts as there are mixtures and unions of the aforesaid. The first mode is to unite air with fire. The second mode is to unite air with the sulphur of nature, and these modes are in the elements of the two luminary bodies. The third mode is to unite the lime arisen from air in fire with the lime arisen from air and the sulphur of nature only by itself. The fourth mode is that fire by itself and air by itself be united with the sulphur of nature only. The fifth mode is to mix the sulphur of nature with two elements at once, namely with fire and air. And these modes of mixing the elements with the sulphur of nature occur in two ways,

--- Page 449 (page 216, Animae Transmutatio cont.) ---
ANIMAE TRANSMVTATIO.

because either they are with their own earth or with a foreign one, and with a foreign earth there are many and manifold modes of mixing the sulphur of nature, since they come at the pleasure of the artist. For, most serene Prince, you will be able to practice many particular works with the intellect truly directing, which the human intellect could scarcely narrate, except for those that are expressed in the third part of this book and in the Magia and in our Testament and in the Codicil, and the Vade Mecum, concerning the number of the philosophers, and in the third book of the fifth essence, and in other books of mine. Who then, most serene Prince, could narrate the particulars that exist in the breadth of the mastery after the fixation and purification of the elements? Know therefore how to purify the elements and first to divide them from metals, because in this the entire mastery lies hidden. Once the elements and the sulphur of nature have been divided from the metals, almost nothing else remains with respect to the work. For the entire labor and toil lies in this, namely in the division of the elements and sulphur. This indeed is the step where artists abandon the mastery, because their division is very tedious, and because the unskilled in the first distillation think

--- Page 450 (page 216 verso, Animae Transmutatio cont.) ---
RAYMVNDI LVLLI

that they can distill all the air of the second degree, and from the first distillation of the third degree can similarly distill all the fire from the earth. But this is impossible and against the course of nature. Indeed, most serene Prince, we propose to reveal to you the truth in the present by word, for the air cannot be divided from metals except in 20 or 22 distillations, or 30. For as many as there are distillations, so many are the putrefactions and reiterations of water and air together, namely of the menstrual water, and in each putrefaction it requires eight days or six continuous days, so that the division of the elements lasts for almost a year. But we completed the division of the elements in seven months, and the fire cannot be divided from the earth except in 40 distillations. Who then is able to endure such labor for a year or for seven months? There are almost none. And therefore the ignorant, not knowing the natural principles, think they can perform the true practice with metals without the division of the elements, and in this they are deceived. Then there are some who, when they do not at once divide their elements and the sulphur of nature at their pleasure, refuse to attend to the natures of minerals, and make their mi-

--- Page 451 (page 217, Animae Transmutatio cont.) ---
ANIMAE TRANSMVTATIO.

xture confused, without any purification or sublimation and subtilization, and they do not arrive at the desired end; attributing no blame to their own ignorance, they instead blame the art which they do not understand and assert that the art is false, among whose number we believe you, O King, to be one. Therefore do not grow weary, nor cease from the work, and you will find what you desire: for know, O King, how to combine the natural principles, and what you seek will come to pass.

On the non-natural principles, how they are understood.

But since these principles are not naturally produced in one individual thing, as many err in thinking, saying that there was one thing in the world that had the power to transmute all metals upon the earth, which is false. Likewise it is that you should seek the extremes of that thing through natural principles by means of the art running through into its mineral, which is D. E. F. But since these are contrary by reason of the extremity of their nature, E must be taken, which is the middle disposition of the extremity of the nature

--- Page 452 (page 217 verso, Animae Transmutatio cont.) ---
RAYMVNDI LVLLI

of that thing, which is collected from L. And thus D. converts X. L. M. C. L. into E., from which indeed D. draws E. into the likeness of living water in our mastery: and thus D. has the power of converting F. under the conservation of its nature and its forms. Then you will take actually everything that was in potency in the work of nature, and of the better means of that thing by reason of the extremes, because in D. and F. there are actually F. N. O. P. R. decocted, purged, and digested by the fire of nature: wherefore, son, E. N. O. P. are suited to F., and when F. has been converted into E. N. O. P., then you will have F. similar to nature, and this is done better with C. than with D. on account of greater experience, and let it be done in F. from which E. N. O. P. may arise, which otherwise are truly not converted into G. in our work. And thus G. is the proximate and prime matter from which we have created all the philosophers' stones, and by some it is called the Aqueous Elixir: and therefore, most serene Prince, we say that all stones are not created except from one single thing, namely from G., that is, from vegetable and mineral quicksilver. Therefore, son, you will proceed to the digestion of H. and draw C. from common drink, from principle to act, and in that you will place F., and it will always be converted into L., and thus will be calci-

ned F. under the conservation of its nature: afterwards you will draw C. L. from the digestion of H. and place them in the digestion of I.; then L. will be such as is fitting and suitable. Afterwards from L. C. you will draw E. N. O. P., and this in perfect metals through the mastery of the operation through K. and through exuberation. And when you have obtained E. N. O. P. through H. I. K. until E. is white and crystalline, which is nearer to the nature of F. for generating the philosophers' stone than L. was. Therefore G. E. N. O. P. are nearer to F. than L., because they are almost the ends of the mastery, but G. is nearer than E. N. O. P., and therefore it is essential that in the greater work E. N. O. P. be turned into G., which is the perfection of the mastery: for everything that we have said is nothing other than to dissolve, to putrefy, to separate the elements, to purify and to fix, and to unite the limes or to condense them, and to rectify our menstruum. See, son, if you take F. and place it in C. and put that in digestion H., the first figure is formed: F. C. H., in the middle of which you place N. And again if you take N. and place it in the digestion of I., L. will result; then a triangle is originated and formed. Likewise, if you take L. and place it in C. and put it in digestion H., afterwards through many

digestions from L. C. H. there arises N. O. P. and another triangular figure is formed. Likewise from N. O. If therefore you take L. and place it in C. and in digestion H., afterwards in digestion I., from L. C. H. I. there arises E. and another quadrangular figure is formed from O. C. L. H. I., in the middle of which is E., that is, the soul of metals, which is generated from the said letters. Likewise if you take E. and place it in Q. and mix it with C. D. Then if you take G. and G. and place them together in wax molds, as is fitting, or if you place G. with E., immediately there arises one figure from two quadrangles, in the middle of which is X. Y., by which we wish to signify the philosophers' stone. But this entire mastery is accomplished through the division of the elements. Let this be done, my son, according to the course of this art, yet in such a way that you know how to reduce any figures into others, and if you corrupt one, you know how to generate another up to the ultimate figure, in which all other figures come to rest. And let these things concerning the Theory suffice for you for the sake of brevity.

ANIMAE TRANSMVTATIO.

We have spoken of the first part, which concerns the Theory: now we must speak of the second part, which concerns the Practice. And first of the first part. Second of the second, which concerns the composition of the stone.

You, in the virtue of A., most serene Prince, take gold and cast it into the vegetable water of which we have spoken in our Codicil, in the chapter that begins, "The spirit of the recipient and formal," otherwise "general," so that you may take of the dry lunaria, etc. And in the chapter that begins, "And we wish to reveal to you concerning the nature of the menstruum, in which there is a fire against nature," and in the same chapter that begins, "Son, since that menstruum without which it cannot," etc., throughout, where we have sufficiently declared its nature and operation. And take gold in proportion to the weight of the vegetable water, and place all of it in digestion H. for six natural days, and the gold will be dissolved into a water the color of saffron. Afterwards distill the menstruum in distillation H. in a bath: then add another fresh lunaria, and place it again in digestion H. for another six natural

--- Page 456 (page 219 verso, Practica cont.) ---
RAYMVNDI LVLLI

days: and then extract it, and put what has been dissolved into another flask, and upon what has remained, when the dissolutions and distillations are finished, again add menstruum in the quantity of the earth, and place it in digestion H. for four natural days, and what has been dissolved of the metal, together with the menstruum, place in a cucurbit as before: and do this up to six inhumations and distillations, and what has remained after the six distillations from the dregs, do not concern yourself with, and the distillations or liquefactions, or even dissolutions, place in digestion H. and distill all the water through the alembic, and upon the dregs place fresh Mercury water, and place it in digestion for one month and a half: afterwards take the Q. in which the gold was putrefied, and distill the water from it in distillation H., and upon the dregs pour an equal amount of fresh menstruum, and again place the vessel in digestion H. for six natural days or ten: when these are finished, place the vessel in digestion I. and distill the air together with the menstruum. After it will no longer distill further, preserve the distillation in its vessel, and upon the dregs put another

another menstruum, and place it in digestion H. for six natural days, and again distill as before in distillation I. And upon the dregs put another fresh lunaria, and place it in digestion I. And thus you will reiterate the inhumations and distillations in H. and I. up to the twentieth, or twenty-fifth distillation and inhumation. But when you are at the twenty-second distillation, that water in which there is some portion of air together with the menstruum, you shall place for distilling in digestion H. And the entire menstruum will come out through that distillation or another: and if there is any air there, it will remain at the bottom in the manner of a splendid or citrine water. And if its quantity is no more than two drops, or three, or four, do not reiterate the inhumations and distillations. But if there is more, reiterate the inhumations and distillations in digestion H. and I. up to twenty-six or thirty distillations, so that the earth may be well evacuated of air. And in each inhumation it requires at least five continuous days, until you have distilled all the air, which without fail will all be distilled through the said distillations, if you have well regulated the master-

y. After this indeed you shall take the vessel in which all the distillations are, and distill them with digestion H. Then all the air will remain at the bottom, because nothing will be able to ascend through distillation of H. together with the menstruum. When indeed you have distilled all the menstruum, all the air will remain in the depths in the color of a most beautiful splendid water, and preserve it, because when you have the air in this way, the process has been excellent: for what you have done with the air, in the same manner we teach you concerning the abstraction of fire from earth, neither more nor less, except that the separation of fire from earth is done by inhumation with air, and each time distilling in digestion of K. Or indeed the distillation can also be done with D. and not C., because just as fire is a nobler element, therefore it requires a nobler menstruum, or its own air, and just as you distilled the air with thirty distillations. For when they have been thus separated, or you have separated the earth from its spirit, which has the power of restricting, that evacuation is done thus: Take the earth from which you extracted the elements, namely fire and air, and cast upon it some vegetable water, and place it in the digestion of H. for three days. When these are finished, extract what has been dissolved

And thus make as many inhumations in H. and evacuations in Q. until little remains of the earth. And when the said evacuation has been done, gather all the dissolutions or evacuations in an alembic, and distill through the alembic all the menstruum, and upon the dregs place as much C. as there is earth, and place it in digestion of H. for one natural day, and again distill. And again, most serene Prince, you will reiterate the distillations and inhumations until all the earth has been converted into an impalpable powder. Then upon it you will put D. or C. Then at last, most serene Prince, when you have completed these things, then you shall take all the distillations, and place the alembic over them, and distill in digestion of I. until the matter is well dried: then extract the earth from digestion of I. and place it in digestion of K. and the earth will be sublimated, and the earth will be in the manner of quicksilver by virtue of sublimation. But you, most serene Prince, when you have completed all the things we have said above, know that you have all the elements which you need for composing the philosophers' stone. When you have putrefied the white composite, the purification of the elements is better done with D. than with C., since C. retains something of superfluity: but D., being of a subtler nature, and through this the chapter of our Testament is elucidated, which begins, "When you shall have divided the body," that is, when you shall have divided the stone into four elements. Likewise in the purification of the earth,

On the sublimation of air.

Take therefore in Christ's name the air, and place it in an alembic, and you shall distill it seven times or six, the sign of which will be when nothing further wishes to be distilled. Likewise, if perchance the air has been converted into a powder, you shall distill and purify it with an equal amount of D. And in the last distillation you shall distill off D. and the air will remain below. You should know, however, most serene Prince, that what we have said concerning the distillation and purification of air is done with digestion of I. Likewise you can well purify the air with its own water, just as indeed you purified the air: so likewise do with fire in all respects as we have said concerning air, except that the purifica-

--- Page 462 (page 222 verso, De sublimatione aeris cont.) ---
RAYMVNDI LVLLI

tion of fire is done with digestion of K. and that of air with digestion of I. Likewise, most serene Prince, what we have said concerning the purification of earth, you will find in full in the Vade Mecum concerning the number of the philosophers, in the chapter that begins, "The birth indeed is through the way of sublimation found," etc., throughout the entire chapter. When therefore, most serene Prince, you have completed these things that are said above, then you will have everything from which the philosophers' stone is composed. You shall indeed make the mixture of the elements and of the things pertaining to composing the stone. Take therefore the purified elements, in which purification it is necessary that dregs remain at the bottom of the vessel. Moreover also preserve the dregs of fire and air to the side, and take fire and air, each of them placed in its own vessel, and seal the vessel with gummed wax, and place it in a clear location for three continuous days. When these are finished, take as much of the dregs of fire as of air, and of the dregs of earth as there is of air, and if the dregs of the elements are not as much as the element of air, add purified earth up to the said quantity, and put everything together with the dregs of fire and air and earth into the proper vessel of air, and seal

--- Page 463 (page 223, De sublimatione aeris cont.) ---
