the vessel very well with wax and mastic and lute: and one of these vessels, namely the vessel of air, you shall bury in the earth, and the other in which the fire is you shall suspend above in the air on another side. And arrange it so that a stone or any object does not damage the glasses, and let the said vessels stand there for one continuous year. When this is done you shall fabricate certain molds or forms of wax, in whatever shape you wish the stone to come out or be made. When these are made, open the vessel that you placed in the earth for a year, and put inside two or three molds which will float on top, and let them remain thus in the vessel and immediately seal it, and let them stay inside for the space of a quarter of an hour, a little more or less. And then indeed the mold or form will be filled with air by a wonderful and invisible constriction, because it ascends upward from the water of the earth. And when that time has elapsed, open the vessel and take the molds or forms, and hold them outside the vessel. Fill them with the water that you suspended in the air for the space of one year, and again put the molds into the first vessel where the terrestrial water is, and immediately seal the vessel, and let it stand for the space aforesaid. When that is finished, with silver forceps draw out the molds or forms, into which you put the ethereal

--- Page 464 (page 223 verso, De sublimatione aeris cont.) ---
RAYMVNDI LVLLI

or aerial water, and turn the molds so that what is above touches below, in such a way that the water you put inside the molds touches the terrestrial water. And let them stand thus for three continuous days in strong sun during summer time, and in autumn for seven days: in spring or in winter for twelve. Know indeed, most serene Prince, that the water which you put into the molds of the terrestrial one, you will find inside a regeneration congealed in the manner of gum: and this gem is indeed called by the philosophers the Elixir. And when the molds have been in the vessel for the aforesaid space of time, and have stood in the terrestrial vessel: then draw your molds out, and place them to the side in a glass vessel, dry with the stones congealed inside, and thus you shall make your stones and more besides. And therefore you should have terrestrial and aerial, or ethereal water. Most illustrious Prince, know that of the restrictive water you have expended little in the operation, since the molds are not filled except by its invisible spirit, by whose virtue the ethereal water is converted into stone: Therefore the Alchemists said, our stone is composed of two waters: of one, namely, which makes the stone volatile, and of another which fixes and hardens it.

--- Page 465 (page 224, Animae Transmutatio) ---
ANIMAE TRANSMVTATIO.

With such an invisible terrestrial spirit from the common quicksilver we indeed make pure silver, good to every assay, just as from a mine, without heat of common fire, which by reason of its virtue and its odor will thus also congeal into the most proven silver, just as the basilisk by its gaze kills every animal in an instant. You indeed, most illustrious Prince, may have your stones, namely, and according to the order of the Magisterium you can bring them to the perfection of D. Therefore place them in a certain clean glass vessel, and let them stand for ten days in the air in such a way that the vessel is suspended: when these are finished, it is necessary to place them for a third decoction, so that they may have the complement of total perfection.

There follows the first decoction, which is done with the heat of the Sun.

Take therefore your stones, and place them in a certain glass vessel, which by the Alchemists is called a perfectorium, and which is an instrument that is oblong, thin in substance: and seal the vessel very well with mastic and with flour of rye mixed with threads of linen cloth, and place it in the Sun for nine days

--- Page 466 (page [224v], Raymundi Lulli) ---
RAYMVNDI LVLLI

in Spring and in Summer or Autumn, and in Winter for eighteen days, and when these are finished, the first decoction will be finished. Then it is necessary for you to proceed to the second, which is the middle between the first and third decoction: the second decoction is that which is done differently with the first heat.

On the second decoction of the stone.

Most serene Prince, the second decoction is called Optates, and is done with the same heat, but differently: because you should take the thinnest glass vessel that you can find, and larger in quantity, and let it be filled with the clearest water, and let it be placed in the ray of the Sun, and in that lower part where it shines most, and where fire is most captured by the common magisterium, and let the aforesaid vessel with the stones be placed in such a way that the brightness of the fire touches the vessel where the stones are, and let it stand there until the Sun cannot be captured through the vessel full of water. And let this decoction be continued in Spring for eighteen days, and in Winter for fifteen days, and in Summer for eight: when these are finished, the second decoction is finished, and it is necessary to administer the third, which is the end and complement of the stone.

--- Page 467 (page 225, Animae Transmutatio) ---
ANIMAE TRANSMVTATIO.

The third decoction, which is done with the heat of common fire.

The third decoction indeed is done with the heat of common fire: and let it be in a certain furnace, which is called the Tripod of Secrets, whose figure is truly set forth in the book of the fifth essence, which we believe is not hidden from you, and let it be in this manner. Take therefore the stones which you have in the perfectorium vessel, and thus with their vessel place them in the Tripod of Secrets for the space of nine days, continuing a fire of burning coals for them: when these are finished, the last decoction is finished, which is called by the Alchemists Sulfur or Sulput D. H. And in this, most serene Prince, you have everything that is desired from the world, an incomparable and innumerable treasure, with which the most perfect end can be achieved. And it is one of the secrets to multiply those stones: but because the multiplication of the stones results in a third or fourth number, two modes of which indeed are very tedious, because they are by reduction of the stones to the first state: therefore here we explain one, but one mode, most serene Prince, is very pleasing to the senses, and it is the one that fol-

--- Page 468 (page [225v], Raymundi Lulli) ---
RAYMVNDI LVLLI

lows. And in what manner the multiplication of the philosophers' stone is done in quantity only.

On the multiplication of the stone.

This multiplication is done with common quicksilver in this manner. Take of the stones in the quantity of one scruple, and cast it upon one hundred thousand parts of common quicksilver, and so great is the fire of the stone multiplied by celestial virtues, that it can reduce all the Mercury into powder, burning all the mineral particles existing in the said Mercury, just as the gaze of the basilisk burns every animal power in an instant and kills every animal: for here you will have one hundred thousand and one weight of quicksilver converted into medicine, of which indeed one hundred thousand parts, a single part cast upon one hundred thousand ounces, reduces it into powder, just as before is evident from this powder. A second and third time place it upon one hundred thousand scruples of Mercury, and it reduces it into powder, with each new multiplication up to one hundred multiplications. Who therefore will be able to recount the multiplied quantity of multiplied medicine?

--- Page 469 (page 226, Animae Transmutatio) ---
ANIMAE TRANSMVTATIO.

Therefore, most serene Prince, because you can multiply one hundred thousand hundreds from one scruple of those multiplied scruples, or you will know how in each new multiplication to place one scruple upon one hundred thousand parts of quicksilver, and let everything become medicine. Most serene Prince, let the projection of the medicine upon Mercury be continued to such a degree, until the fire of the stone is extinguished, and the matter is converted into perfect metal, and powder is no longer made from it. There are moreover other multiplications: one indeed is called virtual, and quantitative at the same time, and each of these is done by reduction to the beginning, as is found in our Testament, in the chapter that begins, "Quam tu auras cicabades les dites coses eages sesta." When you shall have completed the said things, and made projections through the entire circle, also in the same book the multiplication here multiplied, and let it be continued, as is evident in the chapter that begins, "Fil tu prenas vna 3. de la medicina desta multiplicata," etc. Son, you shall take one scruple of the said multiplied medicine. Concerning the other multiplication indeed you will find it in the third book of the fifth essence, in the chapter that treats of multiplication.

On the practice of the branches of our stone.

And if you wish, most illustrious King, to know another method for composing our stone, which by the Alchemists is called the practice of branches, it is that you purify the earth, as we have taught you above in the practice, in that part which treats of the purification of earth in section 6, which begins, "For indeed when all the elements have been separated." Also in our Codicil, in the chapter that begins, "Ara si ercentant aqueste menstrual." Also in the chapter that begins, "Ara si neges chediten": since when you have the purified earth, then the elements from gold and silver, and purified earth of these, as said above, we recall having written in the practice in the third book of the fifth essence, in that part which treats of the purification of the elements, in the chapter that begins, "The rectification indeed of the elements can be accomplished." The earth therefore being conditioned with the number of the philosophers, which begins, "When moreover through the said dissolution M. shall have been diminished from the bodies." On the abbreviated mixture.

Then, most serene Prince, we indeed intend to proceed to the abbreviated mixtures. And the first mixture will be that you give the earth to drink of D. through continuous distillations through four parts of itself, then make it so that the earth is two weights, of air six weights, and of fire twelve. And first give the earth air to drink, incerating in a crucible, until it flows in the manner of honey, then extract the matter, and let it rest for ten days: after this repeat as it is mixed with air in its alembic over a small fire, and put drop by drop on top, until it flows like wax, and let it rest for four days: afterwards use one weight upon one hundred thousand parts, of whatever E. may be, and it will become a metal of gold or silver.

On another mixture.

And let another mixture be made thus, that you conjoin three weights of P. and two of O. and make everything into one body. Then incerate the earth

its own earth, and another. And with this fire you can incerate O. of this metal, or of that one, two together or three together in equal weight, or four.

On another mixture.

Likewise let another mixture be made, so that you take eight parts of P. and one of O. and combine them together, and place them in digestion of H. for eight days: and afterward incerate the earth of this or of any metal or of that one for solution and limus. But if you wish to administer to anyone some of P. of gold, and of O. of silver. So let there be eight parts of P., and one of copper, and mix together, and place in digestion of H. for eight days. Then incerate its own earth or another, and with this medicine you will make a mixture of perfect gold from contraries, namely one weight of O. of gold, and eight weights of P. of silver.

On another mode of mixing.

Composition of pearls.

The work of pearls is indeed prepared thus: first, that you take the water of Mercury, made by the method that we taught you in our fifth essence, in the first book, in the chapter that begins, "Ne putes me mendacium" etc. up to G. which begins, etc. Take therefore that substance etc. Also by the method of our Codicil, in the chapter that begins, The preparation of the stony and fermentable spirit is that you take the juice of lunaria. Also by the method of our Testament, in the chapter that begins, Son, you shall take the liquor of lunaria, and let it be in the quantity of two or three pounds, and in it you shall dissolve 3j. of the purest silver by the heat of digestion for six days, then separate the dregs by distillations, which will be white and beautiful: This is the water that you need for dissolving all six metals, and their quicksilver: with which indeed stones are formed, which are called pearls, by the reduction of them to their first matter.

On the first water we say.

Take 3j. of gold and dissolve it with as much quantity of the said water as seems good to you in digestion of H. just as with the silver, and let it be dissolved into a saffron-colored water, and draw the gilded water through a filter, and do not concern yourself with those dregs, which are worth nothing for the purpose: then distill the water through an alembic, and preserve the second dregs.

On the second water.

The second water is made thus. Take 3j. of Venus, and dissolve as much as you wish of the first water in digestion of H. just as you did with the other: and distill the water through a filter, until all of it has been distilled, and do not concern yourself with these dregs: and distill that water through an alembic, and preserve the second dregs.

On the third water.

The third water is made thus. Take 3j. of lead, and dissolve in as great a quantity of dissolving water as seems good to you in digestion of H. Then distill through a filter, and discard the first dregs, because

You shall make the fourth water thus. On how the fifth water is made.

The fifth water is made thus. Take 3j. of common Mercury and dissolve it in one pound with S. of dissolving water in digestion of I. and preserve that water, distill through a filter, do not concern yourself with the dregs, but preserve the second ones.

On the sixth water.

The sixth water is made thus. Take 3j. of the purest iron, and dissolve it with as great a quantity of water as seems good to you in digestion of H. Then distill as you did the other waters, and preserve the dregs

On the seventh water.

The seventh water is made thus: Take 3j. of the purest silver, and dissolve in as great a quantity of dissolving water as seems good to you, and distill through a filter, and reject the first dregs: then distill through an alembic in digestion of H. and preserve the second dregs, and of all the waters you shall preserve the dregs in their own vessel: and these waters with their dregs are the matter from which all stones are composed.

There follows on the resolution.

When therefore the metals have been resolved into water, and receiving each of those limi in its own vessel: and these limi will be resplendent with mineral virtue, and with great viscosity, which is prepared to receive the virtues of celestial influences. And each of those waters you shall store in its own glass ampulla with a long neck and round bottom, then seal the opening of the ampullae with mastic and with a leather of dressed skin, and each of the ampullae with its water you shall place in a clear open place

You indeed should be cautious in the resolution of the limi, because of each limus you should make two waters or parts, first distilling half of the limus, which you shall set aside, because from this first part is made a terrestrial water, and the other part you shall likewise distill separately, from which is made an aerial water, which is suspended in a clear open place, as was said above, for a year.

On the carbuncle.

The carbuncle is the king of all stones, just as gold is of all metals: whence if you, most serene Prince, wish to compose it artificially, so that it may be so, and more virtuous than the natural one: take from the terrestrial hardening water of gold, which you buried within for a year, and place it in a vessel; which should be made in the shape of a figure described below, round or triangulated, or quadrangulated, as it shall please. And while the wax mold is in that state, navigating in the vessel of terrestrial water for the space of a fifth part of an hour: which being finished, turn the mold, and take the stone with golden forceps or gilded silver ones: and do so in such a way that the surface of the stone touches the hardening water, and let it stand thus for three days in the sun, and then you will extract the wax mold, in which is the aerial water hardened by the virtue of the terrestrial water into the likeness of gum. Most illustrious Prince, the formation of all precious stones is one, and is done in one manner, namely they receive the end of their colors from the transformation of diverse materials until they are terminated by the virtue of the final

--- Page 484 (page 233 verso, De carbunculo cont.) ---
RAYMVNDI LVLLI

formation, which the stone receives by the virtue of common fire in its own form under the preservation of its operative virtues. When therefore you have extracted the mold from the vessel, strip the stone of the wax with which it is clothed, and give it the artetic decoction. But first let it stand for three days in the shade, and then give it that fire for nine days in spring, and in summer for 5. When this decoction is finished, give it the decoction called Artatesis, for the condition of the time stated above. Then let the decoction called sulfur follow for five continuous days, then you will draw out your stone perfected with all the virtues pertaining to it, attributed to it by nature. While indeed, most serene Prince, you make the aforesaid decoctions, know that the stone must stand in the vessel called the perfectorium, whose figure we have demonstrated below. Know therefore that all decoctions are equally administered to all stones, except the diamond, whose decoction is longer by reason of its hardness: for we do not trouble to set down their virtues in the present compendium, since our intention in the present compendium is only to treat of their composition, yet it suffices for you that all

--- Page 485 (page 234, Compositio adamantis) ---

all composed according to this teaching are of as great virtue as those which nature produced, and even stronger and possessing more virtues than natural ones, as we have experienced. And we have treated sufficiently and fully of the virtue of each in our Lapidary which we know was sent to you by the lord King Edward, most illustrious King of England. Therefore here in brief summary we write of some, that is, of their composition, so that we may render it compendious and not prolix.

Composition of the diamond.

The composition of the diamond is made thus from the nature of silver terminated into a lapidific nature. Take therefore from the terrestrial water of silver, and place it in the vessel of hardening to harden, and put inside the earthen mold for the space of time stated above. Afterward put some of the aerial water into the mold, in which the said stone is to be formed. Then give it formation in the shade for three days, which is the first formation, when the aerial water becomes gum by the virtue of the spirit of the terrestrial water. Then indeed administer to it the other formation, which is by turning the mold with forceps of gilded silver or of gold, namely that which is below to above, in such a way that the stone touches the water

--- Page 486 (page 234 verso, De balastio) ---
RAYMVNDI LVLLI

the hardening water, and let it stand there for three continuous days in strong summer sun: in Autumn indeed and Spring for six days, in winter for 12. Which being done, let it pass through the digestion called Optesis, and through the other called Optatesis, for the time that was written above concerning the philosophical stones or of the nature and art called Artatesis. And for four or three continuous days, because by reason of its hardening nature it requires more of this digestion than the others. This stone thus composed is more solid than all others. It is indeed of such solidity that neither iron nor fire can divide it, and this by reason of the loftiness of its nature, and it is more virtuous than the natural one.

On the balas ruby.

The balas ruby is a stone, created or generated from two natures of a double quicksilver equally mixed, namely from the aerial water of silver, and from the aerial water of gold composed together in its mold, place it to harden in the hardening water of silver, and in the hardening water of gold mixed together equally: then let the three digestions or decoctions follow for it, just as we said in the decoction of the philosophical stone.

And keeping the process as in the composition of the carbuncle, otherwise adding nothing to the process of the composition of the carbuncle.

On the sapphire.

The sapphire is composed from a double nature of quicksilver, that is, aquatic, namely from the aerial water of silver, and from the aerial water of tin in a fourth part of the weight of silver, and likewise from the hardening water of silver, and from the hardening water of tin of the aforesaid weight, hardened in the wax mold, and decocted as was said above concerning the others.

On the balandine stone.

The balandine is composed from the quicksilver of iron, and is of a very red color, and it shines by reason of the sulfur decocted and converted into the nature of aerial ignited water, looking toward the nature of quicksilver, and because its nature is from air, therefore it restricts blood. Take therefore from the aerial water of iron, and fill the wax mold after the restrictive virtue has been received, and harden it in the terrestrial restrictive water of iron, and proceed through the formations described above.

On the composition of turquoise.

The turquoise is composed from the nature of two aerial waters, and two

restrictive waters. Take therefore 3 fourths of the aerial water of Venus, and j 3 of the aerial water of Saturn, and place it to harden in the wax mold in the hardening water of Venus, mixed with the terrestrial water of lead by the weight of the said aerial waters, and give it the formations of the other stones.

On the composition of the emerald.

The emerald is composed or generated from one nature of two aerial waters, and two restrictive waters. Take as many parts as you wish of the terrestrial water of silver, and with it mix as much as you wish of the terrestrial water of copper, until you see that it has an intense color or a faint one according to what you wish, and place first the wax mold in the terrestrial waters for the space of a fifth part of an hour, so that it may be better filled with the hardening virtue, first fill the mold with the aerial water of copper, and place it to harden for one part of an hour. Then indeed turn the mold, as was said concerning the carbuncle, in such a way that the hardening or terrestrial water touches the congealed aerial water, and let it stand thus for three continuous days in strong summer sun, and in spring and in autumn for six continuous days, in winter for

On the composition of the Emerald.

The Entropia of the Emerald is composed from one tura, from which the Emerald is composed by weight and mixtures of its waters. But before the water of copper comes to perfect hardening, so that it is in thickness like albumen: take your mold with tongs from the hardening water, and put upon the half-hardened water 4 or 5 mineral drops of the aerial water of iron, then lift the mold with silver tongs, and place it in the hardening water for one part of an hour. Afterward extract it, and place it in the hardening water for one Credo in Deum and one Salve Regina, and give it hardening for three days in strong sunlight in the terrestrial water of the Emerald, rotating the mold as we said of the others: afterward extract it from the wax, and give it informations, as we said of the Emerald.

On the composition of the Cameo.

The Cameo is a stone having many colors and diverse forms: for some are white, and others black, and others red, and others between black and azure and white like chalcedonies. And each of these has its own distinct nature and virtue in composition: [?] other than all the stone [?] the red one is, but both are [?] according to its own proper nature. The white is composed from the aerial water of silver, and from the aerial water of tin, and from the aerial water of lead equally mixed, placed to harden in a mold having the figure of a man in the hardening water of silver and tin in equal weights. The red is composed from the aerial water of gold and the aerial water of iron equally placed to harden in the terrestrial water of gold and in the terrestrial water of iron mixed in equal parts: or it can be composed from the aerial water of silver equally mixed, placed to harden in the hardening water of gold and silver. Likewise, the black is composed from the aerial water of lead, and from the aerial water of silver, and from the aerial water of gold, hardened in the terrestrial water of silver and in the terrestrial water of lead in the aforesaid weight. Afterward placed in the sun-

--- Page 491 (page 237, Animae Transmutatio cont.) ---

in the sun, and let it pass through all three informations.

How the Topaz is composed.

The Topaz is composed from the double nature of quicksilver reduced into the species of air. Take therefore the aerial water of gold, and mix with it a fourth part of the aerial water of iron, and [?] to harden in its hardening water of gold, and [?] as we said of the others.

On the composition of the Chalcedony.

The Chalcedony is composed from the terrestrial water of iron, and from the terrestrial water of silver mixed equally, and placed in the mold to harden in the terrestrial water of iron and of the moon mixed equally. And let it pass through all three informations like the others.

On the composition of the Beryl.

The Beryl is composed from the double substance of quicksilver: one indeed is from the aerial water of silver, and the other from the aerial water of tin, which is brought into the nature of air mixed equally, and fill your mold so that it hardens in the terrestrial water of silver and tin. Then give it informations as we said of the others.

--- Page 492 (page 237 verso, Animae Transmutatio cont.) ---
RAYMVNDI LVLLI

On the composition of pearls.

The composition of pearls is made from the triple substance of quicksilver converted into the substance of aerial water. Take therefore the aerial water of tin, and the aerial water of lead, and the aerial water of silver equally mixed, and let it be hardened in a wax mold in the hardening water of tin, and in the hardening water of silver and lead [?] equally, afterward let it pass through the informations which we described above.

On the composition of pearls by another method.

Pearls are composed by another method thus: Pulverize fine specimens into the most subtle powder, and dissolve them in such water in which you dissolved common silver upon digestion of I, placing of the said water which floats above the pearls to the thickness of half a finger, and after dissolution a certain sliminess will float up in the manner of a certain web. And this web, Most Serene Prince, is the transparent form of oriental pearls. And by this which we have said, that chapter of our testament is elucidated, in that part which treats of the confection of pearls, which begins, The display of the virtues of the overflowing liquor. Then, after you per-

--- Page 493 (page 238, Animae Transmutatio cont.) ---
ANIMAE TRANSMVTATIO.

perceive that the pearls have been liquefied within water M, you will separate the water with the sliminess of the pearls into another vessel, inclining the vessel gently so that nothing of the sediment goes with the water of the sliminess. If indeed with the water of the sliminess there should be any sediment, strain it through a well-cleaned silk cloth: after this place that water, in which the slimi[?] pear[?] with, in a flask with a long neck in digestion of I [?] for [?] days, which having passed, that water in the sliminess of the metal will become one entire body. And by this is elucidated the chapter of our testament which begins, When you neuras cell distes partes est pasta blanca sacram liquefactes per la virtue de laquet. Afterward you shall have your molds of pure gold, or of silver gilded on the interior part, formed in the likeness of whatever pearls fon[?]. And likewise a rod of gold or gilded silver, and make it so that all molds are divided into two parts. And if the molds are round, or made in whatever other manner, and [?] it requires holes, you will perforate those parts of the molds, join one to the other, and fill your molds with the paste of the powder which remained from the slimy water so that each part of the molds is paste,

--- Page 494 (page 238 verso, Animae Transmutatio cont.) ---
RAYMVNDI LVLLI

full, and through the hole of the molds you will thread one filament of gold: and so you will make them all, and allow the paste to stand thus in its molds for one natural day: which having passed, if you see that the molds can be separated from the paste, pull those grains from the molds, and if not, allow them to dry until you can extract those grains. And when you have extracted those grains from the molds already made, place them to dry on a marble wire rack, or on a certain wire rack of glass, or some glass object, which [?] flat and level. And cover with a certain glass, or with some object, lest dust touch those stones. And let them stand there until they are well dried. And by this which we have briefly said, the chapter in our testament is elucidated, in our mastery which begins, moreover afterward the artifi[?]al method will be made of gold and the quantity etc. I[?] after the formation of pearls and [?] their drying, you shall take one long filament of gold, and thread it through the hole of the pearls, and take care that as much as possible, you do not touch it with your hand, but only with certain tongs of gilded silver, or of gold: and when you have them thus, have a certain glass vessel, made in the manner of a hen's egg, and you will hollow it on

--- Page 495 (page 239, Animae Transmutatio cont.) ---
ANIMAE TRANSMVTATIO.

both sides. That which is within must be hollowed. Likewise, place the pearls suspended by a filament of gold in the water of sliminess, which you placed for nine days in digestion of H, in such a way that they are submerged in the water, and immediately place the cover, and let them stand thus for two natural days: which [?] [?] having passed, extract your pearls from the small vessel, and place them in the shade where the sun does not touch them for three natural days. Then administer to them the formation Optasis in this manner: You, Most Serene Prince, shall place three specimens on a filament of gold, then place them in an eggshell which covers all three pearls, or more of their quantity. Then others according to the quantity of the egg which it will wish to contain within itself, and place the glass vessel or egg: and so forth, until they have been arranged. And if it is summertime, let them stand thus in the sun for three natural days. In spring and autumn for six days, in winter for nine. But we advise no coctio Obteticantes. We advise you not to undertake any in winter, and each day you will rotate the eggs or the aforesaid vessels every three hours, and when these days are finished, you will extract your pearls; then

--- Page 496 (page 239 verso, Animae Transmutatio cont.) ---
RAYMVNDI LVLLI

administer to them the formation for three days Insulderh, with them placed in the vessel which is called the perfectorium: which being finished, place your pearls on the tripod of the Athanor, and you will see that they do not fall short of natural ones in every perfection, then use them.

Another composition of pearls.

If however you wish to perform this fabrication and [?] similarly from the shells found on the seashore, which have many colors: for some are white, or common pearls with an oriental color, some saffron-colored in the manner of the purest gold similarly having an oriental color. Others with the color of brazilwood having similarly an oriental color. You can therefore, Most Illustrious Prince, compose your pearls from whatever shells. And the[?] will be of those colors from which the shells were, from which they were composed. And a most beautiful work is made from all of these. We have not seen, however, a region in which the aforesaid shells are found, except in Cyprus near the city of Famagusta, and in the region of Portugal, near the city of Lisbon, on the seashore. And in a certain town of England which is called Conilla, and in another place which is called-

--- Page 497 (page 240, Animae Transmutatio cont.) ---
ANIMAE TRANSMVTATIO.

is called the port of Silvena as far as Saint Vincent at the ends of the earth. For we have seen all these things while we were crossing to England on account of the intercession of the illustrious lord King Edward. Therefore all the things we have told you, both about the philosophers' stones and about the virtuous or precious stones, suffice for the sake of brevity. And because this teaching has been handed down to you, you can compose any others by receiving the color of each stone, and their forms and figures, and then making a mixture of diverse waters according to the teaching previously delivered. And if you, Most Illustrious Prince, have carried out these things which we have written in plain words without any subtlety of operation, you will find everything that we have said. Do not, however, hasten, nor cease from the work. For nature does not act on account of threats. And if nature has turned aside on account of threats, work more than you wish for yourself. If indeed, Most Illustrious Prince, you wish to know the reason which is natural, it is because the water of this teaching has such virtue that by artifice something is composed from their nature: on the one hand these stones, so that they may be more potent in virtue than natural ones; on the other hand the philosophers' stones, which you

--- Page 498 (page 240 verso, Animae Transmutatio - end) ---
RAY. LVL. ANI. TRANSMV.

should seek in our lapidary, because we have treated at sufficient length in the first part how to give an account of this kind. And thus it will have the complement of all things, since our intention in the present compendium is nothing other than bare practice and theory, with the aid of Him who is three in person, one in essence, reigning through all ages of ages. Amen.

*

End of the compendium of the transmutation of the soul of metals.

On the signi-

--- Page 499 (page 241, De significatione literarum) ---

On the signification of the letters.

A Signifies God, who is the first cause of this work.
B Signifies the four elements confused in metals.
C Sig. the rectified lunaria in which metals are dissolved.
D Sig. the fifth essence of wine, or the human fifth essence.
E Sig. the soul of metals, which we call tura or the nature of nature.
F Signifies the metals themselves.
G Signifies the slimes of the elements.
H Sig. the heat of the first degree of the bath.
I Signifies the heat of the second degree, namely of ashes.
K Sig. the heat of the third degree, the sublimation of sulfur.
L Sig. the soul of bodies, that is, the immediate calx generated through the dissolution of the menstruum.
M Signifies the spirits of perfect bodies.
N Signifies the water of metals.
O Signifies air.
P Signifies fire.
Q Signifies glass instruments.
R Signifies the stone itself.
